# Sculpture, Pottery and Iconography - Live Learning Session

> **Learner-first lock:** This session teaches you to pick up any Indian sculpted or moulded object - a steatite seal, a fired-clay figurine, a polished sandstone yaksha, a grey schist Buddha, a limestone narrative medallion, a cast bronze icon, a glazed jar from a modern workshop - and say four things about it with evidence: what it is made of, what it is doing, what system of signs it belongs to, and how far the date and the name attached to it can be trusted. It runs from the Harappan bronze-casters to the potters' workshops that still hold a geographical indication today. The buildings that house this sculpture belong to the architecture topics. The theology of what an image means to a worshipper belongs to the religion topic. Heritage law, protected-monument counts and institutional conservation policy belong to the heritage topic. What is owned here is the object itself: material, technique, form, attribute, school, chronology and the discipline of attribution.

## How to read this session

Work down the lessons in order. Each one opens with the question a learner actually has at that point, draws or tabulates the object before naming its parts, and only then supplies the technical vocabulary. Five habits are built deliberately, because these five are what separate a safe sculpture answer from an unsafe one.

1. **Start with the material, not the adjective.** "Beautiful", "graceful" and "majestic" earn nothing. "Spotted red sandstone", "greenish-white limestone", "bluish-grey sandstone later replaced by stucco", "fire-baked clay worked by pinching" are checkable, and each one restricts what the carver or caster could physically do. Material is the first evidence, not the background.
2. **Separate the object from the label.** Almost every famous Harappan object in this topic carries a modern museum name that reads like an identification - Pashupati, Mother Goddess, Priest-King, Dancing Girl. The script that would confirm or refute those names is undeciphered. Naming the object is safe; asserting the identity is not.
3. **Separate origin from peak.** The dynasty most famous for a form is often not the dynasty that invented it. The topic supplies its own model case: the earliest known Nataraja is not Chola. Whenever a question invites "the Cholas created", "Gandhara first made" or "the Guptas introduced", slow down and check whether the source says *created* or says *perfected*.
4. **Read attributes as a system, not as decoration.** Indian iconography is legible because it is coded. A drum, a flame, a raised palm, a bull below a seated figure, a wheel on an empty seat - each is an assigned sign. Identification and interpretation are therefore the same careful operation, and an answer that names the attribute and then says what the attribute does will always outscore an answer that admires the figure.
5. **State the limit of the evidence inside the answer.** Sculpture is strong evidence of what patrons chose to make visible and of the technique available to make it. It is weak evidence of what ordinary people believed or how they lived. Examiners reward the candidate who says this rather than the candidate who treats a carved panel as a photograph.

Everything is drawn in text. No object, comparison or process in this session needs an image file.

```text
                  |

   archaeological one - the distinction is itself examinable)
                  |
   ATTRIBUTION, PROVENANCE, REPATRIATION  ->  ANSWER CONSTRUCTION
```

**Boundary map:** the architecture topics own the stupa, the cave, the pillar as a structure and the temple as a building, including the programme of ornament that is structurally part of a building; this session takes the sculpted object and the sign system, and touches placement only where placement is what makes the image work. Ancient and medieval history own the political chronology of the Mauryas, Shungas, Kushanas, Satavahanas, Guptas, Cholas and Chandellas; this session uses a dynasty only as a patron and a date bracket. The religion topic owns what an image means doctrinally - what Tandava signifies inside Shaiva theology, what liberation means to a Jain - while this session reads the same image as art-historical evidence of belief and patronage. Classical dance owns posture as performance; the shared vocabulary is noted where it helps identification and routed otherwise. Heritage institutions, protected-monument counts, inscription totals, restitution law and current conservation policy belong to the heritage topic; this session states a status fact only where it identifies the object, and says so when it does.

*Six columns, six separate factual risks. The commonest failure in this topic is the third: treating an assigned sign as ornament, which converts a readable image into a vague adjective.*

### 1. The exact scope, stated once

This topic covers sculpture and pottery from the Harappan civilisation to the Mughal period, together with the iconographic vocabulary - mudras, attributes, the Nataraja reading, the Yaksha and Yakshi figures - that any question on a named image requires. It also carries the living clay and metal craft traditions, because a standard Mains demand asks about styles of pottery making in India and because the category distinction between an excavated ceramic ware and a present-day craft is itself worth a mark.

That scope statement contains a planning instruction. Because this is the only owner of freestanding sculpture, pottery and the iconographic system, nothing may be deferred elsewhere on the ground that another topic knows the building better. But because it owns *objects and signs*, three neighbouring bodies of knowledge are deliberately not yours:

- **The architecture topics own the monument.** The stupa's dome, the pillar's shaft, the cave's excavation sequence, the temple's plan and elevation belong there. You own the figure carved on the gateway, the animal on the capital, the icon in the sanctum, and the sign system they use.
- **Ancient and medieval history own the dynasty.** The Kushana conquest, the Satavahana state, the Chola administration belong there. You own the workshop the dynasty financed and the idiom it produced.
- **The religion topic owns the doctrine.** What the Buddha's enlightenment means, what a Tirthankara's liberation means, what Shiva's dance means theologically belong there. You read the same objects as art-historical evidence of what was worshipped, by whom, and with what means.

The boundary protects you. A sculpture answer that drifts into the Kushana political settlement loses the thread; a sculpture answer that stays with material, technique, attribute, patron and placement can afford to be short, because every sentence is doing work.

### 2. The six variables, applied once to a single object

Take one object and run the six variables to see what they extract. The object is the Parkham Yaksha, found south of Mathura city in Uttar Pradesh.

| Variable | Applied to the Parkham Yaksha | What the answer gives you |
|---|---|---|
| Material | Stone, carved in the Mauryan-period manner with the characteristic high polish | Ties a popular cult image to the technical standard of the court workshop, which is the lesson's real surprise |
| Posture and gesture | Frontal and monumental; the source's own description is that it "speaks of massive strength and gravity in imagery" | Converts "large statue" into a formal judgement that can be argued |
| Iconographic attribute | A Yaksha, that is a folk deity of water, fertility, trees, forest and wilderness, and a figure of worship across Jainism, Hinduism and Buddhism | Explains why the same figure type reappears in three religious settings for centuries |
| Patron | Not a named king; a popular cult whose images were nonetheless made at court-level quality | The best single piece of evidence against a purely court-driven art history |
| Site context | Found south of Mathura city, in the region that becomes the great red-sandstone sculpture centre | Links the Mauryan period to the later Mathura school, which the source itself says modelled its images on earlier yaksha images |
| Cross-site comparison | Set beside the Didarganj Yakshi, the whisk-bearer of the same figure family | Gives you a pair rather than a specimen, and a pair is always the stronger answer unit |

Notice what happened. Six columns turned one statue into an argument about the relationship between popular religion, court technique and a later regional school. That is the whole method, and it will be re-run in this session on a Gandhara Buddha, a Chola bronze, a Khajuraho relief and a pot.

### 3. The three kinds of date, and the collapse that produces false sentences

Three different dates attach to nearly every object here, and they are almost never the same date.

```text
  DATE 1: when the object was made
          e.g. the Kalyanasundara Murti - 9th century AD
                    |
  DATE 2: when the type, school or phase to which it belongs is dated
          e.g. the Amaravati school flourished mainly under the
               Satavahanas, and was later also patronised by the
               Ikshvakus in the 3rd-4th century AD
                    |
  DATE 3: when the object was found, excavated, renamed, moved,
          displayed or returned
          e.g. the Amaravati stupa rediscovered after a visit in 1797;
               first excavated 1845; sculptures collected and taken away
```

The commonest false sentence in this topic is produced by collapsing these. "The Cholas created the Nataraja in bronze in the ninth century" merges an origin claim, a school claim and a date claim, and the source contradicts the first of them outright. The habit that prevents it is to say what kind of date you are writing: *made* in, *belongs to* a phase dated, *recorded or recovered* in.

### 4. Label is not identity: the rule that saves the Harappan section

Several of the most famous objects in this topic carry modern names that sound like identifications:

- the **Pashupati Seal** - a steatite seal from Mohenjo-daro showing a human figure or deity seated cross-legged in a three-horned headgear, surrounded by animals;
- the **Mother Goddess** - a crude standing female figure found at many Indus sites;
- the **Bearded Priest** - a steatite figure from Mohenjo-daro, draped in a trefoil-patterned shawl, eyes elongated and half closed as in meditation;
- the **Dancing Girl** - a four-inch bronze from Mohenjo-daro.

Each name is a convenience of modern scholarship. The Harappan script survives on the seals and remains undeciphered; the writing runs mostly right to left, sometimes bi-directionally, and no reading of it authorises any of these names. So the safe register is: *the object conventionally called the Pashupati seal*, *the figure conventionally called the Mother Goddess*. You lose nothing in precision and you gain the examiner's confidence, because the caution is itself a piece of knowledge.

The corresponding rule for later material is the reverse. When an inscription exists, it outranks a stylistic guess. The headless Mathura sculpture discovered in 1911 outside Mathura carries a Brahmi inscription on its base declaring it to be Kanishka, the great Kushana king. That is an identification of a different order from "Priest-King", and the difference should be visible in your sentence.

### 5. What "where owned" means in practice

Two kinds of honesty are required repeatedly in this topic, and both are examinable.

**The first is source asymmetry.** The core account of this chapter is very full on the Harappan objects, the post-Mauryan schools, the Nataraja and South Indian temple sculpture, and comparatively thin on several tables whose printed columns are cut off in the text that survives for study - the pottery features column, the third column of the school-comparison table and the feature column of the modern craft-pottery list. Where a column is not legible, this session teaches what is legible, says so, and refuses to complete the row from memory.

**The second is category boundaries.** The most dangerous single confusion in the whole topic is between an *archaeological ceramic category* - Painted Grey Ware, Northern Black Polished Ware, Black and Red Ware - and a *modern craft category* - Blue Pottery, Khurja Pottery, Nizamabad Black Pottery. The first is defined by fabric, firing, distribution and stratigraphy and is used to date a site. The second is defined by workshop, region, market and, in several cases, a geographical indication. They are not points on one continuum, and a question that mixes them is testing exactly this distinction.

### Examination use

This lesson is not directly examinable by itself, and that is its function: it is the control layer that keeps the rest of the topic safe. Its six variables supply the paragraph structure for almost any sculpture answer, and they are reusable on an unfamiliar object in a future paper, which is this topic's main insurance. Its three-date rule prevents the commonest factual accident. Its label-versus-identity rule is worth a mark in any Harappan question and is directly relevant to objective items that pair a famous object with a claimed meaning. Its category rule is the spine of any pottery answer. The scope statement supplies both the opening and the closing line: this topic owns objects and signs; the building, the dynasty and the doctrine around them belong elsewhere.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "Pashupati", "Mother Goddess" and "Priest-King" as decoded identities rather than modern labels.
- Writing a school's floruit as if it were an individual object's date of manufacture.
- Calling an assigned iconographic sign "decoration", which forfeits the whole reading method.
- Assuming that an archaeological ware and a living craft pottery belong to one sequence.
- Filling a clipped or illegible source column from general memory to make a table look complete.

### Revision notes

1. Scope: sculpture and pottery from the Harappan civilisation to the Mughal period, plus the iconographic vocabulary and the living clay and metal craft traditions.
2. The architecture topics own the monument; history owns the dynasty; religion owns the doctrine; this topic owns the object, the technique and the sign.
3. Six reading variables: material, posture and gesture, iconographic attribute, patron, site context, cross-site comparison.
4. Applied to the Parkham Yaksha they yield an argument, not a description: popular cult, court technique, later Mathura inheritance.
5. Three kinds of date - made, belongs to a phase, recovered or moved - and the rule to say which one you mean.
6. Label is not identity: Pashupati, Mother Goddess, Priest-King and Dancing Girl are modern names; the script on the seals is undeciphered.
7. Where an inscription exists it outranks style: the headless Mathura figure found in 1911 is identified as Kanishka by a Brahmi inscription on its base.
8. Report source asymmetry rather than filling it; several printed table columns are clipped and are not reconstructed here.
9. Archaeological ware and craft pottery are different categories, defined by different evidence and used for different purposes.
10. Sculpture is strong evidence of patron choice and available technique, and weak evidence of ordinary belief and behaviour; say so inside the answer.
11. Every claim should carry a material, a named object or a dated phase - preferably two of the three.
12. The reading method is transferable: the same six columns work on a seal, a stupa relief, a bronze icon and a pot.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Harappans worshipped Shiva, as proved by the Pashupati seal, and their Priest-King shows that they had a priestly ruling class." Identify what is wrong with the reasoning and write a version that keeps everything the evidence actually supports.

**Model answer:** Both claims convert a modern label into a finding. The seal is a steatite object from Mohenjo-daro showing a human figure or deity seated cross-legged, wearing a three-horned headgear and surrounded by animals - an elephant and a tiger on one side, a rhinoceros and a buffalo on the other, with two antelopes below the seat. Nothing on it is read, because the pictographic script on the seals remains undeciphered; "Pashupati" is a name given by scholars because the figure is surrounded by animals, not a reading of the inscription. The same applies to the bearded steatite figure conventionally called the Priest-King, which is described by its shawl with trefoil patterns and its elongated, half-closed eyes, and which supports no statement about political or priestly office. A safe version reads: "Harappan visual culture included a recurring seated horned figure surrounded by animals, conventionally called the Pashupati seal, and a bearded male figure in a trefoil shawl; both are modern identifications, the script is undeciphered, and later religious categories must not be read backwards into them."

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that caution is a weaker answer. It is the opposite. The examiner already knows the conventional names; what distinguishes a strong script is that it can describe the object precisely *and* state what the object cannot prove, which is exactly the skill a source-criticism directive is testing.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Indian sculpture becomes historical evidence only when it is read through material, technique and attribution rather than through appearance." Comment. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is sound, because each of the three converts an impression into a checkable claim.

Material restricts what could be made: fire-baked clay worked by pinching yields the small, comparatively crude Harappan terracottas the source itself contrasts with the bronzes; the lost-wax method yields the free limbs of a cast icon; spotted red sandstone, bluish-grey sandstone and greenish-white limestone identify three different workshops before a single figure is described. Technique dates and locates: the Mauryan polish, the shift at Gandhara from blue schist and green phyllite to stucco and then to grey stone, and the post-Gupta appearance of the ring-stirrup in equestrian reliefs are all technical facts with chronological consequences. Attribution disciplines the rest: a Brahmi inscription naming Kanishka identifies the headless Mathura figure, whereas "Pashupati" and "Priest-King" identify nothing.

Appearance is not worthless - the source's own judgement of "massive strength and gravity" at Parkham is a formal reading that carries argument - but it must rest on the other three.

Read this way, a sculpture is a document; read only for beauty, it is an illustration.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the evidence-versus-appearance thesis 2 + one worked material claim, one worked technique claim and one worked attribution claim, each with a named object 6 + the qualification that formal reading still has a place 1 + a verdict in the question's own terms 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 2:** The method now needs its materials. Before any school, dynasty or icon can be discussed, you have to know what stone, clay, stucco and metal each allow a maker to do, and what the difference is between cutting a figure back into a surface and freeing it entirely. That is the next lesson.

*Five operations, four materials, five different consequences. The single most useful sentence in a sculpture answer is the one that connects the consequence back to the operation - for example, that a cast bronze can have free, outflung limbs because the metal replaces a wax model that was itself free-standing, whereas a stone figure's limbs must stay attached to the block or to a strut.*

### 1. Why stone, metal and terracotta dominate

The core account opens with a practical explanation that is worth quoting in substance because it is a rare instance of a source giving a physical reason rather than an aesthetic one: the use of stone, metal and terracotta is widespread in the sculpture of the Indian subcontinent *because of the climatic condition of the region*. Organic media - wood, ivory, textile, unfired clay - survive badly in a monsoonal climate with wide temperature swings. What you are studying is therefore already a filtered sample. The sculpture that survives is disproportionately the sculpture made of the things that last.

That is a genuine analytical point and it has two uses. First, it explains a silence: if a period looks poor in sculpture, the correct first hypothesis is preservation, not absence of production. Second, it explains a pattern: terracotta survives best where it was *fired and then buried* - seals, plaques, figurines in occupation debris - or where it was *built into a wall*, which is exactly what happens in the Bengal brick tradition, where burnt bricks and clay, known as terracotta bricks, were the principal building material.

### 2. Terracotta: the cheapest medium, and the one the source is frankest about

Terracotta means the use of fire-baked clay for making sculptures. The Harappan terracottas were made using the **pinching** method - the form is squeezed up out of a lump by hand rather than carved or cast - and the source's own comparative judgement is unusually blunt: compared with the bronze figures, the terracotta sculptures found were **less in number and crude in shape and form**. They have been found mostly at sites in Gujarat and at Kalibangan. The examples given are the figure conventionally called the Mother Goddess, a mask of a horned deity, and toys.

Three points follow, and they matter far beyond the Harappan section.

- **Crudity is a technique statement, not a verdict on a civilisation.** The same society that pinched those figures also cast the Dancing Girl by the lost-wax method and cut fine steatite seals. A corpus can contain several technical registers at once, and an answer that says "Harappan sculpture was crude" has misread the evidence.
- **Pinching is not the only clay technique.** Later Indian terracotta is heavily *moulded*: the Gupta period yields a large collection of terracotta plaques at Bikaner and Ahichchhatra, distinctive terracotta heads from Akhnur in Kashmir, Buddha figures and ornaments at the Devnimori stupa in Gujarat, and numerous objects from Bhita. A mould standardises and repeats; a pinched figure cannot. The shift from pinched to moulded clay is a shift from the household to the workshop.
- **Clay also records technology.** Terracotta models of ploughs at Bahawalpur and Banawali are evidence of the use of the plough. A clay object can be a document about something other than art.

### 3. Metal: the complete lost-wax sequence, learned as a sequence

The single most important technical passage in this entire topic is the description of **cire-perdue**, the lost-wax method, which the source states was known to Indian artists from the time of the Indus Valley culture. Learn it as five ordered steps, because objective items and process questions both reward the order.

```text
  STEP 1   A figure is modelled in WAX.
              |
  STEP 2   The wax figure is coated with WET CLAY and allowed to dry.
              |
  STEP 3   The clay-coated figure is HEATED, so the wax inside melts.
              |
  STEP 4   The wax is POURED OUT through a tiny hole, and LIQUID METAL
           is poured into the hollow mould.
              |
  STEP 5   After the metal cools and solidifies, the CLAY COAT IS
           REMOVED, leaving a metal figure of the same shape as the
           original wax figure.
```

Four consequences follow directly from that sequence, and each is an answer point.

1. **The mould is destroyed to release the object.** Every true lost-wax casting is therefore unique unless the wax itself was produced from a mould. This is why "a Chola bronze" is a type, not an edition.
2. **The sculptor's real work is in wax, not in metal.** Whatever the wax could do, the metal will reproduce - which is why cast figures can have outflung arms, a raised leg and open space between limbs, and why the Nataraja is physically possible as a bronze and awkward as a free-standing stone figure.
3. **Metal is portable.** A cast image can be carried, processed and taken away - which is the technical precondition of the processional icon and, much later, of the trade in removed objects that Lesson 20 handles.
4. **The technique never died.** The source records plainly that even today the same technique is practised in many parts of the country. That single clause is the strongest continuity evidence in the topic, and it is a *technique* continuity, not a style continuity - a distinction Lesson 19 will need.

The named Harappan examples are the bronze Dancing Girl of Mohenjo-daro and the bronze bull of Kalibangan; the chapter opening also traces the sculptural tradition back to the Indus Valley through the small terracotta figurines and the bronze chariot of Daimabad.

### 4. Stone: choosing the block, and the polish that dates a workshop

Stone in this topic is never generic. Five stones do most of the work, and each carries information:

| Stone | Where it identifies a workshop or phase | What it permits |
|---|---|---|
| **Steatite** | The standard Harappan seal material, a soft stone found in river beds | Fine incised detail at a very small scale; the seal figures and the bearded male figure are steatite |
| **Red sandstone** | A Harappan male torso from Harappa; then, decisively, the **spotted red sandstone** of the Mathura school | Medium-grained, workable, warm-coloured; supports bold volume and deep cutting |
| **Bluish-grey sandstone, blue schist and green phyllite** | The early Gandhara workshops | Fine grain that takes the sharp, incised folds and anatomical modelling the Gandhara idiom depends on |
| **Greenish-white limestone** | Amaravati | Takes crisp low-relief narrative carving across a curved surface |
| **Cream-coloured sandstone** | The Gupta school around Sarnath | Even surface, subdued colour, suited to the restrained modelling and thin drapery of that idiom |

Add one more material fact with chronological weight: the Harappan seals were most commonly steatite, but **agate, chert, copper, faience and terracotta** seals were also found, and some instances of **gold and ivory** seals are recorded. A single object class can span six materials, which is why "the Harappans used steatite" needs the word "mostly".

**Polish as a technical marker.** The Mauryan-period sculpture carries a characteristic high polish, and the Ashokan pillars are described as having a highly polished and lustrous finish which, like the bell-shaped capitals, is attributed to Iranian influence. Polish is therefore doing two jobs at once: it is a technical achievement and it is evidence of contact. It is also, honestly, a dating argument that can be contested, which is exactly why the Didarganj figure's chronology is debated - the polish supports a Mauryan attribution, and stylistic arguments for a later date exist. Hold both.

### 5. Stucco: the medium that marks a phase change

Stucco is lime plaster built up over a core or armature. It appears in this topic at one decisive moment: in the Gandhara sequence, the sculptures were initially made from blue schist and green phyllite, then the use of stucco began, and from the third century AD the school shifted completely to grey stone. The Basic owner's shorter formulation is that the early Gandhara school used bluish-grey sandstone while the later period saw the use of mud and stucco.

Two readings are available and you should be able to give both. Stucco is faster and cheaper than cutting hard stone, so a shift to stucco can indicate scale of production - more images, more quickly, for more shrines. It is also less durable, which partly explains why the surviving record is patchier for that phase. The honest position is that the source records the material sequence without explaining the cause; the explanations above are inferences and should be labelled as such in an answer.

### 6. Relief and work in the round: the distinction that organises the rest of the session

This is a formal distinction, and it controls where an object can be placed and what it can show.

```text
  IN THE ROUND                    HIGH RELIEF                 LOW RELIEF
  free on all sides               figures project strongly    figures raised
  |                               from the ground, deeply     slightly from a
  |                               undercut, but still         flat ground
  |                               attached
  |                               |                           |
  can be circumambulated,         reads from a distance,      reads close up,
  carried, installed in a         holds shadow, survives      carries continuous
  sanctum, processed              as architecture             narrative
  |                               |                           |
  e.g. the cast bronze icon;      e.g. the figural            e.g. the Amaravati
  the monolithic Nandi; the       programmes of medieval      narrative medallion
  Parkham Yaksha; the             temple walls                telling a whole
  Didarganj whisk-bearer                                      episode
```

Why the distinction earns marks: **narrative needs a ground**. A story has to unfold across a surface, so narrative art belongs to relief - which is precisely why Amaravati, the school that emphasised dynamic images and narrative art, is a relief school working on limestone railings, panels and medallions, while the schools that focused on single images produce figures that stand alone. Equally, **ritual mobility needs freedom**: an image to be bathed, dressed and carried in procession must be in the round and, for practical weight and strength, is best cast.

Two placement facts follow, and they are the boundary with the architecture topics: sculpture in the early Buddhist context was used primarily for the decoration of stupas, on the **torana** and the **medhi**, and as a form of religious expression; and in temple architecture the **vahana**, the mount or vehicle of the main deity, is placed just before the sanctum sanctorum. In both cases the placement is part of the meaning, which is why this session names it - and the structure that carries it belongs to the architecture topics, which is why this session stops there.

### Examination use

Material and technique questions are a recurring family. A direct Mains demand in the core source's reproduced list asks about the major different styles of unglazed pottery making in India, and the technical vocabulary built here is what makes that answer specific rather than a list of place names. Objective items reward exactly this level: an item reproduced in the chapter asks what the Dancing Girl is made of, and another asks which material was used in making Harappan seals. For descriptive answers, the lost-wax sequence in its five steps is one of the few processes in the whole art-and-culture syllabus that can be written out in order and scored, and the relief-versus-round distinction is the cleanest way to explain why Amaravati is a narrative school and why the Nataraja is a bronze.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing "terracotta" as though it meant only Harappan figurines; it is a continuous Indian medium, pinched at first and later moulded, and it becomes a building material.
- Describing the whole Harappan corpus as crude because the terracottas are described that way.
- Giving the lost-wax steps out of order, or omitting the destruction of the clay coat, which is the step that makes each casting unique.
- Calling Gandhara sculpture "stucco" without the sequence, or "grey stone" without the date from which that became complete.
- Treating relief and work in the round as a stylistic preference rather than as a choice with consequences for narrative and for ritual use.
- Attributing the Mauryan polish so confidently that the Didarganj dating debate disappears.

### Revision notes

1. Stone, metal and terracotta dominate Indian sculpture partly because of the region's climatic conditions; the surviving sample is therefore filtered by durability.
2. Terracotta is fire-baked clay; the Harappan figures were made by pinching and are described as fewer and cruder than the bronzes; found mostly in Gujarat and Kalibangan; examples include the so-called Mother Goddess, a horned-deity mask and toys.
3. Later terracotta is moulded and standardised: Gupta plaques at Bikaner and Ahichchhatra, heads from Akhnur, Buddha figures and ornaments at Devnimori, numerous objects from Bhita.
4. Terracotta plough models at Bahawalpur and Banawali are technological evidence, not only artistic evidence.
5. Cire-perdue in five steps: model in wax; coat in wet clay and dry; heat so the wax melts; pour the wax out through a tiny hole and pour in liquid metal; cool, then remove the clay coat.
6. Consequences: the mould is destroyed, so each casting is unique; the real modelling is in wax; the result is portable; and the technique is still practised in many parts of the country today.
7. Named early metal objects: the Dancing Girl of Mohenjo-daro, the bronze bull of Kalibangan, and the bronze chariot of Daimabad in the tradition's opening statement.
8. Stones that identify workshops: steatite for seals, spotted red sandstone for Mathura, bluish-grey sandstone with blue schist and green phyllite for early Gandhara, greenish-white limestone for Amaravati, cream-coloured sandstone for Gupta Sarnath.
9. Harappan seals were mostly steatite but also agate, chert, copper, faience and terracotta, with some gold and ivory instances.
10. Mauryan polish is both a technical marker and a contact argument; it supports but does not settle the Didarganj dating.
11. Gandhara material sequence: blue schist and green phyllite, then stucco, then complete shift to grey stone from the third century AD.
12. Relief carries narrative; work in the round permits circumambulation, installation and procession; Amaravati is the narrative relief school and the cast icon is the processional form.
13. Early Buddhist sculpture was used primarily to decorate stupas, on the torana and the medhi; in temples the vahana stands just before the sanctum sanctorum.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain, in technical terms, why the Nataraja's posture is achievable in cast bronze and difficult in carved stone, and state one thing this explanation must not be stretched to prove.

**Model answer:** The lost-wax method reproduces in metal whatever existed in wax. The sculptor models a free-standing wax figure, coats it in wet clay, dries it, heats it so the wax melts, pours the wax out through a tiny hole, fills the hollow mould with liquid metal, lets it solidify and breaks away the clay. Because the wax model can have a raised leg, outflung arms and open space between the limbs, and because cooled metal has enough tensile strength to hold those extensions, the cast figure can too. A carved figure is subtractive: every projecting limb has to remain attached to the parent block or be supported by a strut, and any unsupported extension is a fracture waiting to happen - which is why dancing Shiva images also exist as stone reliefs in Pallava cave temples and as panels on Hoysala temple walls, where the wall carries the figure, rather than as free-standing stone dancers. What the explanation must not be stretched to prove is origin or superiority: the earliest known Nataraja sculpture is a rock-cut example at the Ravana Phadi cave at Aihole, made under early Chalukya rule, so the bronze technique explains the form's portable perfection, not its invention.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating lost-wax casting as a Chola invention. The source states that the technique was known to Indian artists from the time of the Indus Valley culture and is still practised today; what changes between the Dancing Girl and a Chola icon is scale, iconographic programme and ritual function, not the underlying process.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In Indian sculpture, the choice of material is an argument, not a background detail." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds, because in this tradition material decides simultaneously what can be shown, where the object can stand and how long it survives.

Consider what each medium permitted. Steatite, a soft riverbed stone, allowed the incised miniature detail of the Harappan seals, where a horned seated figure, an elephant, a tiger, a rhinoceros, a buffalo and two antelopes occupy an object small enough to be carried as an amulet. Fire-baked clay, worked by pinching, allowed rapid domestic production and is correspondingly described as fewer in number and cruder in form than the same civilisation's bronzes - and the same clay, once moulded, produced the standardised Gupta plaque collections at Bikaner and Ahichchhatra. The lost-wax method, in which a wax model is clay-coated, heated, drained and filled with molten metal before the clay is broken away, allowed free limbs, open silhouettes and portability, which is the technical basis of the processional icon. Hard fine-grained stone allowed the crisp folds and modelled anatomy of Gandhara, and greenish-white limestone allowed Amaravati's continuous narrative relief, including a medallion carrying an entire episode of the Buddha taming an elephant.

Material also decides survival, and therefore decides the historical record. The core account attributes the dominance of stone, metal and terracotta to the region's climatic conditions, which means the sculpture we study is already a durability-filtered sample.

Two qualifications keep the claim honest. Material constrains rather than dictates: the same Harappan corpus contains both fine steatite work and crude pinched clay. And material continuity is not meaning continuity - lost-wax casting runs from the Indus period to the present without implying that a Chola icon means what a Harappan figurine meant.

Material is therefore the first premise of a sculpture argument, and an answer that omits it is arguing without one.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the material-as-premise thesis 2 + four named materials each tied to a named object and a specific capability 6 + the climate-and-survival point 2 + the relief-versus-round consequence 2 + both qualifications, constraint-not-dictation and technique-not-meaning 2 + verdict 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 3:** With materials and techniques in hand, the earliest corpus can finally be described properly - because almost every claim made about Harappan sculpture is really a claim about steatite, bronze or pinched clay, and because the most famous objects in it are also the most heavily mislabelled.

## Lesson 3 - The Harappan corpus: seals, bronzes, clay and stone, and the discipline of naming

### Visual: one civilisation, four object classes, four different kinds of evidence

```text
  HARAPPAN THREE-DIMENSIONAL WORK
  "extremely adept at handling three-dimensional volumes"
  most commonly found: SEALS | BRONZE FIGURES | POTTERIES
            |
  +---------+---------------+---------------+---------------+
  |                         |               |               |
 SEALS                   BRONZE          TERRACOTTA       STONE
 steatite mostly;        cire-perdue     fire-baked;      steatite and
 also agate, chert,      Dancing Girl;   pinched;         red sandstone
 copper, faience,        bull of         Mother Goddess,  Bearded figure;
 terracotta; some        Kalibangan      horned-deity     male torso from
 gold and ivory                          mask, toys       Harappa
  |                         |               |               |
 square mostly;          four inches;    "less in number  trefoil shawl,
 also triangular,        tribhanga;      and crude in     half-closed
 rectangular,            ornaments on    shape and form"  eyes; socket
 circular                both arms                        holes for head
  |                         |               |               |
 EVIDENCE OF:            EVIDENCE OF:    EVIDENCE OF:     EVIDENCE OF:
 script, trade,          metallurgical   household and    fine carving
 administration,         skill and       ritual use,      and anatomical
 animal repertoire,      technical       and of           observation
 amulet use,             continuity      technology
 mathematics                             (plough models)
```

*Four classes, four evidentiary functions. The single commonest exam error is to treat all four as one aesthetic story instead of four separate bodies of evidence about four different things.*

### 1. The seals: shape, material, script and motif, in that order

The Harappan sculptors are described as extremely adept at handling three-dimensional volumes, and the most commonly found objects are seals, bronze figures and potteries. Take the seals apart systematically.

**Shape.** Most seals are **square**; **triangular, rectangular and circular** seals were also in use. A question that offers "all Harappan seals were square" is testing exactly this.

**Material.** **Steatite**, a soft stone found in river beds, was the most common material; **agate, chert, copper, faience and terracotta** seals were also found, and some instances of **gold and ivory** seals have been found too.

**Script and its direction.** Most seals carry inscriptions in a **pictographic script which is yet to be deciphered**. The script was written mostly **from right to left**, but a **bi-directional** style - right to left on one line, left to right on the next - has also been found. This has a name in the study of ancient writing, and you can mention the pattern without claiming a reading.

**Layout.** Generally a seal has an animal or human figure on one side and an inscription on the opposite side, or inscriptions on both sides; some carry inscriptions on a third side as well.

**Animal motifs.** Animal impressions are present, generally five, carved **intaglio** - that is, cut into the surface so the impression stands proud - on the seal faces. The common motifs are a **unicorn, humped bull, rhinoceros, tiger, elephant, buffalo, bison, goat, markhor, ibex and crocodile**. And then the fact that carries the most exam weight of anything in the Harappan section: **no evidence of cow and lion has been found on any seal.**

**Uses.** Seals were primarily used for **commercial purposes** and helped in communication. Their discovery in **Mesopotamia** and at sites such as **Lothal** indicates extensive use in trade. Some seals with a hole have been found on dead bodies, indicating use as **amulets** carried by their owners, probably as a form of identification. **Mathematical images** on some seals suggest an educational use. Seals with a symbol similar to the **swastika** design have also been found.

**Named seals.** The famous ones are the **Pashupati Seal** and the **Unicorn Seal**.

### 2. The absence that earns marks

Pause on the negative fact, because negatives are where objective items live and where analytical answers separate.

The humped bull is frequent on the seals. The **lion is absent** from the seal corpus, and so is the **cow**. Two conclusions are available and they are of different strengths:

- **Safe and sourced:** the bull was a privileged image in Harappan visual culture, while the lion does not appear on the seals at all.
- **Analytical, and must be labelled as such:** the lion's career in Indian art therefore begins, on this evidence, *later*, and when it begins it is tied to imperial and royal usage - the Ashokan capitals of Lesson 7 - rather than to the earliest urban civilisation.

What is *not* available is the reverse move: reading later Shaiva meaning back into the Harappan bull because a bull later becomes Shiva's mount. The script is undeciphered; the honest formulation is that the bull mattered to Harappan visual culture and that what it meant to Harappans cannot be recovered.

### 3. The named objects, described precisely

| Object | Material and find-site | Description as recorded | What it evidences | The caution |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Pashupati Seal** | Steatite, Mohenjo-daro | A human figure or deity seated cross-legged, wearing a three-horned headgear, surrounded by animals - elephant and tiger to the left, rhinoceros and buffalo to the right, two antelopes below the seat | A composed, deliberate iconographic arrangement: a central seated figure with a directional animal scheme | "Pashupati" is a modern name; the script is undeciphered; a proto-Shiva identification is disputed |
| **Unicorn Seal** | Steatite | Named by the source as one of the two famous seals; the unicorn is among the common animal motifs | The most frequently recurring single motif class in the corpus | Do not supply a meaning for the unicorn; none is recorded here |
| **Dancing Girl** | Bronze, Mohenjo-daro, four inches | A naked girl wearing only ornaments - bangles on the left arm, an amulet and bracelet on the right - standing in a **tribhanga** dancing posture with the right hand on the hip; described in the source as the world's oldest bronze sculpture | Mastery of lost-wax casting at small scale, and a posture vocabulary that reappears two thousand years later at Amaravati | The dating and the superlative are the source's; "tribhanga" is a later technical term applied retrospectively, and the figure's social identity is not recorded |
| **Mother Goddess** | Terracotta, found at many Indus sites | A crude standing female adorned with necklaces over prominent breasts, wearing a loincloth and a girdle and a fan-shaped headgear; facial features crude and lacking finesse; probably worshipped for prosperity and possibly a goddess of fertility sects | Wide distribution suggests household-level ritual rather than a single central cult | "Probably" and "might" are the source's own hedges and must be preserved; the name is modern |
| **Bearded Priest** | Steatite, Mohenjo-daro | A bearded man draped in a shawl with **trefoil patterns**; eyes elongated and half closed as in meditation | Fine small-scale stone carving and deliberate treatment of the eyes | The name asserts an office the evidence does not establish |
| **Male torso** | Red sandstone, Harappa | Frontal posture, well-baked shoulders, prominent abdomen, with **socket holes in the neck and shoulders**, probably for attaching a head and arms | A composite, assembled figure - a technical fact of real interest | Do not reconstruct the missing head and arms in description; the sockets are the evidence, the attachments are not |
| **Bronze bull of Kalibangan** | Bronze | Given as an example of lost-wax casting alongside the Dancing Girl | The bull as a three-dimensional subject, not only a seal motif | - |
| **Horned-deity mask and toys** | Terracotta | Given as examples alongside the Mother Goddess figure | Ritual and domestic use in the same material | - |

### 4. The one piece of recorded posture vocabulary, and why it matters later

The Dancing Girl stands in a **tribhanga** posture, the body bent at three points. Note carefully what has happened: a term that will later be the signature of the Amaravati school - which made excessive use of the tribhanga posture, the body with three bends - is here applied to an object roughly two millennia older. That is not a claim of continuity of school or meaning. It is a claim that the same formal solution to the problem of representing a body in motion was arrived at early and used again later. Say it that way and the sentence is safe; say "the tribhanga tradition begins with the Dancing Girl and culminates at Amaravati" and you have invented a lineage.

### 5. Ornament and dress: the small corpus that widens the social picture

The Harappans used a wide range of materials for ornaments, from precious metals and gemstones to bones and even baked clay. **Both men and women** wore necklaces, fillets, armlets and finger rings; **girdles, earrings and anklets were worn only by women.** Beads of **carnelian, amethyst, quartz and steatite** were popular and produced on a large scale, as the bead-making evidence at **Chanhudaro and Lothal** shows. **Cotton and wool** were used for fabric and were spun by rich and poor alike, and different styles of hair and beard suggest an awareness of fashion.

This is included here for a specific reason. It is the only body of evidence in the Harappan section that speaks directly about people rather than about objects, and it is therefore the material to reach for when a question asks what art and craft reveal about society. It also supplies a check on the Dancing Girl description: the ornaments she wears are not an artist's invention but a recorded object class.

### Examination use

The Harappan section is the densest objective-item zone in this topic. Items reproduced in the chapter itself ask what the Dancing Girl is made of, which material was used in making Harappan seals, and which motif does **not** appear on Harappan seals - and the last of these is answered by the recorded absence of the cow and the lion. For Mains, this material feeds any demand about the earliest Indian art, about what material culture reveals of society, and about the limits of archaeological inference. The strongest single move available here is the label-versus-identity discipline of Lesson 1, applied concretely: describe the Pashupati seal in full, then say in one clause why the name is a convenience. Examiners reward that clause because most scripts do not contain it.

### Traps introduced here

- Saying all Harappan seals are square, or that steatite was the only seal material.
- Placing a lion or a cow on a Harappan seal.
- Asserting that the Pashupati figure is Shiva, or that the Mother Goddess figure proves an organised fertility cult, or that the bearded figure was a priest or a king.
- Dropping the source's own hedges - "probably worshipped for prosperity", "might also have been a goddess of fertility sects".
- Describing the whole corpus as crude, when the judgement of crudity is made specifically about the terracottas.
- Building a continuous "tribhanga tradition" from the Dancing Girl to Amaravati.
- Inventing dimensions; the only measurement recorded for these objects here is the Dancing Girl's four inches.

### Revision notes

1. The Harappans are described as extremely adept at handling three-dimensional volumes; the commonest finds are seals, bronze figures and potteries.
2. Seals: mostly square, also triangular, rectangular and circular; mostly steatite, also agate, chert, copper, faience and terracotta, with some gold and ivory.
3. The seal script is pictographic and undeciphered; written mostly right to left, sometimes bi-directionally.
4. Animal impressions, generally five, carved intaglio; motifs include unicorn, humped bull, rhinoceros, tiger, elephant, buffalo, bison, goat, markhor, ibex and crocodile.
5. No evidence of cow or lion has been found on any seal - the highest-value negative fact in the topic.
6. Seal uses: commerce and communication; trade evidence at Mesopotamia and Lothal; perforated seals on dead bodies indicating amulet use; mathematical images suggesting educational use; swastika-like symbols found.
7. Famous seals: Pashupati and Unicorn.
8. Pashupati seal: steatite, Mohenjo-daro, cross-legged figure in a three-horned headgear; elephant and tiger left, rhinoceros and buffalo right, two antelopes below the seat.
9. Dancing Girl: bronze, Mohenjo-daro, four inches, naked but for ornaments - bangles on the left arm, amulet and bracelet on the right - in tribhanga with the right hand on the hip; called the world's oldest bronze sculpture.
10. Mother Goddess: terracotta, many sites, crude, necklaces, loincloth, girdle, fan-shaped headgear; probably worshipped for prosperity, possibly a fertility-sect goddess.
11. Bearded figure: steatite, Mohenjo-daro, trefoil-patterned shawl, elongated half-closed eyes.
12. Male torso: red sandstone, Harappa, frontal, with socket holes in neck and shoulders for attaching head and arms.
13. Terracotta was pinched, found mostly in Gujarat and at Kalibangan; examples are the Mother Goddess, a horned-deity mask and toys.
14. Ornaments: necklaces, fillets, armlets and finger rings worn by both sexes; girdles, earrings and anklets only by women; carnelian, amethyst, quartz and steatite beads, with bead-making at Chanhudaro and Lothal; cotton and wool spun by rich and poor alike.

### Concept check

**Question:** An objective item asks which of the following motifs does not appear on Harappan seals and offers humped bull, elephant, lion and crocodile. Explain how the taught corpus settles this, and state what a candidate must not additionally conclude from the answer.

**Model answer:** The recorded motif list for the seals is unicorn, humped bull, rhinoceros, tiger, elephant, buffalo, bison, goat, markhor, ibex and crocodile, and the source states explicitly that no evidence of cow and lion has been found on any seal. Humped bull, elephant and crocodile are therefore all attested; the lion is the one that is not. What must not be additionally concluded is that lions were unknown in the region, that the Harappans avoided the lion for a religious reason, or that the absence proves anything about Harappan beliefs. It is a fact about a body of small carved objects, and the only further claim it safely supports is a comparative one - that the lion's prominence in Indian art appears later and attaches to imperial and royal contexts rather than to the earliest urban civilisation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting a recorded absence into a positive doctrine. "The Harappans did not worship the lion" is not supported; "the lion does not appear in the seal corpus" is. In a topic whose script cannot be read, the difference between these two sentences is the difference between an evidenced answer and an invented one.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Harappan art is best understood as four separate bodies of evidence rather than one aesthetic tradition." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is sound, because each class of Harappan object answers a different historical question.

The seals evidence writing, administration and exchange: a pictographic and undeciphered script, generally five intaglio animal impressions, perforated examples found on dead bodies suggesting amulet use, mathematical images suggesting instruction, and finds at Mesopotamia and Lothal indicating trade. The bronzes evidence technical mastery: the Dancing Girl and the bull of Kalibangan were produced by the lost-wax method known from the Indus period onward. The terracottas evidence household and ritual practice and, in the plough models from Bahawalpur and Banawali, agricultural technology - and the source itself calls them fewer and cruder than the bronzes. The stone figures, the trefoil-shawled bearded man and the socketed male torso from Harappa, evidence fine carving and composite assembly.

Aesthetic unity is therefore the wrong frame; the corpus contains several technical registers simultaneously.

One qualification: the four bodies share a visual repertoire, above all the humped bull, so they are separable in function without being unrelated.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the four-evidence-bodies thesis 2 + one named object and one evidentiary function for each of the four classes 5 + the crudity-and-mastery coexistence point 2 + the shared-repertoire qualification 1. Naming no object forfeits the evidence marks entirely, however fluent the prose.**

**Bridge to Lesson 4:** One of the four classes has been deferred deliberately. Pottery is the largest, dullest-looking and most useful body of material in the whole topic, because it is the one that dates things - and its first difficulty is a naming problem that the sources themselves create.

## Lesson 4 - Pottery to the Harappan phase: fabric, wheel, function, and the Black and Red Ware problem

Progress: 4/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Neolithic, Chalcolithic and Harappan stages of the pottery ladder; plain and painted Harappan wares; wheel-made, handmade and polychrome; the three recorded uses including perforated vessels; the Black and Red Ware naming conflict stated openly; and named early ceramic sites

### Visual: the ladder, and what each rung is defined by

```text
  PERIOD / CULTURE        TIME (approx.)     WARE                  DEFINED BY
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Neolithic               7000-2000 BC       hand-made,            thick fabric;
                                             coarse ware           cord or
                                                                   impressed
                                                                   surface
        |
        v
  Chalcolithic            3000-1000 BC       painted pottery       red ware with
                                             - Ahar, Malwa,        painted
                                               Jorwe               geometric
                                                                   motifs; hand-
                                                                   made or slow
                                                                   wheel
        |
        v
  Indus Valley            2600-1900 BC       Harappan Red Ware     wheel-made;
  (Harappan)                                                       black geometric
                                                                   painting on a
                                                                   red surface
        |
        v
  [Lesson 5 continues the ladder: Painted Grey Ware, then
   Northern Black Polished Ware, then the wider historic and
   megalithic ceramic categories]
```

*Note the three variables that change as you climb: how the vessel was formed (hand, slow wheel, fast wheel), how it was decorated (surface impression, painting, slip and polish), and how fine the fabric is. Those three, not the period name, are what actually define a ware.*

A disclosure about this table, because it affects what you may write. The printed features column is **clipped in the text available for this session**: the Neolithic row breaks after "thick fabric, cord/impress", the Chalcolithic row after "usually hand or slow wheel", and the Harappan row after "wheel-made, black geometric". What is legible is taught above. What is cut off is not reconstructed, and you should not complete such a row from memory in an answer either, because the completion is exactly where invented detail enters.

### 1. Why pottery is worth more than it looks

Pottery is the most abundant surviving artefact class from almost every ancient site, for three converging reasons: it was used by everyone, it broke constantly, and fired clay is chemically stable in the ground. That combination makes it the archaeologist's clock. Fabric, forming method, surface treatment and painted motif change relatively quickly and regionally, so a layer can be dated and a culture named by its ware.

This is why the ware names function as **culture labels** - Painted Grey Ware is a *culture* with a date range and a geography, not merely a kind of pot. Understanding that is what makes the next lesson's dating argument possible, and it is also the root of the category confusion this session keeps warning about: a ware name is an archaeological classification; a craft pottery name is a workshop and market classification.

### 2. Harappan pottery: two kinds, one confusing name

The potteries from the excavation sites fall broadly into **two kinds - plain pottery and painted pottery**.

Now the difficulty, stated openly rather than smoothed over. The core account says that the painted pottery **is also known as Black and Red Ware**, because it used red pigments to paint the background and glossy black paint to draw designs and figures on the red background; and it records that trees, birds, animal figures and geometrical patterns were the recurring themes on this Red and Black Pottery.

The topic's own canonical owner corrects that identification directly. Its position is that Harappan pottery is predominantly **red ware, often painted in black**, with plain and painted varieties, and that it **should not be collapsed into the separate, wider archaeological category "Black-and-Red Ware."**

Both statements are carried here because the conflict is real and you must be able to handle it.

```text
  WHAT IS BEING CONFUSED

  (A) A DESCRIPTION OF DECORATION                (B) A TECHNICAL WARE CATEGORY
  "red surface, black painted designs"           "Black and Red Ware" (BRW)
  - this is what Harappan painted               - a distinct archaeological
    pottery looks like                            category found in several
  - the black is PAINT on a red ground            cultures and periods
                                                - listed separately in the
                                                  Painted Grey Ware discussion
                                                  as one of the wares that
                                                  coexisted with PGW

  THE SAFE FORMULATION FOR AN ANSWER
  "Harappan pottery is predominantly red ware, usually wheel-made, with
   designs painted in glossy black on the red ground. Some accounts call
   this painted variety 'Black and Red Ware'; that phrase is also the name
   of a separate, wider ceramic category attested in other cultures, and
   the two should not be treated as identical."
```

That formulation gives the description, names the usage, and prevents the equation - which is the highest-scoring available response to a genuine source conflict.

Corroboration for the separateness sits inside the source itself: when Painted Grey Ware is described in Lesson 5, BRW is listed alongside plain grey ware and black slipped ware as one of the *other* pottery types with which PGW coexisted. A ware that coexists with PGW in the first millennium BC is not simply the Harappan painted ware of the third.

### 3. Forming and finish: the wheel evidence

Most of the pottery found is **very fine wheel-made ware**, with **very few handmade** pieces. Some examples of **polychrome pottery** have been found, though rarely.

Read that as technology, not decoration. Fine wheel-made ware in bulk implies specialist potters, standardised forms, repeatable output and a distribution system - an industry, not a household activity. Set it beside the Chalcolithic row above, where vessels are hand-made or made on a slow wheel, and you have a real technological change, visible in the pots, that matches the urban character of the Harappan phase. This is one of the few places in the art syllabus where an aesthetic object carries a straightforward economic inference.

### 4. The three recorded uses, including the one that is always asked

The potteries were used for three main purposes:

1. **Plain pottery** for household purposes, mainly the **storage of grains and water**.
2. **Miniature vessels**, generally **less than half an inch** in size, for **decorative purposes**.
3. **Perforated pottery** - with a **large hole at the bottom and small holes across the sides** - which might have been used for **straining liquor**.

The third is the one examiners like, because the form is unusual and the function is stated with a hedge. Keep the hedge. "Might have been used for straining liquor" is the recorded position; "was used for brewing" is an invention. Note also the internal logic: a vessel with a large basal hole cannot hold liquid, so the function must involve passing liquid through, which is why the straining reading is plausible - that reasoning is worth a line because it shows you can argue from form to function.

### 5. Named early ceramic sites and cultures

| Site or culture | Where | What is recorded | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sothi** | Rajasthan | An early pre-Harappan settlement dating back to around **4600 BC** | Anchors the pre-urban end of the ladder with a number |
| **Siswal** | Hisar, Haryana | A **Chalcolithic**-age cultural site dating around **3800 BC**; the pottery of the site exhibits the **"six fabrics of Kalibangan"** | The single best phrase for showing that "fabric" is the working unit of ceramic classification |
| **Lohari Ragho** | Haryana | Another site belonging to the Sothi-Siswal cultural period | Shows the culture as a regional group, not a single site |
| **Burzahom** | Kashmir Valley | A **Neolithic** site; excavations show cultural phases between **3000 BC and 1000 BC** | Extends the Neolithic rung geographically to the north |
| **Gundiyali and Lodai** | Kutch, Gujarat | Recorded as **famous pottery-making villages** | The bridge to Lesson 19: pottery making as a living village craft, kept separate from the ware sequence |
| **Chandraketugarh** | Near the Bidyadhari river, North 24 Parganas district, West Bengal | A site "filled with amazing terracotta sculpture"; **Northern Black Polished Ware relics** and sculptures of the **Maurya, Kushana and Gupta** periods, plus punch-marked and gold coins; its terracotta art exhibits an amazing degree of precision and craftsmanship | The best single site for linking ware, terracotta sculpture and coinage in one answer; carried forward to Lesson 5 |

Two disciplines apply to this table. Do not date Chandraketugarh beyond the periods listed, and do not assign it to a named dynasty beyond them. And note that a reproduced objective item in the core source pairs archaeological sites with states and descriptions - including Chandraketugarh, the Chalcolithic site of Inamgaon in Maharashtra, the megalithic site of Mangadu in Kerala and Salihundam in Andhra Pradesh - which is precisely the format in which site-and-description pairs are tested.

### Examination use

Pottery supports two distinct demand families. The first is objective and factual: ware names, date ranges, forming technique, site pairings. The second is a Mains family about material culture as a source, where the pottery ladder is the cleanest evidence available that Indian prehistory and protohistory can be periodised from artefacts alone. The Black and Red Ware conflict is a gift in this context, because a candidate who states the description, names the usage and refuses the equation demonstrates source criticism on a concrete case. The three recorded uses, with the perforated vessel and its hedge, are the most quotable specifics in the section.

### Traps introduced here

- Equating Harappan painted pottery with the wider Black-and-Red Ware category without stating that the phrase is used in two senses.
- Saying Harappan pottery was handmade; the recorded position is that most of it is very fine wheel-made ware, with very few handmade pieces.
- Claiming polychrome pottery was common; it is recorded as rare.
- Stating a firm function for perforated vessels instead of the recorded "might have been used for straining liquor".
- Giving the miniature vessels a ritual function; the recorded purpose is decorative.
- Completing the clipped features column of the pottery table from general memory.
- Treating Gundiyali and Lodai as archaeological ware types rather than as pottery-making villages.

### Revision notes

1. Pottery is the archaeological clock because it was universal, breakable and chemically stable once fired; ware names therefore function as culture labels.
2. Neolithic, approximately 7000-2000 BC: hand-made coarse ware, thick fabric, cord or impressed surface.
3. Chalcolithic, approximately 3000-1000 BC: painted pottery, with Ahar, Malwa and Jorwe named; red ware with painted geometric motifs; usually hand-made or slow-wheel made.
4. Indus Valley or Harappan, approximately 2600-1900 BC: Harappan Red Ware; wheel-made; black geometric painting.
5. Harappan pottery divides into plain and painted.
6. The core account calls the painted variety "Black and Red Ware" from its red ground and glossy black designs; the topic's canonical owner rejects collapsing it into the separate wider Black-and-Red Ware category. State both, equate neither.
7. Recurring painted themes: trees, birds, animal figures and geometrical patterns.
8. Most Harappan pottery is very fine wheel-made ware; very few handmade; polychrome examples exist but are rare.
9. Three uses: plain pottery for household storage of grain and water; miniature vessels under half an inch for decoration; perforated pottery with a large basal hole and small side holes, possibly for straining liquor.
10. Sothi in Rajasthan about 4600 BC; Siswal at Hisar, Haryana, about 3800 BC, Chalcolithic, its pottery showing the six fabrics of Kalibangan; Lohari Ragho in the same cultural period.
11. Burzahom in the Kashmir Valley is Neolithic, with phases between 3000 BC and 1000 BC.
12. Gundiyali and Lodai in Kutch, Gujarat, are famous pottery-making villages - a craft fact, not a ware.
13. Chandraketugarh, near the Bidyadhari river in North 24 Parganas, West Bengal: terracotta sculpture of high precision, NBPW relics, Maurya-Kushana-Gupta sculpture, punch-marked and gold coins.

### Concept check

**Question:** A statement reads: "Harappan pottery is Black and Red Ware, a category that also defines the Painted Grey Ware culture." Separate what is defensible from what is not, and write the version you would use in an examination.

**Model answer:** One part is a defensible description and the rest is a category error. The defensible part is that Harappan painted pottery has a red ground with designs and figures drawn in glossy black, and that the core account itself calls this Black and Red Ware. The first error is equation: the topic's canonical owner states that Harappan pottery is predominantly red ware painted in black and must not be collapsed into the separate, wider archaeological Black-and-Red Ware category. The second error is chronological and cultural: Black and Red Ware is listed among the wares that *coexisted with* Painted Grey Ware, alongside plain grey ware and black slipped ware, which places that category in the first-millennium-BC context of the PGW culture rather than making it a definition of it. The examination version reads: "Harappan pottery is predominantly wheel-made red ware, plain or painted with black designs on the red ground; some accounts name this painted variety Black and Red Ware, but that phrase also denotes a separate ceramic category attested across several cultures, including among the wares coexisting with Painted Grey Ware, and the two usages must be kept apart."

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because two wares look similar - dark designs on a reddish body - they must be the same archaeological category. Ceramic categories are defined by fabric, firing and stratigraphic association, not by colour impression, and the whole dating value of pottery collapses if colour is allowed to override context.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Pottery, not sculpture, is the true material spine of early Indian chronology." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is largely sound, and it rests on frequency and sensitivity rather than on artistic value.

Pottery is abundant, and its ladder is explicitly periodised: hand-made coarse ware with thick fabric in the Neolithic, roughly 7000-2000 BC; painted red ware with geometric motifs, hand-made or slow-wheel made, in the Chalcolithic at Ahar, Malwa and Jorwe, roughly 3000-1000 BC; fine wheel-made Harappan Red Ware, roughly 2600-1900 BC. Sculpture supplies nothing comparable, because famous objects are few and their dates are contested - the Didarganj whisk-bearer's period is disputed and the earliest anthropomorphic Buddha images cannot be assigned confidently to one school.

Pottery is also stratigraphically sensitive: the pottery of Siswal is described through the six fabrics of Kalibangan, a resolution no sculptural corpus offers.

The qualification matters. Pottery dates a layer, not a meaning; it cannot evidence belief, iconography or patronage, which is where sculpture is decisive and pottery silent.

The two are therefore complementary, with pottery supplying the chronology on which sculptural history is hung.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the frequency-and-sensitivity thesis 2 + the three-rung ladder with approximate dates and forming techniques 4 + one contrasting case of contested sculptural dating 2 + the complementarity qualification 1 + verdict 1. A pure list of ware names without the forming-technique change scores half.**

**Bridge to Lesson 5:** The ladder so far stops at the end of the Harappan phase. Its two most examinable rungs are still to come, and they arrive with a political temptation attached - the habit of calling one of them simply "Mauryan pottery", which the evidence only partly supports.
