### Visual: two wares, compared on the variables that actually distinguish them

| Variable | **Painted Grey Ware (PGW)** | **Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW)** |
|---|---|---|
| Clay and fabric | Made from **very high-quality clay**; **thin fabric**, fine and smooth | Fine ware. ⚠️ **Analytical inference, not a sourced fact:** the recorded description emphasises surface - black paint and a highly lustrous finish - rather than body, and no clay or fabric description is carried for this ware in the owned evidence |
| Colour | Distinct **grey** | **Black** paint with a **highly lustrous** finish |
| Decoration | Painted with **simple geometric patterns** in **black paint** | The lustrous finish itself is the treatment |
| Firing | Uniformity of colour and texture suggests **very advanced firing techniques** | High-quality finish, described as the highest level of pottery |
| Status | **High-quality**, forming only a **small fraction** of the overall pottery collection | **Luxury items** |
| Coexisting wares | **Plain grey ware, BRW and black slipped ware**, perhaps for everyday purposes | Not specified in the same way; the source treats NBPW as a period marker |
| Date | **1100-500/400 BC** | Given in the source as the pottery of the **Mauryan period** |
| Geography | Himalayan foothills to the **Malwa plateau**; **Bahawalpur** region of Pakistan to **Kaushambi** near Allahabad; also the hilly regions of **Kumaon and Garhwal** | **Pataliputra (Patna), Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain and Vidisha** |

*Read down the "status" row before anything else. Both of these are minority, high-end wares. Neither is what most people in either period actually used, and an answer that treats them as the ordinary pottery of their age has misunderstood what a type-fossil is.*

### 1. Painted Grey Ware: a culture, not just a pot

PGW is described as an **exclusive** pottery made from very high-quality clay, with a distinct grey colour and a thin fabric that is fine and smooth. The pots were painted with **simple geometric patterns using black paint**, and the **uniformity of colour and texture suggests that the firing techniques used were very advanced**.

Four consequences, each usable:

- **Uniformity implies control.** To fire grey rather than red, a potter must control the atmosphere in the kiln, restricting oxygen. Doing that consistently across a body of vessels is a technical achievement, and the source's inference from uniformity to advanced firing is exactly the kind of argument-from-object that this topic wants you to reproduce.
- **PGW was not everyday pottery.** It constituted only a small fraction of the overall pottery collection, and it **coexisted with other pottery types, including plain grey ware, Black and Red Ware and black slipped ware**, which were perhaps used for everyday purposes. So the PGW culture is named after its *diagnostic* ware, not its *commonest* ware. This is the single most useful thing to know about ware-named cultures.
- **Its date is a range with an internal uncertainty, and the uncertainty should be kept.** The recorded range is **1100-500/400 BC**. Write it with the slash. A candidate who writes "1100-500 BC" has silently resolved a disagreement the source preserved.
- **Its geography is wide and includes hills.** From the Himalayan foothills to the Malwa plateau in central India, and from the Bahawalpur region of Pakistan to Kaushambi near Allahabad in Uttar Pradesh, with finds also in the hilly regions of Kumaon and Garhwal. That last clause is worth remembering precisely because it resists the familiar shorthand that PGW is only an upper-Ganga-Yamuna doab phenomenon.

The source associates PGW with the **Vedic age** in its heading. Treat that as a chronological association between a ware horizon and a textual period, not as proof that the makers of the ware were the composers of the texts; the identification of an archaeological culture with a literary one is an interpretive step, and it belongs to ancient history rather than to this topic.

### 2. Northern Black Polished Ware: the luxury ware and the attribution problem

The core account states that the pottery of the Mauryan period is generally referred to as **Northern Black Polished Ware**, characterised by black paint and a highly lustrous finish and generally used as **luxury items**; it has often been referred to as the highest level of pottery. Its major sites are **Pataliputra (Patna), Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain and Vidisha**.

The topic's canonical owner adds two refinements that convert this into a safe formulation. First, NBPW is a **fine, highly lustrous tableware, often black or steel-grey but also in other shades** - so the single word "black" describes the commonest appearance, not the whole range. Second, and more important, it is **associated especially with early historic urbanisation, not exclusively with the Mauryan court**.

```text
  THE ATTRIBUTION PROBLEM, IN ONE LINE

  "Mauryan pottery = NBPW"           "NBPW is a ware horizon of early
  - a period label of convenience     historic urbanisation, at its
  - true that NBPW is the             height in the Mauryan period,
    characteristic fine ware of       found across many sites and not
    Mauryan-period levels             confined to one dynasty's patronage"
  - false if it implies the ware      - this is the formulation that
    was made only then, only for      survives a follow-up question
    the court, or only in one shade
```

Notice the structural similarity to the Nataraja problem you will meet in Lesson 17. In both cases a dynasty is famous for something it did not exclusively own, and in both cases the examination-safe move is the same: name the dynasty's association, refuse the monopoly.

**Where NBPW appears outside the imperial heartland.** Chandraketugarh in West Bengal yields NBPW relics alongside sculptures of the Maurya, Kushana and Gupta periods and punch-marked and gold coins. One site therefore carries the ware, the coinage and three periods of sculpture - which is why it is the most economical single site to hold in memory for this half of the topic.

### 3. The later categories, bounded honestly

Beyond PGW and NBPW, three further ceramic categories are commonly expected in a complete answer, and the evidence available for this session supports them only in part. The responsible course is to state what is carried and to mark the rest as outside the owned evidence.

| Category | What the owned evidence carries | What must not be supplied from memory |
|---|---|---|
| **Black and Red Ware (BRW)** | Named as a distinct ware type coexisting with PGW alongside plain grey ware and black slipped ware; also used, in the Harappan discussion, as a name for the red-ground-black-design painted pottery | A definition of BRW's firing technique, a date range, a type site or a cultural attribution; none is recorded here |
| **Black slipped ware and plain grey ware** | Named as everyday coexisting wares in the PGW horizon | Any description of fabric, form or distribution |
| **Megalithic ceramics** | The megalithic *burial* forms are named - **dolmens, cairns, menhirs and cists**, located in various places of India; a reproduced objective item pairs **Mangadu, Kerala** with a megalithic site | The characteristic megalithic pottery type, its colour, its grave-goods assemblage or its regional chronology; the owned evidence describes the burials, not the ware |

That table is itself an examination asset. A question that asks you to trace the pottery sequence can be answered completely for the Neolithic, Chalcolithic, Harappan, PGW and NBPW rungs, and can then say, accurately, that megalithic burial forms are attested across India while the associated ceramic detail is not being asserted here. That is a stronger script than one which invents a megalithic ware description, because the invented description is precisely the sort of statement an objective item is built to catch.

### 4. How ware evidence is used responsibly

Three rules, each of which has a direct exam payoff:

1. **Name the ware, then name what it is diagnostic of.** "NBPW" alone is a word; "NBPW, the fine lustrous tableware associated especially with early historic urbanisation and best known from Mauryan-period levels at Pataliputra, Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain and Vidisha" is evidence.
2. **Never infer ethnicity or language from a ware.** A pot records a workshop tradition and an exchange network. Identifications of ware horizons with named peoples are interpretive and contested, and this topic does not need them.
3. **Keep the archaeological and craft registers apart.** The moment a sentence slides from "Northern Black Polished Ware" to "black pottery of Nizamabad", it has crossed from stratigraphy to a present-day geographical-indication craft, and Lesson 19 will show exactly how much is lost in that slide.

### Examination use

This lesson carries the highest density of directly testable specifics in the pottery half: two ware definitions, one date range with an internal uncertainty, two site lists, and a named set of coexisting wares. It also carries the topic's second great attribution discipline, the refusal to convert "characteristic of a period" into "exclusive to a dynasty". For Mains, the PGW inference from uniformity to advanced firing and the NBPW luxury-status point together supply an argument about social stratification that is visible in ceramics - high-quality wares forming a small fraction of the assemblage while coarser wares serve everyday use - which is the kind of argument a material-culture question rewards.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling PGW the ordinary pottery of its period; it is a small fraction of the assemblage.
- Writing the PGW date as 1100-500 BC and dropping the recorded alternative terminal date of 400 BC.
- Restricting PGW geography to the plains and omitting the Malwa plateau, Bahawalpur, Kaushambi and the Kumaon and Garhwal hills.
- Treating NBPW as exclusively Mauryan or exclusively courtly, or as invariably jet black rather than often black or steel-grey and sometimes other shades.
- Omitting any of the seven recorded NBPW sites when the question asks for distribution.
- Supplying a megalithic pottery description that the owned evidence does not carry.
- Inferring the identity of a people from the distribution of a ware.

### Revision notes

1. PGW is an exclusive ware of very high-quality clay, distinct grey colour, thin fine smooth fabric, painted with simple geometric patterns in black.
2. Uniformity of colour and texture indicates very advanced firing; grey firing requires controlled, oxygen-restricted conditions.
3. PGW was high-quality and only a small fraction of the overall pottery collection.
4. PGW coexisted with plain grey ware, Black and Red Ware and black slipped ware, perhaps for everyday purposes.
5. PGW culture dates to 1100-500/400 BC; keep the slash.
6. PGW geography: Himalayan foothills to the Malwa plateau; Bahawalpur region of Pakistan to Kaushambi near Allahabad; also Kumaon and Garhwal.
7. The Vedic-age association is chronological; do not convert a ware horizon into a literary identity.
8. NBPW is described as the pottery of the Mauryan period, with black paint and a highly lustrous finish, used as luxury items and called the highest level of pottery.
9. The canonical refinement: NBPW is a fine lustrous tableware, often black or steel-grey but also other shades, associated especially with early historic urbanisation rather than exclusively with the Mauryan court.
10. NBPW major sites: Pataliputra (Patna), Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain and Vidisha.
11. Chandraketugarh yields NBPW relics with Maurya, Kushana and Gupta sculpture and punch-marked and gold coins.
12. Megalithic burial types attested across India are dolmens, cairns, menhirs and cists; Mangadu in Kerala appears as a megalithic site in a reproduced objective item; the associated ware detail is not asserted here.
13. Three discipline rules: name what a ware is diagnostic of; never infer a people from a ware; never slide from an archaeological ware to a modern craft pottery.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it more accurate to describe Northern Black Polished Ware as a marker of early historic urbanisation than as "Mauryan pottery", and what would be lost if a candidate refused the shorthand entirely?

**Model answer:** The shorthand is a period label of convenience. NBPW is indeed the characteristic fine ware of Mauryan-period levels, and the core account introduces it exactly that way, describing it as black-painted, highly lustrous, used as luxury items and found at Pataliputra, Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain and Vidisha. But the canonical refinement records it as a fine lustrous tableware, often black or steel-grey though also in other shades, associated especially with early historic urbanisation rather than exclusively with the Mauryan court - and the site list itself, spanning Taxila in the north-west to Ujjain and Vidisha in central India, describes an urban network rather than a single court's output. Refusing the shorthand entirely would nevertheless lose something real: the association with the Mauryan period is genuine, it is how the ware is introduced in the source, and it is the fastest way to place the ware in time. The correct position keeps both - a ware horizon of early historic urban centres, at its best known in Mauryan-period contexts.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that correcting a shorthand means abandoning it. Examiners are not rewarding contrarianism; they are rewarding precision about the kind of claim being made. The sentence to aim for names the association and denies the monopoly in the same breath.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Ceramic evidence reveals social differentiation in early India more directly than monumental art does." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is strong for one specific reason: ceramics survive as whole assemblages, and an assemblage shows the relation between the fine and the ordinary, which a monument cannot.

Painted Grey Ware demonstrates this exactly. It is described as an exclusive ware of very high-quality clay, thin, fine and smooth, painted with simple geometric patterns, whose uniformity indicates very advanced firing - and, decisively, it constituted only a small fraction of the overall pottery collection, coexisting with plain grey ware, Black and Red Ware and black slipped ware which were perhaps used for everyday purposes. That is differentiation visible in a single excavated layer: a scarce high-investment ware alongside abundant utility wares. Northern Black Polished Ware repeats the pattern in the early historic period, being recorded as luxury goods and as the highest level of pottery, distributed across urban centres from Taxila to Ujjain and Vidisha. The Harappan assemblage adds a third register, with plain storage pottery, decorative miniature vessels under half an inch and specialised perforated vessels serving three different functions.

Monumental art evidences differentiation less directly. A stupa, a pillar or a temple records what a patron could command, but it supplies no matching record of what everyone else had, so the comparison that makes stratification visible is missing.

Two qualifications are required. Fineness is not a simple proxy for class, since a fine ware may be ritual, ceremonial or exchange-related rather than elite domestic. And ceramic evidence is silent on the content of belief and on the identity of the people concerned, which is where sculpture speaks and pottery cannot.

Within those limits, the assemblage is the better evidence of differentiation, and the monument the better evidence of authority.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the assemblage-versus-monument thesis 3 + the PGW small-fraction and coexisting-wares evidence 4 + a second ware case with distribution 3 + the Harappan three-function register 2 + both qualifications, fineness-is-not-class and ceramic-silence-on-belief 2 + verdict 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 6:** The ladder has now reached the Mauryan period from below, through its pottery. The same period has to be approached from the other side as well, through its figures - and the most interesting of those figures were not made for the emperor at all.

## Lesson 6 - Mauryan sculpture: the court, the popular cult, and the figures that outlive both

Progress: 6/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: sculpture's recorded functions on the stupa; Yaksha and Yakshi as folk deities absorbed into three religions; Parkham and Didarganj with the dating debate; the Shalabhanjika motif and its thousand-year career; Gajalakshmi; the Jetavana example; and the court-versus-popular reading of Mauryan art

### Visual: two streams in one period, and where they meet

```text
                   THE MAURYAN PERIOD
                           |
      +--------------------+--------------------+
      |                                         |
  COURT / IMPERIAL STREAM                  POPULAR / CULT STREAM
  pillars and animal capitals              Yaksha and Yakshi images
  imperial sermons, edicts,                folk deities of water,
  commemoration of victories               fertility, trees, forest,
  chunar sandstone, monolithic             wilderness
  shaft, lotus or bell capital,            worshipped by rural folk
  abacus, animal figure                    for boons and protection
  HIGH POLISH                              HIGH POLISH
      |                                         |
      +--------------------+--------------------+
                           |
        THE SHARED TECHNICAL SIGNATURE: the Mauryan polish
        -> the same workshop standard serves an empire's message
           and a village's deity
                           |
      +--------------------+--------------------+
      |                                         |
  FEEDS FORWARD TO:                        FEEDS FORWARD TO:
  Lesson 7, the animal capitals            Lesson 10, where the Mathura
  as an iconographic programme             school's images are recorded
                                           as modelled on the earlier
                                           yaksha images
```

*The diagram's centre line is the argument. An art history that reads the Mauryan period only through the imperial pillars misses that the same technical standard was applied to deities who had nothing to do with the empire, and that it was the popular stream, not the imperial one, that shaped the next great school.*

### 1. What sculpture was for

The recorded functions are specific and should be quoted in substance: sculptures were used **primarily for the decoration of stupas, in the torana and the medhi, and as a form of religious expression**. Two of those three are architectural positions - the gateway and the drum or terrace of a stupa - and the third is a general purpose. So from the earliest surviving phase, Indian sculpture is largely **applied** sculpture: made to sit somewhere specific, on a structure, for an audience walking past it.

That has a consequence you will use repeatedly. Where a figure was placed tells you how it was meant to be seen. A bracket figure on a gateway is encountered at an angle, in movement, at the threshold; an image in a sanctum is approached frontally and stationarily; a processional bronze is seen from all sides and in motion. The same subject can be all three, and the form changes each time.

One named example is recorded for the period's achievement: **the Jetavana monastery at Sravasti, with mango trees and the banker Anathapindika**, given as a magnificent example of Mauryan sculpture. It is worth noting precisely because it is narrative and donative in content - a merchant donor and a grove - rather than royal.

### 2. Yaksha and Yakshi: the most transferable figures in the topic

**What they are.** Yakshas were the **folk deities connected with water, fertility, trees, the forest and wilderness**, considered **nature spirits or demigods**. In the Vedic period, Yaksha worship **coexisted as part of Vedic rituals**. They were worshipped by rural folk **for boons and for protection from evil**, and also as **guardian deities who resided in a formless manner on trees and water bodies**.

Sit with that last clause. Before they were sculpted, these deities were held to reside *formlessly* in trees and water. The act of carving them is therefore not a neutral illustration of an existing cult; it is a change in the cult - a formless presence given a body, on a scale and with a finish previously reserved for royal work. That is an analytical point of real strength and it is fully grounded.

**Where they travelled.** They were **objects of worship related to all three religions - Jainism, Hinduism and Buddhism**. In Buddhism and Jainism, the Yaksha is depicted as the **attendant deity of the Bodhisattva and the Tirthankara**. The earliest mention of a **yakshi** is found in the Tamil text **Silappadikaram**, and **all of the Jain Tirthankaras were associated with a yakshi**.

**Where else the word surfaces.** Kalidasa makes a Yaksha the central character of his poem **Meghadoota**; the **Yakshagana** theatre of Malnad in Karnataka literally means the song of Yaksha. Both are recorded, and both are useful in a cultural-continuity answer - with the caution that a shared name across a poem, a theatre form and a sculpture type is evidence of a durable cultural category, not of a single unbroken institution.

**Yakshini or Yakshi** is the **female counterpart**, considered **benign and connected with fertility**. The **Ashoka tree** is closely associated with her, and she is often represented in Indian art as a **gatekeeper of Hindu and Buddhist shrines**.

### 3. The two named Mauryan figures, and the honest chronology

| Figure | Recorded description | What it is used for | Chronological discipline |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Yaksha of Parkham** | Found **south of Mathura city** in Uttar Pradesh; a notable example of Mauryan-period icons; **"speaks of massive strength and gravity in imagery"** | The monumental scale and frontal presence that later temple sculpture inherits; also the link to Mathura, whose school modelled its images on earlier yaksha images | The period attribution is recorded; no individual patron or exact year is available, and none should be supplied |
| **Yakshi of Didarganj** | A prominent example **belonging to the Mauryan period**, depicted as a **chauri** or **whisk bearer** | The best single image of the female cult figure at monumental scale and court finish | The canonical owners record that while the figure is often dated Mauryan and its **polish supports that attribution**, **stylistic and chronological arguments for a later date exist**; state the debate rather than resolving it |

The Didarganj case is the model for how this session handles every contested date. Give the conventional attribution, give the technical argument for it, name the existence of the counter-argument, and refuse to settle it. Two sentences, three marks, no risk.

### 4. Shalabhanjika: one motif, three religions, a thousand years

**Definition.** The **Shalabhanjika** is a common decorative element in Indian sculpture, found **inside and outside religious and secular spaces of Buddhist, Hindu and Jain architecture**, and in many instances also referred to as a **tree deity**.

**In Buddhist art** it is an image of a **yakshi holding a tree**, and Shalabhanjikas appear as **bracket figures on the gateways of the Great Stupa at Sanchi and at Bharhut in Madhya Pradesh**. In Buddhism the motif was regarded as an **auspicious symbol** and incorporated into the decoration of the stupa - and the source draws the inference itself: this **suggests that many people who turned to Buddhism enriched it with their own pre-Buddhist or non-Buddhist practices and beliefs**.

**In Hindu tradition**, Shalabhanjika reliefs have been found in the **Hoysala temples of Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura, in Karnataka**.

```text
  THE SHALABHANJIKA AS A TEST CASE FOR "ABSORPTION"

  origin        a yakshi - a folk fertility figure - holding a tree
     |
  Buddhist      bracket figure on stupa gateways (Sanchi, Bharhut);
  adoption      treated as auspicious, built into the stupa's
                decoration
     |
  the source's  "many people who turned to Buddhism enriched it with
  own reading   their own pre-Buddhist / non-Buddhist practices"
     |
  Hindu         reliefs in the Hoysala temples of Belur, Halebidu
  continuation  and Somanathapura
     |
  what this     a motif that crosses THREE religions and roughly a
  proves        MILLENNIUM without changing its core form - the
                strongest single continuity case this topic owns
     |
  what it       it does not prove doctrinal agreement between the
  does NOT      three religions, nor that the motif meant the same
  prove         thing in each; it proves shared visual inheritance
```

### 5. Gajalakshmi: a second motif, and the discipline of dated evidence

**Gajalakshmi**, or Elephant Lakshmi, is one of the most significant **Ashtalakshmi** aspects of the Hindu goddess of prosperity, Lakshmi. She is the form of the goddess who **stands for animal wealth, as well as other symbols of wealth that represent strength**.

The recorded evidence is unusually precise and should be reproduced exactly:

- a Gajalakshmi image on the **railings from the Buddhist site of Bharhut** in Madhya Pradesh;
- a Gajalakshmi image on a **3rd-century AD coin from Kausambi** in Uttar Pradesh;
- a similar Gajalakshmi relief, **with two elephants and lotuses around the goddess**, on the **vedika of the Sanchi Stupa**;
- **Kalinga temples in Odisha** often carrying a figure of Gajalakshmi in **lalitasana**.

That set is worth its weight because it spans three media - stone railing, coin, temple figure - and two religions, and it carries a posture term, **lalitasana**, which Lesson 15 will place inside the wider asana vocabulary. It is also a reminder that numismatic evidence can settle an iconographic date more firmly than a sculpture can, because coins are datable by issuing authority.

### 6. Reading the period: what the two streams together allow you to argue

The standard examination framing of Mauryan art is court art - pillars, polish, imperial message. The material above supports a fuller and better argument.

- **Technique was shared, not monopolised.** The characteristic polish appears on the popular cult figures as much as on the imperial columns.
- **The popular stream had the longer future.** The Mathura school's images are explicitly recorded as modelled on the earlier yaksha images found during the Mauryan period. The imperial pillar type, by contrast, does not become the basis of a school.
- **Religious boundaries were porous at the level of form.** Yaksha and Yakshi belong to all three religions; Shalabhanjika crosses all three; Gajalakshmi appears at a Buddhist site and on a coin and in Hindu temples.
- **The evidence is about patrons and makers, not about belief statistics.** That a motif was carved widely tells you it was acceptable and valued; it does not tell you what proportion of people worshipped in which way.

### Examination use

This lesson answers directly any demand about early Indian figural sculpture, about folk elements in religious art, or about the interaction of popular cult and institutional religion. A reproduced Mains demand in the core source's list asks how early Buddhist stupa art, while depicting folk motifs and narratives, successfully expounds Buddhist ideals - and the Shalabhanjika material, with the source's own absorption inference, is the sharpest evidence available for the folk-motifs half of that demand, though the stupa as a monument is owned by the architecture topic. The Yaksha and Yakshi material also supplies the "early precedent" clause for any Mathura question, and the Didarganj dating debate is a ready-made source-criticism paragraph.

### Traps introduced here

- Presenting Mauryan art as exclusively imperial court art.
- Dating the Didarganj figure firmly to the Mauryan period without naming the debate.
- Supplying a patron, a king or a year for the Parkham Yaksha.
- Treating Yaksha worship as exclusively Buddhist or exclusively pre-Vedic; it coexisted with Vedic ritual and belongs to all three religions.
- Confusing Yaksha and Yakshi functions: the male figures are guardians and boon-givers; the female counterpart is benign and connected with fertility, and appears as a shrine gatekeeper.
- Treating Shalabhanjika as merely decorative; the source itself reads it as evidence of pre-Buddhist practice enriching Buddhism.
- Assigning Gajalakshmi evidence to the wrong medium; the Bharhut case is a railing, the Kausambi case is a coin of the third century AD, the Sanchi case is on the vedika, and the Odisha case is in Kalinga temples in lalitasana.

### Revision notes

1. Sculpture's recorded functions: decoration of stupas, on the torana and the medhi, and religious expression.
2. Indian sculpture is applied sculpture from the start; placement determines how a figure is meant to be seen.
3. The Jetavana monastery at Sravasti with mango trees and the banker Anathapindika is named as a magnificent example of Mauryan sculpture.
4. Yakshas: folk deities of water, fertility, trees, forest and wilderness; nature spirits or demigods; coexisted with Vedic ritual; worshipped for boons and protection; guardian deities residing formlessly on trees and water bodies.
5. Yakshas and Yakshis are objects of worship in Jainism, Hinduism and Buddhism; in Buddhism and Jainism the Yaksha attends the Bodhisattva and the Tirthankara.
6. Earliest mention of yakshi is in the Tamil Silappadikaram; all Jain Tirthankaras were associated with a yakshi; Kalidasa's Meghadoota has a Yaksha as central character; Yakshagana of Malnad, Karnataka, means the song of Yaksha.
7. Yakshi: female counterpart, benign, connected with fertility; the Ashoka tree is closely associated with her; often shown as a gatekeeper of Hindu and Buddhist shrines.
8. Parkham Yaksha: found south of Mathura city, Uttar Pradesh; a notable Mauryan-period icon; speaks of massive strength and gravity in imagery.
9. Didarganj Yakshi: chauri or whisk bearer, recorded as of the Mauryan period; the polish supports the attribution but stylistic arguments for a later date exist - state the debate.
10. Shalabhanjika: a yakshi holding a tree, also called a tree deity; a common element in Buddhist, Hindu and Jain religious and secular spaces.
11. Shalabhanjika as bracket figures on the gateways of the Great Stupa at Sanchi and at Bharhut; auspicious in Buddhism; reliefs in the Hoysala temples of Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura.
12. The source's own inference: the motif suggests that people who turned to Buddhism enriched it with their pre-Buddhist or non-Buddhist practices and beliefs.
13. Gajalakshmi: an Ashtalakshmi aspect standing for animal wealth and other symbols of wealth representing strength; on Bharhut railings, on a 3rd-century AD Kausambi coin, on the Sanchi vedika with two elephants and lotuses, and in Kalinga temples of Odisha in lalitasana.
14. The Mathura school's images were modelled on the earlier Mauryan-period yaksha images - the popular stream, not the imperial one, founds the later school.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Shalabhanjika motif is better evidence of religious interaction than a statement that Buddhism absorbed folk cults, and state exactly what it cannot establish.

**Model answer:** A general statement about absorption asserts a process; the motif exhibits one. The Shalabhanjika is a yakshi holding a tree - a folk fertility figure with a pre-Buddhist life as a formless tree spirit - and it appears as a bracket figure on the gateways of the Great Stupa at Sanchi and at Bharhut, incorporated into the decoration of the stupa and regarded there as auspicious. The same motif, described in the sources as a tree deity, is found inside and outside Buddhist, Hindu and Jain religious and secular spaces, and it reappears centuries later as reliefs in the Hoysala temples of Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura. So a single identifiable form is documented in three religions, in at least two widely separated periods, in named monuments. The source itself draws the inference that people who turned to Buddhism enriched it with their own pre-Buddhist and non-Buddhist practices. What the motif cannot establish is doctrinal convergence or shared meaning: a form may be borrowed while its significance is reinterpreted, and nothing here shows that a Hoysala patron understood the figure as a Sanchi donor did.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading shared iconography as shared theology. Form travels faster and further than doctrine, which is why an argument built on a motif must stay an argument about visual inheritance and patron choice, not about belief.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In the Mauryan period, popular religious sculpture mattered more for the future of Indian art than imperial court sculpture did." Critically examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely defensible, and the strongest evidence for it is a line of descent rather than a judgement of quality.

The popular stream consists of the Yaksha and Yakshi figures - folk deities of water, fertility, trees, forest and wilderness, worshipped for boons and protection, understood as residing formlessly on trees and water bodies, and shared by Jainism, Hinduism and Buddhism. Its named monuments are the Parkham Yaksha, found south of Mathura and recorded as speaking of massive strength and gravity, and the Didarganj whisk-bearer. Its future is documented: the Mathura school's images are described as modelled on the earlier yaksha images of the Mauryan period, and the Shalabhanjika form runs from Sanchi and Bharhut gateways to Hoysala temple reliefs at Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura. A related motif, Gajalakshmi, crosses from a Bharhut railing to a third-century Kausambi coin to Kalinga temple figures in lalitasana.

The imperial stream produced technically superb work - monolithic shafts, animal capitals and the characteristic polish - but it did not found a school of figural sculpture in the same way.

Two qualifications are essential. The technical achievement, including the polish, is shared across both streams, so the contrast is about descent rather than skill. And the imperial capitals did establish a durable symbolic repertoire, as the survival of the Sarnath lions in modern national usage shows.

The verdict, therefore, is that imperial sculpture set the technical standard and the symbolic vocabulary, while popular sculpture supplied the figural ancestry of everything that follows.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the descent-not-quality thesis 3 + the Yaksha and Yakshi definition with both named figures 3 + the documented Mathura descent 3 + the Shalabhanjika and Gajalakshmi continuity evidence 3 + both qualifications 2 + a verdict that splits technique from ancestry 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 7:** The imperial stream has been named but not examined. It deserves a lesson of its own, because its animals are not decoration and because one examination demand in this topic turns entirely on being able to say what a lion does that a bull does not.

CA found: none required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one capital, read as a system

```text
                    THE SARNATH CAPITAL
                            |
   crowning:  FOUR LIONS standing BACK TO BACK
              -> "Buddha spreading Dharma in all directions"
              -> built in commemoration of the FIRST SERMON,
                 the Dharmachakrapravartana
                            |
   below:     A CIRCULAR ABACUS carrying carvings of
              BULL | HORSE | LION | ELEPHANT  + the DHARMACHAKRA
                            |
   the abacus animals represent FOUR DIRECTIONS:
              galloping HORSE  -> WEST
              ELEPHANT         -> EAST
              BULL             -> SOUTH
              LION             -> NORTH
   "they seem to follow each other turning the wheel of
    existence till eternity"
                            |
   each abacus animal also carries a BIOGRAPHICAL meaning:
              ELEPHANT  = Queen Maya's dream, a white elephant
                          entering her womb
              BULL      = the zodiac sign of Taurus, the month
                          in which Buddha was born
              HORSE     = Kanthaka, used to leave the princely life
              LION      = the attainment of enlightenment
                            |
   modern:    the ABACUS AND THE ANIMAL PART form the official
              NATIONAL EMBLEM OF INDIA, adopted 26 January 1950;
              "Satyameva Jayate", from the Mundaka Upanishad,
              inscribed below the abacus in Devanagari; the capital
              crowned by the Dharma Chakra
```

*Read the diagram twice. The first reading gives you a list of animals. The second gives you the point: within one capital the bull marks the beginning of a life and the lion marks its culmination, and the same lion appears twice - once as an abacus animal and once, four times over, as the crowning figure. The animals are a system, not a menagerie.*

### 1. The pillar as a sculptural problem

The structure first, briefly, because the sculpture sits on it. Under Ashoka, inscriptions on pillars - as a symbol of the state, to commemorate battle victories, and to propagate imperial sermons - assumed great significance. At an average height of **40 ft**, the pillars were usually made of **chunar sandstone** and comprised a **shaft** and a **capital**. A long shaft formed the base, made of a **single piece of stone, a monolith**. On top lay the capital, which was either **lotus shaped or bell shaped**; the bell-shaped capitals were **influenced by the Iranian pillars**, as was the **highly polished and lustrous finish**. Above that was a **circular or rectangular base known as the abacus**, on which an **animal figure** was placed. The excavation of the Lion Capital of Ashoka was done by **F. O. Oertel in 1904-05**.

The pillar itself belongs to the architecture topic. What belongs here is everything above the abacus line and the reading of the animals - and the observation that a monolithic polished shaft of that height is itself a sculptural achievement of quarrying, transport and finishing.

### 2. The comparative table: four capitals, four configurations

| Site | State | Capital design | Associated inscription | What the variation shows |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sarnath** | Uttar Pradesh | **Four lions standing back to back on a circular abacus**, with **bull, horse, lion and elephant** carvings and the **Dharmachakra** | The inscription column of the printed pillar table is clipped in the text available here, reading only "Contains S... of Ashoka"; the same source separately records that **Ashoka's Schism Edicts were found inscribed on the pillars at Sanchi, Sarnath and Allahabad**, originally in Kaushambi, which identifies it | The fullest programme: crowning figure, directional scheme, wheel |
| **Sanchi** | Madhya Pradesh | A **Lion Capital pillar of finely polished sandstone**, erected by **Mauryan emperor Ashoka** and **very similar to the Lion Capital pillar of Sarnath**: **similarly four lions back to back**, but with a **flatter abacus showing alternating pairs of geese and flame palmettes** | **Dated around 250 BC**, and carrying **two sets of inscriptions** - **Ashoka's Schism Edict**, which warns of punishment for dissent in the sangha or order, and a **later Gupta-period inscription in the ornamental Sankha Lipi**, a conch-shaped script which, unlike the Brahmi of the Ashokan pillars deciphered by James Prinsep, **has not yet been deciphered** | The lion capital as a **repeated imperial type with local variation** - the same crowning group, a different abacus programme - and the clearest case of an object carrying inscriptions from two widely separated periods |
| **Vaishali (Kolhua)** | Bihar | **Single lion capital; no abacus** | Contains Major Pillar Edict VI | The lion type without the directional system - proof that the four-animal abacus is a specific choice, not a default |
| **Rampurva** | Bihar | **Two pillars: one with a lion capital, one with a bull capital** | The **lion capital pillar is inscribed with Major Pillar Edicts I to VI**; the **bull capital pillar does not have inscriptions** | One site, one patron, one period, two animals, two different functions |

Take Sanchi carefully, because it carries two distinct chronological claims that must not be merged. The **capital and the pillar** are Ashokan and are **dated around 250 BC** - keep the source's own approximation, "around", rather than converting it into a precise year. The **Sankha Lipi inscription is Gupta-period**, added centuries later to an object already standing. A pillar can therefore be a Mauryan sculpture and a Gupta epigraphic surface at the same time, and an answer that dates the object from its latest inscription has made the commonest error in the whole field of inscribed sculpture.

Two further uses follow. First, Sanchi against Sarnath isolates the **abacus** as the variable: the crowning four lions are the same, so the difference between a circular abacus carrying four directional animals and the Dharmachakra, and a flatter abacus carrying alternating pairs of geese and flame palmettes, is a designed choice about what the pillar is to say. Second, Sanchi against Vaishali shows the type stripped in the other direction - the same lion, no abacus at all.

Rampurva is the strongest single evidence unit in this lesson and possibly in the lesson's whole examination family. It removes every confounding variable - place, patron, period - and leaves only the animal. At that one site the **lion was recruited to carry the edict text and the bull was not**. That is a recorded fact, not an inference, and it licenses the argument that the two animals were not interchangeable decorative choices.

Three cautions attach to it. Do not say the bull pillar is uninscribed *because* it is earlier; the source states only that it lacks inscriptions. Do not assign it to a non-Ashokan dynasty. And do not date it independently of Ashoka. Rampurva is also recorded as one of the **seven locations** of the Major Pillar Edicts, alongside Kandahar, Delhi-Topra, Delhi-Meerut, Allahabad, Lauriya-Araraj and Lauriya-Nandangarh - of which only the Delhi-Topra pillar has all seven Major Pillar Edicts present.

### 3. The lion and the bull as two functions, traced across the whole topic

This is the evidence bank for one of the two exact Mains demands this topic owns, and it is organised as three legs because the evidence for each leg is of a different kind.

**Leg one - the earliest art, where the asymmetry starts.** The Harappan seal motifs include a unicorn, **humped bull**, rhinoceros, tiger, elephant, buffalo, bison, goat, markhor, ibex and crocodile - and **no evidence of cow and lion has been found on any seal**. A second Harappan bull object exists in another medium: the **bronze bull of Kalibangan**, cast by the lost-wax method known from the Indus period. So the bull is a privileged image in the earliest urban visual culture and the lion is simply absent from it. The caution that earns the mark: the script is undeciphered, so what the bull meant to Harappans cannot be recovered, and later Shaiva meaning must not be read backwards into it.

**Leg two - Buddhist meaning, where both animals acquire specific work.** Within the Sarnath programme the **bull marks birth** - the zodiac sign of Taurus, the month in which the Buddha was born - and the **lion marks the attainment of enlightenment**, with four lions symbolising the Buddha spreading Dharma in all directions in commemoration of the first sermon. Independent corroboration comes from the aniconic sign set taught in Lesson 8, where the Buddha's **birth** is indicated by **bull, elephant, lotus and footprint** and his **sermons** by **wheel, lion and deer**. Two different media - a pillar capital and a narrative relief code - carry one consistent assignment.

**Leg three - the medieval architectural careers, where the two animals diverge sharply.**

| | **The bull** | **The lion** |
|---|---|---|
| Form | Remains a **naturalistic bull**, Nandi | Becomes a **composite, hybrid creature** - the **Yali** and the **Vyala** |
| Placement | Given **its own building** on the temple's axis: a **Nandi Mandap** in front of the temple, since the **vahana** is placed just before the sanctum sanctorum | Multiplied and distributed: on **gopurams**, on **pillars**, in **friezes**, at **bases** |
| Named cases | **Brihadishvara, Thanjavur** - a big Nandi Mandap with a **massive monolithic Nandi, the second largest in the country**; **Lepakshi Veerabhadra, Andhra Pradesh** - the **largest Nandi monolith in the country**; **Kailasa, Ellora (Cave 16)** - the **two-storeyed** monolithic temple **housing a shivalingam**, whose **courtyard has two huge pillars with the flagstaff and a Nandi mandapa**; the **Thousand Pillar temple, Warangal** - a **monolithic Nandi** within its premises alongside rock-cut elephants; the **Ramappa temple, Kakatiya** - where **in front of the Nandi Pavilion stands the main temple**, made on a star-shaped platform; and the **Hoysala** group, where the **Nandi Pavilion and a separate kalyani are noted for their ceremonial use** | **Yali** - a **composite lion figure** symbolic of the **Nayaka architectural identity**, on heavily painted gopurams and on narrative and composite columns; in Tamil literature a mythical creature with a **cat-like body, a lion's head with elephant tusks and a serpent's tail**, **more powerful than the lion, the tiger or the elephant**, and a **guardian creature protecting human beings physically and spiritually**; in the South Indian sculpture chapter given as **a mythological creature with the head and body of a lion and the trunk and tusks of an elephant**. **Vyala** - at **Khajuraho**, on temples dedicated to Vishnu, **hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies**, also found in other Indian temples |
| Function | Ritual: the deity's mount, attending the sanctum | Guardian, and structurally load-bearing on pillars and bases |

Note the two recorded anatomies of the Yali. One passage gives a cat-like body with a lion's head, elephant tusks and a serpent's tail; another gives the head and body of a lion with an elephant's trunk and tusks. Both are from the same source. State the **composite, lion-based, elephant-tusked** core and do **not** present either variant as an error.

**The six Nandi cases are not six repetitions; they are six different demonstrations, and an answer gains from choosing the right one.**

```text
  WHAT EACH NANDI CASE PROVES

  THANJAVUR    a massive monolith in a dedicated Nandi Mandap,
               "the second largest in the country"
               -> the bull at IMPERIAL SCALE
  LEPAKSHI     "the largest monolithic Nandi in the country"
               -> the superlative that pairs with Thanjavur and
                  keeps both claims source-checked
  ELLORA       Cave 16, Kailasa: a TWO-STOREYED temple housing a
  (Cave 16)    SHIVALINGAM, with a Nandi mandapa and TWO HUGE
               PILLARS WITH THE FLAGSTAFF in the courtyard
               -> the same axial arrangement executed
                  MONOLITHICALLY, by EXCAVATION rather than
                  construction: the bull's building is cut, not built
  WARANGAL     a monolithic Nandi within the Thousand Pillar
               temple premises, alongside rock-cut elephants
               -> the bull inside a wider carved animal programme
  RAMAPPA      "In front of the Nandi Pavilion stands the main
               temple", on a star-shaped platform
               -> the arrangement stated as a SPATIAL RELATION:
                  the pavilion is the point from which the temple
                  is approached and seen
  HOYSALA      the Nandi Pavilion and a separate kalyani, or temple
               tank, "noted for their ceremonial use"
               -> the bull tied to PROCESSION AND CEREMONY, not
                  only to iconography
```

⚠️ **One attribution caution, disclosed rather than smoothed.** The canonical owner's evidence bank presents the monolithic Nandi and the "main temple in front of the Nandi Pavilion" together under Ramappa. In the core art source the **monolithic Nandi** is recorded for the **Thousand Pillar temple at Warangal**, and the **Nandi Pavilion with the main temple standing in front of it** is recorded for the **Ramappa temple**; no monolithic Nandi is recorded for Ramappa. Both Kakatiya cases are therefore kept separate above, and neither detail is transferred to the other temple.

### 4. The change over time, stated as a sentence you can write

```text
  THE LION                                THE BULL
  Harappan seals: ABSENT                  Harappan seals: FREQUENT (humped bull)
        |                                        |
  Mauryan: SINGULAR, NATURALISTIC,        Mauryan: paired with the lion at
  ELEVATED, INSCRIBED - Sarnath's         Rampurva, but UNINSCRIBED
  four lions, Sanchi's four lions                |
  on a flatter geese-and-palmette         Buddhist code: BIRTH
  abacus, Vaishali's single lion                 |
        |                                 Medieval: NANDI - naturalistic,
  Buddhist code: SERMON /                 monolithic, given its own mandapa
  ENLIGHTENMENT                           on the temple axis - BUILT at
        |                                 Thanjavur and Lepakshi, EXCAVATED
  Medieval: HYBRIDISED (Yali, Vyala)      at Ellora Cave 16, and tied to
  and MULTIPLIED (friezes, pillars),      CEREMONY in the Hoysala pavilion
  doing GUARDIAN and LOAD-BEARING work           |
        |                                 FUNCTION: generative power,
  FUNCTION: authority, doctrine,          service to the deity, ritual
  guardianship                            attendance
        |
  MODERN: the Sarnath abacus and animal part become the
  NATIONAL EMBLEM, adopted 26 January 1950
```

The verdict this supports: the two animals endure because each solved a different representational problem - the bull made generative power and attendance visible, the lion made authority and doctrine visible - and the survival of the Sarnath lions in the national emblem since 26 January 1950 shows that the second problem is still being solved the same way.

### Examination use

This lesson carries one of the two exact Mains questions this topic owns, reproduced and analysed in full in the verified PYQ section: the 2022 demand to discuss the significance of the lion and bull figures in Indian mythology, art and architecture. The three-leg structure above is the answer's spine; the Rampurva pair is its single best evidence unit; the four-capital comparison supplies the "repeated imperial type with local variation" argument that converts a list of pillars into a programme; and the six named Nandi cases give the architecture leg enough range to answer at 15 marks without repetition. The material also supports objective items on capital-and-site pairing, on the national emblem's exact composition, on the Sanchi pillar's two inscriptions, and on Harappan motif absences. For any question on Ashokan art, the distinction between the pillar as architecture and the capital as sculpture is the boundary sentence to open with.

### Traps introduced here

- Placing a lion on a Harappan seal, or forgetting that the cow is equally absent.
- Describing the whole Sarnath pillar as the national emblem; it is the **abacus and the animal part** that were adopted, on 26 January 1950.
- Adding meanings to the four abacus animals beyond the four the source assigns.
- Dating the Sanchi pillar from its Gupta-period Sankha Lipi inscription; the capital is Ashokan and dated **around** 250 BC, and the later inscription was added to an object already standing.
- Converting the Sanchi date's "around 250 BC" into a precise year, or claiming the Sankha Lipi has been read; it has not yet been deciphered.
- Transferring the geese-and-flame-palmette abacus to Sarnath, or the four directional animals and Dharmachakra to Sanchi.
- Narrating a Nandi origin myth, a Vedic bull identification or a "bull of dharma" reading; none is carried in the owned evidence.
- Assigning a monolithic Nandi to Ramappa, or the "main temple in front of the Nandi Pavilion" arrangement to the Thousand Pillar temple; the two Kakatiya records are separate.
- Dating the Rampurva bull pillar independently of Ashoka, or explaining its lack of inscription by an invented chronology.
- Presenting one of the two recorded Yali anatomies as a mistake.
- Stating Yali counts, pillar totals or monolith dimensions; the only comparative scale claims available are "largest" at Lepakshi and "second largest" at Thanjavur.

### Revision notes

1. Ashokan pillars: average height 40 ft, usually chunar sandstone, a monolithic shaft, a lotus- or bell-shaped capital, then a circular or rectangular abacus carrying an animal figure.
2. Bell-shaped capitals and the highly polished lustrous finish are attributed to Iranian influence; the Lion Capital was excavated by F. O. Oertel in 1904-05.
3. Sarnath: four lions back to back on a circular abacus with bull, horse, lion and elephant carvings and the Dharmachakra. Its inscription cell is clipped in the printed pillar table; the same source records Ashoka's Schism Edicts as inscribed on the pillars at Sanchi, Sarnath and Allahabad, originally in Kaushambi.
4. Abacus directions: galloping horse west, elephant east, bull south, lion north; they seem to follow each other turning the wheel of existence till eternity.
5. Biographical meanings: elephant for Queen Maya's dream of a white elephant entering her womb; bull for Taurus, the month of the Buddha's birth; horse for Kanthaka; lion for the attainment of enlightenment.
6. The four lions symbolise the Buddha spreading Dharma in all directions, built in commemoration of the first sermon, the Dharmachakrapravartana.
7. Sanchi: a Lion Capital pillar of finely polished sandstone erected by Ashoka, very similar to Sarnath's, with four lions back to back but a **flatter abacus showing alternating pairs of geese and flame palmettes**; dated **around 250 BC**; carrying **two sets of inscriptions** - Ashoka's **Schism Edict**, warning of punishment for dissent in the sangha or order, and a later **Gupta-period inscription in the ornamental Sankha Lipi**, a conch-shaped script not yet deciphered, although James Prinsep had deciphered the Brahmi of the Ashokan pillars.
8. The Sanchi capital and its later inscription are two separate chronological claims: Mauryan sculpture, Gupta epigraphic surface.
9. Vaishali (Kolhua): single lion capital, no abacus; contains Major Pillar Edict VI.
10. Rampurva, Bihar: two pillars, one lion capital inscribed with Major Pillar Edicts I to VI, one bull capital without inscriptions.
11. The seven Major Pillar Edict locations: Kandahar, Delhi-Topra, Delhi-Meerut, Allahabad, Lauriya-Araraj, Lauriya-Nandangarh and Rampurva; only Delhi-Topra has all seven edicts.
12. The abacus and animal part of the Sarnath pillar form the national emblem, adopted 26 January 1950; Satyameva Jayate from the Mundaka Upanishad is inscribed below the abacus in Devanagari; the capital is crowned by the Dharma Chakra.
13. Harappan seals: humped bull frequent, lion and cow absent; bronze bull of Kalibangan cast by cire-perdue.
14. Aniconic corroboration: birth is indicated by bull, elephant, lotus and footprint; sermons by wheel, lion and deer.
15. Bull in medieval architecture, six named cases: Thanjavur's Nandi Mandap with the monolith recorded as the second largest in the country; Lepakshi's, recorded as the largest; Ellora Cave 16, the two-storeyed Kailasa housing a shivalingam, whose courtyard has two huge pillars with the flagstaff and a Nandi mandapa; the Thousand Pillar temple at Warangal with a monolithic Nandi alongside rock-cut elephants; Ramappa, where the main temple stands in front of the Nandi Pavilion on a star-shaped platform; and the Hoysala Nandi Pavilion with its separate kalyani, noted for ceremonial use.
16. Ellora's case is the arrangement executed monolithically, by excavation rather than construction; Ramappa's states it as a spatial relation; the Hoysala case ties it to ceremony.
17. Lion in medieval architecture: Yali as a composite lion figure symbolic of Nayaka architectural identity, on gopurams and columns, described in two anatomies; Vyala at Khajuraho as a hybrid imaginary animal with a lion's body, also found in other Indian temples.
18. The contrast: the lion moves from singular, naturalistic, elevated and inscribed to hybridised, multiplied and protective; the bull moves from an unreadable Harappan image to a Buddhist birth-symbol to a ritual attendant with its own building.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is Rampurva a better piece of evidence for the lion-bull distinction than the Sarnath capital, and what does the Sarnath capital supply that Rampurva cannot?

**Model answer:** Rampurva controls the variables. Two Ashokan pillars stand at one site, from one patron and one period, one carrying a lion capital and one a bull capital; the lion capital pillar is inscribed with Major Pillar Edicts I to VI while the bull capital pillar has no inscriptions. Because everything except the animal is held constant, the difference in treatment can be attributed to the animal itself, and the inscription asymmetry is a recorded fact rather than an interpretation - which licenses the argument that the lion was recruited to carry the imperial and doctrinal message while the bull was not. Sarnath cannot do this, because its lions and its bull are inside one composition and cannot be compared independently. What Sarnath supplies instead is the meaning system: a crowning group of four lions symbolising the Buddha spreading Dharma in all directions in commemoration of the first sermon; a circular abacus whose bull, horse, lion and elephant represent south, west, north and east and seem to follow each other turning the wheel of existence; and specific biographical assignments in which the bull marks the birth month and the lion the attainment of enlightenment. Rampurva proves the animals were treated differently; Sarnath explains what each was understood to mean.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the abacus animals as generic auspicious creatures. The source assigns each one a distinct directional and biographical meaning, and an answer that reproduces those assignments is demonstrably reading the programme rather than admiring it.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Ashokan animal capitals are better understood as a communication system than as decorative sculpture." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds, because every element of the capital is assigned a job.

The Sarnath capital crowns four lions standing back to back, symbolising the Buddha spreading Dharma in all directions and commemorating the first sermon, the Dharmachakrapravartana. Beneath them a circular abacus carries bull, horse, lion and elephant representing south, west, north and east, described as following each other and turning the wheel of existence, with the Dharmachakra completing the scheme. Each abacus animal also carries a biographical meaning: the elephant recalls Queen Maya's dream, the bull the zodiac month of the Buddha's birth, the horse Kanthaka, and the lion the attainment of enlightenment. Variation confirms deliberate choice: the Sanchi pillar of around 250 BC repeats the four lions back to back but replaces the directional scheme with a flatter abacus of alternating geese and flame palmettes; Vaishali carries a single lion capital and no abacus at all; and at Rampurva the lion pillar bears Major Pillar Edicts I to VI while the bull pillar bears none.

Decoration cannot explain selective inscription or a directional scheme.

One qualification: the pillars were also symbols of the state and commemorations of victory, so the communication is political as well as doctrinal.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the assigned-function thesis 2 + the Sarnath programme with directions and the Dharmachakra 2 + at least two of the four biographical meanings 2 + the Sanchi, Vaishali and Rampurva variations as proof of deliberate choice 3 + the political-message qualification 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 8:** The lion and the bull already stood for events in the Buddha's life before any sculptor carved his body. That is not an accident of taste. It is the central fact of the next lesson: for a long period Indian sculptors represented the Buddha by refusing to depict him.

## Lesson 8 - The Shungas and the aniconic code: showing a figure by not showing him

Progress: 8/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Shunga succession and the ornamental turn; Bharhut and the Bharhut Yavana; the chronological order of the post-Mauryan Buddhist monuments; the complete aniconic symbol set event by event; the four-event and five-event schemes; and the empty seat as a presence-marker

Book context: queried - the core art source's Shunga Period Sculptures section, the chronological list of post-Mauryan Buddhist monuments in the Gandhara discussion, the symbolic-representation passage, the Buddhist symbol table with its event-by-event assignments and the empty-throne note, and the Amaravati ayaka-pillar passage with its five-event scheme; the Basic owner's aniconic evidence bank and its 2026 objective integration
CA search: "not required - symbolic representation lesson"
CA found: none required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the complete symbol code, event by event

```text
  EVENT IN THE BUDDHA'S LIFE          ASSOCIATED SYMBOLS
  ---------------------------------------------------------------
  BIRTH                               Bull; Elephant; Lotus;
                                      Foot Print
  ---------------------------------------------------------------
  GREAT RENUNCIATION                  Horse; Gandhakuti;
                                      EMPTY THRONE; Begging Bowl
  ---------------------------------------------------------------
  ENLIGHTENMENT (Nirvana)             Vajrasana; Bodhi Tree
  ---------------------------------------------------------------
  SERMONS (Teachings)                 Wheel; Lion; Deer
  ---------------------------------------------------------------
  DEATH (Parinirvana)                 Stupa
  ---------------------------------------------------------------

  AND THE SUMMARY RULE AS THE SOURCE STATES IT:

  "The early Buddhist sculptors did not show Buddha in human form.
   They indicated Buddha's presence through EMPTY THRONE, BODHI TREE,
   WHEEL, STUPA - indicating four important events (renunciation,
   enlightenment, first sermon, death) in the life of Buddha."

  A SEPARATE, FIVE-EVENT SCHEME EXISTS IN THE SAME EVIDENCE BASE:
  the AYAKA PILLARS at Amaravati, typically five on each platform at
  the four cardinal directions, are believed to represent
   BIRTH | RENUNCIATION | ENLIGHTENMENT |
   DHARMACHAKRA PRAVARTANA | MAHAPARINIRVANA
```

*Two schemes coexist and both are recorded. The four-symbol summary and the five-event ayaka scheme do not contradict each other - one is a set of representational devices, the other a set of commemorated episodes - but they do not map one to one either, and an answer should not force them to.*

### 1. The Shunga succession and what changed

The Mauryan empire was replaced by the **Shunga dynasty in 185 BCE**. In sculpture there was a **marked development in ornamentation and in the depiction of life in its true form**. Two words there carry the whole judgement: *ornamentation*, meaning surface richness and the filling of available fields, and *life in its true form*, meaning the observed world - people, animals, trees, activities - rather than only sacred emblems.

The named monument of the period is the **Buddhist sculpture of the Bharhut Stupa**, in the **Satna district of Madhya Pradesh**, described as the most noted excellence of the period, with the **northern gateway** the finest example of a work of art. And one dated relief is singled out: the **Bharhut Yavana**, a relief of a warrior, **dated to 100 BCE**.

That last item is worth a sentence in any answer about foreign contact, and the sentence should be careful. A relief called "the Yavana" at a central Indian Buddhist site around 100 BCE is evidence of an awareness of a foreign figure type in Indian sculpture a century or more before the Gandhara school takes shape. It is not evidence of a Greek workshop, a Greek patron or Greek authorship, and none of those should be claimed.

### 2. The chronological order that is routinely got wrong

The most important Buddhist monuments adorned with sculpture in the post-Mauryan period are introduced in the source as a list **in chronological order**, and are printed there in the sequence **Bharhut Stupa (150 BC); Great Stupa of Sanchi (50 BC); Second Stupa at Sanchi (125 BC); Third Sanchi Stupa (25 BC); Bodhgaya (75 BC)**. A discrepancy has to be reported rather than smoothed: the printed sequence is not the sequence the printed dates yield. Arranged by the dates as given, the order is:

```text
  Bharhut Stupa ................ 150 BC
        |
  Second Stupa at Sanchi ....... 125 BC
        |
  Bodhgaya ..................... 75 BC
        |
  Great Stupa of Sanchi ........ 50 BC
        |
  Third Sanchi Stupa ........... 25 BC
```

Use the dates, name them individually, and do not rely on the printed sequence as an ordering. Two observations then make this useful rather than merely memorised. First, the **Second** Stupa at Sanchi is earlier by date than the **Great** Stupa of Sanchi - which defeats the natural assumption that "Great" means "first". Second, the whole set sits between roughly 150 BC and 25 BC, which is the window in which Indian narrative relief sculpture becomes a developed art. The monuments themselves belong to the architecture topic; the dates are carried here because the sculptural programme is dated by them.

### 3. The symbolic phase: what was actually done

The purpose of the earliest Buddhist sculpture was to **glorify the Buddha by recounting episodes from his life and the Jataka**. But the earlier Buddhist monuments **did not depict the Buddha in his human form**; he was represented through symbols - **by his footprint, his throne, and his promenade or stupa, by tree, by wheel**, and so on.

Take the symbol table above apart into its logic, because a list learned as a list is forgotten and a list learned as a logic is not.

| Kind of symbol | Examples | How it works |
|---|---|---|
| **The place where he was** | Empty Throne, Vajrasana, Gandhakuti, promenade | An occupied space shown unoccupied; the viewer supplies the figure |
| **The thing he used or left** | Begging Bowl, Horse (Kanthaka), Foot Print | An index - the object implies the person who handled it |
| **The natural witness** | Bodhi Tree, Lotus, Deer | The setting stands in for the event that happened in it |
| **The teaching itself** | Wheel (Dharmachakra) | The doctrine, not the man, made visible |
| **The body after death** | Stupa | The relic container replaces the person entirely |
| **The animal sign** | Bull (birth month), Elephant (Maya's dream), Lion (enlightenment, preaching) | A coded attribute, shared with the capital programme of Lesson 7 |

Now the analytical point, and it is the one that converts this from a list into an argument: **the empty seat is not an absence but a presence-marker.** The composition is arranged around it, the attendant figures look towards it, and the space is framed as occupied. A sculptor who wanted to omit the Buddha would not build a composition that points at the gap. The refusal to give him a body therefore records a doctrinal reluctance, not an inability - and the later appearance of the human image marks a change in devotional practice, not merely an improvement in skill.

### 4. The honest difficulty: what the empty seat stands for

This has to be handled with care, because it is the point on which an official objective question turns and because the evidence base itself does not speak with one voice.

```text
  WHAT THE OWNED EVIDENCE ACTUALLY SAYS

  (1) The symbol TABLE places "Empty Throne" under
      GREAT RENUNCIATION, together with Horse, Gandhakuti
      and Begging Bowl.

  (2) The summary NOTE immediately below says the sculptors
      indicated the Buddha's presence through Empty Throne,
      Bodhi Tree, Wheel and Stupa - "indicating four important
      events (renunciation, enlightenment, first sermon,
      death)" - which lines the four devices up against four
      events without stating which device carries which.

  (3) The narrative-representation passage lists the throne
      among the symbols used generally to represent the
      Buddha, without assigning it to a single episode.

  THE DISCIPLINED POSITION
  The empty seat or throne is a general presence-marker used
  where the Buddha would otherwise be shown. The owned evidence
  associates it explicitly with the Great Renunciation in its
  table, and lists it among four devices covering renunciation,
  enlightenment, first sermon and death in its summary. Where a
  specific episode is required, the surrounding elements decide:
  a throne beneath a Bodhi tree, a throne with a wheel and deer,
  a throne with a riderless horse. The seat alone is the device;
  the context supplies the event.
```

An official Preliminary item of 2026 asks what an empty seat represents in early Buddhist iconography. It is reproduced in full, answer-neutrally, in the verified PYQ section of this session, together with its four response choices and the reason no answer is recorded: the locally held key for that year is provisional. What this lesson supplies is the reading method that the item is testing - that the empty seat is a presence-marker whose specific reference is fixed by the elements around it - and the explicit statement that the owned evidence contains a table assignment and a summary listing that do not resolve to a single episode on their own.

That is the correct examination behaviour and it is worth stating why. Guessing an episode here would require choosing between two formulations in the same evidence base. Naming the device correctly, naming its recorded associations, and identifying the contextual elements that disambiguate it is both safer and more complete than a guess.

### 5. The five-event scheme, and why it is not a contradiction

Amaravati supplies a parallel scheme in architecture-adjacent sculpture. The **ayaka pillars**, distinctive structures found on the stupa's four cardinal directions and typically **five in number on each platform**, are believed to represent five significant events in the Buddha's life: **Birth, Renunciation, Enlightenment, Dharmachakra Pravartana and Mahaparinirvana**.

Set the two schemes side by side and the relationship is clear:

| | **The four-device summary** | **The five-event ayaka scheme** |
|---|---|---|
| What is being counted | Representational **devices** - empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel, stupa | Commemorated **episodes** |
| The list | renunciation, enlightenment, first sermon, death | birth, renunciation, enlightenment, first sermon, death |
| The difference | **Birth is not in the device summary** - because birth has its own symbols in the table, namely bull, elephant, lotus and footprint | Birth is included as an episode |
| Conclusion | The schemes count different things and differ by one item for a stateable reason |

Being able to write that comparison is worth more than either list alone, because it demonstrates that you know what each list is a list *of*.

### Examination use

This lesson owns one exact Preliminary route through 2026 and supports a reproduced Mains demand about early Buddhist stupa art expounding Buddhist ideals through folk motifs and narrative. The symbol table is directly testable in objective form, most obviously as a pair-matching item or as a "which symbol does not indicate this event" item; the two reproduced items in the core source that ask about Sanchi sculpture and the Shalabhanjika sit in exactly that family and are recorded answer-neutrally later in this session. For descriptive answers, the presence-marker reading and the four-versus-five comparison are the two moves that lift a script above a symbol list. The Shunga dating sequence protects against chronology errors in any post-Mauryan question.

### Traps introduced here

- Saying early Buddhist art "avoided images"; what it avoided was the **human figure of the Buddha**, while animal, human attendant and narrative imagery flourished.
- Assigning the empty seat to one episode as if the owned evidence settled it.
- Asserting an answer to an unkeyed or provisionally keyed official item.
- Placing the Great Stupa of Sanchi before the Second Stupa at Sanchi.
- Claiming the Bharhut Yavana proves Greek authorship or patronage.
- Merging the four-device summary and the five-event ayaka scheme into one list.
- Treating the aniconic phase as a stage of technical incapacity rather than of doctrinal choice.

### Revision notes

1. The Shunga dynasty replaced the Mauryan empire in 185 BCE; sculpture shows marked development in ornamentation and in the depiction of life in its true form.
2. Bharhut Stupa, Satna district, Madhya Pradesh, is the most noted excellence of the period; its northern gateway is the finest example of a work of art.
3. The Bharhut Yavana is a relief of a warrior dated to 100 BCE - evidence of an awareness of a foreign figure type, not of Greek authorship.
4. Chronological order of the post-Mauryan Buddhist monuments: Bharhut Stupa 150 BC, Second Stupa at Sanchi 125 BC, Bodhgaya 75 BC, Great Stupa of Sanchi 50 BC, Third Sanchi Stupa 25 BC.
5. The purpose of early Buddhist sculpture was to glorify the Buddha by recounting episodes of his life and the Jataka.
6. The earlier monuments did not depict the Buddha in human form; he was represented by footprint, throne, promenade or stupa, tree and wheel.
7. Symbol assignments: Birth - bull, elephant, lotus, footprint. Great Renunciation - horse, Gandhakuti, empty throne, begging bowl. Enlightenment - vajrasana, Bodhi tree. Sermons - wheel, lion, deer. Death - stupa.
8. The summary rule: sculptors indicated the Buddha's presence through empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel and stupa, indicating renunciation, enlightenment, first sermon and death.
9. The devices work in six ways: place occupied, object used, natural witness, doctrine itself, relic container, coded animal sign.
10. The empty seat is a presence-marker, not an omission; the composition is arranged to point at it.
11. Where a specific episode is required, the surrounding elements disambiguate - throne with Bodhi tree, throne with wheel and deer, throne with riderless horse.
12. The Amaravati ayaka pillars, typically five per platform at the four cardinal directions, are believed to represent Birth, Renunciation, Enlightenment, Dharmachakra Pravartana and Mahaparinirvana.
13. The four-device summary counts devices and omits birth because birth has its own symbols; the five-event scheme counts episodes and includes it.
14. The later human image marks a change in devotional practice rather than an improvement in skill.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that "the aniconic phase shows that early Buddhism prohibited images." State precisely what is wrong, and give the formulation that the owned evidence supports.

**Model answer:** The claim overreaches in two directions. First, it converts a representational practice into a prohibition; the evidence records that the earlier Buddhist monuments did not depict the Buddha in his human form and that he was represented by footprint, throne, promenade or stupa, tree and wheel, but it records no rule forbidding images. Second, it is factually wrong about images in general: the same monuments are densely populated with imagery - Jataka and life episodes, animals, attendant figures, Shalabhanjika bracket figures on the gateways of the Great Stupa at Sanchi and at Bharhut, Gajalakshmi on the Bharhut railings and on the Sanchi vedika. What was avoided was the human figure of the Buddha specifically. The supported formulation is: "In the earlier phase the Buddha's presence was indicated rather than depicted - through the empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel and stupa, which the source associates with renunciation, enlightenment, the first sermon and death - while narrative, folk and animal imagery was abundant; the later appearance of the anthropomorphic image therefore marks a change in devotional practice rather than the lifting of a ban."

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading absence as prohibition. Aniconism here is a positive compositional device - a seat framed as occupied, with the viewer supplying the figure - and treating it as a negative rule both misdescribes the sculpture and forfeits the best analytical sentence available in the section.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Early Buddhist sculpture made a doctrine visible without making its founder visible." Elucidate. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The statement captures the period's central device exactly.

Doctrine was made visible through assigned signs. The wheel rendered the teaching itself; the Bodhi tree and vajrasana rendered enlightenment; the stupa rendered the parinirvana; the horse, begging bowl and empty throne rendered the renunciation; and bull, elephant, lotus and footprint rendered the birth. Narrative was equally visible: the purpose of the sculpture was to glorify the Buddha by recounting episodes from his life and the Jataka, and the Shunga period shows a marked development in ornamentation and in the depiction of life in its true form, above all in the Bharhut programme whose northern gateway is singled out.

The founder, however, was not shown in human form. The empty throne is the key device: the composition is built around a seat deliberately left unoccupied, so the viewer supplies the presence.

The qualification worth adding is that this was a practice rather than a recorded prohibition, and its later abandonment reflects changed devotional practice, not new skill.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the doctrine-visible-founder-invisible thesis 2 + at least four event-to-symbol assignments 3 + the empty throne read as a presence-marker rather than an omission 2 + one named Shunga-period monument with its recorded quality judgement 2 + the practice-not-prohibition qualification 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 9:** The human image does eventually arrive, and it arrives in a region whose recorded history is one of repeated political change - Persian, Greek, Indian and Central Asian in turn. The next lesson takes that region apart strand by strand, because an examination demand has already asked for exactly that separation.
