*The diagram is the answer plan for an entire demand family. Three strands are named separately in the source, each with its own evidence; the subject underneath them never changes; and the transformation claim at the bottom is what makes the school historically important rather than merely exotic.*

### 1. The recorded political sequence, and what may be inferred from it

Gandhara comprises the **Peshawar Valley, modern Swat, Buner and Bajaur**. Three phases of political control are recorded, and only three: the region was occupied by the **Achaemenid Empire of Persia** in the **sixth century BC**; by **Alexander**, and then by **Chandragupta Maurya**, in the **fourth century BC**; and by the **Kushanas** in the **first century AD**. The source then draws its own conclusion: with such a history behind it, it is not surprising that the people of Gandhara were **thoroughly cosmopolitan in their culture and outlook**.

Three disciplines apply to that sequence, and each of them is worth a mark.

- **Label the fourth-century phase accurately.** It contains two different things. Alexander's occupation is a Greek intervention; **Chandragupta Maurya's is not foreign at all**, and the recorded order - Alexander, *then* Chandragupta - describes a **Greek phase followed by Indian imperial integration**. An answer that folds Mauryan rule into a list of foreign occupations has misread the sentence it is quoting.
- **Do not supply a count of powers or a span of years.** The source names three phases and no total; it is silent on who held the region between the fourth century BC and the first century AD. Later **Indo-Greek**, **Saka** and **Parthian** presences are carried in this topic only as **strands of influence visible in the sculpture** - the source itself names the Parthian connection directly, calls the school's Hellenistic inheritance **"Indo-Greek art"**, and the canonical owner names the Central Asian strand as **Kushana, Parthian and Saka**. They are not converted here into a dated dynastic list, because no such list is recorded.
- **Keep cosmopolitanism on the right side of the fact-inference line.** The phrase is the source's own judgement about the population, offered as a reasonable expectation given the history - not a demonstrated finding. Reproduce it as the source's conclusion, and mark any causal use of it as analytical.

The analytical point that survives all three disciplines is worth stating, because it is better than the usual account. The common explanation attributes Gandhara's appearance to "Greek influence", as though a style arrived by contact alone. The recorded evidence supports something stronger and less tidy: **repeated changes of political control over one region, including an Indian imperial phase, left a population and a workshop tradition able to absorb Persian, Greek, Parthian and Central Asian material without ceasing to be Buddhist.** That reading is an inference from the recorded sequence; the sequence itself is the fact.

### 2. The purpose, and the transformation

The purpose of Gandhara art was **to glorify the Buddha by recounting the episodes from his life and the Jataka** - the same purpose as the aniconic phase of Lesson 8. What changed was the means. The earlier monuments represented the Buddha by his footprint, his throne, his promenade or stupa, by tree and by wheel. Then, as the source states it, **the Buddha's representation in human form under the Gandhara school transformed the course of the sculptural narrative of India**.

Hold the exact scope of that sentence. It says the school's human representation transformed Indian sculptural narrative. It does not say the school was the first anywhere to make a human Buddha image, and Lesson 12 will show why the priority question has to be handled separately and carefully.

### 3. Strand one: the Parthian influence

The evidence is specific and geographically located. A number of Buddhist sculptures found in the **Swat Valley of Pakistan** was closely linked with **Parthian art rather than Greco-Roman art**. Two examples are given:

- the sculpture of a **standing Buddha flanked by the gods Indra and Brahma**, described as **one of the earliest examples of the Buddha's image**;
- an image of a **seated Buddha with a halo**, depicted with **standing Indra and Brahma**.

The stylistic markers are that the **relief carving resembles Parthian style**, as do the **facial features with deeply incised lines**.

Two things follow. First, the Parthian strand is not a footnote: it is attached to some of the **earliest** Buddha images in the region, which means that at the very moment the human image appears, the dominant stylistic reference is not Greek. That is a direct and sourced counterweight to "Gandhara is Greek art". Second, the Indian content is visible in the same objects: a Buddha flanked by **Indra and Brahma** is an Indian religious composition, whatever the incision technique.

### 4. Strand two: the Central Asian or Bactro-Gandhara influence

**Who the strand comprises.** The core account names this strand geographically, as **Bactro-Gandhara**, and supplies its Kushana content. The canonical owner names it by people: the Central Asian strand is **Kushana, Parthian and Saka**. Both namings are carried here, because the examination demand of 2019 asks for the Central Asian elements by name and an answer that supplies only "Kushana" has answered a third of the label.

⚠️ **Two cautions attach to the Saka element and must travel with it.** First, **terminology**: "Saka" is the name of a people, and it is supplied by the canonical owner's disaggregation of the Central Asian strand rather than by the core art source's Gandhara passage, which does not name them. Second, **chronology and attribution**: **no Saka-specific sculptural feature, workshop, date or monument is recorded anywhere in the owned evidence.** Name the Saka as one of the three peoples the owner assigns to the Central Asian strand, and stop there - do not supply a Saka period, a Saka ruler or a Saka stylistic marker, because none is available and the invention would be immediately checkable.

**Where Bactria is.** The region between today's **Afghanistan and Tajikistan**.

**The material sequence, which is also a chronology.** Initially the sculptures were made from **blue schist and green phyllite**; later the use of **stucco, that is lime plaster**, started; and **from the third century AD it completely shifted to grey stone**. Any statement about Gandhara's material must be located in this sequence, because all three statements are true of different phases.

**Subject range.** This strand's output **included the depiction of religious themes as well as royal portraits**. A Buddhist school producing royal portraits is a significant fact: it tells you the workshops served a court as well as a monastic clientele, which is exactly what one would expect under imperial patronage.

**Kushana patronage and its scale.** In the first century AD, **Kushana rule brought major upliftment to the Buddha's portrayal**. **Kanishka's territory covered an area from Mathura in north central India, through Gandhara and Bactria, up to the borders of China.** That single sentence is the mechanism by which influence travelled in both directions - and it explains why the two great schools of the period, Gandhara and Mathura, lie inside one political space.

**The iconographic achievement, which is the strand's most under-used point.** There were **clear iconographic conventions established at this time for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras - for example, the Dharmachakra Parivartan Mudra and the Abhaya Hasta Mudra.**

Pause there, because this is the moment when Indian iconography becomes a *system*. Before conventions are fixed, an image is a depiction; after they are fixed, an image is readable - the same gesture means the same thing in a different workshop, in a different material, a thousand miles away. Every identification procedure taught in Lessons 14 and 15 depends on the standardisation recorded in this one sentence.

### 5. Strand three: the Greco-Roman influence, element by element

Greco-Roman influence on Gandhara art is mainly shown in **anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting**, and is clearly visible in four recorded features:

| Feature | As recorded | What it actually changes in the image |
|---|---|---|
| **Facial features and curly or wavy hair** | **Usha-Nisha**, described as a wavy Greek hairstyle | The cranial protuberance is treated as coiffure rather than as an abstract sign |
| **Slender body with visible muscles** | Hellenistic influence; the **ideal body type is like a very fit Greek athlete** | The body is built from observed anatomy rather than from a symbolic canon |
| **Heavily pleated garments** | Inspired by Greek and Roman, and **also Parthian** | Drapery acquires weight, fall and shadow - a real technical demand on hard stone |
| **Elongated earlobes** | To **symbolise the royal connection**, as in Greek, Roman and Central Asian practice | A princely attribute, rationalised through foreign court convention |

Three further recorded specifics belong here and are frequently the difference between a good answer and a generic one:

- the Buddha figure was **sometimes modelled after the Greek Sun God Apollo**, described as the epitome of grace in sculpture;
- the Buddha was depicted **barefoot**;
- **some of the images of the Buddha have moustaches**.

Note the fourth row of the table carefully. The pleated garments are attributed to Greek and Roman influence **and also Parthian**. That is the source itself refusing a clean separation, and reproducing that refusal is worth a mark, because a demand that asks you to separate strands is best answered by someone who can also say where the strands overlap.

### 6. Bodhisattva iconography, and the contrast that identifies it

Apart from the Buddha figures, **Bodhisattva images were often depicted - for example, Maitreya, followed by Avalokitesvara**. And the identifying contrast is explicit: **unlike the Buddha figures, the Bodhisattva is often heavily ornamented, with elaborate hairdos and sandals.**

```text
  THE FASTEST IDENTIFICATION IN GANDHARA SCULPTURE

     BUDDHA                      BODHISATTVA
     ------                      -----------
     monastic robe               heavily ornamented
     no jewellery                jewellery
     simple hair treatment       elaborate hairdo
     BAREFOOT                    SANDALS
        |                             |
     renunciation already         still in the world,
     complete                     still princely
```

The ornament is not decoration; it is doctrinal status made visible. A being who has not yet renounced the world wears the world's marks. That single observation converts a memorised contrast into an explanation, and it transfers directly to the identification procedures of Lesson 14.

### 7. The Indian core that was not borrowed

Every element above is a surface or a technique. What none of them touches is:

- **the subject** - a Buddhist iconographic programme recounting the Buddha's life and the Jataka;
- **the gestures** - the mudra system, whose conventions were being fixed in this very period;
- **the halo**, present in the Parthian-linked seated Buddha and standardised across the region;
- **the monastic robe** and the renunciant identity it signifies;
- **the companion figures** - Indra and Brahma standing beside the Buddha.

Hence the formulation that survives cross-examination: Gandhara is a **Buddhist iconographic programme executed in a Hellenised visual idiom under Kushana patronage**. The subject is Indian; the surface treatment is layered and partly borrowed; calling it "Greek art" inverts the relationship, and calling it purely indigenous ignores the three documented strands of influence.

### Examination use

This is the most heavily examined single school in the topic and it carries four reproduced Civil Services Mains demands in the core source's own list - a 2019 demand to highlight the central Asian and Greco-Bactrian elements, a 2014 demand on whether Gandhara sculpture owed as much to the Romans as to the Greeks, and short-form demands of 2007 and 2000 on Gandhara art and the Gandhara school. All four are reproduced with their exact wording in the verified PYQ section. The lesson is organised so that the 2019 separation demand can be answered by naming the Central Asian strand - its three peoples, Kushana, Parthian and Saka; Bactria; Kushana patronage; the material sequence; royal portraits; iconographic standardisation - and the Greco-Bactrian or Hellenistic strand - Usha-Nisha hair, athletic anatomy, pleated drapery, elongated earlobes, Apollo modelling - as distinct bodies of evidence, with the separately evidenced Parthian relief manner available as the element that defeats a two-way caricature. The 2014 Romans-and-Greeks demand is answered from the same table plus the recorded attribution of pleated garments to Greek, Roman and Parthian sources together.

### Traps introduced here

- Reducing Gandhara to "Greek art in India", or conversely denying foreign influence.
- Listing Chandragupta Maurya among the region's foreign occupations; the recorded fourth-century phase is a Greek intervention **followed by** Indian imperial integration.
- Asserting a total number of powers or a span of years for the region's political history; the source records three phases and no total.
- Supplying a Saka period, ruler, workshop or stylistic marker; the Saka are named by the canonical owner as one of the Central Asian strand's three peoples, and nothing further about them is recorded in the owned evidence.
- Naming individual Greek, Bactrian or Roman rulers, artists, workshops or dates from memory.
- Asserting that Gandhara produced the first human Buddha image; priority is handled in Lesson 12 and is disputed.
- Giving one material for the school; the recorded sequence is blue schist and green phyllite, then stucco, then complete shift to grey stone from the third century AD.
- Omitting the Parthian strand, which is where the earliest Buddha images in the region are located.
- Treating Bodhisattva ornament as generic decorative excess rather than as an indicator of unrenounced princely status.
- Attributing to Gandhara the features of the comparator school - a larger geometrically decorated halo and spotted red sandstone belong to Mathura.
- Treating the source's "thoroughly cosmopolitan" judgement as a demonstrated finding rather than as its own interpretation of the recorded sequence.

### Revision notes

1. Gandhara comprises the Peshawar Valley, modern Swat, Buner and Bajaur.
2. Three recorded phases of political control, and no more: Achaemenid Persia in the sixth century BC; Alexander and **then** Chandragupta Maurya in the fourth century BC - a Greek intervention followed by Indian imperial integration; and the Kushanas in the first century AD. No total number of powers and no span of years is asserted.
3. The source's own conclusion from that history is that the people of Gandhara were thoroughly cosmopolitan in culture and outlook - an interpretation, carried as the source's judgement.
4. Purpose: to glorify the Buddha by recounting episodes from his life and the Jataka.
5. The Buddha's representation in human form under the Gandhara school transformed the course of Indian sculptural narrative.
6. Parthian strand: Buddhist sculptures of the Swat Valley closely linked with Parthian rather than Greco-Roman art; a standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma as one of the earliest examples of the Buddha's image; a seated Buddha with halo with standing Indra and Brahma; relief carving and deeply incised facial lines in Parthian style.
7. Central Asian or Bactro-Gandhara strand: named geographically by the core source, and by people in the canonical owner as **Kushana, Parthian and Saka**; Bactria lies between today's Afghanistan and Tajikistan. No Saka-specific feature, date or monument is recorded anywhere in the owned evidence.
8. Material sequence: blue schist and green phyllite, then stucco or lime plaster, then a complete shift to grey stone from the third century AD.
9. The strand covered religious themes and royal portraits.
10. Kushana rule in the first century AD brought major upliftment to the Buddha's portrayal; Kanishka's territory ran from Mathura through Gandhara and Bactria to the borders of China.
11. Clear iconographic conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras were established at this time, for example the Dharmachakra Parivartan Mudra and the Abhaya Hasta Mudra.
12. Greco-Roman strand: anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting; wavy hair called Usha-Nisha; slender body with visible muscles on a fit-athlete model; heavily pleated garments inspired by Greek and Roman and also Parthian; elongated earlobes symbolising royal connection.
13. The Buddha was sometimes modelled after the Greek Sun God Apollo; depicted barefoot; some images have moustaches.
14. Bodhisattvas depicted include Maitreya and then Avalokitesvara; unlike the Buddha they are often heavily ornamented, with elaborate hairdos and sandals - ornament as unrenounced princely status.
15. The Indian core that was not borrowed: the subject, the mudra system, the halo, the monastic robe and the Indra-Brahma companions.
16. The school's Hellenistic inheritance is why the source also calls it Indo-Greek art; that label names a style, not a dynasty, and supplies no chronology.

### Concept check

**Question:** The 2019 demand asks specifically for the central Asian **and** Greco-Bactrian elements in Gandhara art. Sort the evidence into those two strands, and identify the single recorded detail that resists a clean sorting.

**Model answer:** The Central Asian strand is named by people in the canonical owner as Kushana, Parthian and Saka, and geographically by Bactria, the region between today's Afghanistan and Tajikistan; it is carried by patronage, material and standardisation. Kushana rule in the first century AD brought a major upliftment to the Buddha's portrayal; Kanishka's territory ran from Mathura through Gandhara and Bactria to the borders of China; the workshops produced royal portraits alongside religious themes; the material moved from blue schist and green phyllite to stucco and, from the third century AD, wholly to grey stone; and clear iconographic conventions were fixed for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras, including the Dharmachakra Parivartan and Abhaya Hasta mudras. The Saka should be named as one of the strand's three peoples and no further, since no Saka-specific feature, date or monument is recorded. The Greco-Bactrian or Hellenistic strand is defined by the treatment of the body and surface: anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting; wavy hair described as Usha-Nisha; a slender body with visible muscles on the model of a very fit Greek athlete; elongated earlobes symbolising royal connection; and the Buddha figure sometimes modelled after the Greek Sun God Apollo. The detail that resists clean sorting is the heavily pleated garments, which the source attributes to Greek and Roman inspiration **and also Parthian** - so drapery cannot be assigned exclusively to either named strand, and saying so is more accurate than forcing it into one column.

**Misconception to avoid:** Answering a two-strand question by writing "Greek influence" six times in different words. The directive names two strands because the marks lie in telling them apart, and the strongest scripts additionally name the Parthian relief manner, which is evidenced separately and which neither label fully covers.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Gandhara sculpture is better described as a layered school than as a hybrid one." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The distinction is worth making, because "hybrid" implies a single fusion event while the record shows successive deposits.

The region's political history is itself a sequence rather than a state. Gandhara, covering the Peshawar Valley, modern Swat, Buner and Bajaur, was occupied by Achaemenid Persia in the sixth century BC, by Alexander and then by Chandragupta Maurya in the fourth - a Greek intervention followed by Indian imperial integration - and by the Kushanas in the first century AD, leaving a population the source judges thoroughly cosmopolitan. No further rulers are recorded, and none should be supplied.

The sculpture records the layers separately. Parthian affinity is attached to Swat Valley works including a standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma, one of the earliest examples of the Buddha's image, with relief carving and deeply incised facial lines in Parthian manner. The Central Asian deposit - Kushana, Parthian and Saka in the owner's naming - is patronal and technical: Kushana rule brought a major upliftment to the Buddha's portrayal, Kanishka's territory reached from Mathura to the borders of China, royal portraits joined religious themes, and iconographic conventions for the Buddha, Bodhisattva and mudras were fixed. The Greco-Roman deposit is anatomical and superficial: Usha-Nisha wavy hair, an athletic muscled body, pleated drapery, elongated earlobes, and Buddha figures sometimes modelled on Apollo.

The layers are also datable against the material: blue schist and green phyllite first, then stucco, then a complete shift to grey stone from the third century AD.

Two qualifications are needed. The layers interpenetrate - the pleated garments are attributed to Greek, Roman and Parthian inspiration together - so the separation is analytical, not absolute. And beneath all layers the subject is constant: a Buddhist programme of life episodes and Jataka, with halo, monastic robe and Indian companion deities.

Layering therefore describes the evidence better, while hybridity describes only its appearance.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the layered-versus-hybrid distinction argued rather than asserted 3 + the three recorded phases stated with the Greek-then-Mauryan correction and no invented total 2 + all three strands with distinct named evidence, including the owner's three-people naming of the Central Asian strand 6 + the datable material sequence 2 + both qualifications, interpenetration and constant subject 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 10:** Gandhara's comparator lies inside the same empire and the same century, and it solved the same problem by entirely different means - without foreign influence, in a different stone, for three religions at once.

*Five columns, and the fourth is the one that transfers. The avayudha method - identify a deity by the attribute it carries - is the single most reusable technique in this entire topic, and it is Mathura's contribution to the syllabus rather than merely to art history.*

### 1. The emergence, in the source's own sequence

The Kushana-period paragraph states the sequence compactly. A new school of art evolved under the patronage of the **Kushan kings** - the **Gandhara school** - where the **Greek custom of representing gods was adopted** and physical details appeared in the image of the Buddha. **In the first century AD, another indigenous school of art flourished at Mathura**, characterised by the use of **red sandstone**, and its noteworthy examples were sculptures of **Yaksha and Yakshi in highly polished sandstone**.

Three points are compressed into that. Mathura is named as **indigenous** in the same breath in which Gandhara is named as adopting a foreign custom - the contrast is the source's, not an interpretation. Mathura's earliest noteworthy works are **Yaksha and Yakshi**, which ties the school directly to the popular cult stream of Lesson 6. And the **highly polished** finish links it to the Mauryan technical standard, which is a continuity of workshop practice across four centuries.

The fuller account adds the location and date: the Mathura School **flourished on the banks of the River Yamuna in the period between the first and third centuries AD**.

### 2. Three religions, one visual grammar

The sculptures of the Mathura School were **influenced by the stories and imageries of all three religions of the time - Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism**. The images were **modelled on the earlier yaksha images found during the Mauryan period**.

This is Mathura's structural distinction and it should be argued, not merely stated. Gandhara and Amaravati are recorded as mainly Buddhist. Mathura is the one school of the three working simultaneously for three religions - and it did so by taking an *already shared* figure type, the yaksha, as its formal starting point. A shared ancestor makes a shared grammar possible: the same body type, the same frontality, the same monumental scale can carry a Buddha, a Tirthankara or a Hindu deity, with the attribute doing the identifying work.

That is why the school's signature technique is symbolic rather than anatomical. Where Gandhara distinguishes figures by modelling the body, Mathura distinguishes them by what the figure carries.

### 3. The avayudha method, and why it is the topic's most portable tool

The Mathura school **showed a striking use of symbolism in the images**. The **Hindu gods were represented using their avayudhas** - their attribute-weapons or emblems. The example given is decisive: **Shiva is shown through the linga and the mukhalinga.**

```text
  THE AVAYUDHA METHOD AS A PROCEDURE

  STEP 1  Is there an inscription?  -> If yes, the inscription rules.
             |  no
  STEP 2  What does the figure CARRY or what STANDS FOR it?
             |
          linga / mukhalinga ................ Shiva
          lotus (held by an attendant) ...... Padmapani
          thunderbolt (held by an attendant)  Vajrapani
             |
  STEP 3  What is the GESTURE?  -> Abhaya Mudra, and the rest of the
          mudra system fixed in this period (Lesson 14)
             |
  STEP 4  What is the SURROUNDING SCHEME?  -> halo size and decoration,
          flanking figures, vehicle, seat
             |
  STEP 5  Only then, style and material -> school and approximate date
```

Two cautions belong with the procedure, and both are canonical. Attribute reading is a **supporting** method, not a substitute for an available inscription or documented provenance - an identification by attribute that contradicts an inscription is simply wrong. And the method identifies a *type*, not an individual object's patron, date or original location, none of which an attribute can supply.

The linga example carries one further piece of information worth noticing. A deity represented by an **aniconic emblem** - a linga is not a figure - sits inside a school otherwise famous for figures. So aniconic and anthropomorphic representation are not successive stages in a single evolution; they coexist within one workshop tradition, serving different deities and different purposes. That observation is a direct corrective to the tidy "symbol to image" narrative of Lesson 8.

### 4. The Buddha image at Mathura: halo, companions, gesture

Three recorded specifics, each of which identifies the school.

- **The halo.** The halo around the head of the Buddha is **larger than in the Gandhara school and decorated with geometrical patterns**. This is the single cleanest visual discriminator between the two schools' Buddha images, and it is a comparative fact, so it must be written comparatively.
- **The companions.** The Buddha is shown **surrounded by two Bodhisattvas - Padmapani holding a lotus and Vajrapani holding a thunderbolt.** Note that these are identified by attribute, which is the avayudha method applied inside a Buddhist composition.
- **The gesture.** In the Mathura School of Art, the Buddha's images are **largely carved in Abhaya Mudra**.

Set that last point beside Gandhara's mudra standardisation from Lesson 9 - the Dharmachakra Parivartan and Abhaya Hasta mudras - and a useful observation emerges: the same gesture vocabulary is in use across both schools in the same period, while the treatment of the body diverges sharply. Iconography converged while style did not, which is exactly what one would expect of two workshop traditions serving one religious system inside one empire.

### 5. The Yakshas as cult objects, and the inscribed figure that disciplines everything

**The named cult Yakshas.** Some of the earliest examples of the Mathura school of art are the **Yakshas**, which are **objects of cult**. The named ones are **Kubera, Manibhadra and Mudgarpani**. Two of those names are worth holding because they demonstrate that "yaksha" is not a vague category: Kubera is a named deity of wealth, and Mudgarpani is itself an attribute name, meaning the one bearing a club - so the avayudha principle is embedded even in the naming.

**The headless figure.** The **Headless sculpture** is an important example of early Mathura school art. It was **discovered in 1911 in a village outside Mathura by Pandit Radha Krishna**, and a **Brahmi inscription on its base declares that this is Kanishka, the great Kushana king.**

That object does three jobs at once and deserves a full sentence in any Mathura answer. It confirms **royal portraiture** in the school. It supplies a **dated dynastic anchor** through a named king. And it is the topic's clearest demonstration that **epigraphic evidence outranks stylistic inference** - a headless statue that cannot be identified by its face is identified beyond dispute by the text at its feet. Contrast that with the Harappan objects of Lesson 3, where no such text exists, and the whole label-versus-identity discipline of this session acquires a positive example rather than only cautions.

### Examination use

Mathura appears in every comparative demand on the post-Mauryan schools, and it is the school most often under-written because candidates know Gandhara's foreign elements better. The three facts that reliably earn marks are the three-religion scope, the spotted red sandstone, and the larger geometrically decorated halo stated as a comparison. The avayudha method is the lesson's transferable asset: it answers any "explain the iconography of a named image" demand and it is the procedure Lessons 14 and 15 formalise. The headless Kanishka is the best single object for a source-criticism paragraph, and the indigenous-development row is what prevents an answer from sliding into the claim that all post-Mauryan sculpture was foreign-influenced.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating Mathura as exclusively Buddhist; its recorded scope is Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism.
- Writing "red sandstone" where the comparative table specifies **spotted** red sandstone.
- Describing the halo without the comparison; the point is that it is larger than Gandhara's and geometrically decorated.
- Swapping the attributes of the two flanking Bodhisattvas; Padmapani holds the lotus and Vajrapani the thunderbolt.
- Presenting the avayudha method as overriding an available inscription.
- Dating the school loosely; the recorded floruit is the first to third centuries AD, on the banks of the Yamuna.
- Forgetting that the headless figure's identification rests on a Brahmi inscription on its base, not on style.

### Revision notes

1. Under the Kushan kings a new school - Gandhara - adopted the Greek custom of representing gods; in the first century AD another indigenous school flourished at Mathura, characterised by red sandstone.
2. Mathura's noteworthy early examples are sculptures of Yaksha and Yakshi in highly polished sandstone.
3. The school flourished on the banks of the River Yamuna between the first and third centuries AD.
4. Its sculptures were influenced by the stories and imageries of all three religions of the time - Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism.
5. Its images were modelled on the earlier yaksha images of the Mauryan period.
6. Material in the comparison table: spotted red sandstone; external influence: developed indigenously, not influenced by external cultures.
7. Striking use of symbolism: Hindu gods represented by their avayudhas, with Shiva shown through the linga and the mukhalinga.
8. The halo around the Buddha's head is larger than in Gandhara and decorated with geometrical patterns.
9. The Buddha is surrounded by two Bodhisattvas - Padmapani holding a lotus, Vajrapani holding a thunderbolt.
10. Buddha images at Mathura are largely carved in Abhaya Mudra.
11. Earliest examples include the Yakshas as objects of cult: Kubera, Manibhadra and Mudgarpani.
12. The Headless sculpture, discovered in 1911 in a village outside Mathura by Pandit Radha Krishna, carries a Brahmi inscription on its base declaring it to be Kanishka, the great Kushana king.
13. The avayudha procedure: inscription first, then attribute, then gesture, then surrounding scheme, then style and material.
14. Aniconic and anthropomorphic representation coexist within one workshop tradition - the linga alongside figural images.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Mathura school's use of avayudhas is a more consequential contribution to Indian art than its choice of red sandstone, and state one limit of attribute-based identification.

**Model answer:** The stone identifies a workshop; the method identifies a deity anywhere. Spotted red sandstone is a locational and technical marker that lets a scholar assign an unlabelled figure to Mathura, which is valuable but bounded to that region and period. The avayudha principle - representing Hindu gods by their attribute-emblems, with Shiva shown through the linga and the mukhalinga - is a general rule for making images legible, and the same school applies it inside Buddhist compositions too, distinguishing Padmapani by his lotus from Vajrapani by his thunderbolt. Because a school working for Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism simultaneously needed a way to tell figures apart without changing the underlying body type inherited from the Mauryan yaksha images, attribute-coding was the necessary solution, and once established it is reusable across materials, centuries and regions - which is why it remains the operative identification method for medieval temple sculpture and for bronze icons. The limit is that an attribute identifies a type, not an object: it cannot supply the patron, the exact date or the original location, and where an inscription exists it must yield to the inscription, as the headless Mathura figure shows, since a Brahmi text on the base identifies it as Kanishka when no attribute could.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating iconographic attributes as decorative variation between schools. They are a functional code, designed to be read; a school that serves three religions with one body type cannot work without one.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The Mathura school's importance lies less in its style than in its solution to a problem of legibility." Comment. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is persuasive, because the school's recorded circumstances created exactly that problem.

Mathura flourished on the Yamuna between the first and third centuries AD, drawing on the stories and imageries of Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism together, and modelling its figures on the earlier Mauryan yaksha images. A single inherited body type serving three religions cannot distinguish its subjects by anatomy, so identification had to be carried by signs. Hence the school's striking use of symbolism: Hindu gods shown through their avayudhas, with Shiva represented by the linga and mukhalinga; the Buddha flanked by Padmapani with a lotus and Vajrapani with a thunderbolt; the Buddha's images largely in Abhaya Mudra; and a halo larger than Gandhara's and decorated with geometrical patterns.

Style is not irrelevant - spotted red sandstone and indigenous development define the school materially.

But the legible attribute system outlived the stone: it is the method by which medieval temple images and bronze icons are still identified.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the legibility-problem thesis 2 + the three-religion scope and yaksha descent as the cause of the problem 3 + three named attribute or gesture solutions 3 + the material and indigenous-development qualification 1 + the afterlife-of-the-method verdict 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 11:** The third school lies far to the south, on a different river, under a different dynasty, in a different stone - and it chose to solve a different problem altogether: not how to identify a figure, but how to tell a whole story on a curved surface.

## Lesson 11 - Amaravati: narrative art, tribhanga, and a school that travelled

### Visual: the school defined by the problem it chose

```text
  THE OTHER TWO SCHOOLS              AMARAVATI
  "focused on single images"         "more emphasis on the use of
        |                             DYNAMIC IMAGES or NARRATIVE ART"
        |                                       |
  ONE FIGURE, ONE MOMENT             A WHOLE EPISODE ON ONE SURFACE
        |                                       |
  identification problem             STORYTELLING problem
  -> attributes, gestures            -> composition, movement,
     (Lesson 10)                        figure grouping, a body that
                                        BENDS
                                              |
                                    THE FORMAL SOLUTION:
                                    TRIBHANGA - the body with
                                    THREE BENDS - used EXCESSIVELY
                                              |
                                    THE MATERIAL THAT ALLOWS IT:
                                    GREENISH-WHITE LIMESTONE
                                    railings, sculpted panels,
                                    gateways, MEDALLIONS
                                              |
                                    THE PROOF CASE:
                                    one medallion displays an ENTIRE
                                    STORY - Gautama Buddha taming an
                                    elephant
                                              |
                                    HENCE THE NAME the art acquired:
                                    "NARRATIVE ART"
```

*The diagram states the lesson's thesis: Amaravati is not a stylistic variant of the other two schools but a school addressing a different formal problem, and its posture, its material and its formats all follow from that choice.*

### 1. Place, patrons and names

The Amaravati School developed **in the southern parts of India, on the banks of the Krishna river, under the patronage of the Satavahana rulers**. It is **also known as the Vengi School**.

The wider site account supplies the frame. The site flourished as a **major trade centre between the third century BC and the twelfth century AD**, with major contributions by the **Satavahana dynasty, which ruled from the second century BC to the third century AD**. The Satavahanas were **primarily followers of Brahmanism but were also great patrons of Buddhism as well as Jainism**.

That religious detail is worth an explicit sentence in an answer, because it repeats the Mathura lesson in a different register: a dynasty whose own affiliation was Brahmanical financed a major Buddhist sculptural programme. The correct inference is about **patronage practice**, not about personal belief - rulers funded what conferred merit, legitimacy and public benefit, and the sculpture records the funding rather than the faith.

Patronage then passes on. The **Ikshvaku dynasty, which came later, also patronised the Amaravati School of Art and Sculpture during their rule in the third and fourth centuries AD**, and the **sculptures found at Nagarjunakonda were largely patronised by Ikshvaku dynasty rulers**. So the school outlives its founding dynasty by roughly a century - a fact that distinguishes a *school* from a *court style*.

### 2. Indigenous development, stated as a contrast

The source is unambiguous: **unlike the Gandhara School, the Amaravati School of Art was developed on purely indigenous lines with no foreign influence.**

Use that sentence carefully. It is a strong claim and it is the source's own, so it can be reproduced with attribution. What must not be done is to transfer it - to argue that because Amaravati was indigenous, Indian art in general owes nothing to foreign contact, or conversely to attribute to Amaravati any of the Hellenistic features that belong to Gandhara. The three-school comparison of Lesson 12 exists precisely so that each school's external-influence row stays where it belongs.

### 3. Narrative art: what it is, and why it needs a different body

While the other two schools **focused on single images**, the Amaravati School put **more emphasis on the use of dynamic images or narrative art**, and its sculptures made **excessive use of the Tribhanga posture, that is the body with three bends**.

The connection between those two statements is the lesson's core teaching and it is rarely made explicit.

- A **single image** is addressed frontally by a worshipper. Frontality, symmetry and stillness serve it.
- A **narrative relief** is read across a surface. Figures must appear to move, turn, react and relate to one another, and a rigidly frontal body cannot do any of that.
- **Tribhanga** - a body bent at three points - introduces a curve through the figure, which reads instantly as movement and allows a figure to turn towards another.

So the posture is not a mannerism. It is the technical requirement of the school's chosen subject matter. And it is the same formal solution recorded two thousand years earlier in the Harappan Dancing Girl, which stands in a tribhanga dancing posture - a recurrence, as Lesson 3 insisted, and not a lineage.

**The proof case.** The great stupa was adorned with **greenish-white limestone narrative reliefs depicting Jataka stories and scenes of the Buddha's life**, and **one of the medallions there displays an entire story about Gautama Buddha taming an elephant**. An entire story in one medallion is the definition of narrative art in a single object, and it is the example to name.

**The formats.** The programme is carried on **ornate limestone railings, sculpted panels and gateways or toranas, depicting Jataka tales, scenes from the Buddha's life and various Buddhist symbols**. Note the last item: Buddhist symbols appear alongside life scenes, so the aniconic code of Lesson 8 and narrative figural relief are present in the same programme.

### 4. The iconic type, and the school that travelled

One specific image type is recorded as having spread: a particular depiction of the Buddha from Amaravati, **with the robe on the left shoulder and the other hand in Abhayamudra - a gesture indicating fearlessness - became so iconic that it was replicated in other parts of South and Southeast Asia.**

And the school's influence is recorded twice over: the Amaravati school of sculpture **had a great influence on the art form of the successive South Indian dynasties**, and the style is seen at the stupa remains of **Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala and Goli in the Krishna River valley region of Andhra Pradesh, and as far west as Ter in Maharashtra**.

```text
  THE DIFFUSION RECORD, IN TWO DIRECTIONS

  WITHIN INDIA                        BEYOND INDIA
  Bhattiprolu                         "replicated in other parts of
  Jaggayyapeta                         South and Southeast Asia"
  Nagarjunakonda                       - carried by the robe-and-
  Ghantasala                             Abhayamudra Buddha type
  Goli                                       |
  (all Krishna valley, AP)            corroborated by the site's
  Ter (Maharashtra - "as far          scholarly reputation: Hiuen
  west as")                           Tsang visited in 640 AD and
       |                              studied the Abhidhamma Pitaka
  plus "great influence on the        there; the Sri Lankan monk
  art form of the successive          Dharmakeerti, called the jewel
  South Indian dynasties"             of Nalanda Mahavihara, referred
                                      to Amaravati as a prominent
                                      ancient centre of Buddhist
                                      learning
```

The two columns together make the strongest available claim for this school: it is the only one of the three whose *sculptural type* is recorded as having been replicated across South and Southeast Asia, and the only one whose site is independently attested as an international centre of learning. When a question asks which Indian art form had the widest external reach, this is the evidenced answer.

### 5. Provenance: what happened to the sculptures

This is where the school's history becomes a case study in attribution and removal, and it is carried here in bounded form because Lesson 20 returns to it.

The Mahachaitya **fell into ruins and completely lost its glory**. In **1797**, **Colonel Colin Mackenzie** visited the place, and this led to the **rediscovery** of the stupa. Its **first excavation was done by Sir Walter Elliot in 1845**, and he **collected what is known as the Amaravati Marbles, which later became famous as the Elliot marbles, for sculptures kept in the British Museum**. The archaeologist **James Burgess** visited and noted findings about the stupa in **1887**. The **Archaeological Survey of India has conserved the site**, and a **replica of the stupa has been created near the original location**.

Four disciplines apply. The stupa as a monument - its 27-metre recorded height, its vedika, its four toranas, its ayaka pillars - belongs to the architecture topic, which owns the structure; this session uses those elements only where the sculpture sits on them. The removal history is recorded as fact without any claim about present legal status, ownership or restitution, which belongs to the heritage topic. The word "believed" in the recorded height must be retained if the figure is cited at all. And the replica is recorded as existing near the original location, with no claim about its current management.

### Examination use

Amaravati is the school most likely to appear in an objective item, because it offers several crisp pairings - river, dynasty, stone, posture, alternative name - and because a 2026 Preliminary item on the Amaravati stupa and its relief sculpture combines a locational claim, a comparative scale claim and a diffusion claim. That item is owned by the monuments topic and is reproduced answer-neutrally in the verified PYQ section of this session, with the note that the scale claim is the weakly evidenced element here. For Mains, Amaravati supplies the narrative-art argument, the tribhanga explanation, the purely-indigenous contrast for any Gandhara comparison, and the best diffusion evidence in the topic.

### Traps introduced here

- Attributing foreign influence to Amaravati; the recorded position is purely indigenous development with no foreign influence.
- Assigning the school only to the Satavahanas; the Ikshvakus patronised it in the third and fourth centuries AD, and Nagarjunakonda's sculptures are largely Ikshvaku-patronised.
- Writing "limestone" without the recorded colour qualification, greenish-white.
- Treating tribhanga as a decorative preference rather than as the posture that makes narrative relief work.
- Claiming a comparative size ranking for the stupa against another named stupa; the only scale statement available here is the hedged "believed to be originally 27 metres tall".
- Confusing the school with the site: the stupa, its vedika, its toranas and its ayaka pillars are owned by the architecture topic.
- Making a legal, ownership or restitution claim from the recorded removal history.

### Revision notes

1. The Amaravati School developed in southern India on the banks of the Krishna river under Satavahana patronage; it is also known as the Vengi School.
2. The site flourished as a major trade centre from the third century BC to the twelfth century AD; the Satavahanas ruled from the second century BC to the third century AD.
3. The Satavahanas were primarily followers of Brahmanism but were great patrons of Buddhism as well as Jainism - patronage practice, not personal belief.
4. The Ikshvakus also patronised the school in the third and fourth centuries AD; Nagarjunakonda's sculptures were largely Ikshvaku-patronised.
5. Unlike Gandhara, the school developed on purely indigenous lines with no foreign influence.
6. The other two schools focused on single images; Amaravati emphasised dynamic images or narrative art.
7. Tribhanga - the body with three bends - was used excessively, because narrative relief requires figures that turn and move.
8. The reliefs are greenish-white limestone, depicting Jataka stories and scenes of the Buddha's life; one medallion displays an entire story of Gautama Buddha taming an elephant.
9. The programme is carried on ornate limestone railings, sculpted panels and gateways or toranas, with Buddhist symbols alongside life scenes.
10. The iconic type - robe on the left shoulder, the other hand in Abhayamudra indicating fearlessness - was replicated in other parts of South and Southeast Asia.
11. The school greatly influenced the art of successive South Indian dynasties; the style is seen at Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala and Goli in the Krishna valley, and as far west as Ter in Maharashtra.
12. Hiuen Tsang visited in 640 AD and studied the Abhidhamma Pitaka there; Dharmakeerti, the Sri Lankan monk called the jewel of Nalanda Mahavihara, referred to Amaravati as a prominent ancient centre of Buddhist learning.
13. Provenance: ruin, then Colin Mackenzie's visit in 1797 leading to rediscovery; first excavation by Sir Walter Elliot in 1845, who collected the Amaravati Marbles, later famous as the Elliot marbles, for sculptures kept in the British Museum; James Burgess noted findings in 1887; the Archaeological Survey of India has conserved the site and a replica has been created near the original location.
14. The Mahachaitya's recorded height is "believed to be originally 27 metres"; keep the hedge, and route the monument's structure to the architecture topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why tribhanga is a functional requirement of Amaravati's programme rather than a stylistic preference, and state what the posture's much earlier appearance does and does not prove.

**Model answer:** Amaravati's defining choice was narrative: while the other two schools focused on single images, this school emphasised dynamic images or narrative art, and its greenish-white limestone railings, panels, gateways and medallions carry whole episodes, one medallion displaying the entire story of the Buddha taming an elephant. A narrative relief is read across a surface, so its figures must turn towards one another, react and appear to move; a strictly frontal, symmetrical body cannot do this. Tribhanga, the body with three bends, introduces a curve that reads as movement and allows a figure to address another figure within the composition - which is why the school used it excessively. The posture also appears roughly two millennia earlier in the Harappan Dancing Girl, who stands in a tribhanga dancing posture with her right hand on her hip. That proves the formal solution was arrived at very early and used again later; it does not prove a continuous tradition, a transmitted workshop practice or a shared meaning, and asserting a lineage from the Dancing Girl to Amaravati would be an invention.

**Misconception to avoid:** Describing tribhanga as "graceful" and leaving it there. The examiner's mark lies in the connection between the posture and the school's narrative purpose; an adjective supplies neither the connection nor any evidence.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Amaravati, not Gandhara, is the Indian sculptural school with the widest and best-documented external reach." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is well supported, provided reach is measured by documented replication rather than by stylistic derivation.

Amaravati's outward record is explicit. A particular depiction of the Buddha from the site - robe on the left shoulder, the other hand in Abhayamudra indicating fearlessness - became so iconic that it was replicated in other parts of South and Southeast Asia. Within India, the school is recorded as having had a great influence on the art of the successive South Indian dynasties, and its style is attested at Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala and Goli in the Krishna valley and as far west as Ter in Maharashtra. Its intellectual standing corroborates the artistic one: Hiuen Tsang visited in 640 AD and studied the Abhidhamma Pitaka there, and the Sri Lankan monk Dharmakeerti referred to Amaravati as a prominent ancient centre of Buddhist learning.

Gandhara's documented external relationship is largely the reverse: it is the school defined by what it received - Parthian relief manner, Bactrian and Kushana patronage and material, Greco-Roman anatomy, drapery and hairstyle.

Two qualifications are necessary. Reach of a different kind is not being denied to Gandhara, whose iconographic conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras were fixed under Kushana rule and became normative. And Amaravati's own diffusion is stated in general terms - replicated in South and Southeast Asia - without named foreign monuments in this evidence base, so the claim should be made at the level at which it is recorded.

Within those limits, Amaravati is the exporting school and Gandhara the importing one.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the reach-as-replication definition stated before arguing 3 + the iconic robe-and-Abhayamudra type and its recorded diffusion 3 + the named Indian diffusion sites 3 + the two textual corroborations 2 + the Gandhara counter-position 2 + both qualifications 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 12:** Three schools are now on the table with their own evidence. They have to be compared properly - along named axes rather than as three descriptive blocks - and the comparison runs straight into the one question the sources refuse to settle.

| Axis | **Gandhara** | **Mathura** | **Amaravati (Vengi)** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **External influence** | Heavy influence of **Greek or Hellenistic sculpture**, so it is also known as **Indo-Greek art**; layered with a separately evidenced **Parthian** relief manner and with the **Central Asian or Bactro-Gandhara** strand, which the canonical owner names by people as **Kushana, Parthian and Saka** | **Developed indigenously and not influenced by external cultures** | **Purely indigenous lines with no foreign influence** |
| **Material** | Early: **bluish-grey sandstone**, with **blue schist and green phyllite**; later: **mud and stucco**; from the **third century AD**, wholly **grey stone** | **Spotted red sandstone** | **Greenish-white limestone** |
| **Religious scope** | **Mainly Buddhist** | **Influence of all three religions** - Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism | **Mainly Buddhist** |
| **Treatment and format** | Anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting; **Usha-Nisha** wavy hair; muscled athletic body; **heavily pleated garments**; **elongated earlobes**; barefoot; some images with **moustaches**; Buddha sometimes modelled on **Apollo**; Bodhisattvas heavily ornamented with elaborate hairdos and **sandals** | **Symbolism**: Hindu gods by their **avayudhas**, Shiva by **linga and mukhalinga**; halo **larger than Gandhara's** and **decorated with geometrical patterns**; Buddha flanked by **Padmapani** with a lotus and **Vajrapani** with a thunderbolt; Buddha largely in **Abhaya Mudra** | **Narrative art** - dynamic images, whole episodes in **medallions**, one displaying the entire story of the **Buddha taming an elephant**; **tribhanga** used excessively; railings, panels and toranas |
| **Patronage** | **Kushana**; Kanishka's territory from Mathura through Gandhara and Bactria to the borders of China | **Kushana**; the **headless figure** inscribed in Brahmi as **Kanishka** | **Satavahana**, then also **Ikshvaku** in the third-fourth centuries AD |
| **Diffusion** | Iconographic conventions for Buddha, Bodhisattva and their mudras fixed here and made normative | Pan-north Indian; the inherited **yaksha** body type carried into three religions | The **robe-on-left-shoulder, Abhayamudra** type **replicated in South and Southeast Asia**; great influence on successive South Indian dynasties; style at Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala, Goli and as far west as Ter |

*The table is built to be written down the columns in an examination. Notice that the third row is the one that breaks the symmetry: two schools are mainly Buddhist and one is not, and that asymmetry is what makes Mathura's attribute method necessary.*

A disclosure about the printed table. In the text available for this session the source's own three-school comparison table is **clipped after the Mathura column**: the external-influence, ingredient-used and religious rows are legible for Gandhara and Mathura, and the Amaravati column and the remaining rows are cut off. The Amaravati values above are therefore taken from the school's own descriptive sections - greenish-white limestone, purely indigenous development, Satavahana and Ikshvaku patronage, narrative art and tribhanga - and not reconstructed from the clipped table. That distinction is recorded here because the honest handling of a damaged source is part of the method this session teaches.

### 1. Comparing along axes, not in blocks

The commonest failure in a comparison answer is three descriptive paragraphs with no axis. The fix is mechanical: choose the axes first, then fill them. The six above are sufficient for any demand on these schools, and four of them - influence, material, scope, treatment - are enough for a ten-mark answer.

The second failure is comparing on quality. "Gandhara was more realistic, Mathura more spiritual" is an aesthetic preference dressed as analysis, and it is not supported by anything in the evidence. Compare on **problem solved**, and the answer becomes arguable.

### 2. The three problems, stated as an analytical reading

This is an inference drawn from the material rather than a sentence in the source, and it should be labelled as an analytical reading when used - but it is the most useful single framing available for these schools.

```text
  EACH SCHOOL SOLVED A DIFFERENT PROBLEM

  GANDHARA   -> How do you give the Buddha a BODY?
                answer: import an available human canon -
                athletic anatomy, real drapery, modelled hair -
                and attach the Buddhist programme to it
                     |
                     evidence: anatomical accuracy, Usha-Nisha,
                     pleated garments, Apollo modelling,
                     and the recorded transformation of Indian
                     sculptural narrative

  MATHURA    -> How do you give MANY DEITIES OF THREE RELIGIONS
                one shared visual grammar?
                answer: keep one inherited body type - the yaksha -
                and distinguish figures by ATTRIBUTE
                     |
                     evidence: three-religion scope, descent from
                     Mauryan yaksha images, avayudhas, linga and
                     mukhalinga, Padmapani and Vajrapani

  AMARAVATI  -> How do you tell a WHOLE STORY on a curved
                stone surface?
                answer: dynamic narrative relief with bodies that
                bend - tribhanga - in a stone fine enough to hold
                continuous carving
                     |
                     evidence: narrative art, the elephant-taming
                     medallion, greenish-white limestone railings
                     and panels
```

### 3. The priority question, and why it must not be settled

The demand "which school made the first human image of the Buddha" is one of the most frequently asked and most frequently mis-answered in this topic. Here is exactly what the owned evidence says.

| Statement | What it establishes | What it does not establish |
|---|---|---|
| Under the Kushan kings a new school evolved - the Gandhara school - where the Greek custom of representing gods was adopted, and physical details appeared in the image of the Buddha | Gandhara produced anthropomorphic Buddha images under Kushana patronage | That it produced them before anyone else |
| **In the first century AD**, another indigenous school of art flourished at **Mathura** | Mathura is active in the same century, inside the same empire | Any ordering between the two |
| A standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma in the Swat Valley is **one of the earliest examples of the Buddha's image**, and is linked to **Parthian** rather than Greco-Roman art | Some of the earliest images are Parthian-affiliated | That these are *the* earliest, or that the Greek strand produced the first image |
| The Buddha's representation in human form under the Gandhara school **transformed the course of the sculptural narrative of India** | The historical consequence of Gandhara's human image was transformative | Priority; "transformed the course" is an impact claim, not a first-in-time claim |
| The canonical owner records that **Gandhara and Mathura both participated, around the early centuries CE, in the shift toward anthropomorphic Buddha images**, and that **priority between them is debated** | That the debate exists and is live in the scholarship | A resolution |

The exam-safe position, which is also the accurate one:

> **Anthropomorphic Buddha imagery emerged in broadly overlapping early-centuries-CE contexts at both Gandhara and Mathura, under Kushana patronage; priority between the two is debated, and parallel development rather than a single origin is the defensible position. What is not in doubt is the consequence: the appearance of the human image transformed the course of Indian sculptural narrative.**

Learn that as a paragraph. It concedes nothing, invents nothing, and converts a trap into a demonstration of source awareness.

### 4. What the aniconic-to-iconic shift actually marks

Lesson 8 established that the earlier phase indicated rather than depicted the Buddha. This lesson can now state what the change means, with the priority question bracketed.

- It is **not** a change in technical capacity. The aniconic monuments carry dense, accomplished figural sculpture - Jataka narratives, attendant figures, Shalabhanjika brackets, Gajalakshmi reliefs. Sculptors who could carve those could have carved a Buddha.
- It **is** a change in devotional practice: the worshipper is now offered a figure to face rather than a space to infer.
- It coincides with **imperial patronage** at a scale that standardises: under Kushana rule the conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras were fixed, which is what allows an image made in one workshop to be read in another.
- It does **not** abolish the symbolic register. The linga stands for Shiva in the same Mathura workshops that carve figures, and Buddhist symbols appear alongside life scenes in the Amaravati programme. Aniconic and iconic representation coexist.

### Examination use

Comparison demands on these three schools are a standing family, and the reproduced Mains list in the core source contains four Gandhara-related demands across three decades - 2019, 2014, 2007 and 2000 - all reproduced exactly in the verified PYQ section. The six-axis table is the spine for every one of them, because even a single-school demand is strengthened by one comparative clause: naming Mathura's indigenous development, spotted red sandstone and three-religion scope proves that Gandhara's features are not simply what Indian sculpture looked like at the time. The priority paragraph is the highest-value single memorised item in this lesson, because it converts the topic's commonest trap into a scoring opportunity.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing three descriptive blocks instead of comparing along named axes.
- Comparing the schools on quality or spirituality rather than on evidence.
- Asserting that Gandhara made the first human Buddha image, or that Mathura did.
- Reading "transformed the course of the sculptural narrative of India" as a statement of priority.
- Assigning Hellenistic features to Amaravati, or indigenous-development status to Gandhara.
- Reconstructing the clipped Amaravati column of the printed comparison table from memory.
- Presenting the aniconic-to-iconic shift as a linear evolution that ends symbolic representation.

### Revision notes

1. Six comparison axes: external influence, material, religious scope, treatment and format, patronage, diffusion.
2. Gandhara: heavy Greek or Hellenistic influence, also known as Indo-Greek art, layered with a separately evidenced Parthian relief manner and with the Central Asian strand, named by the owner as Kushana, Parthian and Saka; bluish-grey sandstone with blue schist and green phyllite, then mud and stucco, then wholly grey stone from the third century AD; mainly Buddhist.
3. Mathura: developed indigenously and not influenced by external cultures; spotted red sandstone; influence of all three religions.
4. Amaravati: purely indigenous with no foreign influence; greenish-white limestone; mainly Buddhist; narrative art with tribhanga.
5. The printed comparison table available here is clipped after the Mathura column; Amaravati's values come from its own descriptive sections.
6. Analytical reading of the three problems: Gandhara gives the Buddha a body; Mathura gives many deities a shared grammar distinguished by attribute; Amaravati tells a whole story on a curved surface.
7. Priority evidence: Gandhara evolved under Kushan kings adopting the Greek custom of representing gods; Mathura flourished in the first century AD; the Swat Valley standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma is one of the earliest examples and is Parthian-linked; the canonical owner records the priority debate.
8. The exam-safe formulation: broadly overlapping early-centuries-CE emergence at both schools under Kushana patronage; parallel development; priority disputed; the consequence - transformation of Indian sculptural narrative - is not disputed.
9. The aniconic-to-iconic shift is not a change in technical capacity; sculptors of the symbolic phase carved dense figural narrative.
10. It is a change in devotional practice, coinciding with imperial patronage that standardised iconographic conventions and mudras.
11. Symbolic representation does not end: the linga stands for Shiva in a figural workshop, and Buddhist symbols accompany life scenes at Amaravati.
12. A single-school answer is improved by one comparative clause, which proves the named features are school-specific rather than period-general.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks you to trace the shift from symbolic to human representation of the Buddha. Write the opening and closing moves of a safe answer, and explain what makes each one safe.

**Model answer:** The opening should establish the symbolic system as a positive practice, not a deficiency: the earlier Buddhist monuments did not depict the Buddha in human form but represented him through his footprint, his throne, his promenade or stupa, by tree and by wheel, with the summary code assigning empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel and stupa to renunciation, enlightenment, the first sermon and death; the empty seat is a presence-marker around which the composition is built. That is safe because every element is recorded and because it forestalls the false claim that early Buddhism prohibited images, which the abundant Jataka, Shalabhanjika and Gajalakshmi imagery of the same monuments refutes. The closing should state the consequence while bracketing the priority: anthropomorphic Buddha imagery appears in broadly overlapping early-centuries-CE contexts at both Gandhara and Mathura under Kushana patronage, priority between them is debated and parallel development is the defensible position, and what is not in doubt is that the Buddha's representation in human form under the Gandhara school transformed the course of Indian sculptural narrative. That is safe because it distinguishes an impact claim from a first-in-time claim, and because it names the debate rather than resolving it in either direction.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming a question that says "trace the shift" is asking who was first. It is asking for the mechanism and the consequence; supplying an unprovable priority claim adds risk without adding marks.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The three post-Mauryan schools are distinguished by the problems they set themselves, not by their relative merit." Elucidate. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Merit-ranking fails because the schools were not attempting the same task; problem-framing succeeds because the evidence supports it.

Gandhara's problem was how to give the Buddha a human body at all. Its answer was to import an available canon: anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting, wavy hair called Usha-Nisha, a slender muscled body on the model of a fit Greek athlete, heavily pleated garments, elongated earlobes, and Buddha figures sometimes modelled on Apollo. The result is recorded as having transformed the course of Indian sculptural narrative.

Mathura's problem was legibility across three religions. Flourishing on the Yamuna between the first and third centuries AD, and drawing on the stories and imageries of Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism, it retained the inherited yaksha body type and distinguished figures by attribute - Hindu gods by their avayudhas, Shiva by linga and mukhalinga, the Buddha flanked by Padmapani with a lotus and Vajrapani with a thunderbolt, with a halo larger than Gandhara's and geometrically decorated.

Amaravati's problem was narration. Where the other schools focused on single images, it emphasised dynamic images and narrative art, using tribhanga extensively and carving greenish-white limestone medallions, one of which displays an entire episode of the Buddha taming an elephant.

Two qualifications hold the argument steady. The problems overlap - all three were producing devotional images - so the framing is analytical rather than exclusive. And the schools were contemporaneous and connected, with Kanishka's territory running from Mathura through Gandhara, so difference is not isolation.

Judged by problem, each school succeeded; judged by merit, none can be ranked without importing a preference the evidence does not supply.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the problem-versus-merit framing argued 3 + one clearly stated problem and three pieces of named evidence for each of the three schools 9 + both qualifications, overlapping problems and political connection 2 + a verdict that rejects ranking on stated grounds 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 13:** The next phase reverses Gandhara's instinct. Where Gandhara added - muscles, folds, ornament, foreign grace - the following school subtracted, and produced an idiom whose restraint became the standard by which later Indian sculpture was judged.

### Visual: subtraction as a style, and the two dating devices it carries
