*Two of the boxes are dating devices rather than aesthetic facts. The intricately decorated halo separates Gupta from Mathura; the ring-stirrup separates post-Gupta from anything earlier. A candidate who can date a sculpture from an object hanging off a horse is demonstrating exactly the analytical skill this topic rewards.*

### 1. The classical-era claim, and the tension inside it

The chapter opening states that the **Gupta Empire, 319 AD to 550 AD, remains a "classical" era for Indian sculpture for rock-cut caves, including Ellora**.

Two things must be said about that sentence, and saying both is the correct handling.

First, the **"classical" designation** is offered in quotation marks in the source itself, which is a signal that it is an evaluative label rather than a technical one. Used in an answer it should carry its sense: a period treated as a standard of balance and restraint against which earlier and later work is measured.

Second, there is a **chronological tension within the same evidence base** and it should be disclosed rather than smoothed. The chapter opening associates the Gupta classical era with rock-cut caves including Ellora; the same source's account of Ellora's murals dates them **mostly to the sixth to ninth centuries AD, during the time of the Rashtrakutas and other dynasties**, and records that the Ellora paintings are newer than those of Ajanta. Those statements are hard to hold together if "Gupta" is read as a strict date bracket. The disciplined position is to treat the "classical era" claim as a judgement about an idiom and its influence, not as an attribution of the Ellora excavations to Gupta patronage - and to attribute Ellora itself, and its dynastic history, to the architecture topic that owns the caves.

### 2. The Sarnath school: what restraint consisted of

During the Gupta period a **new school of sculpture developed around Sarnath**, characterised by:

- the use of **cream-coloured sandstone** and the **use of metal**;
- sculptures that were **immaculately dressed and lacked any form of nakedness**;
- a halo around the head of the Buddha that was **intricately decorated**.

Each of those is a deliberate reversal or refinement of what preceded it, and the comparison is what makes the description analytical:

| Feature | The Gupta position | What it is reacting to or refining |
|---|---|---|
| Stone | Cream-coloured sandstone - pale, even, low-contrast | Mathura's **spotted** red sandstone, with its visible mottling, and Gandhara's grey |
| Drapery and dress | Immaculately dressed, no nakedness; the drapery is thin and close to the body | Gandhara's **heavily pleated garments** with deep folds and strong shadow |
| Surface | Quiet, subdued, subtractive | Gandhara's muscled, anatomically emphatic surface and the Shunga period's ornamental richness |
| Halo | **Intricately decorated** | Mathura's halo, which is larger than Gandhara's and decorated with **geometrical** patterns - geometric against intricate is the discriminator |
| Metal | Explicitly named alongside stone as a Gupta school material | Bronze and copper are now a school medium, not only a folk or portable technique - the bridge to Lesson 17 |

### 3. The Sultanganj Buddha, and how to write a contested date

The named example is the **Sultanganj Buddha, 7.5 ft high, in Bihar, a copper sculpture.**

The canonical owner attaches a caution that must travel with the object: a work of about the **seventh century** is **safer described as late or post-Gupta than as an uncontested Gupta-period work**. So the sentence to write is: "the copper Sultanganj Buddha of Bihar, 7.5 ft high, is the standard named example of the school's metal work, though a c. seventh-century date places it at the late or post-Gupta end rather than securely within the Gupta period."

Notice the structure, because it recurs: name the object, give its recorded specifics, then state the dating qualification in a subordinate clause. That is three marks' worth of precision in one sentence, and it never asserts something an examiner can falsify.

### 4. Gupta terracotta: the medium acquires a full religious programme

The terracotta figures of the Gupta period can be classified under **two heads**:

1. **gods and goddesses**;
2. **male and female figures**.

The named finds are:

| Site | State or region | What was found |
|---|---|---|
| **Devnimori stupa** | Gujarat | Many **Buddha figures and ornaments** |
| **Bikaner and Ahichchhatra** | - | A **large collection of terracotta plaques** |
| **Akhnur** | Kashmir | **Distinctive styles of terracotta heads** |
| **Bhita** | - | **Numerous Gupta terracotta objects** |

This is the phase in which terracotta carries a **full religious programme** rather than only domestic or folk imagery, and the class division records it exactly: one class is divine, the other human. Set that beside the Harappan terracottas of Lesson 2 - pinched, fewer in number, crude in shape and form, comprising a goddess figure, a horned-deity mask and toys - and the trajectory of the medium is visible: from household modelling to plaque production for religious sites.

Note also that **plaques** dominate the record. A plaque is moulded, flat-backed and repeatable, which means standardised religious imagery in a cheap medium, distributed widely. Terracotta is therefore the *democratic* medium of Indian religious art, and this is the phase that proves it.

### 5. The ring-stirrup: a technological marker that dates sculpture

A **ring-stirrup** is a **circular or oval loop attached to the saddle of a horse, used to support the rider's feet**. In sculpture it is **represented as a circular form hanging from the horse's saddle**, showing attention to realistic riding equipment.

The chronology is the point: ring-stirrups **appeared in sculptures from the post-Gupta period onwards**, particularly in **early medieval North Indian and South Indian temples**. They therefore **help to date equestrian sculptures and to understand technological influences**, and **stirrups were introduced in India via Central Asian contacts**.

Important examples of their use:

- **stone reliefs of Hoysala temples** at **Belur and Halebidu**;
- **stone panels of Khajuraho temples**;
- **Chalukyan temples** in **Badami, Aihole and Pattadakal**;
- **horses pulling chariots and warriors depicted on reliefs with stirrups** in the **Sun Temple, Konark**.

```text
  WHY THIS IS THE BEST SMALL OBJECT IN THE TOPIC

  a loop of metal on a saddle
          |
  is a piece of TECHNOLOGY transmitted via Central Asian contacts
          |
  which a sculptor OBSERVED and REPRODUCED because he was
  attending to realistic riding equipment
          |
  so its PRESENCE in a relief is a TERMINUS: the relief cannot
  be earlier than the technology
          |
  => an equestrian panel with a ring-stirrup is POST-GUPTA or later
          |
  and the same detail links ART to TRADE, WARFARE and CONTACT
  without leaving the object
```

A reproduced objective item in the core source tests exactly this material through statements about a Konark horse sculpture, a broad and probably wooden stirrup in Khajuraho sculpture, and iron stirrups in the armies of the Delhi Sultans. That item comes from an examination other than the Civil Services and is reproduced answer-neutrally in the verified PYQ section; what matters here is that the examining body treats the stirrup as a testable dating device, which is the strongest possible endorsement of learning it.

### Examination use

The Gupta section supplies the "classical" reference point that almost every comparative sculpture question needs, and its halo distinction is one of the few reliable single-feature discriminators between two schools. The Sultanganj caution is a model of contested-date handling. The terracotta material answers any demand on the continuity of the terracotta tradition, and the two-class division with four named site groups is unusually easy to reproduce accurately. The ring-stirrup is the topic's best evidence for the proposition that art records technology, and it is directly testable.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "classical" as a technical period label rather than an evaluative judgement offered in quotation marks.
- Attributing the Ellora excavations to Gupta patronage on the strength of the chapter-opening sentence, when the same source dates Ellora's murals to the sixth to ninth centuries under the Rashtrakutas and other dynasties.
- Dating the Sultanganj Buddha firmly to the Gupta period.
- Confusing the halos: Mathura's is larger than Gandhara's and geometrically decorated; the Gupta halo is intricately decorated.
- Omitting metal from the Gupta school's materials; it is named alongside cream-coloured sandstone.
- Reducing Gupta terracotta to "figurines"; the record is of two classes, with plaque collections and distinctive regional heads.
- Placing a ring-stirrup in a pre-Gupta sculpture, or claiming an indigenous origin for the stirrup when the record attributes its introduction to Central Asian contacts.

### Revision notes

1. The chapter opening calls the Gupta Empire, 319 AD to 550 AD, a "classical" era for Indian sculpture for rock-cut caves including Ellora; the quotation marks are the source's own.
2. The same source dates the Ellora murals mostly to the sixth to ninth centuries AD under the Rashtrakutas and other dynasties, and records them as newer than Ajanta's - a tension to disclose, not to resolve silently.
3. A new school developed around Sarnath, using cream-coloured sandstone and metal.
4. Its sculptures were immaculately dressed and lacked any form of nakedness.
5. The halo around the Buddha's head was intricately decorated - as against Mathura's larger, geometrically decorated halo.
6. Named example: the Sultanganj Buddha, 7.5 ft high, Bihar, a copper sculpture.
7. Dating caution: a c. seventh-century work is safer described as late or post-Gupta than as uncontested Gupta.
8. Gupta terracotta figures fall into two classes: gods and goddesses; and male and female figures.
9. Named terracotta finds: Buddha figures and ornaments at the Devnimori stupa in Gujarat; a large collection of plaques at Bikaner and Ahichchhatra; distinctive terracotta heads from Akhnur in Kashmir; numerous objects from Bhita.
10. Plaques are moulded, flat-backed and repeatable - standardised religious imagery in a cheap, widely distributed medium.
11. A ring-stirrup is a circular or oval loop on a horse's saddle, shown as a circular form hanging from the saddle.
12. Ring-stirrups appear in sculpture from the post-Gupta period onwards, particularly in early medieval North and South Indian temples; they help date equestrian sculptures and were introduced via Central Asian contacts.
13. Examples: Hoysala stone reliefs at Belur and Halebidu; stone panels of Khajuraho temples; Chalukyan temples at Badami, Aihole and Pattadakal; and chariot horses and warriors on reliefs at the Sun Temple, Konark.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the presence of a ring-stirrup in a relief can be used to argue about the date of the relief, and state two things this argument cannot do.

**Model answer:** The argument runs from technology to representation. A ring-stirrup is a physical circular or oval loop attached to a saddle to support the rider's feet, introduced into India via Central Asian contacts; sculptors reproduced it because they were attending to realistic riding equipment, and it is shown as a circular form hanging from the horse's saddle. Since it appears in sculpture only from the post-Gupta period onwards, particularly in early medieval North and South Indian temples, a relief that depicts one cannot predate the technology's arrival, so the detail gives a lower limit for the relief's date. The examples record where this reasoning applies: Hoysala reliefs at Belur and Halebidu, Khajuraho stone panels, Chalukyan temples at Badami, Aihole and Pattadakal, and the chariot horses and warriors at the Sun Temple, Konark. What the argument cannot do is give an upper limit - a stirrup appears in sculpture for centuries afterwards, so its presence dates a relief no more precisely than "post-Gupta or later". And it cannot date a relief that does not depict horses at all, which is most of the corpus; it is a device for one subject class, not a general dating method.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a technological marker pinpoints a date. It supplies a boundary - not earlier than - and the strongest answers say which kind of limit they have established rather than implying a precise year.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Gupta sculpture is defined by what it removed rather than by what it added." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The formulation is apt for the school's figural idiom, though it understates one addition.

What was removed is recorded directly. The new school around Sarnath produced sculptures that were immaculately dressed and lacked any form of nakedness, in cream-coloured sandstone - pale and even, against Mathura's mottled spotted red. Set beside Gandhara's heavily pleated garments, muscled athletic bodies and elaborate foreign hairstyles, the Gupta figure is quieter in surface, thinner in drapery and lower in contrast. That subtraction is what earns the period its "classical" label, offered in quotation marks in the source itself.

What was added is not nothing. The halo around the Buddha's head is intricately decorated, which is elaboration rather than restraint, and metal is named alongside stone as a school material, with the 7.5 ft copper Sultanganj Buddha of Bihar as the standard example - though a c. seventh-century date places that object at the late or post-Gupta end.

The idiom is therefore subtractive in the body and additive in the aureole.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the removal thesis with at least two recorded subtractions 3 + an explicit comparison to Gandhara and Mathura on stone and drapery 3 + the intricate halo and metal as counter-evidence 2 + the Sultanganj example with its dating clause 1 + a verdict that resolves the tension rather than restating it 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 14:** The conventions fixed under Kushana patronage and refined here are now a complete system. It is time to use them: to take an unlabelled Buddhist image and work out, from gesture, posture and ornament alone, who it is and what it is doing.

## Lesson 14 - Identifying a Buddhist image: mudra, asana, robe and ornament

Progress: 14/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the complete recorded mudra set with hand positions and meanings; Abhaya and the Dharmachakra Parivartan convention; vajrasana and the asana vocabulary; the Buddha's non-gestural identifiers; the Bodhisattva contrast and the named Bodhisattvas; and a decision procedure for an unlabelled image

Book context: queried - the core art source's Various Mudras of Buddha plate with its hand positions and assigned meanings; the Gandhara statement that iconographic conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras were fixed under Kushana rule, naming the Dharmachakra Parivartan and Abhay Hasta mudras; the Mathura statement that Buddha images are largely carved in Abhaya Mudra; the Amaravati Abhayamudra definition as a gesture indicating fearlessness; the Bodhisattva ornament contrast; the aniconic table's vajrasana entry; the Marichi note on religious integration
CA search: "not required - identification-method lesson"
CA found: none required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the recorded mudra set, as hand position and as meaning

| Mudra | Hand position as recorded | Assigned meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Bhumisparsha** | **Right hand to earth, left in lap** | **Calling the Earth to Witness; Enlightenment** |
| **Dhyana** | **Hands in lap, thumbs form a triangle** | **Deep Meditation and Spiritual Perfection** |
| **Vitarka** | **Thumb and index form a circle, hand at chest** | **Teaching, Debate and Wisdom Flow** |
| **Dharmachakra** | **Hands at chest, circles formed** | **Teaching of Dharma** |
| **Uttarabodhi** | **Fingers interlaced, index fingers up** | **Enlightenment, Energy and Perfection** |
| **Karana** | **Index and little finger up** | **Banishing Negativity and Obstacles** |
| **Vajra** | **Left forefinger in the right fist** | **Supreme Wisdom** - the printed meaning column is clipped after "and", and the remainder is not supplied here |
| **Abhaya** | Raised open palm; recorded in the Gandhara material as the **Abhay Hasta Mudra** | A **gesture indicating fearlessness**; in the Nataraja description, **benediction, reassuring the devotee not to be afraid** |

*Eight gestures, each a fixed pairing of a hand position with a meaning. Learn them as pairs, not as names: an examination item will give you either the position or the meaning and ask for the other.*

A disclosure about this plate. The mudra material in the source is presented as a labelled illustration, and its text layer is partly broken - the **Vajra** row's meaning is cut off after "Supreme Wisdom and", and several labels survive only in fragments. Everything printed above is legible; nothing is completed from memory. Where a gesture you have met elsewhere does not appear in the table, the safe course is to name only what is recorded here and describe the rest by position.

### 1. Why a gesture system exists at all

A cult image has a job: to be recognised instantly, by a viewer who may be illiterate, at a distance, in low light, and in a shrine they have never entered before. A system of fixed hand positions solves that problem in a way that facial expression and physique cannot, because a hand is small, high-contrast, and can be carved sharply in any material.

The historical moment when this became a system is recorded: under Kushana rule there were **clear iconographic conventions established for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras** - with the **Dharmachakra Parivartan Mudra** and the **Abhay Hasta Mudra** named as examples. Before standardisation an image is a depiction; after it, an image is a *statement*, because the same gesture carries the same meaning in a different workshop, a different material and a different century.

The proof that standardisation took hold is regional consistency. At Mathura, the Buddha's images are **largely carved in Abhaya Mudra**. At Amaravati, a particular Buddha type - **robe on the left shoulder, the other hand in Abhayamudra** - became **so iconic that it was replicated in other parts of South and Southeast Asia**. Two schools, two stones, two regions, one gesture doing the same work, and the type travelling beyond India on the strength of it.

### 2. The mudras grouped by what they are doing

Learning eight gestures as a list is hard; learning them in four functional groups is not.

```text
  GROUP 1: THE HAND GOES DOWN - the moment of enlightenment
    BHUMISPARSHA   right hand to earth, left in lap
                   "calling the Earth to Witness" / Enlightenment
                   -> the decisive biographical gesture

  GROUP 2: THE HANDS REST - interiority
    DHYANA         hands in lap, thumbs form a triangle
                   Deep Meditation and Spiritual Perfection
                   -> paired in practice with a seated posture

  GROUP 3: THE HANDS COME UP TO THE CHEST - communication
    VITARKA        thumb and index form a circle, hand at chest
                   Teaching, Debate and Wisdom Flow
    DHARMACHAKRA   hands at chest, circles formed
                   Teaching of Dharma
                   -> the two teaching gestures; Vitarka is the
                      single-hand discussion gesture, Dharmachakra
                      the two-hand doctrinal one

  GROUP 4: THE HANDS ACT ON THE WORLD - power and protection
    ABHAYA         raised open palm; fearlessness, reassurance
    KARANA         index and little finger up; banishing
                   negativity and obstacles
    UTTARABODHI    fingers interlaced, index fingers up;
                   Enlightenment, Energy and Perfection
    VAJRA          left forefinger in the right fist;
                   Supreme Wisdom [meaning column clipped]
```

Two discriminations are worth drilling because they are the ones objective items exploit.

- **Vitarka against Dharmachakra.** Both are at the chest and both involve a circle formed by the fingers. Vitarka is recorded with **one** hand at the chest, thumb and index forming a circle, and means teaching, debate and wisdom flow. Dharmachakra is recorded with **hands**, plural, at the chest with **circles** formed, and means the teaching of Dharma. Number of hands is the discriminator.
- **Karana against Uttarabodhi.** Both raise fingers. Karana raises the **index and little finger** and banishes negativity and obstacles; Uttarabodhi **interlaces** the fingers with the **index fingers up** and signifies enlightenment, energy and perfection. Interlacing is the discriminator.

### 3. Asana: the recorded posture vocabulary

Fewer postures are named in the owned evidence than gestures, and the discipline is to use exactly the ones that are recorded:

| Posture | Where it is recorded | What it marks |
|---|---|---|
| **Vajrasana** | In the aniconic symbol table, as a symbol of **Enlightenment (Nirvana)**, alongside the Bodhi Tree | The seat of enlightenment; note that it functions here as a **symbol** in the aniconic phase as well as a posture in the iconic phase |
| **Tribhanga** | The body with **three bends**; the Harappan Dancing Girl's dancing posture; used **excessively** by the Amaravati school | Movement, narrative engagement, dance |
| **Lalitasana** | Kalinga temples in Odisha often have a figure of **Gajalakshmi in lalitasana** | A relaxed seated posture, recorded for a Hindu goddess rather than for the Buddha |
| **Seated cross-legged** | The Harappan Pashupati seal figure; the general seated Buddha type | The basic meditative configuration |
| **Standing** | The Swat Valley standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma | Accessibility, address, narrative presence |

Do not import posture names that the owned evidence does not carry. If an image shows a posture you cannot name from this list, describe it - "seated with one leg pendant", "standing with the weight on one hip" - which is always accurate and never wrong.

### 4. The non-gestural identifiers of a Buddha figure

A gesture tells you what the figure is doing. Five further features tell you it is a Buddha at all, and all five are recorded in the school material:

1. **The monastic robe**, with the Amaravati type specifying the **robe on the left shoulder**.
2. **The halo**, present from the Parthian-linked seated Buddha onward; its treatment identifies the school - **larger and geometrically decorated** at Mathura, **intricately decorated** in the Gupta idiom.
3. **Elongated earlobes**, treated naturalistically at Gandhara and explained there as symbolising the **royal connection**.
4. **Bare feet** - the Buddha is depicted barefoot.
5. **Absence of jewellery**, which is the decisive contrast with the Bodhisattva.

One Gandhara-specific oddity belongs here because it defeats overconfident generalisation: **some of the images of the Buddha have moustaches**. Anyone who has learned "the Buddha is always shown clean-shaven with a serene face" will misidentify a Gandhara image on that basis. The recorded exception is a reminder that school conventions vary within the shared system.

### 5. Bodhisattvas: the ornament contrast, and the named figures

The named Bodhisattvas in the owned evidence are:

- **Maitreya**, and **followed by Avalokitesvara**, in the Gandhara material;
- **Padmapani holding a lotus** and **Vajrapani holding a thunderbolt**, flanking the Buddha in the Mathura material.

And the identifying rule: **unlike the Buddha figures, the Bodhisattva is often heavily ornamented, with elaborate hairdos and sandals.**

```text
  THE DECISION PROCEDURE FOR AN UNLABELLED BUDDHIST IMAGE

  Q1. Is there an INSCRIPTION?
        yes -> the inscription decides. Stop.
        no  -> continue
  Q2. Is the figure ORNAMENTED - jewellery, elaborate hairdo, SANDALS?
        yes -> BODHISATTVA. Go to Q3.
        no, plain monastic robe, barefoot, no jewellery
            -> BUDDHA. Go to Q4.
  Q3. What does the Bodhisattva CARRY?
        lotus ........... Padmapani
        thunderbolt ..... Vajrapani
        (named types in the owned evidence also include
         Maitreya and Avalokitesvara)
  Q4. What is the MUDRA?
        right hand to earth, left in lap .... Bhumisparsha, enlightenment
        hands in lap, thumbs a triangle ..... Dhyana, meditation
        one hand at chest, thumb-index circle Vitarka, debate
        hands at chest, circles ............. Dharmachakra, teaching
        raised open palm .................... Abhaya, fearlessness
        index and little finger up .......... Karana, banishing obstacles
        interlaced, index fingers up ........ Uttarabodhi
        left forefinger in right fist ....... Vajra
  Q5. What SCHOOL does the treatment indicate?
        muscled body, pleated drapery, wavy Usha-Nisha hair,
        elongated earlobes, grey stone or stucco ....... Gandhara
        spotted red sandstone, large geometric halo,
        Abhaya Mudra, flanking Padmapani and Vajrapani . Mathura
        greenish-white limestone, narrative relief,
        tribhanga, robe on left shoulder ............... Amaravati
        cream-coloured sandstone or metal, fully
        dressed, intricately decorated halo ............ Gupta Sarnath
  Q6. State the LIMIT: the procedure yields a type, a school and an
        approximate period. It does not yield a patron, an exact
        date or an original location.
```

### 6. One image that resists the system, honestly recorded

The source notes a **Marichi sculpture in Bihar** as an example of the **process of integration of different religious beliefs and practices**. It is recorded without further description here, and it is carried into this lesson for one reason: it is the source's own acknowledgement that some images are deliberately syncretic and will not resolve cleanly into a single tradition. A decision procedure is a tool, not a guarantee, and an answer that says so is more credible than one that pretends every image sorts.

### Examination use

Mudra items are a standing objective family, usually in pair-matching form, and the position-meaning pairs above are exactly what such an item tests. For descriptive answers, the identification procedure is the operational content behind any demand to "explain the iconography" of a named image, and the ornament contrast is the fastest correct answer to any Buddha-versus-Bodhisattva question. The standardisation point - conventions fixed under Kushana rule - is the analytical sentence that converts a list of gestures into an argument about how Indian religious art became readable.

### Traps introduced here

- Confusing Vitarka with Dharmachakra; the recorded difference is one hand against hands, one circle against circles.
- Confusing Karana with Uttarabodhi; the recorded difference is index and little finger raised against interlaced fingers with index fingers up.
- Completing the clipped meaning of the Vajra mudra from memory.
- Naming postures that the owned evidence does not carry.
- Assuming every Buddha image is clean-shaven; some Gandhara images have moustaches.
- Identifying an ornamented, sandalled, elaborately coiffed figure as a Buddha.
- Treating the identification procedure as yielding a patron or an exact date.
- Forgetting that an inscription, where present, overrides every iconographic inference.

### Revision notes

1. Bhumisparsha: right hand to earth, left in lap; calling the Earth to Witness; enlightenment.
2. Dhyana: hands in lap, thumbs forming a triangle; deep meditation and spiritual perfection.
3. Vitarka: thumb and index forming a circle, hand at chest; teaching, debate and wisdom flow.
4. Dharmachakra: hands at chest, circles formed; teaching of Dharma.
5. Uttarabodhi: fingers interlaced, index fingers up; enlightenment, energy and perfection.
6. Karana: index and little finger up; banishing negativity and obstacles.
7. Vajra: left forefinger in the right fist; supreme wisdom - the printed meaning is clipped.
8. Abhaya, also recorded as Abhay Hasta Mudra: a gesture indicating fearlessness; benediction and reassurance.
9. Conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras were fixed under Kushana rule, with the Dharmachakra Parivartan and Abhay Hasta mudras named.
10. Mathura's Buddha images are largely in Abhaya Mudra; the Amaravati type with the robe on the left shoulder and the other hand in Abhayamudra was replicated in South and Southeast Asia.
11. Recorded postures: vajrasana as the enlightenment symbol; tribhanga, the body with three bends; lalitasana for Gajalakshmi in Kalinga temples; seated cross-legged; standing.
12. Buddha identifiers: monastic robe, halo, elongated earlobes, bare feet, no jewellery; some Gandhara images have moustaches.
13. Bodhisattva identifiers: heavily ornamented, elaborate hairdos, sandals; named figures are Maitreya, Avalokitesvara, Padmapani with a lotus and Vajrapani with a thunderbolt.
14. The procedure yields a type, a school and an approximate period only; an inscription overrides it; and some images, such as the Marichi sculpture in Bihar, are recorded as integrating different religious beliefs and practices.

### Concept check

**Question:** An unlabelled seated stone figure has a large halo decorated with geometrical patterns, a plain robe, no jewellery, bare feet, and a raised open right palm, and is carved in spotted red sandstone. Work through the identification and state what remains unknown.

**Model answer:** No inscription is reported, so the iconographic procedure applies. The absence of jewellery, the plain robe and the bare feet exclude a Bodhisattva, whose recorded markers are heavy ornament, elaborate hairdos and sandals; the figure is therefore a Buddha. The raised open palm is Abhaya, recorded as the gesture indicating fearlessness and as reassurance, which at Mathura is the gesture in which the Buddha's images are largely carved. Two school markers confirm the attribution independently: spotted red sandstone is the recorded Mathura material, and a halo that is larger than Gandhara's and decorated with geometrical patterns is the recorded Mathura halo treatment, as against the intricately decorated Gupta halo. The identification is therefore a Buddha in Abhaya Mudra from the Mathura school, which flourished on the banks of the Yamuna between the first and third centuries AD, under Kushana and successor patronage. What remains unknown is everything the procedure cannot yield: the patron, the exact year, the shrine the image was made for, whether it was a principal or a subsidiary image, and whether it stood alone or was one of a flanking group - and, absent an inscription, none of these should be asserted.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a confident identification licenses further claims. The procedure fixes type, school and approximate period, and stops. Every additional assertion - patron, date, original location - needs evidence of a different kind, usually epigraphic or excavational.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Indian iconography is legible because it is systematic." Elucidate with reference to Buddhist images. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Legibility here is an engineered property, not an accident of style.

The system is a fixed pairing of position and meaning. Bhumisparsha places the right hand to the earth and the left in the lap and means calling the Earth to witness, the enlightenment; Dhyana rests the hands in the lap with the thumbs forming a triangle and means deep meditation; Vitarka brings one hand to the chest with thumb and index forming a circle and means teaching and debate; Dharmachakra brings both hands to the chest with circles formed and means the teaching of Dharma; Abhaya raises an open palm and means fearlessness and reassurance. Status is equally coded: the Buddha is plainly robed, barefoot and unjewelled, while the Bodhisattva is heavily ornamented with elaborate hairdos and sandals.

The system has a history: clear conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras were established under Kushana rule.

Its success is measurable - the Amaravati robe-and-Abhayamudra type was replicated across South and Southeast Asia because it could be read anywhere.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the engineered-legibility thesis 2 + at least four exact position-and-meaning pairs 4 + the status coding of Buddha against Bodhisattva 2 + the historical moment of standardisation under Kushana rule 1 + the diffusion evidence as proof that the system worked 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 15:** The same system runs across the other two traditions, with different signs. A Tirthankara is told from another Tirthankara by an emblem at his feet, and a Hindu deity by what it carries, what it rides and, in one great case, by a form that is not a figure at all.

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three traditions, one method, three sign-sets

```text
                     AN UNLABELLED IMAGE
                            |
        Which SIGN-SET is being used?
                            |
  +-------------------------+-------------------------+
  |                         |                         |
 BUDDHIST                  JAIN                      HINDU
 sign-set:                 sign-set:                 sign-set:
 MUDRA + ROBE +            LANCHANA (emblem)         AYUDHA (attribute)
 ORNAMENT-STATUS           + NUDITY/ROBE             + VAHANA (mount)
 (Lesson 14)               + YAKSHA-YAKSHI           + ANICONIC EMBLEM
  |                          attendants                (linga)
  |                         |                         |
 Buddha: plain,            Rishabhanatha - BULL      Shiva - linga,
 barefoot, halo            Ajitanatha - ELEPHANT     mukhalinga, Nandi
 Bodhisattva:              Sambhavanatha - HORSE     Vishnu - avatars
 ornamented,               Abhinandananatha -        Lakshmi - elephants
 sandals                     MONKEY                    and lotuses
                           Sumatinatha -             Shaktis - the
                             CURLEW (heron)            Saptamatrikas
                           Padmaprabha -
                             RED LOTUS
                           Mahavira - LION
                            |
                  "their symbols and colours are
                   commonly used in Jain temple
                   iconography"
```

*One method - identify by assigned sign - realised three times with three different sign-vocabularies. That is the sentence to open any comparative iconography answer with.*

### 1. The Jain system: the lanchana and what it solves

Jainism presents the identification problem in its purest form. There are **24 Tirthankaras**, and they are iconographically almost identical: ascetic, calm, frontal, often seated in meditation or standing rigidly upright. No facial or bodily feature distinguishes one from another. The solution is an **emblem** assigned to each.

The recorded emblems available for this session are the first six of the list, together with Mahavira's:

| No. | Tirthankara | Symbol | Colour |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Rishabhanatha (Adinath)** | **Bull** | Golden |
| 2 | **Ajitanatha** | **Elephant** | Golden |
| 3 | **Sambhavanatha** | **Horse** | Golden |
| 4 | **Abhinandananatha** | **Monkey** | Golden |
| 5 | **Sumatinatha** | **Curlew (heron)** | Golden |
| 6 | **Padmaprabha** | **Red Lotus** | **Red** |
| 24 | **Mahavira** | **Lion** | - |

And the operative rule, stated by the source: **their symbols and colours are commonly used in Jain temple iconography.**

A disclosure: the printed list of 24 is **clipped after the sixth entry** in the text available for this session, and the "key highlights" column is cut off throughout. The seven emblems above are what is legible, with Mahavira's lion recorded separately in the biographical passage. The remaining seventeen emblems are not supplied here, and should not be produced from memory in an answer.

Two analytical points follow, and both are strong.

- **Colour is part of the sign-set, not decoration.** Five of the six recorded Tirthankaras are golden and one, Padmaprabha, is red - and the name itself means lotus-splendour, so name, colour and emblem reinforce one another. In painted or polychrome contexts, colour is an identifier.
- **The system is necessitated by doctrine.** All 24 Tirthankaras **attained kevala jnana, omniscience, and liberation, moksha**, and all **taught the path of ahimsa, truth, non-possession, celibacy and self-discipline**. Because they are doctrinally equivalent, they cannot be distinguished by attribute of character or achievement - so an arbitrary, learnable emblem is the only workable device. The iconography is a direct consequence of the theology, and saying so is an analytical sentence worth a mark.

### 2. Jain images: what else is visible

**Attendants.** In **Buddhism and Jainism the Yaksha is depicted as the attendant deity of the Bodhisattva and the Tirthankara**, and **all of the Jain Tirthankaras were associated with a yakshi**. So an attendant pair flanking an ascetic figure is itself a Jain or Buddhist marker.

**Sectarian markers.** The two major schools differ in a way that is directly visible in images. Both traditions agree that Mahavira and his early disciples had moved around naked; the **Digambaras follow that tradition strictly**, holding that a monk must renounce all possessions including clothes, while the **Svetambaras wear white robes**, viewing nudity as a practice that had fallen into abeyance. A monk carries only a **rajoharana**, a small broom for brushing insects away before sitting down, and a **kamandalu**, a water gourd. Digambara female monastics wear unstitched plain white sarees and are called **Aryikas**.

That is iconographically usable with care. Nudity or its absence in a Jain monastic image corresponds to a sectarian tradition, and the broom and the water gourd are identifiable objects. What must not be done is to date or locate an image from the sectarian marker alone, since both traditions persisted in overlapping regions.

**Auspicious symbols.** The source records an **ashtamangala** - one of the **eight auspicious symbols of the Svetambara sect** - described as a symbol with **four arms with compulsory nine corners or turns**, associated with the **18th Tirthankara Aranatha**, and illustrated alongside a **hand with a wheel on the palm**. The eight-symbol set itself is not enumerated in the text available here, and is not supplied.

**A named site.** The **Sittanavasal** Jain cave shrine in Tamil Nadu, whose development is traced from the first century BC to the tenth century AD, is recorded as **mainly famous for the sculptures of Tirthankaras** as well as for its murals - a useful named case, with its mural material routed to the painting topic.

### 3. The Hindu system: ayudha, vahana and the aniconic emblem

**The attribute.** The Mathura evidence gives the rule: **Hindu gods were represented using their avayudhas**, with **Shiva shown through the linga and mukhalinga**. Attribute-reading is therefore the operative Hindu identification method, exactly as the lanchana is for Jain images and the mudra for Buddhist ones.

**The mount.** The **vahana** is defined as **the mount or vehicle of the main deity**, and - this is the detail that turns iconography into architecture - **it is placed just before the sanctum sanctorum.** So in a temple, the mount is not merely an attribute carved on the image; it is a separate sculpture with a fixed position in the building, which is why the Nandi of a Shiva temple can be a colossal monolith with a hall of its own, as Lesson 7 recorded at Thanjavur, Lepakshi and Warangal.

**The aniconic emblem.** The **linga** represents Shiva without depicting him; the **mukhalinga** is the form bearing a face, and is therefore the hinge between aniconic and anthropomorphic representation within a single deity's iconography. The related narrative form is **Lingodbhava**, recorded as **Shiva appearing as an infinite column of light**, and placed at **Chidambaram and Mahabalipuram**.

### 4. The named Shiva manifestations

The source presents these as a plate of mythical manifestations of Shiva in ancient and early medieval India. The text layer is partly broken, and what follows reproduces only the legible pairings of name, meaning and location.

| Manifestation | Recorded meaning | Recorded locations |
|---|---|---|
| **Lingodbhava** | Shiva appearing as an **infinite column of light** | Chidambaram; Mahabalipuram |
| **Dakshinamurthy** | **Teacher of wisdom, silent meditation** | Chola temples; Badami cave temples |
| **Kalyanasundara** | The **divine wedding of Lord Shiva and Goddess Parvati** | Pallava, Chola and Hoysala period sculptures |
| **Ardhanarishvara** | **Unity of masculine and feminine energies** | Elephanta Caves; Mathura sculptures |
| **Nataraja** | The **cosmic cycle of creation, preservation and destruction** | Aihole; Badami; Thanjavur; Chidambaram; Mahabalipuram |

The Kalyanasundara case is described in full elsewhere in the chapter and is worth learning as a complete unit, because it is the single best "sculpture depicts a social ritual" example the topic owns: the **9th-century AD Kalyanasundara Murti** is highly remarkable for the manner in which **Panigrahana, the ceremony of marriage, is represented by two separate statuettes** - **Shiva with his extended right hand accepts Parvati's, the bride's, right hand, and she is depicted with a bashful expression and taking a step forward.**

Notice the technical ingenuity: a ritual that consists of a *joining* is rendered by two *separate* sculptures, so the ceremony exists in the space between them. That is a compositional argument, and it is the kind of observation that lifts a description into analysis.

### 5. The Saptamatrikas and the recorded Krishna types

**Saptamatrika sculptures** are a distinct feature of South Indian temples. The **Saptamatrikas are shaktis of the Hindu deities like Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara**, and one of the **oldest depictions** can be seen in the **Kailasanatha temple in Kanchipuram**. They are a group form - seven goddesses derived from named male deities - and therefore identified as a set rather than individually, which is a third mode of identification alongside the emblem and the attribute.

**Krishna** is recorded through five named depiction types, each with a meaning and a regional distribution. The plate's text is partly garbled; the legible content is:

| Type | Recorded content | Recorded regions |
|---|---|---|
| **Bala Krishna** | The **playful, mischievous** child and the **innocence of Krishna** | Mathura; South India |
| **Govardhan Leela** | **Krishna lifting Govardhan Hill and protecting devotees** | Mathura; Rajasthan; South India |
| **Kaliya Mardan** | The **triumph of good over evil** | Mathura; Khajuraho; Tamil Nadu; Karnataka |
| **Raas Leela** | **Krishna dancing with the Gopis** | Khajuraho; Pahari; South India |
| **Venugopala** | **Krishna playing a flute**, a graceful posture showing **divine love** | Tamil Nadu; Karnataka |

Two points make this more than a table. First, **Mathura recurs in three of the five rows**, which is consistent with its recorded status as an indigenous school serving Hindu as well as Buddhist and Jain subjects. Second, the types are **episodic** - each depicts a narrative moment rather than a static attribute - so Vaishnava iconography leans on story in a way Shaiva aniconic iconography does not, and the contrast between a linga and a Govardhan Leela is a genuine structural difference between two devotional visual languages.

### 6. The unified decision procedure

```text
  STEP 1  INSCRIPTION present?          -> it decides. Stop.
  STEP 2  ASCETIC, unadorned, frontal, with an EMBLEM at the base
          or an attendant yaksha-yakshi pair?
                                        -> JAIN. Read the LANCHANA:
                                           bull / elephant / horse /
                                           monkey / curlew / red lotus /
                                           lion ... (only the recorded
                                           emblems may be named)
  STEP 3  MONASTIC ROBE, barefoot, halo, no jewellery, a MUDRA?
                                        -> BUDDHIST. Run Lesson 14's
                                           procedure.
  STEP 4  Does the figure CARRY attributes, or is it accompanied by
          a MOUNT, or is it an ANICONIC EMBLEM?
                                        -> HINDU. Read:
                                           AYUDHA -> the deity
                                           VAHANA -> confirms the deity,
                                             and in a temple stands just
                                             before the sanctum
                                           LINGA / MUKHALINGA -> Shiva
                                           GROUP OF SEVEN -> Saptamatrikas
                                           EPISODE -> a Krishna type or a
                                             Shiva manifestation
  STEP 5  Then and only then, read MATERIAL and TREATMENT for school
          and period.
  STEP 6  State the limit, and note that some images - the Marichi
          sculpture in Bihar is the source's own example - record the
          integration of different religious beliefs and practices and
          will not sort cleanly.
```

### Examination use

Iconographic identification is tested objectively through pair-matching - deity with attribute, Tirthankara with emblem, manifestation with meaning - and descriptively through demands to explain the iconography of a named image. The Jain emblem table is unusually high-yield per line. The vahana definition, with its architectural position, is the sentence that connects this lesson to the temple material of Lesson 16 and to the Nandi evidence of Lesson 7. The Kalyanasundara description is the single best case for a social-ritual-in-sculpture demand, and the Shiva manifestation table supplies named locations, which is what converts a general answer into an evidenced one.

### Traps introduced here

- Supplying Tirthankara emblems beyond those recorded here; only seven are legible in the available text.
- Ignoring the colour column; Padmaprabha is recorded as red where the first five are golden.
- Dating or locating an image from a Digambara or Svetambara marker alone.
- Enumerating the eight auspicious symbols when the source text available here names only one and its association with the 18th Tirthankara Aranatha.
- Treating the vahana purely as an image attribute, when the recorded definition also fixes its architectural position just before the sanctum.
- Confusing the manifestations: Lingodbhava is the column of light, Dakshinamurthy the teacher in silent meditation, Kalyanasundara the divine wedding, Ardhanarishvara the union of masculine and feminine, Nataraja the cosmic cycle.
- Describing the Kalyanasundara Murti as a single sculpture; the marriage ceremony is represented by two separate statuettes.
- Treating the Saptamatrikas as individually identifiable rather than as a group of shaktis of Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara.

### Revision notes

1. One method, three sign-sets: mudra and ornament-status for Buddhist images, lanchana for Jain images, ayudha and vahana for Hindu images.
2. Recorded Tirthankara emblems: Rishabhanatha or Adinath - bull; Ajitanatha - elephant; Sambhavanatha - horse; Abhinandananatha - monkey; Sumatinatha - curlew or heron; Padmaprabha - red lotus; Mahavira - lion.
3. Colours: golden for the first five recorded, red for Padmaprabha; symbols and colours are commonly used in Jain temple iconography.
4. All 24 Tirthankaras attained kevala jnana and moksha and taught ahimsa, truth, non-possession, celibacy and self-discipline - doctrinal equivalence is why an arbitrary emblem system is necessary.
5. The Yaksha attends the Bodhisattva and the Tirthankara; all Tirthankaras were associated with a yakshi.
6. Digambaras follow strict nudity; Svetambaras wear white robes; the monk's permitted possessions are the rajoharana and the kamandalu; Digambara female monastics are Aryikas in unstitched plain white sarees.
7. An ashtamangala of the Svetambara sect is recorded with four arms and nine compulsory corners, associated with the 18th Tirthankara Aranatha, alongside a hand with a wheel on the palm.
8. Sittanavasal in Tamil Nadu, traced from the first century BC to the tenth century AD, is famous for Tirthankara sculptures.
9. Hindu gods are represented using their avayudhas; Shiva is shown through the linga and mukhalinga.
10. The vahana is the mount or vehicle of the main deity, placed just before the sanctum sanctorum.
11. Shiva manifestations: Lingodbhava as an infinite column of light at Chidambaram and Mahabalipuram; Dakshinamurthy as teacher of wisdom in silent meditation in Chola temples and Badami cave temples; Kalyanasundara as the divine wedding in Pallava, Chola and Hoysala sculptures; Ardhanarishvara as the unity of masculine and feminine at the Elephanta Caves and in Mathura sculptures; Nataraja as the cosmic cycle at Aihole, Badami, Thanjavur, Chidambaram and Mahabalipuram.
12. The 9th-century Kalyanasundara Murti represents Panigrahana, the marriage ceremony, through two separate statuettes, Shiva accepting Parvati's right hand with his extended right hand as she steps forward with a bashful expression.
13. Saptamatrikas are shaktis of deities like Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara; one of the oldest depictions is at the Kailasanatha temple, Kanchipuram.
14. Krishna types: Bala Krishna at Mathura and in South India; Govardhan Leela at Mathura, Rajasthan and South India; Kaliya Mardan at Mathura, Khajuraho, Tamil Nadu and Karnataka; Raas Leela at Khajuraho, in Pahari work and South India; Venugopala in Tamil Nadu and Karnataka.
15. Vaishnava types are episodic while Shaiva aniconic representation is emblematic - a structural difference between two devotional visual languages.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Jain iconography needs an emblem system while Buddhist iconography can rely largely on gesture, and identify the one feature the two systems share.

**Model answer:** The difference follows from what each tradition needs to distinguish. Buddhist images mostly need to distinguish *states and roles*: whether the figure is meditating, teaching, reassuring or calling the earth to witness, and whether it is a Buddha or a Bodhisattva. Gesture does that directly, which is why the mudra set pairs a hand position with a meaning - right hand to earth for enlightenment, hands in lap with thumbs forming a triangle for meditation, an open palm for fearlessness - and why status is carried by ornament, the Buddha plain and barefoot and the Bodhisattva heavily ornamented with elaborate hairdos and sandals. Jain images need to distinguish *individuals* who are doctrinally identical: all 24 Tirthankaras attained kevala jnana and liberation and taught the same path of ahimsa, truth, non-possession, celibacy and self-discipline, so no gesture, expression or attribute of achievement can separate them. An arbitrary assigned emblem is therefore the only workable device, and the source records that these symbols and colours are commonly used in Jain temple iconography - bull for Rishabhanatha, elephant for Ajitanatha, horse for Sambhavanatha, monkey for Abhinandananatha, curlew for Sumatinatha, red lotus for Padmaprabha, lion for Mahavira. The shared feature is the attendant convention: in both traditions the Yaksha is depicted as the attendant deity, of the Bodhisattva in Buddhism and of the Tirthankara in Jainism, with every Tirthankara additionally associated with a yakshi.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the two systems as arbitrary stylistic choices of different religions. Each is a solution to a different identification problem, and naming the problem is what turns a memorised table into an argument.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In Indian religious art, the sign carries the theology." Discuss with reference to Jain, Hindu and Buddhist iconography. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds in a precise sense: in each tradition, the identifying sign encodes what that tradition most needs a viewer to know.

In Jain art the sign carries individuation without hierarchy. All 24 Tirthankaras attained kevala jnana and moksha and taught the same path of ahimsa, truth, non-possession, celibacy and self-discipline, so no image can rank or characterise them; an assigned emblem - bull for Rishabhanatha, elephant for Ajitanatha, horse for Sambhavanatha, monkey for Abhinandananatha, curlew for Sumatinatha, red lotus for Padmaprabha, lion for Mahavira - names the individual while asserting nothing about relative attainment. The doctrine of equivalent liberation is thereby preserved in the iconography, and the sectarian question of clothing is likewise visible, the Digambaras strictly unclothed and the Svetambaras in white robes.

In Hindu art the sign carries relation and power. The deity is identified by its avayudha, Shiva through the linga and mukhalinga; the vahana states the relationship of service and is placed just before the sanctum sanctorum; and named manifestations condense whole theological propositions - Lingodbhava as an infinite column of light, Ardhanarishvara as the unity of masculine and feminine, Nataraja as the cosmic cycle of creation, preservation and destruction.

In Buddhist art the sign carries action and status: Bhumisparsha calls the earth to witness, Dharmachakra teaches, Abhaya reassures, while ornament or its absence divides the renounced Buddha from the still-princely Bodhisattva.

Two qualifications: signs also travel independently of doctrine, as the shared yaksha attendant shows; and some images, such as the Marichi sculpture recorded in Bihar, integrate different beliefs and resist a single reading.

The sign therefore carries the theology reliably, but not exclusively.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the sign-encodes-need thesis 3 + Jain individuation with at least four emblems and the equivalence argument 4 + Hindu attribute, vahana and two named manifestations 4 + Buddhist gesture and ornament-status 2 + both qualifications, travelling signs and syncretic images 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 16:** The vahana has already put a sculpture inside a building. The next lesson stays there, in the South Indian temple, where a set of recurring elements - a monster face, a pillar that sings, a group of seven goddesses and a hybrid lion - shows what sculpture becomes when it is permanently attached to architecture.

### Visual: the recurring elements and where each one sits


*The right-hand column is the lesson's real content. Every one of these elements is defined partly by where it is placed, which is why the boundary with the architecture topics has to be drawn carefully rather than ignored.*

### 1. The corpus and its patrons

South Indian temple sculpture includes the sculpture of different temples built by different dynasties - the **Chalukyas, Pallavas, Pandyas and Cholas**. That four-dynasty span is worth stating at the start of any answer on this material, because it establishes that the features described are regional and long-lived rather than the property of one court.

### 2. Kirtimukha: the face that is a threshold

**Kirtimukha is the monster face with huge fangs**, and it has been seen **frequently as a decorative motif in the iconography of South Indian temple architecture**. It is **very common in the iconography of Hindu temple architecture in India and Southeast Asia**, and is **often found in Buddhist architecture**.

Three things are worth extracting.

- **It is a motif with an enormous geographical and religious range** - Hindu temples across India and Southeast Asia, and Buddhist architecture too. That places it alongside Shalabhanjika and the Yaksha as a form that crosses religious boundaries, and it can be used as evidence in exactly the same way.
- **It is described as decorative, and the description should be respected.** No meaning is assigned to it in the owned evidence. Supplying a mythological origin story for the Kirtimukha would be precisely the kind of invention this session refuses.
- **Its placement is characteristically over openings and at terminations** - which is why it functions as a threshold motif. That observation follows from the form rather than from a source statement and should be offered as a reading.

### 3. Musical sculptures: granite that sounds

**Sculpture with musical instruments of granite architectural elements is a unique feature of south Indian temples.** These sculptures are **frequently found in Tamil Nadu**. **One of the earliest musical sculptures was recovered from Kodumbalur in Tamil Nadu.** And the celebrated case: **the Musical Pillars of the Vittala Temple in Hampi, the SaReGaMa pillars.**

This is the most interesting single element in the lesson, because it collapses three categories at once.

```text
  A MUSICAL PILLAR IS SIMULTANEOUSLY

  ARCHITECTURE     it carries load; it is part of a mandapa
        +
  SCULPTURE        it is carved in granite with figures and
                   instruments
        +
  INSTRUMENT       the slender colonnettes are tuned, so the
                   element PRODUCES SOUND
        |
  => the only class of object in this topic that must be
     EXPERIENCED rather than only seen, and therefore the
     strongest single case for the claim that Indian temple
     sculpture was made for use, not for display
```

Two cautions. The owned evidence records the existence, the location and the name of these pillars; it does not supply pillar counts, tuning descriptions, acoustic mechanisms or dimensions, and none should be invented. And the Vittala temple itself, as a building, belongs to the architecture topic; what this session owns is the sculpted element.

### 4. Saptamatrikas in their setting

The Saptamatrikas were introduced in Lesson 15 as an identification problem - a group form rather than an individually attributed one. Here their temple context matters: **Saptamatrika sculptures are a distinct feature of South Indian temples**, they are the **shaktis of the Hindu deities like Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara**, and **one of the oldest depictions can be seen in the Kailasanatha temple in Kanchipuram**.

The set form has a consequence for placement. A group of seven cannot occupy a single niche; it requires a panel, a row of niches or a subsidiary shrine, which means the temple's plan must accommodate it. This is the clearest small example of iconography *shaping* architecture rather than merely decorating it, and it is a good sentence to have available for a question about the relationship between the two.

### 5. Animals, birds, hybrids: the zoological register

The other unique features of South Indian temple sculpture include **frequent uses of animal and bird motifs**, **depiction of elephants and snakes**, **motifs of the swan in Pallava temples**, and **Yali motif sculpture**.

The **Yali** is recorded in this chapter as **a mythological creature with the head and body of a lion and the trunk and tusks of an elephant**. The Nayaka material adds the second description and the architectural role: the Nayakas **used narrative and composite columns**, and their **gopurams were heavily painted depicting warriors, deities and animals - especially the Yali, which is a composite lion figure that becomes symbolic of the Nayaka architectural identity**; in **Tamil literature** the Yali is **a mythical creature with a cat-like body, the head of a lion with the tusks of an elephant and the tail of a serpent**, **described as more powerful than the lion, the tiger or the elephant**, and **said to be a guardian creature protecting human beings both physically and spiritually**.

As Lesson 7 established, the two recorded anatomies are both from the same source and neither is to be presented as an error; state the composite, lion-based, elephant-tusked core.

The **Vyala** is the northern counterpart in the same family: at **Khajuraho**, sculptures on temples dedicated to Vishnu include the **Vyalas, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies**, also found in other Indian temples.

```text
  WHY THE HYBRIDS MATTER MORE THAN THE NATURALISTIC ANIMALS

  a naturalistic elephant or snake      a YALI or VYALA
  - is drawn from observation           - is assembled from parts
  - carries a symbolic association      - cannot exist, so its meaning
                                          is entirely conventional
  - can be misread as ornament          - is unmistakably a DESIGNED SIGN
        |                                       |
  both are common, but only the hybrid proves that the sculptor is
  working inside a sign system rather than depicting the world
        |
  and the hybrid does architectural work: the Yali becomes symbolic
  of NAYAKA ARCHITECTURAL IDENTITY, and appears on the pillars and
  gopurams that bear load and frame entry
```

### 6. The boundary, stated exactly

This is the lesson where the handover to the architecture topics has to be precise, because the two bodies of material physically overlap.

| Owned here | Owned by the architecture topics |
|---|---|
| The **figure, face, motif or group** as a carved object - Kirtimukha, Saptamatrika set, Yali, Vyala, swan, elephant, snake | The **gopuram, vimana, mandapa, prakaram, garbhagriha** as structural elements |
| The **sign system** and the identification of what a motif is | The **plan, elevation, style school** - Dravida, Vesara, Nagara - and their regional histories |
| The **musical sculpture** as a carved granite element | The **temple** as a building, its patron dynasty's architectural programme and its structural history |
| The **vahana as an image**, and the fact that it is placed just before the sanctum | The **Nandi mandapa as a building** on the temple's axis |
| **Placement where placement is what makes the image work** - a threshold face, a group needing a panel, a mount on the axis | **Placement as architectural composition** - axial arrangement, enclosure, sequence of courts |

State this boundary in one sentence at the start of an answer and it will keep the script inside the question: "the sculptural programme is read here as a system of signs and figures; the structures carrying it belong to the architectural account."

### Examination use

This material is tested both ways. Objectively, the pairings are crisp - Kirtimukha with the monster face, musical pillars with the Vittala temple at Hampi, the earliest musical sculpture with Kodumbalur, the oldest Saptamatrika depiction with Kailasanatha at Kanchipuram, the swan motif with Pallava temples, the Yali with Nayaka identity. Descriptively, the lesson supplies the elements list that any question on South Indian temple sculpture needs, and the hybrid-animal argument, which is the strongest available answer to a demand about the significance of animal figures in Indian art. It also feeds the 2022 lion-and-bull demand owned by this topic, supplying the lion's medieval architectural career.

### Traps introduced here

- Assigning South Indian temple sculpture to the Cholas alone; the recorded corpus spans Chalukyas, Pallavas, Pandyas and Cholas.
- Supplying a mythological origin for the Kirtimukha; the owned evidence records it as a decorative monster face with huge fangs and assigns no narrative.
- Claiming that Kirtimukha is exclusively Hindu; it is recorded as often found in Buddhist architecture too.
- Stating pillar counts, tuning details or acoustic explanations for the musical pillars.
- Treating the Saptamatrikas as seven independent goddesses rather than as shaktis of deities like Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara.
- Attributing the swan motif generally when it is recorded specifically for Pallava temples.
- Presenting either recorded Yali anatomy as the correct one and the other as wrong.
- Drifting into gopuram and vimana description, which belongs to the architecture topics.

### Revision notes

1. South Indian temple sculpture draws on temples built by the Chalukyas, Pallavas, Pandyas and Cholas.
2. Kirtimukha is the monster face with huge fangs, a frequent decorative motif in South Indian temple iconography, very common in Hindu temple architecture in India and Southeast Asia and often found in Buddhist architecture.
3. No narrative meaning is assigned to the Kirtimukha in the owned evidence; do not supply one.
4. Musical sculptures - sculpture with musical instruments of granite architectural elements - are a unique feature of south Indian temples, frequent in Tamil Nadu.
5. One of the earliest musical sculptures was recovered from Kodumbalur, Tamil Nadu.
6. The Musical Pillars of the Vittala Temple in Hampi are known as the SaReGaMa pillars.
7. A musical pillar is architecture, sculpture and instrument at once - the strongest case that temple sculpture was made for use.
8. Saptamatrika sculptures are a distinct feature of South Indian temples; they are shaktis of deities like Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara; one of the oldest depictions is at the Kailasanatha temple, Kanchipuram.
9. A group of seven requires a panel, a row of niches or a subsidiary shrine - iconography shaping plan.
10. Other features: frequent animal and bird motifs; depiction of elephants and snakes; swan motifs in Pallava temples; Yali motif sculpture.
11. Yali in this chapter: a mythological creature with the head and body of a lion and the trunk and tusks of an elephant.
12. Yali in the Nayaka material: a composite lion figure symbolic of Nayaka architectural identity, on narrative and composite columns and heavily painted gopurams depicting warriors, deities and animals; in Tamil literature a cat-like body, lion's head with elephant tusks and serpent's tail, more powerful than lion, tiger or elephant, and a guardian protecting human beings physically and spiritually.
13. Vyala: at Khajuraho on temples dedicated to Vishnu, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies, also found in other Indian temples.
14. Hybrids prove a sign system where naturalistic animals do not, because a composite creature cannot be observed and its meaning is therefore wholly conventional.
15. The boundary: the figure, motif, group and sign system are owned here; the gopuram, vimana, mandapa, plan, elevation and style school belong to the architecture topics.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why are the musical pillars of the Vittala Temple a better illustration of the relationship between sculpture and architecture in South India than a wall relief is, and what must not be claimed about them?

**Model answer:** A wall relief is carried by architecture; a musical pillar is architecture. Sculpture with musical instruments of granite architectural elements is recorded as a unique feature of south Indian temples, frequent in Tamil Nadu, with one of the earliest such sculptures recovered from Kodumbalur, and the Musical Pillars of the Vittala Temple at Hampi - the SaReGaMa pillars - as the celebrated case. A pillar of that kind performs three functions simultaneously: it bears load, so it is structural; it is carved in granite with figures and instruments, so it is sculpture; and it produces sound, so it is an instrument. The relationship is therefore not decoration applied to a structure but a single element in which carving, support and use are inseparable, which is exactly the claim a question about sculpture and architecture is testing. What must not be claimed is any of the detail the owned evidence does not carry: the number of pillars or colonnettes, the notes produced, the tuning method, the acoustic mechanism or the dimensions. The existence, the location, the name and the general character are recorded; the physics is not, and supplying it would convert an evidenced point into an invented one.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "unique feature" as licence for elaboration. A recorded distinctive feature is a fact about a class of objects; everything beyond the record has to be sourced separately or left out.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"South Indian temple sculpture shows that iconography can shape architecture rather than merely ornament it." Comment. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is supported by three recorded cases in which a sculptural requirement determines a structural provision.

The vahana is defined as the mount or vehicle of the main deity and is placed just before the sanctum sanctorum, which fixes a position on the temple's axis for a sculpture; at Shiva temples this produces the Nandi mandapa, a building whose purpose is to house a carved bull. The Saptamatrikas, the shaktis of deities like Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara and a distinct feature of South Indian temples with one of the oldest depictions at the Kailasanatha temple in Kanchipuram, are a group of seven and therefore cannot occupy one niche; the plan must supply a panel, a row of niches or a subsidiary shrine. Musical sculptures of granite architectural elements, including the SaReGaMa pillars of the Vittala Temple at Hampi, are load-bearing members that are simultaneously carved and sounded.

The qualification is that much temple sculpture genuinely is applied ornament - Kirtimukha is recorded as a decorative motif.

The relationship therefore runs both ways, but the shaping direction is real and demonstrable.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the shaping-versus-ornamenting distinction 2 + the vahana position and its structural consequence 2 + the group-of-seven planning requirement 2 + the musical pillar as a triple-function element 2 + the ornament qualification with a named case 1 + verdict 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 17:** One South Indian image has been deferred through six lessons because it deserves its own. It is the topic's most complete iconographic system in a single object, it is the best case for the origin-versus-peak discipline, and it exists because of a casting technique learned in Lesson 2.

### Visual: the Nataraja read attribute by attribute
