```text
                often referred to as ARDHANARISHVARA
                              |
   REAR RIGHT HAND                            REAR LEFT HAND
   holds the DRUM (damru)                     holds the ETERNAL FIRE
   = the SOUND OF CREATION;                   = DESTRUCTION, the
     all creations spring from                  precursor and inevitable
     the great sound of the damru               counterpart of creation
                              |
   FRONT RIGHT HAND                           FRONT LEFT HAND
   raised in ABHAYA MUDRA                     points towards the
   = benediction, reassuring                  UPRAISED FOOT, indicating
     the devotee not to be                    the PATH OF SALVATION;
     afraid                                   the pose is GAJAHASTA
                                              ("elephant hand")
                              |
        a SNAKE twisted around the arm
        = KUNDALINI power, residing dormant in the human
          spine; if aroused, one can attain true consciousness
                              |
   LEFT LEG in the BHUJANGATRASITA stance
   = TIROBHAVA, kicking away the veil of MAYA or illusion
     from the devotee's mind
                              |
   Shiva dances on the figure of a small dwarf, MUYALAKA
   = the dwarf or demon symbolises IGNORANCE and the EGO
     of an individual
```

*Ten assigned signs on one figure. This is the fullest worked example of the reading method in the entire topic, and it is the model to apply to any other named image.*

### 1. Origin and peak, stated in the source's own words

An important feature of the Chola temples was **the importance placed on decoration through sculptures**, and an important piece of Chola sculpture was the sculpture of **Lord Shiva as Nataraja, Lord of Dance, in the Tandava dance posture**.

Then the sentence that governs everything written about this image: **though the earliest known Nataraja sculpture, which has been excavated at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole, was made during the early Chalukya rule, the sculpture reached its peak under the Cholas.**

```text
  THE ORIGIN-VERSUS-PEAK DISCIPLINE

  WRONG                              RIGHT
  "The Cholas created the            "The earliest known Nataraja
   Nataraja."                         sculpture was excavated at the
        |                             Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole and
  fails against the source's          was made during early Chalukya
  own explicit statement              rule; the form reached its peak
                                      under the Cholas, who gave it
                                      its canonical bronze expression."
        |
  THE GENERAL RULE THIS MODELS:
  never assume that the dynasty most famous for a form also
  originated it. Test the same rule on:
    - NBPW, famous as "Mauryan pottery", but a ware horizon of
      early historic urbanisation (Lesson 5)
    - the human Buddha image, famous as Gandhara's, but produced
      in overlapping contexts at Mathura too (Lesson 12)
```

The Shiva manifestations plate adds the geographical spread of the Nataraja form: **Aihole, Badami, Thanjavur, Chidambaram and Mahabalipuram**, with the meaning recorded as the **cosmic cycle of creation, preservation and destruction**. Aihole and Badami are early Chalukya sites; Thanjavur and Chidambaram are Chola; Mahabalipuram is Pallava. The form crosses three dynasties, which is the strongest available support for the origin-versus-peak reading.

### 2. The complete attribute reading, in the source's order

Each feature is a sign with an assigned meaning, and the list should be reproduced in order because the order itself is a logic - creation, destruction, reassurance, salvation, then the conditions of the devotee's mind.

| Element | Assigned meaning |
|---|---|
| **The rear right hand holds the drum** | The **sound of creation**; all creations spring from the great sound of the damru |
| **The rear left hand holds the eternal fire** | **Destruction**, which is the precursor and an inevitable counterpart of creation |
| **The front right hand raised in abhaya mudra** | **Benediction**, reassuring the devotee not to be afraid |
| **The front left hand points towards the upraised foot**, in the pose called **Gajahasta, "elephant hand"** | Indicates the **path of salvation** |
| **The left leg in bhujangatrasita stance** | **Tirobhava** - kicking away the **veil of maya or illusion** from the devotee's mind |
| **Shiva dances on the figure of a small dwarf, Muyalaka** | The dwarf or demon symbolises **ignorance and the ego of an individual** |
| **The matted and flowing locks** | The **flow of the River Ganges** |
| **One ear with a male earring, the other with a female earring** | The **fusion of male and female**, often referred to as **Ardhanarishvara** |
| **A snake twisted around the arm** | The **kundalini power**, residing dormant in the human spine; if aroused, one can attain true consciousness |
| **A surrounding nimbus of glowing lights** | The **vast unending cycles of time** |

Two structural observations convert this from a list into an argument.

- **The two rear hands are a pair, and the two front hands are a pair.** The rear pair states the cosmology - creation against destruction. The front pair addresses the worshipper - do not fear, and here is the way. So the image speaks on two registers simultaneously, outward to the universe and inward to the devotee, which is an unusually economical piece of design.
- **Three separate elements concern illusion and ignorance** - the raised leg kicking away maya, the dwarf as ignorance and ego, and the snake as dormant power awaiting arousal. The image is therefore weighted towards the obstacle and its removal rather than towards the dance as spectacle.

A note on names. The dwarf is recorded here as **Muyalaka**, and the canonical owner records the pair **Apasmara/Muyalaka**; both refer to the same figure and either is safe, with the recorded meaning - ignorance and the ego - attached.

### 3. Placement, and the named comparative set

**Many Shiva temples of South India have a separate natana-sabha, where the image of Nataraja is placed.** That is a dedicated hall for a single iconographic type, which puts the Nataraja in the same category as the Nandi of Lesson 7: an image important enough to generate architecture.

The notable Nataraja sculptures recorded are:

| Object | Medium and setting |
|---|---|
| **Nataraja Chola bronze masterpiece in Chidambaram Temple** | Cast bronze, in the temple that the manifestations plate also associates with Lingodbhava |
| **Stone reliefs in Pallava cave temples** | Relief, rock-cut |
| **Stone panels on Hoysala temple walls** | Relief, on a structural wall |
| **Thanjavur Brihadeeswara Temple Nataraja** | In the great Chola temple |

Use that set deliberately. It demonstrates the same iconographic programme executed in **three different formats** - free-standing bronze, rock-cut relief, structural wall panel - across at least **three dynasties**. The iconography is constant; the medium and placement vary; and that is exactly the distinction between a *type* and an *object* that this session has been building since Lesson 1.

### 4. The Chola bronze: what is owned about the process

The casting process itself was taught in Lesson 2 and is the same **cire-perdue** sequence - wax model, wet clay coat, drying, heating so the wax melts, pouring the wax out through a tiny hole, filling the hollow mould with liquid metal, cooling, then breaking away the clay. The Chola contribution is not a new technique but the application of an old one at a scale and with an iconographic precision that made the type canonical, portable and ritually usable.

What the owned evidence adds specifically:

- **The Cholas are recorded as still more famous for their extraordinary temples and bronze sculptures**, which places bronze casting alongside temple building as the dynasty's twin achievement.
- **The material's antiquity and composition.** Bronze work is one of the oldest art forms, as the bronze Dancing Girl from Mohenjo-daro - **dated to around 2500 BC** - shows. The **earliest non-ferrous metals used by man were copper and tin, which were mixed to form bronze.**
- **A textual record of the technology exists.** The **Matsya Purana describes bronze casting methods**, while texts like the **Rasaratnakara discuss metal purity and zinc distillation.**

That last point deserves emphasis because it is easy to miss. Indian metal casting is not only an archaeological and art-historical subject; it has a **textual tradition** describing method, purity and distillation. A question about Indian science and technology, or about the transmission of craft knowledge, can be answered from this topic - and the named texts are the evidence.

Two disciplines apply. Do not state alloy percentages, weights, heights or dimensions for any bronze; none is carried here. And do not claim that the Cholas invented lost-wax casting, which the Indus-period evidence refutes directly.

### 5. Patronage, including a named queen

**Sembiyan Mahadevi** was a **10th-century AD Chola queen, a patron of the arts and one of the most powerful queens of the Chola Empire**, who **built many temples such as at Kutralam, Virudhachalam and Aduthurai.**

She is worth a sentence in any Chola sculpture answer for three reasons. She is a **named individual patron**, which is rare in this topic and always more persuasive than "royal patronage". She is a **woman patron of monumental religious art**, which directly answers any demand about women's agency in cultural production. And her temple foundations at three named sites give the claim geography.

The other named Chola-period object in the chapter is the **Kalyanasundara Murti of the 9th century AD**, treated in Lesson 15, which represents **Panigrahana, the ceremony of marriage, through two separate statuettes** - Shiva with his extended right hand accepting Parvati's right hand as she steps forward with a bashful expression.

### Examination use

The Nataraja is the most likely single image to be named in a question, and the attribute table above is the complete answer to "explain its iconography". The origin-versus-peak sentence is the highest-value correction in the topic and should be deployed in any Chola art question, including the 2024 General Studies demand on Chola achievements in art and architecture, which is owned by the temple-architecture topic and reproduced in the verified PYQ section of this session for boundary clarity. The three-format comparative set answers demands about medium and technique. The Matsya Purana and Rasaratnakara references convert a sculpture answer into a technology answer where the question invites it. Sembiyan Mahadevi is the named-patron evidence that most scripts lack.

### Traps introduced here

- Stating that Chola patronage originated the Nataraja iconography; the earliest known example is at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole under early Chalukya rule.
- Swapping the hands: the rear right holds the drum, the rear left the fire, the front right is in abhaya mudra, the front left in gajahasta points to the upraised foot.
- Attaching the wrong term to the wrong element: gajahasta is the hand pose, bhujangatrasita the leg stance, tirobhava the meaning of that stance.
- Describing the dwarf as a defeated enemy rather than as the recorded symbol of ignorance and the ego of an individual.
- Claiming the Cholas invented lost-wax casting.
- Supplying alloy compositions, weights, heights or dates for individual bronzes.
- Treating the natana-sabha as a general temple hall rather than as a hall dedicated to the Nataraja image.
- Omitting the named comparative set, which is what proves the type crossed dynasties and media.

### Revision notes

1. An important feature of Chola temples was the importance placed on decoration through sculptures; the key piece is Shiva as Nataraja, Lord of Dance, in the Tandava posture.
2. The earliest known Nataraja sculpture was excavated at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole and made during early Chalukya rule; the sculpture reached its peak under the Cholas.
3. The manifestations plate records the Nataraja's meaning as the cosmic cycle of creation, preservation and destruction, at Aihole, Badami, Thanjavur, Chidambaram and Mahabalipuram.
4. Rear right hand: the drum, the sound of creation, from which all creations spring.
5. Rear left hand: the eternal fire, destruction, the precursor and inevitable counterpart of creation.
6. Front right hand: abhaya mudra, benediction, reassuring the devotee not to be afraid.
7. Front left hand: points to the upraised foot, indicating the path of salvation, in the pose called Gajahasta or elephant hand.
8. Left leg in bhujangatrasita stance: tirobhava, kicking away the veil of maya or illusion from the devotee's mind.
9. The small dwarf Muyalaka beneath the foot symbolises ignorance and the ego of an individual.
10. Matted flowing locks: the flow of the River Ganges. One male and one female earring: the fusion of male and female, often referred to as Ardhanarishvara.
11. A snake twisted around the arm: kundalini power, dormant in the human spine, which if aroused permits true consciousness.
12. A surrounding nimbus of glowing lights: the vast unending cycles of time.
13. Many South Indian Shiva temples have a separate natana-sabha where the Nataraja image is placed.
14. Notable Nataraja sculptures: the Chola bronze masterpiece at Chidambaram; stone reliefs in Pallava cave temples; stone panels on Hoysala temple walls; the Thanjavur Brihadeeswara Nataraja.
15. The Cholas are recorded as still more famous for their extraordinary temples and bronze sculptures; the technique is the Indus-period cire-perdue method applied with canonical precision.
16. Bronze is copper mixed with tin; the Mohenjo-daro Dancing Girl is dated to around 2500 BC; the Matsya Purana describes bronze casting methods and texts like the Rasaratnakara discuss metal purity and zinc distillation.
17. Sembiyan Mahadevi, a 10th-century AD Chola queen and patron of the arts, built temples at Kutralam, Virudhachalam and Aduthurai.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the Nataraja, explain the difference between an iconographic type and an individual object, and show why the difference matters for an examination answer.

**Model answer:** The type is the programme of assigned signs; the object is one execution of it. The type prescribes that the rear right hand holds the drum as the sound of creation, the rear left the eternal fire as destruction, the front right is raised in abhaya mudra as reassurance, the front left points in gajahasta to the upraised foot indicating the path of salvation, the left leg stands in bhujangatrasita representing tirobhava, the foot subdues the dwarf Muyalaka as ignorance and ego, the matted locks carry the Ganges, the two earrings signify the male-female fusion called Ardhanarishvara, the snake denotes dormant kundalini and the nimbus the unending cycles of time. The individual objects vary in everything else: the recorded set includes the Chola bronze masterpiece at Chidambaram, stone reliefs in Pallava cave temples, stone panels on Hoysala temple walls and the Thanjavur Brihadeeswara Nataraja, and the earliest known example is a rock-cut sculpture at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole made under early Chalukya rule. The difference matters because it fixes what can be claimed: attribute statements belong to the type and hold everywhere, while claims about medium, dynasty, date and placement belong to particular objects and must be attached to a named one - which is precisely why "the Cholas created the Nataraja" fails, since it converts an object-level achievement into a type-level origin.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that an iconographic description dates or locates an image. The attributes are constant across five centuries, three dynasties and three media; only material, treatment, find-site and inscription can place a particular object.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The Nataraja bronze is the most complete example of Indian sculpture as an argument rather than a depiction." Discuss. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The image qualifies because every element of it is an assigned proposition rather than an observation, and because the propositions are ordered.

The cosmological claim is stated by the rear pair of hands: the drum is the sound of creation, from which all creations spring, and the eternal fire is destruction, described as the precursor and inevitable counterpart of creation. Creation and destruction are therefore not opposites but a pair, and the image asserts this by giving them symmetrical positions.

The devotional claim is stated by the front pair: the right hand in abhaya mudra is benediction, reassuring the devotee not to be afraid, and the left in gajahasta points to the upraised foot, indicating the path of salvation. The image addresses the universe with one pair of hands and the worshipper with the other.

A third claim concerns the obstacle. The left leg in bhujangatrasita represents tirobhava, kicking away the veil of maya from the devotee's mind; the dwarf Muyalaka beneath the foot is ignorance and the ego; the snake on the arm is kundalini power, dormant in the spine, which if aroused yields true consciousness. Three separate elements therefore concern illusion and its removal.

The frame completes the argument: matted locks carry the Ganges, the paired male and female earrings signify the Ardhanarishvara fusion, and the nimbus of glowing lights is the vast unending cycles of time.

Two qualifications. The reading is conventional, not self-evident; the signs are legible because they were standardised, and a viewer outside the tradition reads only a dancer. And the argument is the type's, not one object's, since it is executed in Chola bronze at Chidambaram, in Pallava rock-cut relief, on Hoysala wall panels and at Thanjavur, with the earliest known example at Aihole under early Chalukya rule.

Within the tradition that made it, the Nataraja is a proposition in metal, and its completeness is why it, rather than any single narrative relief, is the tradition's summary statement.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the argument-versus-depiction thesis 3 + the rear-pair cosmological claim 3 + the front-pair devotional claim 3 + all three illusion elements 4 + the frame elements 3 + the conventionality qualification 2 + the type-not-object qualification with the Aihole antecedent 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 18:** The Chola case has shown what a mature regional programme looks like. The next lesson widens the frame to the other medieval and regional programmes, and asks the hardest question a sculpture examiner can ask: what, exactly, can a carved wall tell you about the society that paid for it?

## Lesson 18 - Medieval and regional programmes, and sculpture as social evidence

Progress: 18/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the Khajuraho figural corpus and its material; the Hoysala programme with its stone, friezes and named sculptors; Kakatiya sculpture at Warangal; the sculpture of the Ellora caves within the boundary; and the disciplined reading of temple sculpture as evidence of social life

CA found: none required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four regional programmes on the variables that distinguish them

| Variable | **Khajuraho (Chandella)** | **Hoysala** | **Kakatiya (Warangal)** | **Ellora** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Stone** | **Fine-grained sandstone, in varying shades of buff, pink and pale yellow**; the second group entirely sandstone, some temples partly granite and partly sandstone | **Chloritic schist, or soapstone** - soft and easy to carve when quarried, but it **hardens over time** | Not specified here beyond the temple's granite inscription pillar | Excavated living rock |
| **Format** | Relief and figure sculpture on lofty temples **without any enclosure wall**, erected on a **high platform terrace** | **Outer walls covered with sculptural friezes**; **lathe-turned pillars** inside the mandapas; friezes on a **stellate jagati** platform | **One thousand richly carved pillars**, **perforated screens**, **decorative icons**, **rock-cut elephants** and a **monolithic Nandi** | Rock-cut sculpture integral to excavated caves |
| **Subjects recorded** | **Maithuna reliefs** on the walls of both Hindu and Jain temples; **Vyalas**, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies, on temples dedicated to Vishnu | Scenes from the **Ramayana and Mahabharata**, **mythological scenes**, **animals** and **floral motifs**; frieze bands of **elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans** and **deities**; **Shalabhanjika** reliefs at Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura | Decorative icons and animal sculpture in a temple of three shrines, the **Trikutalayam** | **Vaishnavite sculptures** depicting the **avatars of Vishnu**; the **Atiriktanga-Bhairava**, a **skeleton form** of Shiva; large figures of **Ganga and Yamuna** flanking the entrance of Cave 21 |
| **Religious range** | Shaiva, Vaishnava **and Jain** sects; six temples to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras | Hindu, with a walled complex of **three Jain basadi** of the Hoysala period at Halebidu | Shaiva-centred, with the three shrines Rudreswara, Vasudeva and Surya | Buddhist, Hindu and Jain caves in one complex |
| **Named makers** | Not recorded here | **Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma** are recorded as prominent architects and sculptors of the dynasty | Not recorded here | Not recorded here |

*The last row is the rarest and most valuable. Indian sculpture is overwhelmingly anonymous; a dynasty whose sculptors are named by the source is therefore evidence of a different order, and it belongs in any answer about artistic agency.*

### 1. Khajuraho: the figural corpus, described without the sensationalism

The material first: the Khajuraho temples were built of **fine-grained sandstone, in varying shades of buff, pink and pale yellow**. As Lesson 2 established, fine grain permits precise, deeply undercut relief, so the density of the figural programme is enabled by the stone before it is explained by anything else.

The recorded subjects are three:

- **Maithuna reliefs.** Both the Hindu and Jain temples at Khajuraho feature a variety of artwork, **including erotic elements depicted through maithuna reliefs on the temple walls**. Note the two qualifiers in that sentence and keep them: the reliefs are part of a **variety** of artwork, and they appear on **Jain as well as Hindu** temples. Both qualifiers defeat the popular account in which Khajuraho is reducible to erotic sculpture and in which that sculpture is a Shaiva-tantric peculiarity.
- **Vyalas.** Some sculptures at the Khajuraho monuments dedicated to Vishnu include the **Vyalas, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies**, also found in other Indian temples. This is the northern member of the Yali-Vyala family of Lesson 16.
- **The Chausath-yogini shrine.** The earliest building at Khajuraho, with **circular and open-air structures reflecting Tantric and Shaiva traditions**; the wider class of Chausath Yogini temples is recorded as open-to-sky shrines dedicated to the worship of **Yoginis, female divine figures associated with the Shakti and Tantric traditions**. The structure itself belongs to the temple-architecture topic; what is owned here is the iconographic programme it implies - a cult of multiple female divinities requiring a circular arrangement of niches.

The sect distribution is worth reproducing because it is precise and because it disciplines any generalisation: of the surviving temples, **six are dedicated to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras**, and the **Matangeshvara temple remains an active site of worship**.

The 2025 General Studies demand on the Chandella artform - that the sculptors filled it with resilient vigour and breadth of life - is owned by the temple-architecture topic and is reproduced exactly in the verified PYQ section of this session. What this topic contributes to it is the reading method: material that permits deep relief, a figural programme whose subjects range from divine to erotic to hybrid-animal, and a religious range that includes a Jain group - which is what "breadth of life" can be evidenced with, as against paraphrased.

### 2. Hoysala: the stone that explains the carving

The Hoysala case is the clearest instance in the topic of **material determining style**, and the source states the mechanism explicitly: a notable feature of Hoysala temples was the use of **chloritic schist, or soapstone**. This stone is **soft and easy to carve when quarried, but it hardens over time.**

```text
  WHY SOAPSTONE PRODUCES THE HOYSALA LOOK

  SOFT WHEN QUARRIED                 HARDENS OVER TIME
  -> can be cut rapidly,             -> the fine detail SURVIVES
     deeply and minutely                instead of weathering away
  -> permits LATHE-TURNING of
     pillars - a stone shaped
     as if it were wood
  -> permits CONTINUOUS FRIEZE
     BANDS at small scale
          |
  RESULT: outer walls COVERED with sculptural friezes - scenes from
  the Ramayana and Mahabharata, mythological scenes, animals and
  floral motifs - and ordered bands of ELEPHANTS, LIONS, SCROLLS,
  HORSES, MAKARAS, SWANS and DEITIES
          |
  plus the STELLATE JAGATI platform, whose zig-zag wall surface
  MULTIPLIES the available frontage for carving
```

Two further recorded facts complete the programme. The **Nandi Pavilion and a separate kalyani, or temple tank, are noted for their ceremonial use** - which places the Hoysala Nandi in the same axial-attendant tradition as Thanjavur and Lepakshi. And **Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma** are recorded as prominent architects and sculptors of the dynasty.

The named sculptors matter for an argument, not just for recall. Indian sculpture is anonymous almost everywhere in this topic: not one Gandhara, Mathura, Amaravati or Gupta sculptor is named, and the Chola bronzes are attributed to a dynasty rather than to a hand. A tradition that records its makers is evidence of a change in the status of the craftsman, and where a question asks about artists and artisans in Indian history, this is the named evidence.

### 3. Kakatiya sculpture at Warangal

The **Thousand Pillar temple** in Warangal, believed to have been constructed between the twelfth and fourteenth centuries by the order of the Kakatiya king **Rudra Deva**, is dedicated primarily to Shiva and known as the **Sri Rudreshwara Swamy Temple**. Its recorded sculptural content is **one thousand richly carved pillars, perforated screens, decorative icons, rock-cut elephants and a monolithic Nandi within its premises**. It is built on a **double platform** with a central mandapa and three shrines - the Rudreswara, the Vasudeva and the Surya - known collectively as the **Trikutalayam**, and it was built in the **later Chalukyan and early Kakatiyan style** with the **sand-box technique** used to strengthen its foundation.

Two elements are this topic's: the **carved pillars, screens and icons** as a sculptural programme, and the **monolithic Nandi** as another instance of the vahana given monumental independent form. The plan, the platform, the style attribution and the sand-box foundation belong to the architecture topic.

The dynasty's other recorded Nandi arrangement is at the **Ramappa temple**, where **in front of the Nandi Pavilion stands the main temple**, made on a star-shaped platform. Keep the two Kakatiya cases distinct: the **monolith** is recorded at the Thousand Pillar temple, the **pavilion-and-approach relation** at Ramappa, and neither detail is carried across to the other. Both belong to the six-case Nandi set assembled in Lesson 7.

The temple's later history is also recorded - it was **damaged by the Tughlaq dynasty in the fourteenth century during their invasion in the Deccan** - and this is carried here only as a fact about the object's survival, with any conservation or restoration claim routed to the heritage topic.

### 4. Sculpture at Ellora, kept inside the boundary

Four sculptural facts from the Ellora caves belong to this topic, while the caves themselves belong to the architecture topic:

- among the **Vaishnavite sculptures**, the **different avatars of Vishnu** are depicted;
- one of the striking features is the **Atiriktanga-Bhairava sculpture, a skeleton form of the representation of Lord Shiva**;
- **Cave 21, Rameshwar Lena**, has **large sculptures of the goddesses Ganga and Yamuna flanking the entrance, representing the religious significance of two major Himalayan rivers**;
- **Cave 16, the Kailasa temple**, is a **two-storeyed** structure **housing a shivalingam**, and its **courtyard has two huge pillars with the flagstaff and a Nandi mandapa** - the axial bull arrangement of Lesson 7 executed by excavation rather than by construction, which is why it is the most instructive of the six Nandi cases and is taught there rather than repeated here.

The third of these is iconographically important beyond Ellora: the Nagara temple convention recorded elsewhere in the same source places **images of the river goddesses Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha**. So a river-goddess pair at an entrance is a recognisable convention rather than a local invention, and recognising a convention is always worth more than describing an instance.

The skeleton form of Shiva is worth one sentence for a different reason: it is a reminder that the iconographic system accommodated terrifying and ascetic forms as readily as benign ones, which is a useful corrective in any answer that treats Indian religious sculpture as uniformly serene.

### 5. What sculpture can and cannot evidence about society

This is the analytical heart of the lesson and it answers one of the two exact Mains demands this topic owns - the 2022 demand on medieval Indian temple sculptures as a representation of the social life of those days, reproduced exactly in the verified PYQ section.

```text
  THE EVIDENCE LADDER, FROM SAFEST TO MOST DANGEROUS

  LEVEL 1  WHAT WAS TECHNICALLY POSSIBLE                    [SAFEST]
           soapstone carved when soft and hardening later;
           lathe-turned pillars; fine-grained sandstone
           permitting deep undercutting; granite musical
           elements; cire-perdue casting
           -> evidence about MATERIALS, TOOLS and SKILL

  LEVEL 2  WHAT WAS COMMISSIONED
           six Shiva, eight Vishnu, one Ganesha, one Sun and
           four Jain temples at Khajuraho; a queen, Sembiyan
           Mahadevi, founding temples at three named sites;
           a Nandi Pavilion and kalyani noted for ceremonial use
           -> evidence about PATRON CHOICE and RITUAL PROVISION

  LEVEL 3  WHAT WAS DEPICTED
           epic scenes; mythological scenes; animals; floral
           motifs; maithuna reliefs; Shalabhanjika; a marriage
           ceremony rendered as Panigrahana in two statuettes
           -> evidence about the REPERTOIRE OF ACCEPTABLE
              SUBJECTS

  LEVEL 4  WHAT PEOPLE BELIEVED AND HOW THEY LIVED    [MOST DANGEROUS]
           -> NOT directly evidenced. A carved panel records a
              commissioned representation, not a census, a survey
              or a photograph.
```

The disciplined position, which should appear **inside** the answer rather than as an afterthought: temple sculpture is strong evidence of **what elite patrons chose to make visible** and of the **techniques and materials available to make it**; it is weak evidence of ordinary belief, ordinary behaviour or the condition of ordinary people. Two specific cautions follow. Do not use Khajuraho's erotic sculpture as a demographic or moral claim about medieval society. And do not generalise from royal and elite temples to village life, since the surviving corpus is precisely the part of the material world that was best funded and most durably built.

What can be said positively is considerable, and the best single case is the **Kalyanasundara Murti of the ninth century AD**, in which **Panigrahana, the ceremony of marriage, is represented by two separate statuettes**, with **Shiva's extended right hand accepting Parvati's right hand as she steps forward with a bashful expression**. That is a named, dated object rendering a **social ritual** with a specific gesture sequence - which evidences that the ritual existed in a recognisable form, that it was thought fit to represent, and that sculptors had a compositional convention for it. Those three claims are defensible; a fourth claim about how ordinary marriages were conducted is not.

### Examination use

This lesson owns half of the 2022 temple-sculpture demand - the reading method and the source criticism that the directive "how will you explain" actually rewards - while the register-by-register social evidence is shared with the temple-architecture topic that owns the monuments. It also supplies the regional-programme comparison that any medieval sculpture question needs, and the Hoysala material-to-style argument, which is the cleanest instance of the material thesis in the whole session. The named sculptors are the evidence to reach for in any question about artisans and artistic agency, and the evidence ladder is reusable in any question that asks what a class of artefact can prove.

### Traps introduced here

- Reducing Khajuraho to erotic sculpture; the recorded position is a variety of artwork including maithuna reliefs, on Jain as well as Hindu temples.
- Omitting the sect distribution, which is recorded precisely and disciplines generalisation.
- Explaining Hoysala detail by artistic temperament rather than by the recorded property of chloritic schist.
- Attributing the named sculptors to another dynasty; Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma are recorded for the Hoysalas.
- Treating the Thousand Pillar temple's plan, platform and sand-box foundation as this topic's material.
- Using a carved panel as a statistical or behavioural claim about medieval society.
- Generalising from royal temples to village life.
- Losing the qualifiers in the Kalyanasundara case; it is two separate statuettes, and the ritual named is Panigrahana.

### Revision notes

1. Khajuraho temples were built of fine-grained sandstone in varying shades of buff, pink and pale yellow; some are partly granite and partly sandstone.
2. Both the Hindu and Jain temples at Khajuraho feature a variety of artwork including erotic elements depicted through maithuna reliefs on the walls.
3. Sculptures at the Khajuraho monuments dedicated to Vishnu include Vyalas, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies, also found in other Indian temples.
4. The Chausath-yogini temple is the earliest building at Khajuraho, its circular open-air structure reflecting Tantric and Shaiva traditions; the class is dedicated to Yoginis, female divine figures of the Shakti and Tantric traditions.
5. Sect distribution at Khajuraho: six temples to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun, four to Jain Tirthankaras; the Matangeshvara temple remains an active site of worship.
6. Hoysala temples used chloritic schist or soapstone - soft and easy to carve when quarried, hardening over time.
7. Hoysala outer walls are covered with sculptural friezes of Ramayana and Mahabharata scenes, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs; lathe-turned pillars were used inside the mandapas.
8. Hoysala frieze bands include elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans and deities; the Nandi Pavilion and a separate kalyani are noted for ceremonial use.
9. Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma are recorded as prominent architects and sculptors of the Hoysala dynasty - rare named makers in an otherwise anonymous tradition.
10. Shalabhanjika reliefs are found in the Hoysala temples of Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura.
11. The Thousand Pillar temple at Warangal, ordered by the Kakatiya king Rudra Deva and built between the twelfth and fourteenth centuries, has one thousand richly carved pillars, perforated screens, decorative icons, rock-cut elephants and a monolithic Nandi; its three shrines are the Trikutalayam.
12. Ellora sculpture within this topic's boundary: Vaishnavite avatars of Vishnu; the Atiriktanga-Bhairava skeleton form of Shiva; large Ganga and Yamuna figures flanking the entrance of Cave 21, Rameshwar Lena.
13. Nagara convention places images of the river goddesses Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha - the Ellora pair is a convention, not a local invention.
14. The evidence ladder: technical possibility, then patron commission, then depicted repertoire, then - not directly evidenced - belief and everyday life.
15. Temple sculpture is strong evidence of patron choice and available technique, weak evidence of ordinary belief and behaviour; say so inside the answer.
16. The 9th-century Kalyanasundara Murti renders Panigrahana, the marriage ceremony, through two separate statuettes, Shiva accepting Parvati's right hand as she steps forward with a bashful expression.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate argues that Khajuraho's sculpture proves medieval Indian society was sexually liberal. Dismantle the inference and state what the same evidence does support.

**Model answer:** The inference fails at three points. First, it misdescribes the corpus: the record states that both the Hindu and Jain temples at Khajuraho feature a **variety** of artwork **including** erotic elements depicted through maithuna reliefs, so the erotic material is one register among many, alongside deities, Vyalas and the temple's wider figural programme, and the surviving temples are distributed across six Shiva, eight Vishnu, one Ganesha, one Sun and four Jain foundations. Second, it mistakes commissioned representation for social description; a temple wall records what an elite patron chose to make visible on a religious monument, which is a statement about acceptable subject matter in sacred art, not a survey of conduct. Third, it generalises from an exceptional royal and elite site to a whole society, when the surviving corpus is precisely the best-funded and most durably built part of the material world. What the evidence does support is narrower and firmer: that fine-grained sandstone permitted a dense, deeply cut figural programme; that the repertoire of subjects thought fit for temple walls was unusually wide and included erotic imagery on Jain as well as Hindu foundations; and that this breadth was itself a patron's deliberate choice, since the same dynasty funded temples of four different religious affiliations.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating sculpture as a mirror. The mirror metaphor concedes the whole argument before it starts; the accurate metaphor is a commission, in which somebody decided what would be shown, to whom, on what building, and at what cost.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Regional variation in early medieval Indian sculptural programmes is explained better by available stone and by patronage than by regional taste." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds for the programmes whose material is recorded, and the argument should be built from the quarry outwards.

Stone first. Hoysala temples used chloritic schist, or soapstone, which is soft and easy to carve when quarried but hardens over time - a property that permits both minutely cut continuous friezes and lathe-turned pillars, and then preserves them. The recorded Hoysala output follows directly: outer walls covered with friezes of Ramayana and Mahabharata scenes, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs, and ordered bands of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans and deities. Khajuraho, by contrast, used fine-grained sandstone in varying shades of buff, pink and pale yellow, which supports deep, precise relief on lofty temples raised on a high platform terrace without any enclosure wall. In Tamil temples, granite architectural elements carry the musical sculptures that are recorded as a unique southern feature.

Patronage supplies the second half. The Khajuraho foundations divide into six temples to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras, which is a record of funding decisions rather than of taste; a named Chola queen, Sembiyan Mahadevi of the tenth century AD, built temples at Kutralam, Virudhachalam and Aduthurai; and the Hoysala record even names its makers, Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma.

Two qualifications. Material constrains rather than dictates, since the same soapstone could have carried a sparser programme. And convention travels independently of both stone and patron - Shalabhanjika appears at Sanchi and Bharhut and again in the Hoysala temples of Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura.

Regional difference is therefore best read as quarry plus commission, with inherited convention running across both.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the quarry-outwards framing stated before the evidence 3 + the soapstone property argued to its recorded sculptural consequence 4 + a second and third stone each tied to a named programme 3 + the patronage evidence including a named patron and named sculptors 3 + both qualifications, constraint-not-dictation and travelling convention 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 19:** Two bodies of material remain, and they sit at opposite ends of the topic. One is the figural sculpture of a period usually said not to have any. The other is the clay and metal work still being made today, which a candidate must be able to discuss without confusing it with anything excavated.

## Lesson 19 - Mughal-period figural work, and the living clay and metal traditions

Progress: 19/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the recorded Mughal sculptural material and the tension in its opening claim; the named commissions and motifs; the craft-pottery register with its geographical indications; bronze and brass craft centres and their textual record; the archaeological-versus-craft category rule; and the honest limits of the living-tradition evidence

### Visual: two registers that must never be merged

```text
  THE ARCHAEOLOGICAL REGISTER            THE CRAFT REGISTER
  ------------------------------         ------------------------------
  Named by WARE                          Named by PLACE and PRODUCT
    Harappan Red Ware                      Khurja Pottery (GI)
    Painted Grey Ware                      Nizamabad Black Pottery (GI)
    Northern Black Polished Ware           Blue Pottery (GI), Jaipur
    Black and Red Ware                     Pokhran Pottery (GI)
    black slipped ware                     Dalgate Pottery, J&K
    plain grey ware                        Karigari Pottery, Tamil Nadu
  ------------------------------         ------------------------------
  Defined by FABRIC, FIRING,             Defined by WORKSHOP, REGION,
  DISTRIBUTION, STRATIGRAPHY             MARKET and, in several cases,
                                         a GEOGRAPHICAL INDICATION
  ------------------------------         ------------------------------
  USED TO: date a layer, name a          USED TO: identify a living
  culture, trace exchange                tradition, protect a name,
                                         sustain a livelihood
  ------------------------------         ------------------------------
  EVIDENCE TYPE: excavated               EVIDENCE TYPE: contemporary
                                         production and legal
                                         registration
  ------------------------------         ------------------------------
  THE RULE: these are two classification systems answering two
  different questions. A sentence that slides from "Northern Black
  Polished Ware" to "Nizamabad Black Pottery" has changed subject
  without saying so, and an objective item that pairs one with the
  other is testing exactly that slide.
```

*The same rule applies on the metal side: cire-perdue as an archaeologically attested technique from the Indus period is one thing; Bastar Dhokra as a registered contemporary craft is another; and the honest link between them is that the technique is recorded as still practised in many parts of the country.*

### 1. Mughal-period sculpture: the claim and the evidence that qualifies it

The section opens with a general statement and then supplies evidence that complicates it, and the correct handling is to present both.

**The general claim:** **Islamic rulers were not interested in producing figurative sculptures.**

**The evidence recorded immediately afterwards:**

| Item | As recorded |
|---|---|
| **The dynastic sequence** | **Akbar was concerned with architecture and sculpture**, and the tradition **was continued by Jahangir and Shah Jahan** |
| **The workforce** | He **summoned guilds, salats, of indigenous artisans to experiment in art**, and **a unity in the art of India was developed** |
| **Architectural sculpture** | **Animate motifs, carved in red sandstone**, can be seen in the monuments at **Agra and Fatehpur Sikri** |
| **Commissioned statues** | **Statues of Jai Mal and Fatha, Rajput heroes of Chittor, were commissioned by Akbar** |
| **Architectural animal carving** | **Beautiful stone elephant mouths were carved in the Delhi gate of the Agra fort** |
| **A named monument** | **Akbar's tomb, built in 1605-1613 by Jahangir at Sikandra**, is another fine example of exquisite Mughal sculpture |
| **Motif borrowing** | **Hindu motifs like svastika, srivatsa and lotus** are used in the **Mayura Mandapa, or Peacock Hall, of the Agra Fort** |
| **The later phase** | **Mini-monuments of marble, jalis and miniature animals** are noticeable from the time of **Shah Jahan** |

```text
  HOW TO HANDLE THE TENSION HONESTLY

  the opening general claim says          the recorded evidence shows
  Islamic rulers were NOT interested      a ruler CONCERNED WITH
  in figurative sculpture                 SCULPTURE, commissioning
                                          STATUES OF TWO NAMED HUMAN
                                          FIGURES, carving ANIMATE
                                          MOTIFS and ANIMAL FORMS, and
                                          a successor phase producing
                                          MINIATURE ANIMALS
          \                                        /
           \                                      /
            THE FORMULATION THAT FITS BOTH
  "Figural sculpture was not a central Mughal artistic programme in
   the way it was for temple traditions, and the general judgement
   in the sources reflects that. But the record is not empty:
   Akbar is described as concerned with sculpture, he summoned
   guilds of indigenous artisans, he commissioned statues of the
   Rajput heroes Jai Mal and Fatha, animate motifs in red sandstone
   appear at Agra and Fatehpur Sikri, elephant mouths were carved
   on the Delhi gate of the Agra fort, and miniature animals appear
   under Shah Jahan. The accurate statement is one of DIFFERENT
   EMPHASIS, not of absence."
```

That paragraph is worth memorising, because "the Mughals produced no sculpture" is a common and falsifiable claim, and the correction is available entirely from the owned evidence.

Two further analytical points. The **salat guilds** matter: summoning **indigenous artisans** to experiment means the carvers were drawn from the same craft population that served temple building, which is the mechanism behind the recorded development of "a unity in the art of India". And the **Hindu motifs in the Mayura Mandapa** - svastika, srivatsa and lotus - show motif transfer at the level of ornament inside an imperial palace, which is a concrete instance of that unity rather than a general assertion of it.

### 2. Living clay: the craft register, with its recorded names

The craft chapter cross-refers to the sculpture chapter explicitly - pottery work is discussed in detail there - and then records that **currently, each part of India specialises in a particular type of clay work**:

| Craft pottery | Place of origin |
|---|---|
| **Khurja Pottery (GI)** | **Bulandshahr district, Uttar Pradesh** |
| **Nizamabad Black Pottery (GI)** | **Azamgarh district, Uttar Pradesh** |
| **Blue Pottery (GI)** | **Jaipur, Rajasthan** |
| **Pokhran Pottery (GI)** | **Pokhran, Rajasthan** |
| **Dalgate Pottery** | **Jammu and Kashmir** |
| **Karigari Pottery** | **Tamil Nadu** |

A disclosure that directly affects what may be written. The **features column of this table is clipped** in the text available for this session: the entries break off after "Colourf...", "Dark sh...", "Made o...", "Used fo...", "Uses a..." and "Specia...". The names, the geographical indications and the places of origin are legible and are reproduced above; the feature descriptions are not, and are not supplied. Four of the six carry a **GI** marking in the source, and two - Dalgate and Karigari - do not; that difference is reproduced as printed rather than harmonised.

To this add two further recorded facts about living clay work: **Gundiyali and Lodai** are recorded as **famous pottery-making villages of Kutch, Gujarat**; and in the regional architecture of Bengal, **burnt bricks and clay, known as terracotta bricks, were the principal building material used** - the point at which clay stops being an object and becomes a building.

**A reproduced Mains demand and how to handle it.** The core source's list of previous Main Examination questions includes a 2011 demand asking about the major different styles of **unglazed** pottery making in India, reproduced exactly in the verified PYQ section. The owned evidence supports a partial, honest answer and not a complete one: it supplies **named centres with their regions and their GI status**, it supplies **village-level pottery production in Kutch**, and it supplies the **archaeological background** of hand-made, slow-wheel and fast-wheel forming. What it does **not** supply, because the feature column is clipped, is a classification of these centres by glazing technique. The disciplined answer therefore names the centres and the mechanism - local clay, hereditary artisan communities, regional forms, festival and domestic demand, and geographical-indication protection - and states plainly where a technique classification would require evidence not held. That is a stronger response than a confident glazed-versus-unglazed table built from memory.

### 3. Living metal: the craft register, and the textual record of the technique

The bronze material begins with the continuity claim and its evidence: bronze work is **one of the oldest art forms, as evinced by the bronze statue of a Dancing Girl from Mohenjo-daro, which has been dated to around 2500 BC**. The composition is recorded too: the **earliest non-ferrous metals used by man were copper and tin, which were mixed to form bronze.**

Then the textual record, which is this section's most under-used material: **the Matsya Purana describes bronze casting methods, while texts like the Rasaratnakara discuss metal purity and zinc distillation.**

The regional centres are recorded as follows:

| Region or craft | Recorded content |
|---|---|
| **Uttar Pradesh** | **Takes precedence** among bronze-craft producing areas, with major centres at **Etawah, Sitapur, Varanasi and Moradabad**; these produce **decorative items such as flower pots and images of gods and goddesses**, and are famous for ritual objects - **tamrapatra, kanchantal and panchpatra** |
| **Tamil Nadu** | Known for **statues inspired by Pallava, Chola, Pandya and Nayaka art** |
| **Bastar Dhokra (GI)** | Recorded among current important centres of brass work, **used for brass ornaments** |
| **Pembarthi metal craft (GI)** | **Exquisite sheet metal, brass, art to adorn chariots and temples** |
| **Pahaldar Lamps** | **Copper and brass lamps in different styles and shapes** |
| **Gaja Tandava** | **Shiva dancing in the Tandava posture** - recorded in the brass-work list |
| **Rare Jain imagery and icons** | Recorded as reflecting **the presence of ancient Jain pilgrimage centres in Karnataka** |
| **Other metal crafts** | Casting in **iron, copper and bell metal**; the **Marori work of Rajasthan**, which uses metal to create **etchings on the base metal before filling the gaps with black lac**; and the general techniques of **repousse, embossing, for raised designs** and **engraving for cut-line patterns** |

Three observations convert this list into argument.

- **The Tamil Nadu entry closes a loop opened in Lesson 17.** Contemporary statues are recorded as **inspired by Pallava, Chola, Pandya and Nayaka art** - which is a living workshop tradition explicitly referring itself to the medieval schools taught earlier in this session. That is the cleanest available evidence of continuity, and it is continuity of *model and technique*, not of ritual context.
- **The Gaja Tandava entry closes another.** A brass-work item named for Shiva dancing in the Tandava posture is the Nataraja type persisting as a craft product, and it should be described exactly that way - a type persisting in a craft register - rather than as "the Chola bronze tradition surviving", which would blur an object class with a dynastic school.
- **Geographical indications are the modern mechanism** by which a craft name is tied to a place. Four pottery entries and two metal entries carry a GI marking in the source. That is a fact about legal protection of craft identity and it is the right place to stop, since GI law, its administration and any claim about registration status belong outside this topic.

### 4. The prohibition that keeps this section honest

The canonical owner records an explicit limit and it is reproduced here because it is the single easiest place in the topic to fabricate: **no source in the owned evidence names Bankura horses, Molela plaques, or any specific present-day potter community as a terracotta tradition.** Those must not be supplied as facts.

What *is* supported for continuity is exactly three things:

1. the **cire-perdue technique**, recorded as **still practised in many parts of the country today**;
2. **Gundiyali and Lodai in Kutch, Gujarat**, recorded as famous **pottery-making villages** - pottery, not figurative terracotta, and the distinction must be kept;
3. the **Bengal terracotta-brick** building tradition.

If a question demands living terracotta centres by name, write the **mechanism** - hereditary artisan communities, local clay, shrine and festival demand, geographical-indication-type protection - and say plainly that named centres lie outside the verified evidence here, rather than inventing them.

### Examination use

The Mughal material answers any demand on sculpture under the Mughals and, more valuably, supplies a correction to a common overstatement - which is exactly the kind of move that distinguishes a script. The craft registers answer the reproduced 2011 unglazed-pottery demand as far as the evidence honestly allows, and they supply India-specific, contemporary examples for any question about traditional crafts, livelihoods or cultural continuity. The Matsya Purana and Rasaratnakara references let a cultural answer reach into the history of technology. The archaeological-versus-craft rule is directly testable in objective form and is the single most likely place for a well-prepared candidate to be caught out.

### Traps introduced here

- Stating flatly that the Mughals produced no sculpture, when the same passage records Akbar as concerned with sculpture and names two commissioned statues.
- Omitting the salat guilds of indigenous artisans, which are the mechanism behind the recorded unity in the art of India.
- Misdating Akbar's tomb; it is recorded as built in 1605-1613 by Jahangir at Sikandra.
- Assigning the marble mini-monuments, jalis and miniature animals to Akbar; they are recorded from the time of Shah Jahan.
- Completing the clipped feature descriptions of the craft-pottery table.
- Adding a GI marking to Dalgate or Karigari pottery, which the source does not mark.
- Naming Bankura, Molela or any other living terracotta centre as verified evidence.
- Describing Gundiyali and Lodai as figurative terracotta centres; they are recorded as pottery-making villages.
- Merging an archaeological ware with a craft pottery in one sequence.

### Revision notes

1. The recorded general claim is that Islamic rulers were not interested in producing figurative sculptures; the evidence that follows qualifies it.
2. Akbar was concerned with architecture and sculpture, and the tradition was continued by Jahangir and Shah Jahan.
3. He summoned guilds, salats, of indigenous artisans to experiment in art, and a unity in the art of India was developed.
4. Animate motifs carved in red sandstone appear in the monuments at Agra and Fatehpur Sikri.
5. Statues of Jai Mal and Fatha, Rajput heroes of Chittor, were commissioned by Akbar.
6. Beautiful stone elephant mouths were carved in the Delhi gate of the Agra fort.
7. Akbar's tomb, built in 1605-1613 by Jahangir at Sikandra, is recorded as a fine example of exquisite Mughal sculpture.
8. Hindu motifs such as svastika, srivatsa and lotus are used in the Mayura Mandapa or Peacock Hall of the Agra Fort.
9. Mini-monuments of marble, jalis and miniature animals are noticeable from the time of Shah Jahan.
10. Craft pottery: Khurja Pottery (GI) of Bulandshahr, Uttar Pradesh; Nizamabad Black Pottery (GI) of Azamgarh, Uttar Pradesh; Blue Pottery (GI) of Jaipur; Pokhran Pottery (GI) of Pokhran; Dalgate Pottery of Jammu and Kashmir; Karigari Pottery of Tamil Nadu. The feature column is clipped and is not reconstructed.
11. Gundiyali and Lodai are famous pottery-making villages of Kutch, Gujarat; in Bengal, burnt bricks and clay known as terracotta bricks were the principal building material.
12. Bronze is one of the oldest art forms, evinced by the Mohenjo-daro Dancing Girl dated to around 2500 BC; the earliest non-ferrous metals used were copper and tin, mixed to form bronze.
13. The Matsya Purana describes bronze casting methods; texts like the Rasaratnakara discuss metal purity and zinc distillation.
14. Uttar Pradesh takes precedence in bronze craft, with centres at Etawah, Sitapur, Varanasi and Moradabad producing flower pots, images of gods and goddesses, and the ritual objects tamrapatra, kanchantal and panchpatra; Tamil Nadu is known for statues inspired by Pallava, Chola, Pandya and Nayaka art.
15. Brass work includes Bastar Dhokra (GI) for brass ornaments, Pembarthi metal craft (GI) in sheet brass to adorn chariots and temples, Pahaldar Lamps in copper and brass, the Gaja Tandava showing Shiva dancing in the Tandava posture, and rare Jain imagery reflecting ancient Jain pilgrimage centres in Karnataka.
16. Other metal crafts: casting in iron, copper and bell metal; the Marori work of Rajasthan, etching the base metal and filling the gaps with black lac; repousse for raised designs and engraving for cut-line patterns.
17. Verified continuity is limited to three things: cire-perdue still practised in many parts of the country, the Kutch pottery villages, and the Bengal terracotta-brick tradition. Named living terracotta centres such as Bankura or Molela are not supported here and must not be supplied.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks about the major different styles of pottery making in India today. Outline what the owned evidence allows you to write, and state precisely where you must stop and why.

**Model answer:** The evidence supports a centre-by-centre answer with regions and legal status. Khurja Pottery, carrying a geographical indication, comes from Bulandshahr district in Uttar Pradesh; Nizamabad Black Pottery, also GI-marked, from Azamgarh district in the same state; Blue Pottery, GI-marked, from Jaipur in Rajasthan; Pokhran Pottery, GI-marked, from Pokhran in the same state; Dalgate Pottery from Jammu and Kashmir; and Karigari Pottery from Tamil Nadu, with the last two not carrying a GI marking in the source. To these can be added Gundiyali and Lodai in Kutch, Gujarat, recorded as famous pottery-making villages, and the Bengal tradition in which burnt bricks and clay known as terracotta bricks were the principal building material. The mechanism can also be written: local clay, hereditary artisan communities, regional forms, domestic and ritual demand, and the tying of a craft name to a place through a geographical indication. Where I must stop is any description of the techniques that distinguish these styles, including any classification by glazing, because the feature column of the source table is clipped after a few characters in every row. Stating a glazing technique for a named centre would therefore be an invention rather than a citation, and a question that turns on glazed against unglazed would have to be answered with the centres, the mechanism and an honest statement that the technical classification is not carried in the evidence held.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that an incomplete source licenses a confident reconstruction. In a craft question the specific technical claim - what clay, what glaze, what firing - is exactly what an examiner can check, and it is the one thing that must never be supplied from impression.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Indian metal and clay traditions show continuity of technique without continuity of meaning." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The distinction is exact, and both halves can be evidenced.

Technical continuity is directly recorded. The cire-perdue or lost-wax method - wax model, wet clay coat, heating so the wax melts, the wax poured out through a tiny hole, liquid metal poured into the hollow mould, the clay coat removed after cooling - was known from the time of the Indus Valley culture and is recorded as still practised in many parts of the country today. The Mohenjo-daro Dancing Girl, dated to around 2500 BC, stands at one end of that line and the contemporary Tamil Nadu workshops, known for statues inspired by Pallava, Chola, Pandya and Nayaka art, at the other. The technique also has a textual record: the Matsya Purana describes bronze casting methods and texts like the Rasaratnakara discuss metal purity and zinc distillation. On the clay side, terracotta runs from pinched Harappan figures through moulded Gupta plaques at Bikaner and Ahichchhatra to Bengal's terracotta bricks and to living pottery villages at Gundiyali and Lodai in Kutch.

Meaning, however, does not transfer. A Harappan bronze whose script is undeciphered, a processional Chola icon housed in a natana-sabha, and a brass Gaja Tandava sold as craft belong to three different systems of use, patronage and belief, even where the casting sequence is identical.

Two qualifications. Some meaning does persist as model: contemporary statues explicitly cite medieval schools. And the craft register is legally and economically distinct, with geographical indications tying names such as Khurja, Nizamabad Black, Blue, Pokhran and Bastar Dhokra to places - a modern form of identity with no ancient counterpart.

Technique therefore travels; meaning is remade at each stage.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the technique-versus-meaning distinction stated precisely 3 + the full cire-perdue sequence with its recorded antiquity and survival 4 + the clay continuity from pinched to moulded to architectural to living 3 + the three-different-systems argument for discontinuity of meaning 3 + both qualifications, model persistence and the GI register 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 20:** One question has been deferred throughout: how much of what has been taught depends on names and dates that could be wrong, and what happens when the object itself has been moved? The final lesson handles attribution, provenance and status, records the topic's single current linkage, and turns everything into an answer.

## Lesson 20 - Attribution, provenance and status, and building the answer
