━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four kinds of claim about an object, ranked by evidential weight

```text
  STRONGEST
     |
   1. INSCRIPTIONAL          text on or with the object naming it
      e.g. the headless Mathura figure, whose Brahmi base
      inscription declares it to be Kanishka, the great
      Kushana king
     |
   2. EXCAVATIONAL /         a recorded find-context, excavator
      ARCHAEOLOGICAL         and date
      e.g. the earliest known Nataraja, excavated at the
      Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole; the Lion Capital excavated
      by F. O. Oertel in 1904-05; Amaravati first excavated
      by Sir Walter Elliot in 1845
     |
   3. TECHNICAL /            material, finish, technique or a
      TYPOLOGICAL            datable device
      e.g. the Mauryan polish; the ring-stirrup, which appears
      only from the post-Gupta period onward; the Gandhara
      material sequence ending in grey stone from the 3rd
      century AD
     |
   4. STYLISTIC /            "it looks like" - resemblance to a
      IMPRESSIONISTIC        school or period
      e.g. the arguments for and against a Mauryan date for
      the Didarganj figure
     |
  WEAKEST - and the level at which almost every contested
  attribution in this topic actually sits
```

*The ranking is the lesson. A claim at level one cannot be overturned by a claim at level four; a claim at level four should never be written in the register of a claim at level one.*

### 1. The complete list of contested attributions in this topic

These are the points at which a confident sentence becomes a wrong sentence. Each is stated with its safe formulation.

| Contested point | The unsafe claim | The safe formulation |
|---|---|---|
| **Harappan identities** | "The Pashupati seal shows Shiva"; "the Mother Goddess proves a fertility cult"; "the Priest-King was a ruler" | Modern labels for described objects; the script on the seals is undeciphered, and the source's own hedges - "probably worshipped for prosperity", "might also have been a goddess of fertility sects" - must be kept |
| **Didarganj chronology** | "The Didarganj Yakshi is a Mauryan work" | A chauri or whisk bearer recorded as belonging to the Mauryan period, whose polish supports that attribution, while stylistic and chronological arguments for a later date exist |
| **First human Buddha image** | "Gandhara made the first Buddha image" | Anthropomorphic imagery emerged in broadly overlapping early-centuries-CE contexts at Gandhara and Mathura under Kushana patronage; priority is debated; what is certain is that the human form under Gandhara transformed Indian sculptural narrative |
| **Sultanganj Buddha** | "A Gupta-period bronze" | A 7.5 ft copper sculpture from Bihar, standard example of the school's metal work, whose c. seventh-century date places it at the late or post-Gupta end |
| **Nataraja origin** | "The Cholas created the Nataraja" | The earliest known example was excavated at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole under early Chalukya rule; the form reached its peak under the Cholas |
| **NBPW attribution** | "Mauryan pottery" full stop | A fine lustrous tableware, often black or steel-grey but also other shades, associated especially with early historic urbanisation and best known from Mauryan-period levels at seven named sites |
| **Black and Red Ware** | "Harappan painted pottery is Black and Red Ware" | The description - red ground, glossy black designs - is recorded, and the phrase is used in the source; it also names a separate wider ceramic category attested elsewhere, including among wares coexisting with Painted Grey Ware |
| **Gupta "classical era" and Ellora** | "Ellora is Gupta" | The "classical" label is offered in quotation marks as a judgement about an idiom; the same source dates the Ellora murals to the sixth-ninth centuries under the Rashtrakutas and other dynasties |
| **Empty seat reference** | "The empty seat represents [one named event]" | A presence-marker; the source's table places the empty throne under the Great Renunciation while its summary lists it among four devices covering renunciation, enlightenment, first sermon and death; the surrounding elements disambiguate |
| **Yali anatomy** | "The correct description is X and the other is wrong" | Two formulations exist in the same source; state the composite, lion-based, elephant-tusked core |
| **Mughal figural sculpture** | "The Mughals produced no sculpture" | A matter of different emphasis rather than absence; Akbar is recorded as concerned with sculpture, commissioned statues of Jai Mal and Fatha, and animate motifs and carved elephant mouths are recorded |
| **Living terracotta centres** | Naming Bankura, Molela or any present-day potter community | Not carried in the owned evidence; write the mechanism instead and say so |

### 2. Provenance: an object's recoverable history

Provenance is the record of where an object was, and with whom, from its making to the present. This topic supplies one worked example, and it is unusually complete.

```text
  THE AMARAVATI PROVENANCE CHAIN, AS RECORDED

  made       under Satavahana patronage, with the site flourishing as
             a major trade centre between the 3rd century BC and the
             12th century AD; sculptures in greenish-white limestone
                |
  ruin       "the Mahachaitya later fell into ruins and completely
             lost its glory"
                |
  1797       Colonel Colin Mackenzie visits the place, leading to the
             rediscovery of the stupa
                |
  1845       first excavation by Sir Walter Elliot, who collected what
             is known as the AMARAVATI MARBLES, later famous as the
             ELLIOT MARBLES, for sculptures kept in the BRITISH MUSEUM
                |
  1887       James Burgess visits and notes findings about the stupa
                |
  present    the Archaeological Survey of India has conserved the site,
             and a replica of the stupa has been created near the
             original location
```

Read the chain for what it demonstrates rather than for its individual dates. An object's history did not stop when it was made. Rediscovery, excavation, removal, display and replication are all events in that history, and they determine what a student can see, where, and in what condition. That is why provenance belongs in an art-history topic and not only in a law or heritage topic.

Two disciplines. The chain above is recorded as fact; **no claim about present legal status, ownership or restitution is made from it**, because those belong to the heritage topic. And the word "believed" in the Mahachaitya's recorded height must survive any citation of that figure.

### 3. The single current linkage for this topic

This is the one place in the session where a contemporary item is used, and it is used because it belongs precisely to this lesson's subject - attribution, provenance and the movement of sculpted objects.

**The item.** *The New Indian Express*, in a report dated **14 May 2026**, records that the Union Ministry of Culture reclaimed three antiquities that had been part of the collection at the Smithsonian's National Museum of Asian Art in the United States, and that had been **illegally taken away from temples in Tamil Nadu decades ago**. The three objects are all **bronze castings**: a **Chola-period "Shiva Nataraja"**; **"Saint Sundarar with Paravai"**, of the **sixteenth century, Vijayanagara period**; and **"Somaskanda - Shiva and Uma"**, of the **twelfth century, Chola period**. The report states that the latter two **reached New Delhi on Tuesday night**, while the **Shiva Nataraja will return in 2028**, since it is displayed in an ongoing exhibition at the United States museum and the government agreed to leave it there until then as a **long-term loan**. Announcing the repatriation, the culture minister is reported as saying that the **Archaeological Survey of India had traced the three artefacts**, and that **after thorough provenance research using archival records, field documentation and photographs of the temple taken in the 1950s and 1960s, it was established that they were taken out of the country in an unauthorised manner**. The retrieved idols are to be placed in the **Gallery of Retrieved Antiquities at the Red Fort**.

**Why it belongs in this lesson, stated exactly.** It is a direct, dated illustration of the method taught here.

| What the report records | Which part of this session it illustrates |
|---|---|
| Three **bronze** objects, one a **Nataraja** | The cire-perdue tradition of Lesson 2 and the Nataraja type of Lesson 17 - and the portability that Lesson 2 identified as a consequence of casting |
| Two Chola-period objects and one Vijayanagara-period object, each dated to a century | The origin-and-period discipline of Lesson 17: the type persists across dynasties, and each object carries its own date |
| Provenance established using **archival records, field documentation and photographs of the temple** from the 1950s and 1960s | The attribution ladder of this lesson: documentary and photographic evidence of an original location is stronger than stylistic resemblance |
| The objects are described as having been **removed from temples** | The placement point running through Lessons 15 to 17: these were **installed, worshipped images**, not gallery objects, and their meaning is bound to a sanctum |

**The limits observed here.** The item is attributed to a dated newspaper report of a ministerial announcement. No primary government document was opened for this session. Nothing is asserted about the wider legal framework of restitution, about the number of antiquities returned in any period, about any institution's policy, or about the present condition or display arrangements of any object; all of that belongs to the heritage topic. And no other current item is used anywhere in this session - the remaining nineteen lessons record truthfully that no current linkage is required.

### 4. The demand map: what a question is really asking

| If the question says | It is really testing | Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Highlight the central Asian and Greco-Bactrian elements in Gandhara art* | Named elements sorted into two distinct external strands, with the Indian core identified | "Greek influence" restated six times |
| *Gandhara sculpture owed as much to the Romans as to the Greeks* | Whether you can separate Greek, Roman and Parthian contributions and handle the drapery attribution that spans them | A general essay on foreign influence |
| *Compare the Gandhara, Mathura and Amaravati schools* | Comparison along named axes - influence, material, scope, treatment, patronage, diffusion | Three descriptive paragraphs with no axis |
| *Discuss the significance of animal figures in Indian art* | Symbol, then context, then function, across religions and periods, with the change over time | A list of animals with one-word meanings |
| *Medieval temple sculpture as evidence of social life* | Commissioned representation read carefully, with the evidence ladder made explicit | Treating carvings as photographs |
| *Explain the iconography of a named image* | The systematic attribute-by-attribute method, as applied to the Nataraja | Generic admiration |
| *Trace the shift from symbolic to human representation of the Buddha* | The aniconic sign set, the change in devotional practice, and the bracketed priority question | "Gandhara made the first Buddha image" |
| *Styles of pottery making in India* | Named centres, regions, protection mechanism, and the archaeological-versus-craft distinction | A merged sequence running from Harappan ware to modern craft |
| *Sculpture and technique* | Material determining what carving or casting can achieve | Ignoring material entirely |

### 5. Five qualified theses, each reusable

- **The method thesis, the most transferable.** Any Indian sculpture can be read through six variables - material, posture and gesture, iconographic attribute, patron, site context and cross-site comparison - and an answer that applies them in order will outperform one that describes appearance.
- **The influence thesis.** Gandhara is best described as a Buddhist iconographic programme executed in a Hellenised visual idiom under Kushana patronage: the subject is Indian, the surface treatment is layered and partly borrowed, and calling it "Greek art" inverts the relationship.
- **The parallel-development thesis.** Anthropomorphic Buddha imagery emerged in broadly overlapping early-centuries-CE contexts at both Gandhara and Mathura; priority is disputed, and the exam-safe position is parallel development rather than a single origin.
- **The peak-not-origin thesis.** Chola bronzes perfected rather than invented the Nataraja: the earliest known example is at Aihole under early Chalukya rule, and what the Cholas contributed was the canonical, portable, ritually processional form.
- **The material thesis.** Material is not a background detail: cire-perdue bronze permits the free limbs and open silhouette of the Nataraja, soapstone permits the covered friezes and lathe-turned pillars of Hoysala temples, and fine-grained sandstone permits the deep undercutting of Khajuraho's figures - technique makes the aesthetic possible.

### 6. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis, then the school or image identified, then two or three named attributes or features with their meanings, then one comparison, then one caution, then a verdict |
| **15** | Thesis, then the six-variable method named, then two schools compared on material, influence and scope, then one worked iconographic example, then a chronological correction - Aihole against Chola, or Gandhara against Mathura - then a verdict |
| **20** | All of the above, plus the pottery or material-culture sequence where relevant, plus the aniconic-to-iconic shift, plus the social-history reading with its limits, plus an attribution or provenance caution, then a verdict answering the exact wording |

### 7. The complete risk list

Do not, anywhere:

- treat Pashupati, Mother Goddess or Priest-King as decoded identities, or drop the source's hedges;
- place a lion or a cow on a Harappan seal;
- state that Chola patronage originated Nataraja iconography;
- date the Didarganj figure firmly to the Mauryan period, or the Sultanganj Buddha firmly to the Gupta period;
- claim that Gandhara or Mathura produced the first human Buddha image;
- reduce Gandhara to "Greek art", or attribute foreign influence to Amaravati;
- narrate a Nandi, Rudra-bull or "bull of dharma" myth, or add meanings to the Sarnath abacus animals beyond the four recorded;
- date the Rampurva bull pillar apart from Ashoka;
- present either recorded Yali anatomy as an error;
- invent sculpture heights, weights, alloy compositions, pillar counts, firing temperatures or museum accession details;
- complete a clipped source column - the pottery features, the third school column, the Tirthankara list beyond the sixth entry, the Vajra mudra meaning, or the craft-pottery feature descriptions;
- name a living terracotta centre, community or product as verified;
- make a legal, ownership, policy or restitution claim, or state any current conservation, display or institutional status beyond the single dated report recorded in this lesson;
- merge an archaeological ware with a craft pottery, or an iconographic type with an individual object.

### Examination use

This lesson is the control layer for the whole topic, and its risk list is what converts knowledge into marks by preventing forfeitures. The demand map decides which of the preceding nineteen lessons a question actually wants; the theses supply openings and verdicts; the mark-scaled structures decide what fits. The attribution ladder is also independently examinable wherever a question asks how art-historical claims are established, and the provenance chain is the topic's best worked example of an object's post-manufacture history.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing a stylistic judgement in the register of an inscriptional fact.
- Treating a contested date as settled because one formulation is more commonly repeated.
- Turning a provenance record into a legal or restitution argument.
- Citing a current item without its date and its source, or extending it into policy claims it does not make.
- Choosing a thesis that the question's directive does not support - for example, offering the parallel-development thesis to a question about material and technique.

### Revision notes

1. Four kinds of attribution claim, in descending evidential weight: inscriptional, excavational, technical or typological, and stylistic.
2. The headless Mathura figure is the topic's model of an inscriptional claim; the Didarganj dating debate is its model of a stylistic one.
3. Contested points to handle with a safe formulation: Harappan identities, Didarganj chronology, first human Buddha image, Sultanganj date, Nataraja origin, NBPW attribution, Black and Red Ware, the Gupta "classical" label and Ellora, the empty seat's reference, the two Yali anatomies, Mughal figural sculpture, living terracotta centres.
4. Provenance is an object's recoverable history from manufacture to the present, and rediscovery, excavation, removal, display and replication are all events in it.
5. The Amaravati chain: Satavahana manufacture; ruin; Mackenzie's visit in 1797 leading to rediscovery; first excavation by Sir Walter Elliot in 1845, who collected the Amaravati or Elliot marbles for sculptures kept in the British Museum; James Burgess's findings in 1887; ASI conservation and a replica near the original location.
6. The single current linkage, from a report dated 14 May 2026: three bronzes from the Smithsonian's National Museum of Asian Art, illegally taken from Tamil Nadu temples decades ago - a Chola Shiva Nataraja, a sixteenth-century Vijayanagara "Saint Sundarar with Paravai" and a twelfth-century Chola "Somaskanda - Shiva and Uma"; two reached New Delhi, the Nataraja returns in 2028 under a long-term loan; the ASI traced them through archival records, field documentation and 1950s-1960s temple photographs; the objects go to the Gallery of Retrieved Antiquities at the Red Fort.
7. The current item illustrates portability as a consequence of casting, period-specific dating within a persisting type, documentary provenance outranking style, and the fact that these were installed worshipped images.
8. Conservation policy, restitution law, institutional structure and any status claim beyond that dated report belong to the heritage topic.
9. The demand map: nine question shapes, each with what it is really testing and what must not be written.
10. Five reusable theses: the method thesis, the influence thesis, the parallel-development thesis, the peak-not-origin thesis and the material thesis.
11. Mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20 marks, with the pottery sequence, the aniconic shift, the social-history limits and the attribution caution added only at 20.
12. The risk list is a list of forfeitures; avoiding all of it is worth more than any single additional fact.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the four-level attribution ladder would be applied to decide between two competing dates for a sculpture, and illustrate with a case from this topic.

**Model answer:** The ladder ranks claims by the kind of evidence behind them, and a lower-level claim cannot overturn a higher-level one. At level one, an inscription on or with the object settles the question, as the Brahmi text on the base of the headless Mathura figure settles that it represents Kanishka. At level two, a recorded find-context, excavator and date place the object, as with the earliest known Nataraja excavated at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole, or the Lion Capital excavated by F. O. Oertel in 1904-05. At level three, a technical or typological feature supplies a boundary: the Mauryan polish, the Gandhara material sequence ending in a complete shift to grey stone from the third century AD, or the ring-stirrup, which appears in sculpture only from the post-Gupta period onward and therefore fixes a lower limit for any relief showing one. At level four, stylistic resemblance is the weakest evidence and is where most disputes actually sit. The Didarganj whisk-bearer is the case. It is recorded as belonging to the Mauryan period and its high polish is a level-three argument for that attribution; but stylistic and chronological arguments for a later date exist, and they too are level-four or level-three claims. With no inscription and no decisive excavational record, neither side can be settled, so the disciplined answer states the conventional attribution, states the technical argument for it, names the counter-argument and stops.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the more frequently repeated date is the better-evidenced one. Repetition is not evidence; it usually reflects which formulation entered textbooks first, which is exactly why the ladder asks what kind of claim is being made rather than how often it is made.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Attribution, not aesthetics, is the central problem of Indian art history." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition overstates a genuine insight, and the examination is worth conducting carefully.

Its strength is that attribution determines what any aesthetic judgement is a judgement about. In this topic the most famous objects carry modern labels rather than identifications: the seal called Pashupati, the figure called the Mother Goddess, the bearded man called the Priest-King - all described precisely, none read, because the pictographic script on the Harappan seals remains undeciphered. Dates are contested where it matters most: the Didarganj whisk-bearer's polish supports a Mauryan attribution while stylistic arguments for a later date persist; the 7.5 ft copper Sultanganj Buddha sits at the late or post-Gupta end; and priority between Gandhara and Mathura for the first anthropomorphic Buddha image is unresolved. Where attribution is secure it transforms the evidence, as the Brahmi inscription naming Kanishka on the base of the headless Mathura figure shows. Provenance compounds the problem: the Amaravati sculptures pass from ruin, to Mackenzie's visit of 1797, to Elliot's excavation of 1845 and the removal of the marbles.

Its weakness is that aesthetics is not merely decorative in this tradition. The sources themselves make formal judgements that carry argument - the Parkham Yaksha "speaks of massive strength and gravity in imagery" - and the iconographic system is aesthetic and semantic at once, since the Nataraja's meaning is delivered entirely through form.

Two qualifications complete the picture. Technique often settles what style cannot, as the ring-stirrup's post-Gupta appearance shows. And attribution problems are unevenly distributed, being severe for Harappan and early material and slight for inscribed or well-excavated objects.

Attribution is therefore the prior problem rather than the only one, and the mature position treats the two as dependent rather than rival.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the prior-problem framing, distinguished from "the only problem" 3 + four contested attributions each stated with its safe formulation 6 + one secure inscriptional case and the contrast it makes 3 + the provenance dimension with its recorded chain 3 + the counter-case that aesthetics carries argument, with a sourced formal judgement 3 + both qualifications 2.**

## The exact owned Mains questions

### 2022 - lion and bull figures

> **Discuss the significance of the lion and bull figures in Indian mythology, art and architecture.**
>
> *(CSE, 2022, reproduced from the core art source's list of previous years' Main Examination questions.)*

**Ledger record.** General Studies Paper I, question 13; directive **Discuss the significance**; **15 marks**; **250 words**.

**Directive.** *Discuss the significance* - not describe, not list. The demand is for what the figures *did*, across three named domains, and the marks lie in keeping the domains apart.

**Conceptual demand.** Three legs, three kinds of evidence: **mythology** is textual and devotional association; **art** is sculpture, seal, relief and symbol; **architecture** is structural and programmatic placement. An answer that merges them into "animals were important in Indian culture" scores as a list.

**Answer approach.** Open with the two-functions thesis of Lesson 7 - the bull attached to birth, generative power and service to the deity, the lion to authority, doctrine and guardianship. Run the mythology leg briefly and only where sourced: the bull as the Taurus sign of the Buddha's birth month and as the vahana placed before the sanctum; the lion as the attainment of enlightenment and as the Yali of Tamil literature, a guardian protecting human beings physically and spiritually. Run the art leg chronologically: the humped bull frequent on Harappan seals with the lion absent, and the bronze bull of Kalibangan; then the aniconic code where bull marks birth and lion marks sermon; then the capital programme read as a repeated imperial type with local variation - Sarnath's four lions over a circular abacus of four directional animals and the Dharmachakra, Sanchi's four lions of around 250 BC over a flatter abacus of alternating geese and flame palmettes carrying the Schism Edict and a later Gupta Sankha Lipi inscription, and Vaishali's single lion with no abacus; then Rampurva's inscribed lion pillar and uninscribed bull pillar as the single controlled comparison. Run the architecture leg as placement, choosing two or three of the six Nandi cases rather than listing all: Thanjavur's mandapa monolith recorded as the second largest and Lepakshi's as the largest; Ellora Cave 16, where the same axial arrangement is excavated rather than built, with a Nandi mandapa and two flagstaff pillars in the courtyard of a two-storeyed temple housing a shivalingam; the Thousand Pillar temple's monolith and Ramappa's main temple standing in front of the Nandi Pavilion; and the Hoysala pavilion with its kalyani, noted for ceremonial use - against the lion hybridised as Yali and Vyala and multiplied on gopurams, columns and friezes. Close with change over time and with the qualification that symbol meanings are reconstructed from context and from later texts, and that the Harappan corpus cannot be read through later categories. No model answer is supplied here.

### 2022 - medieval temple sculpture as social evidence

> **Medieval Indian temple sculptures represent the social life of those days. How will you explain this statement?**
>
> *(CSE, 2022, General Studies Paper I. Wording carried from the local routing and audit records; no official 2022 paper is held locally, so the reproduction is recorded with that provenance.)*

**Ledger record.** General Studies Paper I, question 1; directive **How will you explain**; **10 marks**; **150 words**.

**Directive.** *How will you explain* is a demand for a **method of inference**, not for a catalogue of carvings. It is asking what entitles you to move from a carved surface to a social claim.

**Conceptual demand.** The evidence ladder of Lesson 18: what was technically possible, what was commissioned, what was depicted, and - not directly evidenced - what people believed and how they lived.

**Answer approach.** State the inference rule first: sculpture is commissioned representation, strong evidence of what patrons chose to make visible and of available technique, weak evidence of ordinary belief and behaviour. Then evidence each safe level with named material: soapstone and fine-grained sandstone determining what could be cut; the Khajuraho sect distribution and Sembiyan Mahadevi's foundations as commissions; the Hoysala frieze repertoire of epic scenes, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs, the maithuna reliefs on both Hindu and Jain temples, Shalabhanjika across three religions, and the ninth-century Kalyanasundara Murti rendering the marriage ceremony in two statuettes. Close by naming what the evidence cannot support - demographic or moral claims, and generalisation from royal temples to village life. At 150 words the method must occupy roughly a third of the answer. The register-by-register social bank is shared with the temple-architecture topic. No model answer is supplied here.

### 2019 - Gandhara's external elements

> **Highlight the central Asian and Greco-Bactrian elements in Gandhara art.**
>
> *(CSE, 2019, reproduced from the core art source's list of previous years' Main Examination questions. Note the printed lower-case "central Asian" and reproduce it as printed when quoting.)*

**Ledger record.** General Studies Paper I, question 1; directive **Highlight**; **10 marks**; **150 words**.

**Directive.** *Highlight* demands named elements, each attributed to the right source of influence. The commonest failure is to answer "Greek influence" six times.

**Conceptual demand.** Two distinct external strands must be separated by name, and the Indian core must be identified so that the list becomes an argument.

**Answer approach.** Use the Lesson 9 sorting. Central Asian: the strand's three peoples as the owner names them - **Kushana, Parthian and Saka**, with the Saka named and nothing further claimed, since no Saka-specific feature or date is recorded; Bactria between today's Afghanistan and Tajikistan; Kushana rule bringing major upliftment to the Buddha's portrayal; Kanishka's territory from Mathura through Gandhara and Bactria to the borders of China; royal portraits alongside religious themes; the material sequence from blue schist and green phyllite to stucco to wholly grey stone from the third century AD; and the fixing of iconographic conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras. Greco-Bactrian or Hellenistic: anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting; Usha-Nisha wavy hair; a slender muscled body on the fit-athlete model; elongated earlobes symbolising royal connection; Apollo as an occasional model. State that the heavily pleated garments are attributed to Greek and Roman and also Parthian inspiration, which is why they resist exclusive assignment. Add one Mathura clause - indigenous development, spotted red sandstone, three-religion scope - to prove the features are school-specific. Close on the Indian core: the Buddhist programme, the mudras, the halo, the robe and the Indra and Brahma companions. ❌ Do not open with a political history of the region: three phases of control are recorded, the fourth-century phase is Greek followed by Mauryan integration rather than two foreign occupations, and no total or span is available to assert. No model answer is supplied here.

### 2014 - Romans and Greeks

> **Gandhara sculpture owed as much to the Romans as to the Greeks. Explain.**
>
> *(CSE, 2014, reproduced from the core art source's list of previous years' Main Examination questions.)*

**Directive.** *Explain* a proposition that contains a comparison of degree. The answer must test the "as much as" rather than simply agree with it.

**Conceptual demand.** Whether Roman and Greek contributions can be distinguished at all, and what else is present that the proposition omits.

**Answer approach.** Note first that the owned evidence treats the Hellenistic and Roman contributions as a single recorded category - "Greco-Roman influence" - whose elements are anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting, wavy hair, a muscled athletic body, heavily pleated garments and elongated earlobes. Then identify the one recorded element where Roman and Parthian inspiration are named together with Greek: the heavily pleated garments. Then supply what the proposition omits entirely - the Parthian strand, attached to Swat Valley works including a standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma recorded as one of the earliest examples of the Buddha's image, with relief carving and deeply incised facial lines in Parthian manner; and the Central Asian strand of Kushana patronage, material sequence and iconographic standardisation. Conclude that the proposition is defensible as a correction to "Greek art in India" but incomplete as a description, because the layering is at least fourfold. No model answer is supplied here.

### 2011 - styles of unglazed pottery

> **What are the major different styles of unglazed pottery making in India?**
>
> *(CSE, 2011, reproduced from the core art source's list of previous years' Main Examination questions.)*

**Directive.** A direct descriptive demand for a classification of styles.

**Conceptual demand.** Contemporary craft pottery, by style and region - the craft register of Lesson 19, not the archaeological ware sequence.

**Answer approach, with its limit stated.** The owned evidence supports the named centres with their regions and their geographical-indication status - Khurja Pottery of Bulandshahr district and Nizamabad Black Pottery of Azamgarh district in Uttar Pradesh, Blue Pottery of Jaipur and Pokhran Pottery of Pokhran in Rajasthan, Dalgate Pottery of Jammu and Kashmir, and Karigari Pottery of Tamil Nadu - together with Gundiyali and Lodai in Kutch as famous pottery-making villages, the Bengal terracotta-brick tradition, and the mechanism of hereditary artisan communities, local clay, domestic and ritual demand and geographical-indication protection. It does **not** support a classification of these centres by glazing technique, because the features column of the source table is clipped in every row. An answer should therefore organise by region and product, state the mechanism, and not assert a glazed-against-unglazed technical division from memory. No model answer is supplied here.

### 2007 and 2000 - short-form Gandhara demands

> **Write about Gandhara art.** *(CSE, 2007)*
>
> **Write about the Gandhara school of art.** *(CSE, 2000)*
>
> *(Both reproduced from the core art source's list of previous years' Main Examination questions.)*

**Directive and scale.** Both are short-form *write about* demands. No marks or word limit is printed in the reproduction and none is supplied here.

**Answer approach.** At short length only the spine fits: the region - Peshawar Valley, modern Swat, Buner and Bajaur; the patronage - Kushana; the purpose - glorifying the Buddha through episodes of his life and the Jataka; the material sequence; two or three named foreign elements correctly attributed; the Bodhisattva ornament contrast; and the consequence, that the human form under this school transformed Indian sculptural narrative. Omit the comparison and the priority debate unless the length allows. No model answer is supplied here.

### 2016 - a shared demand

> **Early Buddhist stupa art while depicting folk motifs and narratives successfully expounds Buddhist ideals. Elucidate.**
>
> *(CSE, 2016, reproduced from the core art source's list of previous years' Main Examination questions.)*

**Ownership.** Shared. The stupa as a monument and its programme belong to the monuments topic; **this topic owns the sculptural reading** - the folk motifs and the narrative devices.

**What this topic supplies.** Shalabhanjika as a yakshi holding a tree, appearing as bracket figures on the gateways of the Great Stupa at Sanchi and at Bharhut, regarded as auspicious and incorporated into stupa decoration, with the source's own inference that people who turned to Buddhism enriched it with pre-Buddhist and non-Buddhist practices; Gajalakshmi on the Bharhut railings and on the Sanchi vedika with two elephants and lotuses; Yaksha and Yakshi as folk deities of water, fertility, trees, forest and wilderness absorbed into all three religions; the Shunga marked development in ornamentation and the depiction of life in its true form, with Bharhut's northern gateway singled out; and the aniconic code by which doctrine was made visible without depicting the founder. No model answer is supplied here.

## The exact owned Preliminary item

### 2026, Preliminary examination, General Studies Paper I

> **12.** What does an empty seat represent in early Buddhist iconography?
>
> (a) The meditation of the Buddha
> (b) The Buddha's First Sermon
> (c) The Buddha's Mahaparinibbana
> (d) The Buddha's Mahabhinishkramana

*(Wording and response choices read directly from the locally held official Set-A paper.)*

**Key status.** A **provisional** 2026 Set-A key is held locally. **No answer letter is recorded or inferred here**, and no option is marked, eliminated or corrected.

**Demand.** The item tests whether a candidate knows what the empty-seat device does in the aniconic register and which episode it is conventionally attached to.

**What this session supplies, and where it stops.** Lesson 8 teaches the device fully: the empty seat is a **presence-marker**, not an omission, around which the composition is built. It also records the two formulations in the owned evidence that do not resolve to a single episode on their own - the symbol table placing the **Empty Throne** under the **Great Renunciation** alongside horse, Gandhakuti and begging bowl, and the summary note listing empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel and stupa as devices indicating renunciation, enlightenment, the first sermon and death. Because those two formulations would have to be chosen between, **no episode is asserted here**. The disambiguating rule taught instead is contextual: a throne beneath a Bodhi tree, a throne with wheel and deer, a throne with a riderless horse.

### 2026, Preliminary examination, General Studies Paper I - the Amaravati item

**Ledger record.** 2026, question **14**, recorded demand "Amaravati Stupa location, scale, sculpture school, and external influence", routed to the **monuments topic**. A provisional Set-A key is held locally and **no answer letter is recorded or inferred**.

The four response choices follow the standard "one only / two only / all three" pattern; the scan's option text is degraded and is therefore not reproduced verbatim.

**Why it is adjacent rather than owned, and what this topic contributes.** The stupa as a monument is the other topic's. What this topic supplies is the school: its development on the banks of the Krishna river under Satavahana patronage, its recorded great influence on the art of successive South Indian dynasties, its diffusion to Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala, Goli and as far west as Ter, and the robe-and-Abhayamudra type replicated in other parts of South and Southeast Asia. **A numbering discrepancy is recorded rather than resolved**: the 2026 routing ledger places this item at question 14, this topic's own knowledge file records it in an integration block at question 13, and the degraded scan's numbering places it adjacent to item 15. The ledger is followed here as the controlling routing record.

## Neutral answer approach for the whole topic

The topic's examination profile is unusual and should be planned for rather than discovered in the hall. **Mains demands cluster on Gandhara** - four entries across 2019, 2014, 2007 and 2000 - which makes the three-strand separation of Lesson 9 the single highest-yield preparation in the topic. **A second cluster concerns animals and symbols**, answered from Lessons 7 and 8. **A third concerns sculpture as social evidence**, answered from Lesson 18's evidence ladder. **Pottery appears once, in craft rather than archaeological form**, which is why Lesson 19's register distinction matters. **Preliminary items concentrate on Harappan material, iconographic identification and dating devices**, answered from Lessons 3, 14, 15 and 13.

No further claim is made. No question is reconstructed from memory, no wording is invented, no marks or word limit is supplied where none is recorded, and no key - official, provisional or from a reproducing book - is recorded anywhere in this session.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

## Block A - Method, materials, the Harappan corpus and the pottery ladder, Lessons 1-5

**A1. Question:** Set out the six reading variables, explain what each one protects an answer from, and name the single variable whose neglect does the most damage.

**Model answer:** The six are material, posture and gesture, iconographic attribute, patron, site context and cross-site comparison. Material stops an answer calling every stone simply stone, and therefore stops it losing the workshop identifications - steatite for Harappan seals, spotted red sandstone for Mathura, greenish-white limestone for Amaravati, cream-coloured sandstone for Gupta Sarnath, chloritic schist for the Hoysalas. Posture and gesture stop the substitution of adjectives for description, since "graceful" says nothing while tribhanga, abhaya and bhujangatrasita say a great deal. Iconographic attribute stops an assigned sign being called decoration. Patron stops the collapse of all early art into royal commission, which the Yaksha and Yakshi material refutes directly. Site context stops a processional bronze being confused with a wall figure. Cross-site comparison stops a local type being written up as a universal one. The most damaging neglect is the third: once an attribute is treated as ornament, the whole identification method fails, the image becomes unreadable, and the answer falls back on appearance, which is the register that earns least.

**A2. Question:** Explain the complete lost-wax sequence and derive from it three properties of the resulting object.

**Model answer:** A figure is modelled in wax; it is coated with wet clay and allowed to dry; the clay-coated figure is heated so that the wax inside melts; the wax is poured out through a tiny hole and liquid metal is poured into the hollow mould; after the metal has cooled and solidified the clay coat is removed, leaving a metal figure of the same shape as the wax original. The technique is recorded as known from the time of the Indus Valley culture and as still practised in many parts of the country. Three properties follow. First, uniqueness: the mould is destroyed to release the casting, so each object is singular unless the wax itself was mould-made. Second, formal freedom: whatever the wax could do the metal reproduces, which is why cast figures can have raised legs, outflung arms and open space between the limbs where a carved figure's projections must stay attached to the block. Third, portability: a cast image can be lifted, carried and processed, which is the technical precondition of the ritual icon - and, as the recorded removal of temple bronzes shows, of its vulnerability to being taken away.

**A3. Question:** Reconstruct the pottery ladder from the Neolithic to the Mauryan period, and state two different kinds of inference it supports.

**Model answer:** Neolithic, approximately 7000-2000 BC: hand-made coarse ware with thick fabric and cord or impressed surface. Chalcolithic, approximately 3000-1000 BC: painted pottery at Ahar, Malwa and Jorwe, red ware with painted geometric motifs, usually hand-made or on a slow wheel. Harappan, approximately 2600-1900 BC: Harappan Red Ware, wheel-made, with black geometric painting, most of it very fine wheel-made ware with very few handmade pieces and rare polychrome. Painted Grey Ware, 1100-500/400 BC: very high-quality clay, thin fine smooth fabric, simple geometric patterns in black, a small fraction of the assemblage, coexisting with plain grey ware, Black and Red Ware and black slipped ware, distributed from the Himalayan foothills to the Malwa plateau and from Bahawalpur to Kaushambi, with finds in Kumaon and Garhwal. Northern Black Polished Ware: black-painted, highly lustrous luxury ware at Pataliputra, Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain and Vidisha. The first inference is technological: forming moves from hand to slow wheel to fine fast-wheel work, and firing control improves to the point where uniform grey ware is possible. The second is social: a scarce high-investment ware alongside abundant utility wares in one layer is direct evidence of differentiation, which is why both PGW and NBPW are described as high-quality or luxury rather than ordinary.

## Block B - Mauryan figures, animal capitals, the aniconic code and Gandhara, Lessons 6-9

**B1. Question:** Explain why the Yaksha and Yakshi material is the strongest evidence against a purely court-driven history of early Indian sculpture.

**Model answer:** Because the same technical standard served a cult the court did not create. Yakshas are recorded as folk deities connected with water, fertility, trees, the forest and wilderness, considered nature spirits or demigods, worshipped by rural folk for boons and protection from evil and understood as residing formlessly on trees and water bodies; Yakshi is the benign female counterpart connected with fertility and closely associated with the Ashoka tree. They were objects of worship in Jainism, Hinduism and Buddhism alike, with the Yaksha depicted as the attendant of the Bodhisattva and the Tirthankara. Yet the Parkham Yaksha, found south of Mathura and described as speaking of massive strength and gravity in imagery, and the Didarganj whisk-bearer both carry the period's characteristic high polish - the same finish as the imperial pillars. The descent is documented too: the Mathura school's images were modelled on the earlier yaksha images of the Mauryan period, so the popular stream, not the imperial one, founds the great later school. A court-only account cannot explain either the shared finish or the inheritance.

**B2. Question:** Reconstruct the Sarnath capital as a complete programme and explain what Rampurva adds that Sarnath cannot.

**Model answer:** The Sarnath capital carries four lions standing back to back on a circular abacus bearing carvings of bull, horse, lion and elephant together with the Dharmachakra. The abacus animals represent four directions - galloping horse for west, elephant for east, bull for south, lion for north - and are described as following each other, turning the wheel of existence till eternity. Each also carries a biographical meaning: the elephant recalls Queen Maya's dream of a white elephant entering her womb, the bull the zodiac sign of Taurus and the month of the Buddha's birth, the horse Kanthaka by which he left the princely life, and the lion the attainment of enlightenment. The four crowning lions symbolise the Buddha spreading Dharma in all directions and commemorate the first sermon, the Dharmachakrapravartana; the abacus and animal part were adopted as India's national emblem on 26 January 1950, with Satyameva Jayate from the Mundaka Upanishad inscribed below in Devanagari and the Dharma Chakra crowning the capital. Rampurva adds a controlled comparison Sarnath cannot: two Ashokan pillars at one site, one with a lion capital and one with a bull capital, where the lion capital pillar is inscribed with Major Pillar Edicts I to VI and the bull capital pillar has no inscription. With patron, place and period held constant, the difference in treatment attaches to the animal itself.

**B3. Question:** State what the aniconic phase actually did, and explain why the shift to the human image is not a story about skill.

**Model answer:** The earlier Buddhist monuments did not depict the Buddha in human form; his presence was indicated through his footprint, his throne, his promenade or stupa, by tree and by wheel. The code was specific: birth by bull, elephant, lotus and footprint; the great renunciation by horse, Gandhakuti, empty throne and begging bowl; enlightenment by vajrasana and the Bodhi tree; the sermons by wheel, lion and deer; death by the stupa. The summary formulation records empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel and stupa as indicating renunciation, enlightenment, the first sermon and death. The empty seat is a presence-marker, not an omission - the composition is built around a space framed as occupied. The shift is therefore not about skill, because the same monuments carry dense accomplished figural work: Jataka and life narratives, Shalabhanjika brackets at Sanchi and Bharhut, Gajalakshmi on the Bharhut railings and the Sanchi vedika, and the Shunga period's marked development in ornamentation and the depiction of life in its true form. Sculptors who carved those could have carved a Buddha. What changed was devotional practice - the worshipper is offered a figure to face rather than a space to infer - alongside imperial patronage that standardised the conventions for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras.

## Block C - Mathura, Amaravati, the comparison and the Gupta idiom, Lessons 10-13

**C1. Question:** Explain what problem each of the three post-Mauryan schools set itself, naming three pieces of evidence for each, and say why this framing is better than ranking them.

**Model answer:** Gandhara's problem was how to give the Buddha a human body. Its evidence is imported anatomy - anatomical accuracy, spatial width and foresighting; Usha-Nisha wavy hair and a slender muscled body on the model of a very fit Greek athlete; and the recorded consequence that the Buddha's representation in human form under this school transformed the course of Indian sculptural narrative. Mathura's problem was legibility for three religions at once. Its evidence is the recorded scope - influenced by the stories and imageries of Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism - the descent of its images from earlier Mauryan yaksha images, and the striking use of symbolism by which Hindu gods are shown through their avayudhas, Shiva through the linga and mukhalinga. Amaravati's problem was telling a whole story on a curved surface. Its evidence is the recorded contrast with schools that focused on single images, the excessive use of the tribhanga posture, and the greenish-white limestone medallion displaying an entire story of the Buddha taming an elephant. Ranking fails because it imports an aesthetic preference the evidence does not supply and because the three were not attempting the same task; problem-framing is arguable from recorded material and explains why each school's material, posture and format differ.

**C2. Question:** Write the safe paragraph on the priority of the first anthropomorphic Buddha image and justify each clause.

**Model answer:** The paragraph is: "Anthropomorphic Buddha imagery emerged in broadly overlapping early-centuries-CE contexts at both Gandhara and Mathura, under Kushana patronage; priority between the two is debated, and parallel development rather than a single origin is the defensible position. What is not in doubt is the consequence: the appearance of the human image transformed the course of Indian sculptural narrative." Each clause is justified. "Broadly overlapping early-centuries-CE contexts" reflects that the Gandhara school evolved under the Kushan kings while another indigenous school flourished at Mathura in the first century AD. "Under Kushana patronage" reflects Kanishka's territory running from Mathura through Gandhara and Bactria to the borders of China, which places both schools in one political space. "Priority is debated" reflects the canonical record that both participated in the shift and that priority between them is disputed, and it is reinforced by the Parthian-linked Swat Valley standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma being described as one of the earliest examples rather than the earliest. "The consequence is not in doubt" reflects the recorded transformation claim, which is an impact statement and not a first-in-time statement - and distinguishing those two kinds of claim is what the paragraph exists to do.

**C3. Question:** Explain how the Gupta idiom is recognised, and how the ring-stirrup extends the topic's dating toolkit.

**Model answer:** The Gupta school developed around Sarnath and is recognised by four recorded features: cream-coloured sandstone and the use of metal; sculptures immaculately dressed and lacking any form of nakedness; an intricately decorated halo around the Buddha's head, as against Mathura's larger geometrically decorated halo; and the named example, the 7.5 ft copper Sultanganj Buddha of Bihar, which should be described as late or post-Gupta given its c. seventh-century date. The idiom is subtractive in the body and additive in the aureole, which is why it reads as restraint against Gandhara's pleated drapery and muscled anatomy. The ring-stirrup extends the toolkit in a different direction: it is a circular or oval loop attached to a horse's saddle to support the rider's feet, shown as a circular form hanging from the saddle, introduced into India via Central Asian contacts, and appearing in sculpture only from the post-Gupta period onwards, particularly in early medieval North and South Indian temples - at Hoysala reliefs at Belur and Halebidu, Khajuraho stone panels, Chalukyan temples at Badami, Aihole and Pattadakal, and the Sun Temple at Konark. It therefore supplies a lower limit for any equestrian relief that depicts one, which is a technological rather than a stylistic argument and is correspondingly harder to dispute.

## Block D - Identification systems, temple elements and the Nataraja, Lessons 14-17

**D1. Question:** Write out the decision procedure for an unlabelled religious image and state the two rules that govern its use.

**Model answer:** First, look for an inscription; if one exists it decides, as the Brahmi text on the base of the headless Mathura figure decides that it represents Kanishka. Second, test the ornament: a figure heavily ornamented with an elaborate hairdo and sandals is a Bodhisattva, while a plainly robed, barefoot, unjewelled figure with a halo is a Buddha. Third, if it is a Bodhisattva, read what it carries - a lotus for Padmapani, a thunderbolt for Vajrapani, with Maitreya and Avalokitesvara as further named types. Fourth, if it is a Buddha, read the mudra: right hand to earth with left in lap for Bhumisparsha and enlightenment; hands in lap with thumbs forming a triangle for Dhyana and meditation; one hand at the chest with thumb and index forming a circle for Vitarka and debate; hands at the chest with circles formed for Dharmachakra and the teaching of Dharma; a raised open palm for Abhaya and fearlessness; index and little finger raised for Karana; interlaced fingers with index fingers up for Uttarabodhi; left forefinger in the right fist for Vajra. Fifth, if the figure is ascetic and frontal with an emblem or a yaksha-yakshi attendant pair it is Jain, and the lanchana names the Tirthankara - bull for Rishabhanatha, elephant for Ajitanatha, horse for Sambhavanatha, monkey for Abhinandananatha, curlew for Sumatinatha, red lotus for Padmaprabha, lion for Mahavira. Sixth, if it carries attributes, is accompanied by a mount or is an aniconic emblem it is Hindu, read by ayudha, by vahana, by linga or mukhalinga, by a group of seven for the Saptamatrikas, or by episode for a Krishna type or a Shiva manifestation. Only then read material and treatment for school and period. The two governing rules are that an inscription outranks every iconographic inference, and that the procedure yields a type, a school and an approximate period and never a patron, an exact date or an original location.

**D2. Question:** Explain how the Nataraja's ten recorded elements divide into three groups, and what each group is addressed to.

**Model answer:** The first group is cosmological and is carried by the rear pair of hands: the rear right holds the drum, the sound of creation from which all creations spring, and the rear left holds the eternal fire, destruction described as the precursor and inevitable counterpart of creation. The second group is devotional and is carried by the front pair: the front right raised in abhaya mudra is benediction, reassuring the devotee not to be afraid, and the front left points in gajahasta, the elephant-hand pose, towards the upraised foot, indicating the path of salvation. The third group concerns the obstacle and comprises three elements: the left leg in bhujangatrasita represents tirobhava, kicking away the veil of maya from the devotee's mind; the dwarf Muyalaka beneath the foot symbolises ignorance and the ego of an individual; and the snake twisted around the arm is kundalini power, dormant in the human spine, which if aroused permits true consciousness. Framing these three groups are the matted flowing locks representing the Ganges, the paired male and female earrings signifying the fusion often called Ardhanarishvara, and the nimbus of glowing lights representing the vast unending cycles of time. The first group addresses the universe, the second the worshipper, and the third the condition of the worshipper's mind - which is why the image is a proposition rather than a portrait.

**D3. Question:** Explain the difference between the placement logic of a Nandi and the placement logic of a Yali, and what each reveals about the animal's role.

**Model answer:** Nandi is placed by the vahana rule: the mount or vehicle of the main deity stands just before the sanctum sanctorum. That fixes one position on the temple's principal axis, facing the deity, which is why the bull receives its own structure - a Nandi Mandap in front of the Brihadishvara temple at Thanjavur housing a massive monolith recorded as the second largest in the country, the largest being at Lepakshi Veerabhadra in Andhra Pradesh, with further monolithic examples such as the Nandi within the premises of the Thousand Pillar temple at Warangal and the Hoysala Nandi Pavilion noted with its kalyani for ceremonial use. Singular, axial and attendant: the bull's role is service to the deity, and it is given architecture to perform it. The Yali is placed by multiplication: it is a composite lion figure symbolic of the Nayaka architectural identity, appearing on heavily painted gopurams depicting warriors, deities and animals and on narrative and composite columns, with its northern counterpart the Vyala found at Khajuraho on temples dedicated to Vishnu and in other Indian temples. Plural, peripheral and structural: the lion's role is guardianship - in Tamil literature the Yali is described as a guardian creature protecting human beings both physically and spiritually - and load-bearing, which is why it belongs on thresholds and supports rather than on the axis.

## Block E - Regional programmes, the living registers and the answer, Lessons 18-20

**E1. Question:** State the evidence ladder for reading sculpture as social evidence, with a named case at each level, and identify the level at which most weak answers fail.

**Model answer:** The first and safest level is what was technically possible: chloritic schist, soft when quarried and hardening over time, permitting Hoysala friezes and lathe-turned pillars; fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale yellow permitting Khajuraho's deep relief; granite carrying the musical sculptures of Tamil temples. The second is what was commissioned: at Khajuraho six temples to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras; Sembiyan Mahadevi's foundations at Kutralam, Virudhachalam and Aduthurai; the Hoysala Nandi Pavilion and kalyani noted for ceremonial use. The third is what was depicted: Ramayana and Mahabharata scenes, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs in Hoysala friezes; maithuna reliefs on both Hindu and Jain temples at Khajuraho; the ninth-century Kalyanasundara Murti rendering Panigrahana through two separate statuettes. The fourth level - what people believed and how they lived - is not directly evidenced, because a carved panel is a commissioned representation rather than a census, a survey or a photograph. Most weak answers fail at exactly that fourth level, treating an elite religious monument as a description of ordinary society and generalising from royal temples to village life.

**E2. Question:** Explain the distinction between the archaeological and craft registers of Indian pottery and metalwork, and state the only continuities the owned evidence supports.

**Model answer:** The archaeological register names wares - Harappan Red Ware, Painted Grey Ware, Northern Black Polished Ware, Black and Red Ware, black slipped ware, plain grey ware - and defines them by fabric, firing, distribution and stratigraphic association, using them to date a layer and name a culture. The craft register names places and products - Khurja Pottery of Bulandshahr district, Nizamabad Black Pottery of Azamgarh district, Blue Pottery of Jaipur, Pokhran Pottery of Pokhran, Dalgate Pottery of Jammu and Kashmir, Karigari Pottery of Tamil Nadu, with several carrying a geographical indication - and defines them by workshop, region, market and legal protection. They answer different questions and must never be run into one sequence. The continuities the owned evidence does support are exactly three: the cire-perdue technique, recorded as known from the Indus period and as still practised in many parts of the country; Gundiyali and Lodai in Kutch, Gujarat, recorded as famous pottery-making villages, a pottery rather than a figurative-terracotta continuity; and the Bengal tradition in which burnt bricks and clay known as terracotta bricks were the principal building material. Named living terracotta centres such as Bankura or Molela are not carried in this evidence and must not be supplied.

**E3. Question:** Take a single named object and demonstrate the whole session's method on it, ending with an explicit statement of what cannot be claimed.

**Model answer:** Take a Chola bronze Nataraja. Material and technique: bronze, copper mixed with tin, cast by the cire-perdue method in which a wax model is clay-coated, dried, heated so the wax melts, drained through a tiny hole, filled with liquid metal and freed by breaking the clay - a technique known from the Indus period, described in textual terms in the Matsya Purana and still practised today, and the reason the figure can have a raised leg and open limbs. Posture and gesture: the Tandava dance posture, the left leg in bhujangatrasita, the front right hand in abhaya and the front left in gajahasta. Attributes: drum for the sound of creation, fire for destruction, the dwarf Muyalaka for ignorance and ego, the Ganges in the matted locks, paired male and female earrings for the Ardhanarishvara fusion, the snake for kundalini, the nimbus for the unending cycles of time. Patron and site: Chola, with the type's masterpiece recorded at Chidambaram and many South Indian Shiva temples keeping a separate natana-sabha for the image; a named individual patron of Chola religious art is Sembiyan Mahadevi of the tenth century AD. Cross-site comparison: the same programme appears as stone reliefs in Pallava cave temples, as panels on Hoysala temple walls and at Thanjavur, and the earliest known Nataraja is a rock-cut example at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole under early Chalukya rule. What cannot be claimed: that the Cholas originated the iconography; any alloy composition, weight or height; any individual object's exact date or original sanctum without inscriptional or excavational evidence; and any present-day location, ownership or conservation status beyond what a dated official record states.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These questions are original to this session. None reproduces or closely restates any official question recorded in the verified PYQ section above.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"The technique of a sculpture is a better guide to its history than its subject." Comment. (Answer in 150 words)**

### Model

The claim is broadly sound, because technique is datable and locatable in a way that subject is not.

Subject recurs across centuries and schools: a seated Buddha is carved at Gandhara, at Mathura, at Sarnath and in the Deccan, so the subject alone places nothing. Technique discriminates. The Mauryan polish marks a workshop standard shared by imperial pillars and popular Yaksha images. The Gandhara material sequence - blue schist and green phyllite, then stucco, then a complete shift to grey stone from the third century AD - is a chronology in itself. The ring-stirrup, introduced via Central Asian contacts and appearing in sculpture only from the post-Gupta period onward, supplies a lower limit for any equestrian relief at Konark, Khajuraho or Belur. Chloritic schist, soft when quarried and hardening later, explains the Hoysala frieze in a way no subject can.

The qualification is that technique supplies boundaries rather than points; it says not earlier than, rarely exactly when.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the datability contrast stated at the outset 2 + the subject-recurs-across-schools argument 2 + three named technical markers each with its chronological consequence 4 + the boundary-not-point qualification 1 + verdict 1. Listing techniques without their chronological use scores half.**

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Examine the proposition that early Indian sculpture absorbed popular cults more readily than it invented new forms. (Answer in 150 words)**

### Model

The proposition is well supported for the pre-Gupta centuries, with one significant exception.

Absorption is documented. Yakshas and Yakshis, folk deities of water, fertility, trees, forest and wilderness once held to reside formlessly on trees and water bodies, become monumental sculpture at Parkham and Didarganj with the period's characteristic polish, and are worshipped across Jainism, Hinduism and Buddhism, attending the Bodhisattva and the Tirthankara. Shalabhanjika, a yakshi holding a tree, becomes a bracket figure on the gateways at Sanchi and Bharhut and is regarded as auspicious; the source itself infers that converts enriched Buddhism with pre-Buddhist practices. Gajalakshmi crosses from the Bharhut railings to a third-century Kausambi coin to Kalinga temple figures. Crucially, the Mathura school's images are recorded as modelled on the earlier Mauryan yaksha images.

The exception is invention where doctrine required it: the anthropomorphic Buddha image had no popular prototype, and its appearance transformed Indian sculptural narrative.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the absorption thesis with its period limit 2 + the Yaksha-Yakshi definition and its monumentalisation 2 + two further travelling motifs with named sites 3 + the documented Mathura descent 2 + the invention exception 1.**

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"Indian religious sculpture solved the problem of recognition before it solved the problem of representation." Discuss. (Answer in 250 words)**

### Model

The ordering is defensible if recognition is understood as the assigning of readable signs and representation as the depiction of a body.

Recognition came first and worked without bodies. In the aniconic register the Buddha was indicated rather than depicted - by footprint, throne, promenade or stupa, tree and wheel - with an assigned code in which bull, elephant, lotus and footprint marked birth, horse, Gandhakuti, empty throne and begging bowl the renunciation, vajrasana and the Bodhi tree the enlightenment, wheel, lion and deer the sermons, and the stupa the death. The Sarnath capital shows the same coding on a different object, where the bull marks the birth month and the lion the attainment of enlightenment. Hindu practice provides the parallel case: Shiva is represented by the linga and mukhalinga, which recognise without representing at all.

Representation followed and did not displace recognition. The human Buddha image appears under Kushana patronage, and under that patronage clear iconographic conventions were fixed for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras - so the arrival of the body was accompanied by a further intensification of the sign system rather than by its retirement. Mathura, serving three religions with one inherited yaksha body type, had to distinguish figures by avayudha because representation alone could not.

Two qualifications. The ordering is about emphasis, not about absolute sequence, since accomplished figural narrative existed throughout the aniconic phase. And the two problems are not independent: the Nataraja is recognised entirely through its represented body, with drum, fire, abhaya and gajahasta legible only because they are limbs.

Recognition therefore has priority, and representation is built on top of it.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the definition of both problems before arguing 3 + the complete aniconic code as recognition-without-body 4 + the linga as the Hindu parallel 2 + the Kushana standardisation showing signs intensify rather than retire 3 + the Mathura legibility case 2 + both qualifications 1.**

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Assess the claim that material culture rather than monumental art provides the more reliable chronology for early India. (Answer in 250 words)**

### Model

The claim is strong on reliability and weak on scope, and the assessment should separate the two.

Ceramic evidence is reliable because it is abundant, stratified and sensitive. The ladder is explicit: hand-made coarse ware of thick fabric in the Neolithic; painted red ware with geometric motifs at Ahar, Malwa and Jorwe in the Chalcolithic, hand-made or slow-wheel made; fine wheel-made Harappan Red Ware; Painted Grey Ware of very high-quality clay, thin and smooth, dated 1100-500/400 BC and distributed from the Himalayan foothills to the Malwa plateau and from Bahawalpur to Kaushambi; and Northern Black Polished Ware, black-painted and highly lustrous, at Pataliputra, Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain and Vidisha. Resolution can be finer still, as the description of Siswal's pottery through the six fabrics of Kalibangan shows.

Monumental art is comparatively unreliable for dating. Its most famous objects are contested: the Didarganj whisk-bearer's period is disputed despite its polish, the 7.5 ft copper Sultanganj Buddha sits at the late or post-Gupta end, and priority between Gandhara and Mathura for the first anthropomorphic Buddha image is unresolved.

Scope, however, reverses the comparison. Pottery cannot evidence belief, iconography, patronage or doctrine; only sculpture records that a bull marked a birth month, that a queen named Sembiyan Mahadevi founded temples, or that a marriage ceremony was rendered in two statuettes.

Two qualifications. Sculpture can date when it carries a technical marker such as the post-Gupta ring-stirrup or an inscription such as the Brahmi text naming Kanishka. And a ware dates a layer, not an idea.

The disciplined verdict is division of labour: ceramics supply the chronology, sculpture supplies the content, and each should be asked only what it can answer.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the reliability-versus-scope separation 3 + the full ceramic ladder with at least two date ranges and one site list 4 + three contested sculptural datings 3 + the scope reversal with three content examples 3 + both qualifications, datable sculpture and the layer-not-idea limit 2.**

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Every major controversy in Indian sculpture is a controversy about evidence rather than about art." Critically examine. (Answer in 250 words)**

### Model

The proposition is largely correct, and testing it requires sorting the controversies by the kind of claim at stake.

Several disputes are purely evidential. The Harappan identities - the seal called Pashupati, the figure called the Mother Goddess, the bearded man called the Priest-King - are unsettled because the pictographic script on the seals remains undeciphered, so the objects are describable and unnameable. The Didarganj dating turns on whether the characteristic polish outweighs stylistic arguments for a later date, which is a contest between two levels of evidence. The Sultanganj Buddha's placement at the late or post-Gupta end is a dating question about a c. seventh-century object. The priority of the first anthropomorphic Buddha image is unresolved because Gandhara evolved under Kushan patronage while another indigenous school flourished at Mathura in the first century AD, with the earliest Swat Valley images linked to Parthian rather than Greco-Roman art. The Nataraja dispute is settled by evidence - the earliest known example was excavated at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole under early Chalukya rule - and persists only because a famous patron is mistaken for an originator.

Some controversies, however, are genuinely interpretive. Whether the empty seat is an absence or a presence-marker is a question about how a composition means. Whether Khajuraho's programme evidences social liberality is a question about inference from commissioned representation. Whether Gandhara is "Indo-Greek" is a question about how to weigh borrowed surface against Indian subject.

Two qualifications. Interpretive questions are still disciplined by evidence, since the source's own table and note constrain the empty-seat reading. And source damage creates a third class of dispute - clipped columns in the pottery, school-comparison, Tirthankara and mudra tables - which is neither artistic nor evidential but archival.

Evidence therefore dominates, but interpretation and archival loss each retain a real share.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the sorting-by-claim-type method stated first 3 + four evidential controversies with their exact grounds 6 + the Nataraja case distinguished as settled-but-repeated 2 + three genuinely interpretive controversies 5 + the archival third class 2 + both qualifications 2.**

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Portability, more than patronage, explains what has survived and what has been lost of Indian sculpture." Discuss. (Answer in 250 words)**

### Model

Portability is a genuine and under-recognised explanatory variable, but it competes with two others rather than replacing patronage.

Portability explains movement and loss. Lost-wax casting, in which the clay coat is broken away to free a metal figure, produces objects that can be lifted, processed and therefore removed. The consequence is documented at the top of the tradition: a report dated 14 May 2026 records three bronzes - a Chola-period Shiva Nataraja, a sixteenth-century Vijayanagara Saint Sundarar with Paravai and a twelfth-century Chola Somaskanda - reclaimed from a United States museum collection after provenance research using archival records, field documentation and 1950s-1960s temple photographs established that they had been taken out of the country in an unauthorised manner. The same logic operates for carved stone that can be detached: the Amaravati sculptures, collected by Sir Walter Elliot after the first excavation of 1845, survive as the Amaravati or Elliot marbles in a museum far from the Krishna valley.

Durability, however, is the stronger determinant of survival overall. The use of stone, metal and terracotta is attributed to the region's climatic conditions, so the corpus is already filtered by what lasts; terracotta survives best where it was fired and buried or built into a wall.

Patronage determines what was made at all - six Shiva, eight Vishnu, one Ganesha, one Sun and four Jain temples at Khajuraho; a named queen's foundations at three sites - and therefore sets the population from which survival selects.

Two qualifications. Immovability protects: a monolithic Nandi in its own mandapa has not travelled. And portability also preserves, since a removed object may survive a ruined site, as at Amaravati.

Survival is therefore a three-variable outcome - durability, patronage and portability - in which portability governs relocation rather than existence.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the three-variable framing, refusing the single-cause claim 4 + the casting-to-portability mechanism 3 + the dated current case with its provenance method 4 + the Amaravati removal chain 3 + the durability and climate argument 3 + the patronage-sets-the-population argument 2 + both qualifications, immovability and preservation-by-removal 1.**

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | The error | Why it happens | The correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | "The Pashupati seal proves Shiva worship" | The label is treated as a finding | A steatite seal from Mohenjo-daro showing a cross-legged figure in a three-horned headgear surrounded by animals; the script is undeciphered and the name is modern |
| 2 | "Harappan sculpture was crude" | A judgement made about the terracottas is generalised | The terracottas are recorded as fewer and cruder than the bronzes; the same corpus contains lost-wax casting and fine steatite carving |
| 3 | A lion on a Harappan seal | The lion's later prominence is projected backwards | No evidence of cow or lion has been found on any seal |
| 4 | "All Harappan seals were square and made of steatite" | The commonest case is taken as the only case | Mostly square, also triangular, rectangular and circular; mostly steatite, also agate, chert, copper, faience and terracotta, with some gold and ivory |
| 5 | "Harappan pottery is Black and Red Ware" | Two uses of one phrase are collapsed | The description is recorded, but the phrase also names a separate wider ceramic category, listed among the wares coexisting with Painted Grey Ware |
| 6 | "Perforated pots were used for brewing" | A hedge is dropped | They might have been used for straining liquor |
| 7 | "PGW dates to 1100-500 BC" | An internal uncertainty is silently resolved | 1100-500/400 BC |
| 8 | "NBPW is Mauryan court pottery" | A period label becomes an exclusive attribution | A fine lustrous tableware, often black or steel-grey but also other shades, associated especially with early historic urbanisation and best known from Mauryan-period levels |
| 9 | A megalithic ware described in detail | A gap is filled to complete a sequence | The burial forms - dolmens, cairns, menhirs and cists - are attested; the ware detail is not carried here |
| 10 | "Mauryan art is court art" | The pillars dominate the memory | The same polish appears on Yaksha and Yakshi images, and the Mathura school descends from them |
| 11 | "The Didarganj Yakshi is Mauryan" | A conventional attribution is stated as settled | Recorded as of the Mauryan period, with the polish supporting it and stylistic arguments for a later date existing |
| 12 | "The Sarnath pillar is the national emblem" | The part is confused with the whole | The abacus and the animal part were adopted, on 26 January 1950 |
| 12a | Dating the Sanchi pillar from its Sankha Lipi inscription | The latest text on an object is taken as its date | The capital is Ashokan and dated around 250 BC; the Sankha Lipi inscription is Gupta-period, added later, and that script is not yet deciphered |
| 12b | Transferring the geese-and-flame-palmette abacus to Sarnath, or the four directional animals and Dharmachakra to Sanchi | Two similar capitals are merged | The four lions back to back are shared; the abacus programmes differ, and that difference is the evidence of deliberate choice |
| 12c | Listing Chandragupta Maurya among Gandhara's foreign occupations | A recorded sequence is read as a list of foreign powers | The fourth-century phase is Alexander **and then** Chandragupta Maurya - a Greek intervention followed by Indian imperial integration |
| 12d | Asserting a number of powers or a span of years for Gandhara | A narrative wants a figure | Three phases of control are recorded and no total; the source is silent between the fourth century BC and the first century AD |
