| 12e | Supplying a Saka ruler, period or stylistic feature | A named people invites detail | The Saka are named by the canonical owner as one of the Central Asian strand's three peoples; nothing further about them is recorded in the owned evidence |
| 12f | Assigning a monolithic Nandi to Ramappa | The owner's combined formulation is read as one temple | The monolith is recorded at the Thousand Pillar temple; Ramappa records the main temple standing in front of the Nandi Pavilion |
| 13 | Extra meanings added to the abacus animals | Familiar symbolism is imported | Only four meanings are recorded: Maya's dream, the Taurus birth month, Kanthaka, and the attainment of enlightenment |
| 14 | "The Rampurva bull pillar is earlier, hence uninscribed" | An explanation is invented for a recorded fact | The source states only that the bull capital pillar does not have inscriptions |
| 15 | "Early Buddhism prohibited images" | Aniconism is read as a rule | The human figure of the Buddha was not depicted; other imagery was abundant |
| 16 | The empty seat assigned to one episode as settled | A single formulation is chosen | The table places it under the Great Renunciation; the summary lists it among four devices; context disambiguates |
| 17 | The Great Stupa of Sanchi placed before the Second Stupa | "Great" is read as "first" | Bharhut 150 BC, Second Sanchi 125 BC, Bodhgaya 75 BC, Great Stupa of Sanchi 50 BC, Third Sanchi 25 BC |
| 18 | "Gandhara is Greek art" | One strand is taken for the whole | Parthian, Central Asian and Greco-Roman strands over a Buddhist programme under Kushana patronage |
| 19 | One material given for Gandhara | The sequence is compressed | Blue schist and green phyllite, then stucco, then wholly grey stone from the third century AD |
| 20 | "Gandhara made the first Buddha image" | An impact claim is read as a priority claim | Overlapping emergence at Gandhara and Mathura; priority debated; the transformation claim is about consequence |
| 21 | "Mathura was a Buddhist school" | The comparator's scope is transferred | Influenced by the stories and imageries of Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism |
| 22 | Padmapani and Vajrapani attributes swapped | Two flanking figures are learned as a pair without their signs | Padmapani holds the lotus, Vajrapani the thunderbolt |
| 23 | Halos confused between schools | Both are "decorated" | Mathura's is larger than Gandhara's and geometrically decorated; the Gupta halo is intricately decorated |
| 24 | Foreign influence attributed to Amaravati | Symmetry is assumed across the three schools | Developed on purely indigenous lines with no foreign influence |
| 25 | "Amaravati was a Satavahana school only" | A founding patron is taken as the only patron | The Ikshvakus also patronised it in the third and fourth centuries AD |
| 26 | Tribhanga called "graceful" and left there | The posture is treated decoratively | It is the posture narrative relief requires, which is why Amaravati used it excessively |
| 27 | "Ellora is a Gupta excavation" | A chapter-opening phrase is read as an attribution | The "classical" label is an evaluative judgement; the same source dates Ellora's murals to the sixth-ninth centuries under the Rashtrakutas and other dynasties |
| 28 | The Sultanganj Buddha dated firmly to the Gupta period | A school example is assumed to be central to the school's dates | A c. seventh-century copper sculpture, safer described as late or post-Gupta |
| 29 | A ring-stirrup in a pre-Gupta relief | A familiar object is assumed timeless | It appears in sculpture only from the post-Gupta period onward, introduced via Central Asian contacts |
| 30 | Vitarka and Dharmachakra confused | Both are chest gestures with circles | Vitarka is one hand with a thumb-index circle; Dharmachakra is hands at the chest with circles formed |
| 31 | An ornamented sandalled figure called a Buddha | Ornament is read as splendour | The Bodhisattva is heavily ornamented with elaborate hairdos and sandals; the Buddha is plainly robed and barefoot |
| 32 | Tirthankara emblems supplied beyond the recorded list | A partial table is completed | Only seven are legible here: bull, elephant, horse, monkey, curlew, red lotus and Mahavira's lion |
| 33 | The vahana treated only as an image attribute | The architectural clause is dropped | It is the mount of the main deity, placed just before the sanctum sanctorum |
| 34 | Shiva manifestations confused | Five names are learned without their meanings | Lingodbhava the column of light; Dakshinamurthy the teacher in silent meditation; Kalyanasundara the divine wedding; Ardhanarishvara the union of masculine and feminine; Nataraja the cosmic cycle |
| 35 | The Kalyanasundara described as one sculpture | The composition is not read | Panigrahana is represented by two separate statuettes |
| 36 | A myth supplied for the Kirtimukha | A monster face invites narrative | Recorded as a decorative motif with huge fangs; no narrative is assigned |
| 37 | Musical-pillar acoustics explained | A striking fact invites elaboration | Existence, location and name are recorded; counts, tuning and mechanism are not |
| 38 | One Yali anatomy declared correct | Two descriptions look contradictory | Both are from the same source; state the composite, lion-based, elephant-tusked core |
| 39 | "The Cholas created the Nataraja" | A famous patron is taken as an originator | The earliest known example is at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole under early Chalukya rule; the Cholas brought it to its peak |
| 40 | Nataraja hands or terms swapped | Ten elements are memorised without structure | Rear right drum, rear left fire, front right abhaya, front left gajahasta; gajahasta is the hand pose, bhujangatrasita the leg stance, tirobhava its meaning |
| 41 | Khajuraho reduced to erotic sculpture | The famous register displaces the rest | A variety of artwork including maithuna reliefs, on Jain as well as Hindu temples, with a recorded sect distribution |
| 42 | Hoysala detail explained by temperament | Style is separated from material | Chloritic schist is soft when quarried and hardens over time |
| 43 | "The Mughals produced no sculpture" | A general claim is taken without its evidence | Akbar was concerned with sculpture, summoned salat guilds, commissioned statues of Jai Mal and Fatha, and animate motifs and carved elephant mouths are recorded |
| 44 | Shah Jahan's miniature animals attributed to Akbar | A dynasty is treated as one phase | Mini-monuments of marble, jalis and miniature animals are recorded from the time of Shah Jahan |
| 45 | Craft pottery features described | A clipped table is completed | The feature column is cut off in every row and is not reconstructed |
| 46 | Bankura or Molela named as verified terracotta centres | A gap is filled from general knowledge | Not carried in the owned evidence; write the mechanism and say so |
| 47 | An archaeological ware and a craft pottery run into one sequence | Both are "pottery" | Two classification systems answering different questions |
| 48 | A provenance record turned into a restitution argument | A moving object invites a legal claim | The chain is recorded as fact; law, policy and status belong to the heritage topic |

## Targeted drills

1. **The absence drill.** Write, from memory, the complete Harappan seal motif list and then the two animals recorded as absent. Repeat until the absences come faster than the presences.
2. **The sequence drill.** Write the five steps of the lost-wax process in order, then the three consequences - unique casting, formal freedom, portability - without looking.
3. **The ladder drill.** Write the pottery ladder with its date ranges, then add for each rung one forming technique and one diagnostic feature. Mark yourself down for any rung written without a forming technique.
4. **The two-column drill.** Draw the Gandhara-Mathura comparison on four axes from memory - influence, material, scope, treatment - and check that the halo entry is written comparatively rather than absolutely.
5. **The strand drill.** List the Parthian, Central Asian and Greco-Roman elements of Gandhara in three separate columns, then mark the one element that appears in more than one column.
6. **The gesture drill.** Cover the meaning column of the mudra table and reconstruct it from the hand positions; then cover the positions and reconstruct them from the meanings.
7. **The emblem drill.** Write the seven recorded Tirthankara emblems, then write the sentence that explains why an emblem system is necessary in that tradition.
8. **The Nataraja drill.** Write the ten elements in the recorded order, then group them into cosmological, devotional and obstacle-related, then name the three framing elements.
9. **The origin-peak drill.** Write the Nataraja origin-versus-peak sentence, then apply the same structure to NBPW and to the first anthropomorphic Buddha image.
10. **The ladder-of-inference drill.** Take any carved panel and write four sentences, one at each level of the evidence ladder, ending with an explicit statement of what cannot be inferred.
11. **The clipped-source drill.** List the five places where a source column is clipped in this session - the pottery features, the third school column, the Tirthankara list beyond the sixth entry, the Vajra mudra meaning and the craft-pottery features - and rehearse the sentence that reports a gap instead of filling it.
12. **The register drill.** Write the archaeological ware names in one column and the craft pottery names in another, then write the one-sentence rule that keeps them apart.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master causal chain: from a lump of material to a repatriated icon

```text
  CLIMATE AND GEOLOGY
  monsoonal climate destroys organic media; stone, metal and
  terracotta dominate "because of climatic condition of the region"
        |
        v
  MATERIAL CHOICE DETERMINES POSSIBILITY
  steatite -> miniature incised seals
  fire-baked clay, pinched -> small domestic figures, "crude in
     shape and form"; later MOULDED -> repeatable plaques
  hard fine stone -> modelled anatomy and pleated drapery
  soft-then-hardening soapstone -> continuous minute friezes
  bronze via cire-perdue -> free limbs, open silhouette, PORTABILITY
        |
        v
  EARLY CORPUS: HARAPPAN
  seals (script undeciphered) | bronzes | terracotta | small stone
  -> LABELS are modern; IDENTITIES are not recoverable
  -> pottery ladder begins: Neolithic -> Chalcolithic -> Harappan
     Red Ware
        |
        v
  MAURYAN PERIOD: TWO STREAMS, ONE POLISH
  court: pillars, animal capitals, imperial message, NBPW
  popular: Yaksha and Yakshi, folk deities given bodies
  -> the POPULAR stream founds the later school
        |
        v
  SHUNGA AND THE ANICONIC CODE
  narrative relief; "marked development in ornamentation and
  depiction of life in its true form"
  the Buddha INDICATED, not depicted: empty throne, Bodhi tree,
  wheel, stupa
        |
        v
  KUSHANA PATRONAGE: THE SYSTEM IS FIXED
  "clear iconographic conventions established at this time for
   Buddha Boddhisattva and their Mudras"
        |
   +----+--------------------+--------------------+
   |                         |                    |
 GANDHARA                 MATHURA              AMARAVATI
 body imported            attribute system     narrative relief
 (Parthian + Central      (avayudha, linga,    (tribhanga,
  Asian + Greco-Roman)     three religions)     medallions)
   |                         |                    |
   +----+--------------------+--------------------+
        |
        v
  PRIORITY DEBATE: who gave the Buddha a body first?
  -> unresolved; parallel development is the defensible position
  -> but the CONSEQUENCE is not disputed: "transformed the course
     of the sculptural narrative of India"
        |
        v
  GUPTA: SUBTRACTION IN THE BODY, ELABORATION IN THE HALO
  cream sandstone and metal; fully dressed figures; intricate halo
  terracotta acquires a full religious programme (plaques, heads)
  post-Gupta: the RING-STIRRUP becomes a dating device
        |
        v
  TEMPLE SCULPTURE: THE SIGN SYSTEM MEETS ARCHITECTURE
  vahana placed just before the sanctum -> Nandi gets a building
  Saptamatrikas need a panel -> iconography shapes plan
  Kirtimukha, musical pillars, Yali, Vyala, swan, elephant, snake
        |
        v
  CHOLA BRONZE: THE TYPE PERFECTED
  Nataraja read attribute by attribute; natana-sabha; the same
  programme in bronze, Pallava rock-cut relief and Hoysala panel
  -> ORIGIN at Aihole under early Chalukya rule; PEAK under the Cholas
        |
        v
  REGIONAL PROGRAMMES
  Khajuraho (fine sandstone, maithuna reliefs, Vyalas, four sects)
  Hoysala (soapstone, frieze bands, NAMED SCULPTORS)
  Kakatiya (carved pillars, screens, monolithic Nandi)
        |
        v
  MUGHAL PERIOD: DIFFERENT EMPHASIS, NOT ABSENCE
  salat guilds of indigenous artisans; animate motifs; commissioned
  statues; Hindu motifs in an imperial hall
        |
        v
  LIVING REGISTERS: CRAFT, NOT STRATIGRAPHY
  GI-marked potteries; bronze and brass centres; cire-perdue "even
  today... practised in many parts of the country"
        |
        v
  THE OBJECT'S LATER LIFE
  ruin -> rediscovery -> excavation -> removal -> display -> replica
  -> and, where provenance can be documented, RETURN
```

## Master comparison 1: the three post-Mauryan schools, on six axes

| Axis | Gandhara | Mathura | Amaravati |
|---|---|---|---|
| Influence | Greek or Hellenistic, layered with a separately evidenced Parthian relief manner and with a Central Asian strand the owner names as Kushana, Parthian and Saka | Indigenous, not influenced by external cultures | Purely indigenous, no foreign influence |
| Material | Blue schist and green phyllite, then stucco, then grey stone from the third century AD | Spotted red sandstone | Greenish-white limestone |
| Scope | Mainly Buddhist | Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism | Mainly Buddhist |
| Treatment | Anatomy, Usha-Nisha hair, pleated drapery, elongated earlobes, Apollo model, barefoot, some moustaches | Avayudhas, linga and mukhalinga, large geometric halo, Abhaya Mudra, Padmapani and Vajrapani | Narrative medallions, tribhanga, railings and toranas |
| Patronage | Kushana | Kushana; the inscribed Kanishka figure | Satavahana, then Ikshvaku |
| Diffusion | Conventions for Buddha, Bodhisattva and mudras fixed here | Pan-north Indian; yaksha body type into three religions | Robe-and-Abhayamudra type into South and Southeast Asia; named Krishna-valley sites and Ter |

## Master comparison 2: the four dating devices in this topic

| Device | What it dates | Direction of the limit | Caution |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ceramic ware** | A layer, a culture, a site phase | Both, within a range | A ware dates a layer, not an idea; never infer a people |
| **Mauryan polish** | A workshop standard | Suggestive only | Supports the Didarganj attribution but does not settle it |
| **Gandhara material sequence** | A phase within one school | Forward - grey stone wholly from the third century AD | Applies to Gandhara only |
| **Ring-stirrup** | An equestrian relief | Lower limit - not earlier than post-Gupta | Gives no upper limit and applies only to horse subjects |

## Master comparison 3: the three identification systems

| | Buddhist | Jain | Hindu |
|---|---|---|---|
| Primary sign | **Mudra** - hand position with assigned meaning | **Lanchana** - an assigned emblem, with colour | **Ayudha** - carried attribute |
| Secondary sign | Ornament status: plain and barefoot for the Buddha, ornamented with hairdos and sandals for the Bodhisattva | Nudity or white robe for sect; the rajoharana and kamandalu | **Vahana** - the mount, placed just before the sanctum |
| Aniconic option | Empty throne, wheel, stupa, Bodhi tree | Not carried here | **Linga** and **mukhalinga** |
| Group form | Buddha flanked by two Bodhisattvas | Tirthankara with yaksha and yakshi attendants | **Saptamatrikas** - a group of seven |
| Why the system takes this form | Roles and states must be distinguished | Doctrinally identical individuals must be distinguished | Many deities with relations, powers and vehicles must be distinguished |

## Master comparison 4: the two registers of Indian pottery and metalwork

| | Archaeological register | Craft register |
|---|---|---|
| Named by | Ware | Place and product |
| Examples | Harappan Red Ware, Painted Grey Ware, NBPW, Black and Red Ware, black slipped ware | Khurja, Nizamabad Black, Blue, Pokhran, Dalgate, Karigari; Bastar Dhokra, Pembarthi |
| Defined by | Fabric, firing, distribution, stratigraphy | Workshop, region, market, geographical indication |
| Evidence type | Excavation | Contemporary production and registration |
| Used to | Date a layer, name a culture, trace exchange | Identify a living tradition, protect a name, sustain a livelihood |
| Legitimate bridge | The **technique** - cire-perdue known from the Indus period and still practised in many parts of the country - and the **medium**, not the category |

## Argument map: what this topic actually proves

```text
  CLAIM: Indian sculpture can be read as historical evidence.
     |
     +-- SUPPORT 1: it uses an assigned, standardised sign system
     |     evidence: conventions for Buddha, Bodhisattva and mudras
     |     fixed under Kushana rule; avayudhas at Mathura; lanchanas
     |     in Jain temple iconography; the ten Nataraja attributes
     |        |
     |        +-- OBJECTION: signs are conventional, so a reader
     |        |   outside the tradition sees only a dancer
     |        +-- REPLY: conventionality is what makes the reading
     |            checkable; the meanings are recorded, not guessed
     |
     +-- SUPPORT 2: material and technique are physical facts
     |     evidence: the climate argument; the Gandhara material
     |     sequence; soapstone hardening; the ring-stirrup limit
     |        |
     |        +-- OBJECTION: technique gives boundaries, not dates
     |        +-- REPLY: a boundary is still evidence, and it is
     |            harder to dispute than a stylistic impression
     |
     +-- SUPPORT 3: patronage is recorded and sometimes named
     |     evidence: Kushana, Satavahana, Ikshvaku, Chandella,
     |     Hoysala; Sembiyan Mahadevi; the named Hoysala sculptors
     |        |
     |        +-- OBJECTION: patronage records elite choice only
     |        +-- REPLY: conceded, and that is why the evidence
     |            ladder stops before belief and behaviour
     |
     +-- LIMIT 1: identities are unrecoverable where the script is
     |   undeciphered - Pashupati, Mother Goddess, Priest-King
     +-- LIMIT 2: several key dates are contested - Didarganj,
     |   Sultanganj, the first anthropomorphic Buddha image
     +-- LIMIT 3: the sources themselves are damaged - clipped
     |   columns in five tables used in this session
     +-- LIMIT 4: objects move, so find-site is not always
         original site - Amaravati's marbles; the bronzes returned
         after documentary provenance research
     |
  QUALIFIED CONCLUSION: Indian sculpture is reliable evidence of
  technique, patron choice and the sign system in use, and it is
  unreliable evidence of ordinary belief and behaviour. An answer
  that states this distinction inside itself is doing the
  historian's work rather than the tourist's.
```

## Answer spines, side by side

| Demand family | Spine |
|---|---|
| **Gandhara external elements** | Thesis: Buddhist programme in a Hellenised idiom under Kushana patronage -> Central Asian strand named -> Greco-Bactrian strand named -> the drapery that spans both -> one Mathura contrast -> the Indian core -> verdict |
| **School comparison** | Axes named -> influence -> material -> scope -> treatment -> patronage -> diffusion -> problem-solved framing -> verdict |
| **Animal symbolism** | Two-functions thesis -> mythology leg, bounded -> art leg chronologically -> architecture leg by placement -> change over time -> source qualification -> national-emblem close |
| **Symbol to image** | Aniconic code as positive practice -> the empty seat as presence-marker -> what did not change, the abundance of other imagery -> Kushana standardisation -> priority bracketed -> transformation claim |
| **Named image iconography** | Identify school and material -> attribute by attribute in order -> group the attributes by function -> placement -> comparative set -> origin-versus-peak correction if relevant |
| **Sculpture as social evidence** | Inference rule first -> technical level -> commission level -> depiction level -> the stop before belief and behaviour -> named case -> what cannot be claimed |
| **Pottery styles** | Register distinction first -> archaeological ladder or craft centres as the question requires -> mechanism -> the clipped-source limit stated -> verdict |
| **Material and technique** | Material determines possibility -> two or three worked cases -> the survival filter -> relief against work in the round -> verdict |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. Scope, the six variables and the three kinds of date

- Owns: sculpture and pottery from the Harappan civilisation to the Mughal period; the iconographic vocabulary; the living clay and metal craft registers.
- Routes out: the monument to the architecture topics; dynastic politics to history; doctrinal meaning to the religion topic; dance posture as performance to the performing-arts topic; heritage law, counts, institutions and changeable status to the heritage topic.
- Six variables: material; posture and gesture; iconographic attribute; patron; site context; cross-site comparison.
- Three kinds of date: when made; the phase or school the object belongs to; when found, excavated, moved, displayed or returned. Say which kind you mean.
- Label is not identity: Pashupati, Mother Goddess, Priest-King, Dancing Girl are modern names; an inscription, where it exists, outranks every stylistic inference.
- Two registers never merged: archaeological ware against craft pottery.

## 2. Materials, techniques and the relief-round distinction

- Stone, metal and terracotta dominate partly because of the region's climatic conditions; the surviving corpus is durability-filtered.
- Terracotta is fire-baked clay; Harappan examples were pinched and are recorded as fewer and cruder than the bronzes, from Gujarat sites and Kalibangan - the so-called Mother Goddess, a horned-deity mask, toys; plough models at Bahawalpur and Banawali are technological evidence.
- Cire-perdue in five steps: wax model; wet clay coat, dried; heated so the wax melts; wax poured out through a tiny hole and liquid metal poured in; cooled, then the clay coat removed. Known from the Indus period; still practised in many parts of the country.
- Consequences: unique casting, formal freedom, portability, unbroken technique.
- Workshop stones: steatite for seals; spotted red sandstone for Mathura; bluish-grey sandstone with blue schist and green phyllite for early Gandhara; greenish-white limestone for Amaravati; cream-coloured sandstone for Gupta Sarnath; chloritic schist for the Hoysalas.
- Stucco is lime plaster; it marks the middle phase of the Gandhara material sequence.
- Relief carries narrative; work in the round permits circumambulation, installation and procession. Early Buddhist sculpture decorated stupas on the torana and medhi; the vahana stands just before the sanctum.

## 3. The Harappan corpus and the limits of naming

- Commonest finds: seals, bronze figures, potteries; the sculptors were extremely adept at handling three-dimensional volumes.
- Seals: mostly square, also triangular, rectangular, circular; mostly steatite, also agate, chert, copper, faience, terracotta, with some gold and ivory; pictographic undeciphered script, mostly right to left, sometimes bi-directional; generally five intaglio animal impressions.
- Motifs: unicorn, humped bull, rhinoceros, tiger, elephant, buffalo, bison, goat, markhor, ibex, crocodile. **No cow and no lion on any seal.**
- Uses: commerce and communication; trade evidence at Mesopotamia and Lothal; perforated examples on dead bodies as amulets; mathematical images suggesting instruction; swastika-like symbols.
- Named seals: Pashupati and Unicorn. The Pashupati figure sits cross-legged in a three-horned headgear, with elephant and tiger on one side, rhinoceros and buffalo on the other, two antelopes below the seat.
- Dancing Girl: bronze, Mohenjo-daro, four inches, ornaments only - bangles on the left arm, amulet and bracelet on the right - in tribhanga with the right hand on the hip; recorded as the world's oldest bronze sculpture and dated to around 2500 BC.
- Bearded figure: steatite, trefoil-patterned shawl, elongated half-closed eyes. Male torso: red sandstone from Harappa, frontal, socket holes in neck and shoulders.
- Ornaments: necklaces, fillets, armlets, finger rings for both sexes; girdles, earrings, anklets for women only; carnelian, amethyst, quartz and steatite beads; bead-making at Chanhudaro and Lothal; cotton and wool spun by rich and poor alike.

## 4. The ceramic ladder and its two naming problems

- Neolithic, c. 7000-2000 BC: hand-made coarse ware, thick fabric, cord or impressed surface.
- Chalcolithic, c. 3000-1000 BC: painted pottery at Ahar, Malwa, Jorwe; red ware with painted geometric motifs; hand-made or slow wheel.
- Harappan, c. 2600-1900 BC: Harappan Red Ware, wheel-made, black geometric painting; two kinds, plain and painted; most very fine wheel-made, very few handmade, polychrome rare.
- Three uses: plain pottery for household storage of grain and water; miniature vessels under half an inch for decoration; perforated vessels with a large basal hole and small side holes, possibly for straining liquor.
- **Naming problem one:** the core account calls the painted variety Black and Red Ware; the canonical owner refuses the equation with the separate wider ceramic category. State both, equate neither.
- Painted Grey Ware, 1100-500/400 BC: very high-quality clay; thin, fine, smooth; simple geometric patterns in black; uniformity indicating very advanced firing; a small fraction of the assemblage; coexisting with plain grey ware, Black and Red Ware and black slipped ware; from the Himalayan foothills to the Malwa plateau, from Bahawalpur to Kaushambi, and in Kumaon and Garhwal.
- Northern Black Polished Ware: black paint, highly lustrous, luxury items, called the highest level of pottery; Pataliputra, Kaushambi, Rajghat, Vaishali, Taxila, Ujjain, Vidisha.
- **Naming problem two:** "Mauryan pottery" is a period label of convenience; NBPW is a fine lustrous tableware, often black or steel-grey but also other shades, associated especially with early historic urbanisation.
- Sites: Sothi in Rajasthan c. 4600 BC; Siswal at Hisar c. 3800 BC with the six fabrics of Kalibangan; Lohari Ragho; Burzahom in Kashmir, 3000-1000 BC; Chandraketugarh with terracotta sculpture, NBPW relics, Maurya-Kushana-Gupta sculpture, punch-marked and gold coins.
- Megalithic burial forms attested: dolmens, cairns, menhirs, cists; the associated ware detail is not carried here.

## 5. Mauryan sculpture: two streams, one polish

- Functions: decoration of stupas on the torana and medhi, and religious expression. The Jetavana monastery at Sravasti with mango trees and the banker Anathapindika is a named example.
- Yakshas: folk deities of water, fertility, trees, forest, wilderness; nature spirits or demigods; coexisted with Vedic ritual; worshipped for boons and protection; guardians residing formlessly on trees and water bodies; worshipped in all three religions; attendants of the Bodhisattva and the Tirthankara.
- Yakshi: benign female counterpart, connected with fertility, associated with the Ashoka tree, often a gatekeeper of Hindu and Buddhist shrines; earliest mention in the Tamil Silappadikaram; every Tirthankara associated with a yakshi.
- Parkham Yaksha: south of Mathura; massive strength and gravity in imagery. Didarganj Yakshi: chauri or whisk bearer, recorded Mauryan, polish supporting it, later dating argued.
- Shalabhanjika: a yakshi holding a tree, also called a tree deity; bracket figures on the gateways at Sanchi and Bharhut; auspicious in Buddhism; Hoysala reliefs at Belur, Halebidu, Somanathapura; the source's own inference about pre-Buddhist practices enriching Buddhism.
- Gajalakshmi: an Ashtalakshmi aspect standing for animal wealth and other symbols of wealth representing strength; Bharhut railings; a third-century AD Kausambi coin; the Sanchi vedika with two elephants and lotuses; Kalinga temples in lalitasana.
- Mathura's images were modelled on the earlier Mauryan yaksha images - the popular stream founds the school.

## 6. The animal capitals and the lion-bull argument

- Pillars: average 40 ft, chunar sandstone, monolithic shaft, lotus- or bell-shaped capital, abacus, animal figure; bell capitals and the lustrous polish attributed to Iranian influence; the Lion Capital excavated by F. O. Oertel in 1904-05.
- Sarnath: four lions back to back on a circular abacus with bull, horse, lion and elephant and the Dharmachakra; horse west, elephant east, bull south, lion north, turning the wheel of existence till eternity; elephant for Maya's dream, bull for the Taurus birth month, horse for Kanthaka, lion for enlightenment; the four lions for Dharma spread in all directions, commemorating the Dharmachakrapravartana.
- National emblem: the abacus and the animal part, adopted 26 January 1950; Satyameva Jayate from the Mundaka Upanishad below the abacus in Devanagari; crowned by the Dharma Chakra.
- Sanchi: an Ashokan Lion Capital pillar of finely polished sandstone, very similar to Sarnath's - four lions back to back, but a flatter abacus with alternating pairs of geese and flame palmettes; dated around 250 BC; two sets of inscriptions, Ashoka's Schism Edict warning of punishment for dissent in the sangha or order, and a later Gupta-period inscription in the ornamental Sankha Lipi, a conch-shaped script not yet deciphered although Prinsep had deciphered the Brahmi of the Ashokan pillars. Capital and later inscription are separate chronological claims. Schism Edicts are recorded at Sanchi, Sarnath and Allahabad, originally in Kaushambi - which identifies the clipped Sarnath inscription cell.
- Vaishali (Kolhua): single lion capital, no abacus; Major Pillar Edict VI. Rampurva: lion capital pillar inscribed with Edicts I-VI, bull capital pillar uninscribed. Seven Major Pillar Edict sites; only Delhi-Topra has all seven.
- The six Nandi cases: Thanjavur's Nandi Mandap monolith, the second largest in the country; Lepakshi's, the largest; Ellora Cave 16, the two-storeyed Kailasa housing a shivalingam, its courtyard carrying two huge pillars with the flagstaff and a Nandi mandapa - the arrangement excavated rather than built; the Thousand Pillar temple at Warangal, a monolithic Nandi with rock-cut elephants; Ramappa, where the main temple stands in front of the Nandi Pavilion on a star-shaped platform; and the Hoysala Nandi Pavilion with a separate kalyani, noted for ceremonial use. No monolithic Nandi is recorded for Ramappa, and the owner's combined formulation is kept disaggregated.
- The argument: bull attached to birth, generative power and attendance; lion to authority, doctrine and guardianship. Lion absent from Harappan seals; bronze bull of Kalibangan present. Medieval divergence: Nandi stays naturalistic, singular and axial; the lion hybridises into Yali and Vyala and multiplies on gopurams, pillars and friezes.

## 7. The Shunga turn and the aniconic code

- Shungas replace the Mauryas in 185 BCE; marked development in ornamentation and the depiction of life in its true form; Bharhut Stupa in Satna district, its northern gateway the finest example; the Bharhut Yavana, a warrior relief dated 100 BCE.
- Chronological order: Bharhut 150 BC, Second Sanchi 125 BC, Bodhgaya 75 BC, Great Stupa of Sanchi 50 BC, Third Sanchi 25 BC.
- The code: birth by bull, elephant, lotus, footprint; great renunciation by horse, Gandhakuti, empty throne, begging bowl; enlightenment by vajrasana and Bodhi tree; sermons by wheel, lion, deer; death by stupa.
- The summary rule: presence indicated through empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel and stupa, for renunciation, enlightenment, first sermon and death.
- The empty seat is a presence-marker; context - Bodhi tree, wheel and deer, riderless horse - disambiguates the episode. The table's assignment and the summary listing are both recorded and are not resolved here.
- The Amaravati ayaka pillars, typically five per platform at four cardinal directions, are believed to represent birth, renunciation, enlightenment, Dharmachakra Pravartana and Mahaparinirvana - an episode scheme, not a device scheme.
- The shift to the human image is a change in devotional practice, not in skill.

## 8. Gandhara: region, strands, materials, conventions

- Region: Peshawar Valley, modern Swat, Buner, Bajaur. Three recorded phases of political control, and no more: Achaemenid Persia in the sixth century BC; Alexander and then Chandragupta Maurya in the fourth - a Greek intervention followed by Indian imperial integration, not a second foreign occupation; Kushanas in the first century AD. No total number of powers and no span of years is asserted. The source's own conclusion, carried as its interpretation, is that the people were thoroughly cosmopolitan in culture and outlook.
- Purpose: glorify the Buddha through his life episodes and the Jataka. Consequence: the human form under this school transformed the course of Indian sculptural narrative.
- Parthian strand: Swat Valley sculptures closely linked with Parthian rather than Greco-Roman art; a standing Buddha flanked by Indra and Brahma as one of the earliest examples; a seated Buddha with a halo with standing Indra and Brahma; relief carving and deeply incised facial lines.
- Central Asian or Bactro-Gandhara strand: named geographically by the core source and by people in the owner as **Kushana, Parthian and Saka**, with the caution that no Saka-specific feature, date, ruler or monument is recorded anywhere in the owned evidence; Bactria between today's Afghanistan and Tajikistan; blue schist and green phyllite, then stucco, then wholly grey stone from the third century AD; religious themes and royal portraits; Kushana rule bringing major upliftment to the Buddha's portrayal; Kanishka's territory from Mathura through Gandhara and Bactria to the borders of China; clear conventions fixed for the Buddha, the Bodhisattva and their mudras, including Dharmachakra Parivartan and Abhay Hasta.
- Greco-Roman strand: anatomical accuracy, spatial width, foresighting; Usha-Nisha wavy hair; slender muscled body on a fit-athlete model; heavily pleated garments inspired by Greek and Roman and also Parthian; elongated earlobes symbolising royal connection; Apollo as an occasional model; barefoot; some images with moustaches.
- Bodhisattvas: Maitreya, then Avalokitesvara; heavily ornamented with elaborate hairdos and sandals, unlike the Buddha.
- The Indian core not borrowed: subject, mudras, halo, monastic robe, Indra and Brahma companions.

## 9. Mathura: indigenous school, three religions, attribute method

- Emerges in the first century AD as an indigenous school characterised by red sandstone, its noteworthy early works Yaksha and Yakshi in highly polished sandstone; flourishes on the Yamuna between the first and third centuries AD.
- Influenced by the stories and imageries of Buddhism, Hinduism and Jainism; images modelled on earlier Mauryan yaksha images.
- Comparison-table values: developed indigenously, not influenced by external cultures; spotted red sandstone; influence of all three religions.
- Avayudha method: Hindu gods represented by their attribute-emblems; Shiva through linga and mukhalinga. Procedure: inscription, then attribute, then gesture, then surrounding scheme, then style and material.
- Buddha image: halo larger than Gandhara's and geometrically decorated; flanked by Padmapani with a lotus and Vajrapani with a thunderbolt; largely carved in Abhaya Mudra.
- Cult Yakshas: Kubera, Manibhadra, Mudgarpani.
- The headless figure discovered in 1911 outside Mathura by Pandit Radha Krishna, with a Brahmi base inscription declaring it to be Kanishka - royal portraiture, a dated anchor, and the proof that epigraphy outranks style.

## 10. Amaravati: narrative art and the school that travelled

- Southern India on the Krishna river under Satavahana patronage; also the Vengi School; the site a major trade centre from the third century BC to the twelfth century AD; the Satavahanas primarily Brahmanical but great patrons of Buddhism and Jainism; the Ikshvakus patronising the school in the third-fourth centuries AD, with Nagarjunakonda's sculptures largely Ikshvaku-patronised.
- Developed on purely indigenous lines with no foreign influence.
- Narrative art against single images; tribhanga used excessively; greenish-white limestone railings, panels, gateways and medallions; one medallion displaying the entire story of the Buddha taming an elephant.
- The iconic type - robe on the left shoulder, the other hand in Abhayamudra indicating fearlessness - replicated in other parts of South and Southeast Asia.
- Great influence on successive South Indian dynasties; style at Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala, Goli and as far west as Ter.
- Hiuen Tsang visited in 640 AD and studied the Abhidhamma Pitaka there; Dharmakeerti called Amaravati a prominent ancient centre of Buddhist learning.
- Provenance: ruin; Mackenzie's visit in 1797 leading to rediscovery; Elliot's first excavation in 1845 and the Amaravati or Elliot marbles in the British Museum; Burgess's findings in 1887; ASI conservation and a replica near the original location. The Mahachaitya's height is "believed to be originally 27 metres".

## 11. Comparing the schools and bracketing priority

- Six axes: external influence, material, religious scope, treatment and format, patronage, diffusion.
- The printed comparison table available here is clipped after the Mathura column; Amaravati's values come from its own sections.
- Analytical framing: Gandhara gives the Buddha a body; Mathura gives many deities one grammar distinguished by attribute; Amaravati tells a story on a curved surface.
- Priority: overlapping early-centuries-CE emergence at both northern schools under Kushana patronage; debated; parallel development is the defensible position; the transformation claim is about consequence, not sequence.
- Symbolic and figural representation coexist: the linga inside a figural workshop; Buddhist symbols alongside life scenes at Amaravati.

## 12. The Gupta idiom, its terracotta and a dating device

- The Gupta Empire, 319 AD to 550 AD, is called a "classical" era in quotation marks; the same source dates Ellora's murals to the sixth-ninth centuries under the Rashtrakutas and other dynasties - a tension to disclose.
- Sarnath school: cream-coloured sandstone and metal; sculptures immaculately dressed with no nakedness; an intricately decorated halo.
- Sultanganj Buddha: 7.5 ft, Bihar, copper; a c. seventh-century date makes late or post-Gupta the safer description.
- Gupta terracotta in two classes - gods and goddesses; male and female figures - with Buddha figures and ornaments at Devnimori in Gujarat, plaque collections at Bikaner and Ahichchhatra, distinctive heads from Akhnur in Kashmir, and numerous objects from Bhita. Plaques are moulded, repeatable and widely distributed.
- Ring-stirrup: a circular or oval loop on a saddle, shown hanging from it; appears from the post-Gupta period onwards, particularly in early medieval North and South Indian temples; introduced via Central Asian contacts; examples at Hoysala Belur and Halebidu, Khajuraho panels, Chalukyan Badami, Aihole and Pattadakal, and Konark. It gives a lower limit only.

## 13. The Buddhist identification system

- Mudras: Bhumisparsha, right hand to earth and left in lap, calling the Earth to witness and enlightenment; Dhyana, hands in lap with thumbs forming a triangle, deep meditation and spiritual perfection; Vitarka, thumb and index forming a circle with the hand at the chest, teaching, debate and wisdom flow; Dharmachakra, hands at the chest with circles formed, teaching of Dharma; Uttarabodhi, fingers interlaced with index fingers up, enlightenment, energy and perfection; Karana, index and little finger up, banishing negativity and obstacles; Vajra, left forefinger in the right fist, supreme wisdom, with the printed meaning clipped; Abhaya or Abhay Hasta, a gesture indicating fearlessness, benediction and reassurance.
- Discriminations: Vitarka is one hand and one circle, Dharmachakra is hands and circles; Karana raises index and little finger, Uttarabodhi interlaces with index fingers up.
- Recorded postures: vajrasana as an enlightenment symbol; tribhanga; lalitasana for Gajalakshmi in Kalinga temples; seated cross-legged; standing.
- Buddha identifiers: monastic robe, sometimes on the left shoulder; halo, its treatment naming the school; elongated earlobes; bare feet; no jewellery; some Gandhara images with moustaches.
- Bodhisattva identifiers: heavy ornament, elaborate hairdos, sandals; Maitreya, Avalokitesvara, Padmapani with a lotus, Vajrapani with a thunderbolt.
- The procedure yields type, school and approximate period only; an inscription overrides it; the Marichi sculpture in Bihar is recorded as integrating different religious beliefs and practices.

## 14. The Jain and Hindu identification systems

- Jain: 24 Tirthankaras, doctrinally equivalent - all attained kevala jnana and moksha and taught ahimsa, truth, non-possession, celibacy and self-discipline - so an assigned emblem is necessary. Recorded lanchanas: Rishabhanatha or Adinath bull, golden; Ajitanatha elephant, golden; Sambhavanatha horse, golden; Abhinandananatha monkey, golden; Sumatinatha curlew or heron, golden; Padmaprabha red lotus, red; Mahavira lion. Symbols and colours are commonly used in Jain temple iconography. The printed list is clipped after the sixth entry.
- Sectarian markers: Digambara strict nudity, Svetambara white robes; the monk's rajoharana and kamandalu; Digambara Aryikas in unstitched plain white sarees. An ashtamangala of the Svetambara sect is recorded with four arms and nine compulsory corners, associated with the 18th Tirthankara Aranatha. Sittanavasal in Tamil Nadu, first century BC to tenth century AD, is famous for Tirthankara sculptures.
- Hindu: gods represented by their avayudhas; Shiva through linga and mukhalinga; the vahana is the mount of the main deity, placed just before the sanctum sanctorum.
- Shiva manifestations: Lingodbhava, an infinite column of light, at Chidambaram and Mahabalipuram; Dakshinamurthy, teacher of wisdom in silent meditation, in Chola temples and Badami cave temples; Kalyanasundara, the divine wedding, in Pallava, Chola and Hoysala sculptures; Ardhanarishvara, the unity of masculine and feminine, at Elephanta and in Mathura sculptures; Nataraja, the cosmic cycle, at Aihole, Badami, Thanjavur, Chidambaram and Mahabalipuram.
- The 9th-century Kalyanasundara Murti renders Panigrahana, the marriage ceremony, in two separate statuettes.
- Saptamatrikas: shaktis of deities like Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesvara; one of the oldest depictions at the Kailasanatha temple, Kanchipuram; identified as a group.
- Krishna types: Bala Krishna at Mathura and in South India; Govardhan Leela at Mathura, Rajasthan and South India; Kaliya Mardan at Mathura, Khajuraho, Tamil Nadu and Karnataka; Raas Leela at Khajuraho, in Pahari work and South India; Venugopala in Tamil Nadu and Karnataka. Vaishnava types are episodic; Shaiva aniconic representation is emblematic.

## 15. South Indian temple sculpture and the architectural boundary

- Corpus from temples of the Chalukyas, Pallavas, Pandyas and Cholas.
- Kirtimukha: a monster face with huge fangs, a frequent decorative motif, very common in Hindu temple architecture in India and Southeast Asia and often found in Buddhist architecture; no narrative meaning is assigned.
- Musical sculptures: sculpture with musical instruments of granite architectural elements, a unique southern feature, frequent in Tamil Nadu; earliest recovered from Kodumbalur; the Musical Pillars of the Vittala Temple at Hampi, the SaReGaMa pillars. Architecture, sculpture and instrument at once; counts, tuning and mechanism are not recorded.
- Saptamatrikas as a distinct southern feature, requiring a panel or subsidiary shrine - iconography shaping plan.
- Other features: animal and bird motifs; elephants and snakes; swan motifs in Pallava temples; the Yali, with the head and body of a lion and the trunk and tusks of an elephant, and in the Nayaka material a composite lion figure symbolic of Nayaka architectural identity, described in Tamil literature with a cat-like body, a lion's head with elephant tusks and a serpent's tail, more powerful than lion, tiger or elephant, and a guardian protecting human beings physically and spiritually. Vyala at Khajuraho, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies.
- Boundary: figure, motif, group and sign system here; gopuram, vimana, mandapa, prakaram, plan, elevation and style school to the architecture topics.

## 16. The Nataraja and the Chola bronze

- Chola temples placed importance on decoration through sculptures; Shiva as Nataraja, Lord of Dance, in the Tandava posture.
- Earliest known Nataraja excavated at the Ravana Phadi Cave at Aihole under early Chalukya rule; the sculpture reached its peak under the Cholas.
- Ten elements: rear right hand, the drum, the sound of creation; rear left hand, the eternal fire, destruction as the precursor and counterpart of creation; front right hand, abhaya mudra, benediction and reassurance; front left hand, gajahasta, pointing to the upraised foot and the path of salvation; left leg in bhujangatrasita, tirobhava, kicking away the veil of maya; the dwarf Muyalaka, ignorance and the ego; matted flowing locks, the Ganges; one male and one female earring, the Ardhanarishvara fusion; a snake on the arm, dormant kundalini; a nimbus of glowing lights, the vast unending cycles of time.
- Grouping: rear pair cosmological, front pair devotional, three elements concerning illusion and ignorance, three framing elements.
- Many South Indian Shiva temples keep a separate natana-sabha. Notable examples: the Chola bronze masterpiece at Chidambaram; stone reliefs in Pallava cave temples; stone panels on Hoysala temple walls; the Thanjavur Brihadeeswara Nataraja.
- Process: the Indus-period cire-perdue sequence; bronze is copper mixed with tin; the Matsya Purana describes bronze casting methods and texts like the Rasaratnakara discuss metal purity and zinc distillation; the Cholas are recorded as still more famous for their extraordinary temples and bronze sculptures.
- Patron: Sembiyan Mahadevi, a 10th-century AD Chola queen and patron of the arts, built temples at Kutralam, Virudhachalam and Aduthurai.

## 17. Regional programmes and the evidence ladder

- Khajuraho: fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale yellow shades; lofty temples without enclosure walls on a high platform terrace; maithuna reliefs on both Hindu and Jain temples; Vyalas on the Vishnu temples; the Chausath-yogini shrine as the earliest building, circular and open-air, reflecting Tantric and Shaiva traditions; six temples to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun, four to Jain Tirthankaras; Matangeshvara still active.
- Hoysala: chloritic schist or soapstone, soft when quarried and hardening over time; outer walls covered with friezes of Ramayana and Mahabharata scenes, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs; lathe-turned pillars; frieze bands of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans and deities; the Nandi Pavilion and a separate kalyani noted for ceremonial use; Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma recorded as prominent architects and sculptors.
- Kakatiya: the Thousand Pillar temple at Warangal, ordered by Rudra Deva and built between the twelfth and fourteenth centuries; a thousand richly carved pillars, perforated screens, decorative icons, rock-cut elephants and a monolithic Nandi; three shrines called the Trikutalayam; damaged by the Tughlaq dynasty in the fourteenth century. At the Ramappa temple, separately, the main temple stands in front of the Nandi Pavilion on a star-shaped platform; no monolithic Nandi is recorded there.
- Ellora sculpture within the boundary: Vaishnavite avatars of Vishnu; the Atiriktanga-Bhairava, a skeleton form of Shiva; Ganga and Yamuna flanking the entrance of Cave 21, Rameshwar Lena - and the Nagara convention placing the river goddesses outside the garbhagriha. Cave 16, the two-storeyed Kailasa housing a shivalingam, carries the Nandi mandapa and two huge pillars with the flagstaff in its courtyard - the axial bull arrangement excavated rather than built (register notes 6).
- The evidence ladder: technically possible, commissioned, depicted, and - not directly evidenced - believed and lived. Strong evidence of patron choice and available technique; weak evidence of ordinary belief and behaviour.

## 18. Mughal-period figural work

- The recorded general claim is that Islamic rulers were not interested in producing figurative sculptures; the evidence qualifies it.
- Akbar was concerned with architecture and sculpture, continued by Jahangir and Shah Jahan; he summoned salat guilds of indigenous artisans to experiment in art, and a unity in the art of India was developed.
- Animate motifs carved in red sandstone at Agra and Fatehpur Sikri; statues of Jai Mal and Fatha, Rajput heroes of Chittor, commissioned by Akbar; stone elephant mouths on the Delhi gate of the Agra fort; Akbar's tomb built in 1605-1613 by Jahangir at Sikandra; Hindu motifs - svastika, srivatsa, lotus - in the Mayura Mandapa or Peacock Hall of the Agra Fort; mini-monuments of marble, jalis and miniature animals from the time of Shah Jahan.
- The accurate formulation is different emphasis, not absence.

## 19. The living clay and metal registers

- Craft pottery: Khurja Pottery (GI), Bulandshahr district, Uttar Pradesh; Nizamabad Black Pottery (GI), Azamgarh district, Uttar Pradesh; Blue Pottery (GI), Jaipur; Pokhran Pottery (GI), Pokhran; Dalgate Pottery, Jammu and Kashmir; Karigari Pottery, Tamil Nadu. The feature column is clipped and is not reconstructed.
- Gundiyali and Lodai, famous pottery-making villages of Kutch, Gujarat; in Bengal, burnt bricks and clay known as terracotta bricks were the principal building material.
- Bronze: one of the oldest art forms, evinced by the Dancing Girl dated to around 2500 BC; copper and tin mixed to form bronze; the Matsya Purana on casting methods and the Rasaratnakara on metal purity and zinc distillation; Uttar Pradesh takes precedence with Etawah, Sitapur, Varanasi and Moradabad producing flower pots, images of gods and goddesses and the ritual objects tamrapatra, kanchantal and panchpatra; Tamil Nadu known for statues inspired by Pallava, Chola, Pandya and Nayaka art.
- Brass: Bastar Dhokra (GI) for brass ornaments; Pembarthi metal craft (GI) in sheet brass for chariots and temples; Pahaldar Lamps in copper and brass; the Gaja Tandava showing Shiva dancing in the Tandava posture; rare Jain imagery reflecting ancient Jain pilgrimage centres in Karnataka. Other metal crafts: casting in iron, copper and bell metal; the Marori work of Rajasthan, etching the base metal and filling the gaps with black lac; repousse for raised designs and engraving for cut-line patterns.
- Verified continuity is limited to three things: cire-perdue still practised in many parts of the country; the Kutch pottery villages; the Bengal terracotta-brick tradition. Bankura, Molela and any other living terracotta centre are not supported here.

## 20. Attribution, provenance, status and answer construction

- Attribution ladder: inscriptional, then excavational, then technical or typological, then stylistic. A lower-level claim cannot overturn a higher-level one.
- Contested points with safe formulations: Harappan identities; Didarganj chronology; first human Buddha image; Sultanganj date; Nataraja origin; NBPW attribution; Black and Red Ware; the Gupta "classical" label and Ellora; the empty seat's reference; the two Yali anatomies; Mughal figural sculpture; living terracotta centres.
- Provenance is an object's recoverable history; the Amaravati chain from manufacture through ruin, 1797, 1845, 1887 to conservation and replica is the worked example.
- The single dated current linkage: a report of 14 May 2026 recording the reclaiming of three bronzes from a United States museum collection - a Chola Shiva Nataraja, a sixteenth-century Vijayanagara Saint Sundarar with Paravai and a twelfth-century Chola Somaskanda - after Archaeological Survey of India provenance research using archival records, field documentation and 1950s-1960s temple photographs established unauthorised removal from Tamil Nadu temples; two reached New Delhi and the Nataraja returns in 2028 under a long-term loan; the objects are to go to the Gallery of Retrieved Antiquities at the Red Fort. Law, policy and any further status claim belong to the heritage topic.
- Demand map: nine question shapes with what each is really testing. Five theses: method, influence, parallel development, peak-not-origin, material. Mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20.
- The risk list is a list of forfeitures; avoiding all of it outperforms any single additional fact.

## Grounding sources deliberately not opened

4. **The canonical Basic owner's second and third grounding sources were not opened, and this is a deliberate restriction rather than an oversight.** That owner records its grounding as Nitin Singhania's *Indian Art and Culture* together with **R.S. Sharma, *India's Ancient Past*, PDF pp. 211-223 and 347-355**, and **R.S. Sharma, *Ancient India*, PDF pp. 121-123 and 156-159**. The bounded-research rule for this workflow permits **one** core OCR source for the topic, and that slot was assigned to the Nitin Singhania volume because it is the direct chapter-level spine for sculpture, pottery and iconography. The two R.S. Sharma volumes were therefore **not opened, not extracted, not searched and not cited** at any point in this pass, and **no second OCR research pass was run to reach them**.
5. **What was carried instead, and how.** The findings for which the Basic owner credits R.S. Sharma are carried **through the owner's own formulations**, at the owner's authority rather than at the book's, and are labelled in the lessons as canonical positions rather than as book citations. They are two, and both are cautions rather than new facts: first, that **both Gandhara and Mathura produced anthropomorphic Buddha images in the Kushana age, so priority between them remains debated** - carried in Lesson 12's priority table and exam-safe paragraph; and second, that the **proto-Shiva identification of the so-called Pashupati seal is explicitly doubtful**, the script being undeciphered - carried in Lessons 1, 3 and 20 and in the risk list. ⚠️ Because these are carried at owner authority, this session does **not** attribute any page, wording or further claim to either R.S. Sharma volume, and a reader who needs the underlying book passages must consult them separately.

## Bounded material-fact pass

11. **One finite pass, pre-declared and then closed.** The bounded-research rule permits a single material-fact pass for genuinely disputed points or for a short pre-declared list of standard connecting facts whose omission would create a real syllabus gap. The list was fixed at three items before the pass began, and the pass was closed after them.
   - **Item one, the exact wording and response choices of the owned 2026 Preliminary item.** The routing ledger records only a neutral demand rendering, which would not support teaching the item's actual discrimination. The locally held official Set-A paper was opened and the question and its four response choices were read directly. No key was consulted and none is recorded.
   - **Item two, the exact wording of the two adjacent 2024 and 2025 Mains sculpture questions.** The ledgers record neutral renderings; the locally held official papers were opened and the printed English wording, marks and word limits were read directly, including the printed spelling "vigor" in the 2025 item, so that the boundary between this topic and the temple-architecture topic could be stated against the actual text rather than against a paraphrase.
   - **Item three, the disputed internal chronology of the empty-seat device.** The owned evidence contains a table assignment and a summary listing that do not resolve to one episode. Both were re-read in the core source at the relevant page and are reported side by side; **no external source was consulted to resolve them**, and the session records the point as unresolved rather than expanding research or choosing a convenient formulation.
12. No other external research was conducted for factual material. No optional source, no exhaustive corroboration across multiple books and no decorative current affairs was pursued, and the pass was closed after these three items. A later surgical repair pass **re-read five pages of the same single core OCR source** - PDF pp. 43, 47, 61, 107 and 109 - to restore the Sanchi lion-capital comparator and the two owned Nandi cases, and additionally searched pp. 108 and 110 for a Ramappa monolithic Nandi. That was verification inside the already-declared core source, not a second OCR research pass, and no new source of any kind was opened.

## Current-affairs source used

13. **Lesson 20, the single current linkage.** Search run: "Chola bronze repatriation India 2026 Archaeological Survey of India provenance research museum return". Item used: *The New Indian Express*, "'Shiva Nataraja' among Chola period bronze sculptures repatriated from US", report dated **14 May 2026**, read directly. Recorded content used: that the culture ministry reclaimed three antiquities which had been part of the collection at the Smithsonian's National Museum of Asian Art in the United States and which had been illegally taken away from temples in Tamil Nadu decades ago; that the three are bronze castings - a Chola-period "Shiva Nataraja", "Saint Sundarar with Paravai" of the sixteenth century Vijayanagara period, and "Somaskanda - Shiva and Uma" of the twelfth century Chola period; that the latter two reached New Delhi on Tuesday night while the Shiva Nataraja will return in 2028 because it is displayed in an ongoing exhibition at the United States museum under an agreed long-term loan; that the culture minister, announcing the repatriation, said the Archaeological Survey of India had traced the three artefacts and that thorough provenance research using archival records, field documentation and photographs of the temple taken in the 1950s and 1960s established that they were taken out of the country in an unauthorised manner; and that the retrieved idols are to be placed in the Gallery of Retrieved Antiquities at the Red Fort. The item is attributed to that dated newspaper report of a ministerial announcement; no primary government document was opened, and nothing is asserted about restitution law, policy, totals, institutional arrangements or the present condition or display of any object.
14. No other current item is used anywhere in this session. Nineteen of the twenty lessons record truthfully in their pre-teach checklists that no current linkage is required.

## Evidence, discrepancy and prohibition controls

- **Disclosed source discrepancy one - Black and Red Ware.** The core source calls Harappan painted pottery "Black and Red Ware" from its red ground and glossy black designs; the canonical Basic owner states that Harappan pottery is predominantly red ware painted in black and must not be collapsed into the separate wider archaeological category of that name, and the same core source separately lists Black and Red Ware among the wares coexisting with Painted Grey Ware. Both positions are stated openly in Lesson 4 and recorded in the error map and register notes; neither is dropped and the equation is refused.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy two - the Gupta "classical era" and Ellora.** The chapter opening calls the Gupta Empire of 319-550 AD a "classical" era for Indian sculpture for rock-cut caves including Ellora, while the same source dates the Ellora murals mostly to the sixth to ninth centuries AD under the Rashtrakutas and other dynasties and records them as newer than Ajanta's. The tension is stated in Lesson 13 and the "classical" label is treated as an evaluative judgement about an idiom rather than as an attribution of the Ellora excavations.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy three - the empty seat's episode.** The symbol table places the Empty Throne under the Great Renunciation; the summary note lists empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel and stupa as indicating renunciation, enlightenment, the first sermon and death; and the narrative-representation passage lists the throne among the general symbols without assigning an episode. All three are reported in Lesson 8 and the point is left unresolved, which is also why no answer is inferred for the owned 2026 objective item.
- **Disclosed correction five - the Gandhara political sequence.** An earlier draft of this session described the region as having passed through "four foreign powers" over "five centuries of successive foreign rule". Neither claim is supported: the source records **three** phases of control and no total or span, and **Chandragupta Maurya's rule is Indian, not foreign**, so the fourth-century phase is a Greek intervention followed by Indian imperial integration. Both claims were deleted; Lesson 9 now teaches only the recorded sequence with accurate labels, declines to supply a count or a span, names the later Indo-Greek, Saka and Parthian presences only as strands of influence visible in the sculpture, and carries "thoroughly cosmopolitan" as the source's own interpretation rather than as a demonstrated finding.
- **Disclosed owner-versus-source difference - the Kakatiya Nandi records.** The canonical owner's lion-and-bull evidence bank presents a monolithic Nandi and the "main temple in front of the Nandi Pavilion" together under Ramappa. In the core art source the monolithic Nandi is recorded for the **Thousand Pillar temple at Warangal** and the pavilion-and-approach relation for the **Ramappa temple**, and pp. 108 and 110 record no monolithic Nandi at Ramappa. The two cases are kept separate in Lessons 7 and 18 and in the register notes, and the difference is reported rather than resolved by transferring a detail.
- **Disclosed attribution of a strand name - the Saka.** The Central Asian strand is named by people only in the canonical owner, as Kushana, Parthian and Saka; the core art source's Gandhara passage names the strand geographically and does not mention the Saka. The Saka are therefore carried at owner authority, with an explicit statement that no Saka-specific feature, ruler, date or monument is recorded anywhere in the owned evidence.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy four - the post-Mauryan monument sequence.** The core source introduces its list of the most important post-Mauryan Buddhist monuments as being in chronological order, but prints them in the sequence Bharhut 150 BC, Great Stupa of Sanchi 50 BC, Second Stupa at Sanchi 125 BC, Third Sanchi Stupa 25 BC, Bodhgaya 75 BC, which is not the order the printed dates yield. Lesson 8 reports the printed sequence, reports the mismatch and teaches the date-derived order, instructing that the dates rather than the printed sequence be relied on.
- **Disclosed source variation - the Yali's anatomy.** Two formulations exist in the same source, one giving a cat-like body with a lion's head, elephant tusks and a serpent's tail and the other the head and body of a lion with an elephant's trunk and tusks. Both are recorded and neither is presented as an error.
- **Disclosed internal tension - Mughal figural sculpture.** The section's opening general claim that Islamic rulers were not interested in producing figurative sculptures is followed immediately by evidence of commissioned statues, animate motifs and carved animal forms. Both are reproduced in Lesson 19 and the resolution offered is one of different emphasis rather than absence.
- **Disclosed source damage - five clipped tables.** The ancient-pottery features column, the third column of the three-school comparison, the Tirthankara list beyond its sixth entry, the meaning of the Vajra mudra and the feature column of the craft-pottery list are clipped in the text layer available for this session. Each clipping is disclosed at the point of use, and no clipped cell is completed from memory anywhere in this session.
- **Disclosed coverage boundary - megalithic ceramics.** The megalithic burial forms are attested in the owned evidence - dolmens, cairns, menhirs and cists - and a reproduced objective item pairs Mangadu in Kerala with a megalithic site, but no megalithic ware description, type site or regional ceramic chronology is carried. Lesson 5 states the boundary explicitly and supplies the safe formulation.
- **Attribution prohibitions observed absolutely.** No patron, king or year is supplied for the Parkham Yaksha. The Didarganj figure is never dated firmly. The Sultanganj Buddha is never described as an uncontested Gupta-period work. No school is stated to have made the first human Buddha image. Chola patronage is never described as the origin of Nataraja iconography. The Rampurva bull pillar is never dated apart from Ashoka, and no explanation is invented for its lack of inscription. No meaning is added to the Sarnath abacus animals beyond the four recorded. No Nandi, Rudra-bull or "bull of dharma" myth is narrated. No mythological origin is supplied for the Kirtimukha. Neither recorded Yali anatomy is called an error.
- **Numerical prohibitions observed absolutely.** No alloy composition, weight, height or dimension is stated for any bronze. No firing temperature, vessel dimension or production figure is stated for any pottery. No pillar count, colonnette count, tuning or acoustic mechanism is stated for the musical pillars. The only measurements anywhere in this session are those carried in the sources - the Dancing Girl's four inches, the Sultanganj Buddha's 7.5 ft, the Ashokan pillars' average 40 ft, the miniature vessels' under half an inch, and the Amaravati Mahachaitya's hedged "believed to be originally 27 metres".
- **Hedges preserved.** The Mother Goddess figure is "probably worshipped for prosperity" and "might also have been a goddess of fertility sects". Perforated vessels "might have been used for straining liquor". The Painted Grey Ware range is written as 1100-500/400 BC with its internal alternative intact. The Amaravati height carries "believed". The Amaravati ayaka pillars "are believed to represent" five events. The Thousand Pillar temple is "believed to have been constructed" between the twelfth and fourteenth centuries.
- **Living-tradition prohibition observed absolutely.** No living terracotta centre, community or product - Bankura, Molela or any other - is named as verified evidence anywhere in this session, in accordance with the canonical owner's explicit instruction. The three supported continuities are stated instead, and the mechanism is offered where a question would demand names.
- **Status-claim discipline.** Conservation, restitution law, heritage institutions, protected-monument counts, inscription totals and current display arrangements are routed to the heritage topic throughout. The only current claims made anywhere are those carried in the single dated report recorded in Lesson 20, and they are attributed to that report.
- **Boundary discipline.** The stupa, the pillar as a structure, the cave, the temple plan, elevation and style school, the gopuram, vimana, mandapa and prakaram are routed to the architecture topics. Dynastic political chronology is routed to ancient and medieval history. Doctrinal meaning is routed to the religion topic. Dance posture as performance is routed to the performing-arts topic. Geographical-indication law and administration are left outside the topic.
- **Bounded-research discipline.** The substantive base is the complete canonical Basic owner, the complete canonical Advanced owner and **one** core OCR art source. The owner's two further grounding volumes were deliberately left unopened under that one-source rule, and their owner-derived cautions were carried at owner authority instead. External work was limited to exact PYQ verification and routing, one pre-declared three-item material-fact pass, and one brief dated current linkage, after which the pass was closed; the later surgical repair pass re-read five pages of the same core source and opened nothing new.
- **Prohibited-source exclusion observed.** No artifact under the permanently excluded final-learner-package directory was read, searched, cited or compared against at any point in this pass, and no learner-v2 artifact was used.
